L34: 1. Executive Summary
L35: 1.1. Product Introduction
L36: Fluorodopa F 18 injection (18F-FDOPA) is a PET radiopharmaceutical that crosses the blood-brain barrier and is converted to F-18 fluorodopamine by aromatic amino acid decarboxylase (AAAD) in the striatum. The resultant F-18 fluorodopamine is then stored in the vesicles of the striatal terminals of dopaminergic neurons.
L37: 18F-FDOPA is proposed as a Positron Emission Tomography (PET) agent for use in the evaluation of patients with suspected Parkinsonian syndromes (PS). The Parkinsonian symptoms of PS are caused by degeneration of dopaminergic neurons in the brain. Due to the infeasibility of histopathological confirmation of this process, diagnosis of PS relies on clinical evaluation. However, significant overlap in clinical features between PS and a host of non-Parkinsonian syndromes (non-PS) can lead to uncertainty in clinical diagnosis. In contradistinction to PS, the Parkinsonian symptoms of non-PS are not due to degeneration of dopaminergic neurons. Diagnostic distinction between PS and non-PS is clinically important because of differences in patient management.
L38: The mechanism of 18F-FDOPA allows it to act as a marker of dopaminergic neurodegeneration when imaged by PET. In the setting of PS, the uptake and storage of 18F-FDOPA in the striatum will be reduced. The resultant PET imaging will show decreased signal unilaterally or bilaterally in the putamen and possibly the caudate. In the setting of non-PS, 18F-FDOPA PET imaging will show normal signal in the caudate and putamen bilaterally. Of note, even patients at the onset of PS often have substantial degeneration of dopaminergic neurons in the brain. PET imaging with 18F-FDOPA is thus proposed as an adjunct to other clinical evaluation in the assessment of patients with suspected PS.
L39: 18F-FDOPA was one of the unapproved PET drugs in clinical use pending the development by FDA of cGMP regulations governing the production of PET drugs. In 2000, FDA review of the published literature concluded that 18F-FDOPA “marks the dopaminergic system and may be used as a biomarker”. Since the establishment of FDA cGMP regulation of PET drugs, 18F-FDOPA has continued to be studied in the US under INDs and has also been used under expanded access INDs.
L40: 18F-FDOPA PET was approved in France in 2010 followed by several other countries in the European Union. 18F-FDOPA approval in France and other EU countries was based solely on published clinical studies, particularly the study by Eshuis et al. (2009)1 discussed in Section 8.1.2.
L41: 1 Eshuis et al. Direct comparison of FP-CIT SPECT and F-DOPA PET in patients with Parkinson’s disease and healthy controls. Eur J Nucl Med Mol Imaging (2009) 36:454–462
L42: 1.2. Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L43: This resubmission addressed all deficiencies identified in previous review cycles. The first FDA review in 2012 concluded that the submitted retrospective chart review and published literature were inadequate to support a 505(b)(2) application, and the FDA recommended that the Applicant conduct a prospective clinical study. In response, the Applicant prospectively conducted Study #17. However, this study was found to have the following major deficiencies that lead to a complete response in a review cycle ending in 2016:
L44: • An incomplete reference standard since a substantial number of patients did not complete clinical follow-up at the pre-specified minimum time interval of 1-year.• Potential bias in generating the clinical reference standard since the image interpretation results of 18F-FDOPA PET were known to the reference standard clinicians prior to their determination of follow-up diagnosis.
L45: Study #18 from the Applicant's current submission adequately addressed these prior deficiencies. In this prospective trial, a total of 56 patients with possible PS out of the 68 enrolled completed the pre-specified requirement of at least 6 months of clinical follow-up. Furthermore, the reference standard was established by a movement disorder specialist who remained blinded to 18F-FDOPA PET results. Thus, the clinical reference standard used in Study #18 was found to be satisfactory by the review team. Visual interpretation of 18F-FDOPA PET imaging with a binary reading method demonstrated a high negative percent agreement (with a 95% confidence interval as low as 61% and as high as 99%) relative to the clinical reference standard in all 3 blinded, independent readers but lower positive percent agreement (with a 95% confidence interval as low as 31% and as high as 87%) among these same 3 readers. The review team concluded that these results support the effectiveness of 18F-FDOPA PET for use as an adjunct to other diagnostic evaluations in patients with suspected PS.
L46: 1.3. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L47: Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
L48: Parkinsonism is a general clinical term used to describe any combination of bradykinesia, rest tremor, rigidity, and postural instability. Movement disorders featuring Parkinsonism can be divided into those that are associated with degeneration of dopaminergic neurons (referred to as Parkinsonian syndromes (PS)) and those that are not (referred to as non-Parkinsonian syndromes (non-PS)).
L49: PS includes progressive and serious disorders such as Parkinson's disease, multiple system atrophy (MSA), progressive supranuclear palsy (PSP), and corticobasal degeneration (CBD). Non-PS includes essential tremor, dystonic tremor, psychogenic Parkinsonism, drug-induced Parkinsonism, vascular Parkinsonism, and other conditions.
L50: Diagnostic distinction between PS and non-PS is clinically important because of respective differences in patient management. Due to the infeasibility of histopathological confirmation, differentiation of patients with PS from those with non-PS continues to be based on clinical evaluation. Unfortunately, overlap in clinical features between PS and non-PS often makes distinguishing between them challenging. Strategies to address such diagnostic uncertainty are limited and not ideal, such as trial of levodopa therapy and continued long-term clinical follow-up.
L51: 18F-FDOPA PET provides an adjunctive non-invasive approach to evaluation of this patient population. As a radiolabeled analog of levodopa, 18F-FDOPA uptake in the striatum can be imaged by PET to assess dopaminergic neurons. In the setting of PS, 18F-FDOPA PET imaging should show decreased striatal signal. In the setting of non-PS, 18F-FDOPA should show normal striatal signal.
L52: In this resubmission, the Applicant provided results from an adequate and well-controlled prospective trial (Study #18) that addressed deficiencies noted by the FDA in prior review cycles. A clinical reference standard was established in 56 patients with suspected PS through at least 6 months of clinical follow-up by a movement disorder specialist who remained blinded to 18F-FDOPA PET results. Visual interpretation of 18F-FDOPA PET imaging with a binary reading method demonstrated a high negative percent agreement (with a 95% confidence interval as low as 61% and as high as 99%) in all 3 blinded, independent readers relative to the clinical reference standard but lower positive percent agreement (with a 95% confidence interval as low as 31% and as high as 87%) among these same 3 readers. The review team concluded that these results adequately support the effectiveness of 18F-FDOPA PET for use an adjunct to other diagnostic evaluations in patients with suspected PS.
L53: Safety of 18F-FDOPA is supported by extensive clinical experience with much higher and long-term dosing of the closely related therapeutic drug, levodopa. Additionally, review of safety data collected from 121 patients exposed to 18F-FDOPA in Study #17 and Study #18 raises no concerns. Patient radiation exposure from 18F-FDOPA PET (estimated effective dose of 5.6 mSv) is similar to that of other commonly used diagnostic radiopharmaceuticals. The review team concluded that risks of 18F-FDOPA are low and further mitigated by its recommended single dosage.
L54: The low risks of 18F-FDOPA PET are outweighed by its benefit as an adjunct to other diagnostic evaluations in patients with suspected PS.
L55: DimensionEvidence and UncertaintiesConclusions and Reasons




Analysis of Condition

Movement disorders featuring Parkinsonism that is caused by dopaminergic degeneration (referred to as Parkinsonian syndromes (PS)) include:

Idiopathic Parkinson's disease (IPD)
Progressive supranuclear palsy (PSP)
Multiple system atrophy (MSA)
Corticobasal degeneration (CBD)

Movement disorders featuring Parkinsonism that is not caused by dopaminergic degeneration (referred to as non-Parkinsonian syndromes (non-PS)) include:

Essential tremor (ET)
Isolated resting tremor
Dystonic tremor
Drug-induced Parkinsonism
Vascular Parkinsonism
Psychogenic Parkinsonism


PS includes several serious, progressive disorders that may respond to therapy directed at dopaminergic degeneration, such as oral levodopa.
Diagnostic distinction between PS and non-PS is clinically important because of respective differences in patient management.
Differentiation of patients with PS from patients with non-PS is currently based on clinical evaluation.
Clinical differentiation of PS and non-PS is often difficult due to shared signs and symptoms between these groups of disorders.
L56: NDA 200655 / Fluorodopa F 18 Injection: Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
L57: DimensionEvidence and UncertaintiesConclusions and Reasons




Current Treatment Options

DaTscan is a SPECT imaging agent approved to help differentiate essential tremor (non-PS) from tremor due to PS.
Brain CT and MRI contribute little to diagnosis or differentiation of PS and non-PS beyond potentially excluding other pathology.
Other diagnostic imaging options for differentiating PS and non-PS are investigational at this time.

Other than DaTscan, no approved imaging tests typically contribute to traditional clinical evaluation of patients with suspected PS.
DaTscan is for use with SPECT while 18F-FDOPA is for use with PET.
PET is a higher resolution imaging technique than SPECT, allowing improved image appearance and reduced scan time.
18F-FDOPA has a different molecular mechanism of action than DaTscan.



Benefit

In Study #18, 18F-FDOPA PET demonstrated high negative percent agreement but low positive percent agreement with a reference standard of PS or non-PS that was established by clinical follow-up.
Similar results were demonstrated in the clinically relevant subgroup of patients who had symptoms for 2 years or less.

Study #18 adequately addressed deficiencies noted in the previous complete response.
18F-FDOPA PET can be used as an adjunct to other diagnostic evaluations for patients with suspected PS.



Risk and Risk Management

18F-FDOPA safety is supported by extensive clinical experience with much higher and long-term dosing of the closely related therapeutic drug, levodopa.
Available safety data from Study #17 and Study #18 demonstrate a benign safety profile for 18F-FDOPA.
Patient radiation exposure from 18F-FDOPA PET (estimated effective dose of 5.6 mSv) is similar to that of other diagnostic radiopharmaceuticals.

Potential risks of 18F-FDOPA use are low.
18F-FDOPA is intended for single use thus further limiting risks from radiation and drug exposure.
L58: 2. Therapeutic Context
L59: 2.1. Analysis of Condition
L60: Parkinsonism is a general clinical term used to describe any combination of bradykinesia, rest tremor, rigidity, and postural instability. Movement disorders featuring Parkinsonism can be divided into those that are associated with degeneration of dopaminergic neurons and those that are not. In this review, the former group of disorders in which Parkinsonism is caused by dopaminergic degeneration will be referred to as Parkinsonian syndromes (PS) and the latter group in which Parkinsonism is not caused by degeneration of dopaminergic neurons will be termed non-Parkinsonian syndromes (non-PS).
L61: PS includes Idiopathic Parkinson's disease (IPD) and Atypical Parkinson's disease (APD). APD can be further subdivided into multiple system atrophy (MSA), progressive supranuclear palsy (PSP), corticobasal degeneration (CBD), and other disorders. These diseases are progressive and serious, often resulting in significant patient disability. In the setting of PS, 18F-FDOPA PET imaging should show decreased striatal signal, as referred to in this review as a positive scan.
L62: Non-PS includes essential tremor, dystonic tremor, psychogenic Parkinsonism, drug-induced Parkinsonism, vascular Parkinsonism, and other conditions. In the setting of non-PS, 18F-FDOPA should show normal striatal signal, as referred to in this review as a negative scan.
L63: Diagnostic distinction between PS and non-PS is clinically important because of respective differences in patient management. Patients with PS, particularly IPD, may benefit from therapies directed at dopaminergic degeneration, such as oral levodopa. Conversely, levodopa treatment response would typically not be expected in patients with non-PS.
L64: Although definitive distinction of PS and non-PS could be made histopathologically, obtaining brain tissue for this purpose is impractical. Thus, differentiation of patients with PS from those with non-PS continues to be based on clinical evaluation. However, overlap in clinical features between PS and non-PS often makes distinguishing between them challenging. Strategies to address such diagnostic uncertainty are limited and not ideal, such as trial levodopa therapy and continued long-term clinical follow-up.
L65: 2.2. Analysis of Current Treatment Options
L66: DaTscan (loflupane I 123 Injection, I-123 FP-CIT) is FDA approved for very similar indications to those currently proposed for 18F-FDOPA:
L67: DaTscan is a radiopharmaceutical indicated for striatal dopamine transporter visualization using single photon emission computed tomography (SPECT) brain imaging to assist in the evaluation of adult patients with suspected Parkinsonian syndromes (PS). In these patients, DaTscan may be used to help differentiate essential tremor from tremor due to PS (idiopathic Parkinson's disease, multiple system atrophy and progressive supranuclear palsy). DaTscan is an adjunct to other diagnostic evaluations.
L68: While both DaTscan and 18F-FDOPA are targeted to the terminals of dopaminergic neurons in the striatum, their precise mechanisms of localization differ. DaTscan binds directly to the dopamine transporter (DaT) while 18F-FDOPA is a levodopa analog. The I-123 radiolabel of DaTscan makes it compatible with SPECT imaging. PET has higher spatial resolution than SPECT and is compatible with the F-18 radiolabel of 18F-FDOPA.
L69: Although numerous CT and MRI contrast agents are approved for brain imaging and are used extensively for this purpose, CT and MRI have very limited utility for the imaging and differentiation of PS and non-PS. Some studies suggest a possible role for imaging glucose metabolism with 18F-FDG PET in the setting of Parkinson's disease, although this use currently remains investigational.
L70: 3. Regulatory Background
L71: 3.1. U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
L72: The first New Drug Application (NDA 200655) for 18F-FDOPA was submitted to FDA on October 29, 2009. This submission was based on peer-reviewed publications. FDA refused to file the application on the basis that the submission did not contain adequate information to allow a substantial review.
L73: The second New Drug Application (NDA 200655) for 18F-FDOPA was submitted to FDA on October 23, 2012. The submitted clinical data was based on a retrospective chart review of 185 patients at the Applicant's institution. FDA review found the submitted clinical data to be inadequate to support the proposed indication and issued a Complete Response (CR) letter dated 10/22/2013. The FDA recommended that the Applicant conduct a prospective clinical study to support the proposed indication.
L74: The Applicant re-submitted the NDA on December 15, 2015. The submission included a prospective clinical study (Study #17) of 45 patients that was conducted under an expanded access IND at the Applicant's own institution. FDA review found this study to be poorly conducted with a substantial percentage of patients lacking the prespecified one-year clinical follow-up as the standard of reference. In addition, FDA review noted that the PET image interpretations were known to the physicians providing clinical follow-up prior to their determination of the clinical diagnosis, leading to potential bias in the resultant reference standard. FDA again issued a CR letter dated 09/15/2016.
L75: The current resubmission is a response to the most recent FDA CR letter dated 09/15/2016. This resubmission is based on a new prospective clinical study (Study #18) that was developed with FDA guidance and completed at the Applicant's own institution.
L76: 3.2. Foreign Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
L77: 18F-FDOPA PET was approved in France in 2010 followed by several other countries in the European Union. Fluorodopa F 18 PET approval in France and other EU countries was based solely on clinical studies in the published literature. These clinical studies were not conducted by the pharmaceutical companies who market the drug.
L78: European tradenames for 18F-FDOPA are Dopacis and IASODopa. Approved indications include uses in neurology and oncology. The neurology indication for Dopacis according to the product's labeling is as follows:
L79: PET with fluorodopa (18F) is indicated for detecting loss of functional dopaminergic neuron terminals in the striatum in patients with clinically uncertain parkinsonian syndromes. It can be used to differentiate essential tremor from parkinsonian syndromes related to degenerative diseases affecting the nigrostriatal system (Parkinson disease (PD), multisystem atrophy and progressive supranuclear palsy).
L80: PET with fluorodopa (^{18}\text{F}), on its own, is unable to discriminate between different parkinsonian syndromes related to degenerative diseases affecting the nigrostriatal system. It is also unable to discriminate between PD with and without tremor.
L81: 4. Significant Issues From Other Review Disciplines Pertinent to Clinical Conclusions on Efficacy and Safety
L82: 4.1. Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI)
L83: Applicant-investigator inspection of The Feinstein Institutes for Medical Research was performed as a data audit for NDA 200655 during the first review cycle in 2013. The inspections revealed that available records did not appear to meet the good clinical practice (GCP) standards for an NDA. A prospective study protocol and case report forms (CRFs) were not used and important study records (including source records) were not available for inspectional review. A Form 483 was issued for: (1) failure to prepare and maintain adequate case histories, and (2) inadequate investigational drug disposition records with respect to dates, quantity, and use by subjects.
L84: During the subsequent review cycle of the resubmitted NDA in 2016, Study #17 was audited with GCP inspection of the Applicant-investigator site. No significant deficiencies were noted. The overall study conduct appeared GCP-compliant, including oversight of study conduct. All audited data were adequately verifiable against source records and case report forms. Given these recent satisfactory inspection findings at the same site where Study #18 was conducted, no audit was performed for the current resubmission.
L85: 5. Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology Executive Summary
L86: This NDA should be approved from a nonclinical perspective.
L87: The Applicant did not conduct any nonclinical studies to support the application. The initial nonclinical submission for approval of “Fluorodopa F 18 Injection” was reviewed by Dr. Sally Hargus. Nonclinical recommended the approval of the NDA based upon:
L88: • Structural similarities between 18F-FDOPA and levodopa.• The availability of extensive nonclinical and clinical databases on levodopa.• The availability of a nonclinical pharmacology data on 18F-FDOPA.• The safety of 18F-FDOPA demonstrated by historical use of levodopa for treatment of Parkinson’s disease at prescribed doses of up to 2000 mg per day for chronic use (Carbidopa/Levodopa Monograph 2013), which is more than (b) (4)-fold the one-time intravenously-administered 18F-FDOPA mass dose of (b) (4) mg or less.
L89: Moreover, in 2000, FDA conducted a multidisciplinary review and evaluation of 18F-FDOPA injection in preparation for a public meeting with the clinical PET community. Dr. Laniyonu2 wrote the nonclinical portion of FDA review based on publicly available nonclinical information on 18F-FDOPA and nonclinical and clinical literature on levodopa. Nonclinical reiterates the initial recommendation for the NDA application.3 4
L90: Recommendation
L91: The application is recommended for approval from a nonclinical perspective.

L101: Post-Marketing Requirements and Commitments
L102: None.
L103: 6.1. Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Assessment
L104: 6.1.1. Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
L105: There are no new clinical pharmacology studies conducted as a part of this new submission. This re-submission contains only one additional clinical study, Study #18.
L106: In this study, 150 mg of carbidopa was administered to patients orally 60 minutes (approximately) prior to the administration of 18F-FDOPA. The use of carbidopa may increase 18F-FDOPA bioavailability to the brain by inhibiting peripheral decarboxylase activity and restricting peripheral 18F-FDOPA metabolism.
L107: The study dose was 5±10% mCi of 18F-FDOPA, which is not significantly different than that used in the clinical study submitted in the original NDA (Study #15) and first CR resubmission (Study #17).
L108: At about 70 minutes post injection patients were asked to void to remove radioactivity collected in the bladder. Patients were then positioned for scanning. Images were acquired as follows: a 9 second CT scan was initiated at about 79 minutes post injection followed by a 3D PET scan from 80 to 100 minutes.
L109: 5 Clinical Pharmacology Primary Review dated June 13, 2013
L110: 6 Clinical Pharmacology Primary Review dated April 14, 2016
L111: For this clinical study, ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA PET scans were interpreted by visual observation of the scan, based upon the appearance of the putamen and caudate in the striatum. In patients with PS, ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA PET images are expected to reflect degeneration of dopaminergic neurons through a decrease in striatal signal, referred to in this review as a positive scan. In non-PS patients, ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA PET is expected to show normal striatal signal, referred to in this review as a negative scan.
L112: Three radiologists evaluated the PET images. High negative percent agreement (point estimate range of 83% to 91%) and lower positive percent agreement (point estimate range of 49% to 73%) were obtained, confirming that ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA PET can be used as an adjunct to other diagnostic evaluations.
L113: In addition, the Applicant conducted an analysis based on semi-quantitative measurements using striatal/occipital ratio (SOR) to assess correlation with visual PET scan results. (b) (4)
L114: This SOR quantification does not require any special software program. For Study 18, the SOR values were not used for the visual determination of the PET scan results. The PET image readers and the physician establishing the reference standard were blinded to the SOR values.
L115: For Study 18, the average values of the left posterior putamen for the positive scans was 1.790 compared to the average values of the left posterior putamen for the negative scans of 2.627. The average SOR value calculated for negative PET scans is similar to that measured in normal subjects from the Applicant's previous retrospective study (2.545). The average SOR value for positive scans is reduced considerably relative to that of negative scans due to reduced uptake of ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA which in turn is attributed to loss of dopaminergic neurons. The SOR values in the caudate and putamen of subjects with PS are expected to be lower than normal.
L116: Observed overlap in the SOR values of Study #18 points to their limited diagnostic utility.
L117: It should be noted that all patients on Study 18 received 150 mg carbidopa 60 minutes prior to ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA Injection administration. The use of carbidopa may increase ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA bioavailability to the brain by inhibiting peripheral decarboxylase activity and restricting peripheral ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA metabolism.
L118: The Applicant had not submitted any information on effect of other concomitant drug on the quality of ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA injection. An information request was sent to the Applicant to "describe drug interaction potential for ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA and effect of the concomitant drugs on ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA PET images. Include discussion of the following drugs: 1) Haloperidol, 2) Monoamine oxidase inhibitors, 3) Reserpine, 4) Aripiprazole, and 4) Dopamine agonist and antagonist drugs." The Applicant submitted the concomitant medications taken by each patient. The data showed that for all patients with the standard of reference, 34 patients received no concomitant medication for movement disorder and were roughly equally distributed across positive and negative scans. 15 patients were taking levodopa in some form and this group had both positive and negative scans. Use of other medication combinations was limited to small numbers of subjects and could not be reliably interpreted.
L119: The Applicant's data showed that in patients with a reference standard diagnosis of PS, no dopamine agonists, amantadine, anticholinergics, or catechol-O-methyltransferase (COMT) inhibitors were used. The Applicant has not conducted any concomitant drug effect study on the quality of 18F-FDOPA PET scan. Patients with PS could potentially be taking dopamine agonists, dopamine re-uptake inhibitors, and other dopamine releasing agents such as psychostimulants of the amphetamine class that could affect the quality of 18F-FDOPA scan as per literature reports. Therefore, the following labeling language is recommended for Section 7 of package insert:
L120: Aromatic L-amino acid decarboxylase (AADC) inhibitors
L121: Prior to Fluorodopa F 18 Injection administration, use of AADC inhibitors (e.g., carbidopa, benserazide, etc.) may increase Fluorodopa F 18 bioavailability to the brain by inhibiting peripheral decarboxylase activity and restricting peripheral Fluorodopa F 18 metabolism [see Dosage and Administration (2.3)].
L122: Dopamine agonists, dopamine reuptake inhibitors, dopamine releasing agents (DRAs), peripheral catechol-O-methyltransferase (COMT) inhibitors, and monoamine oxidase (MAO) inhibitors
L123: Therapy for Parkinson's syndromes includes dopamine agonists, dopamine reuptake inhibitors, dopamine releasing agents (DRAs) such as psychostimulants of the amphetamine class, peripheral catechol-O-methyltransferase (COMT) inhibitors, and monoamine oxidase (MAO) inhibitors. Whether discontinuation of these drugs prior to Fluorodopa F 18 administration may minimize the interference with a Fluorodopa F 18 image is not fully known; however, if use of these drugs can be safely suspended, discontinue use 12 hours before administration of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection [see Dosage and Administration 2.3].
L124: 7. Sources of Clinical Data and Review Strategy
L125: In response to the 2016 FDA CR letter in which FDA identified serious design and conduct deficiencies in clinical Study #17, the Applicant conducted a new prospective clinical study (Study #18) under the Expanded Access IND 78861 for ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA at the Feinstein Institutes. Since Study #18 was submitted to address the clinical and statistical deficiencies listed in the FDA CR Letter, the current review is focused on this study.
L126: The current review also summarizes relevant clinical studies from prior review cycles that are supportive of ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA efficacy for the proposed indication.
L127: 8. Statistical and Clinical and Evaluation
L128: 8.1. Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
L129: 8.1.1. Study #18
L130: Overview and Objective
L131: The protocol for Study #18 was submitted by the Applicant in response to the 9/15/16 CR letter issued by the FDA following the review of the re-submitted NDA 200655 containing Study #17. Study #18 was designed to address deficiencies cited in the CR letter and planned to recruit a minimum of 50 patients with possible PS. The primary objective of the study was to assess the imaging performance 18F-FDOPA PET in 3 blinded readers relative to a clinical reference standard generated through at least 6 months of patient follow-up.
L132: Trial Design
L133: Study #18 was a prospective, single center study. Binary interpretations of 18F-FDOPA scans by 3 independent readers were analyzed for agreement with a clinical reference standard of PS or non-PS, as established by a minimum of 6 months of patient follow-up.
L134: Enrollment Criteria and Patient Population (Disease Characteristics)
L135: Enrollment Criteria: All patients had to be 18 years of age or older with at least one of the cardinal symptoms of PS (defined by the Applicant as bradykinesia, rigidity, tremor, and postural instability). The patients were referred by neurologists for further evaluation due to uncertainty of clinical diagnosis. Because the Applicant expected difficulty enrolling patients with at least 2 cardinal symptoms of PS, both Study #17 and #18 had pre-specified entry criteria of only at least one cardinal symptom. However, most of the patients enrolled in Study #18 had at least 2 cardinal symptoms at enrollment, as shown in Table 2 below.
L136: Disease duration included those newly diagnosed (months) to those having the disease for years as also listed in Table 2.
L137: Table 2. Cardinal Symptom(s) and Disease Duration of Each Patient at Study Enrollment
L138: Subject IDSymptom 1Symptom 2Symptom 3Symptom 4Disease Duration



(b) (4)
L139: Subject IDSymptom 1Symptom 2Symptom 3Symptom 4Disease Duration
L140: (b) (4)
L141: Source: Applicant response to FDA Clinical Information Requests Dated 06-28-2019 and 07-10-2019
L142: Among the 56 patients who completed the study, 38 (68%) were male, 18 (32%) were female. Mean age was 66 years. Thirty-three of the patients had symptoms for 2 years or less.
L143: Patient Disposition
L144: Out of the 69 recruited patients, 68 patients underwent ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA and 56 completed at least 6-month follow-up clinical assessment to establish the reference standard. The clinical follow-up period ranged from 6 to 9 months post-imaging in these 56 patients, all of whom were included in the analysis of efficacy.
L145: Study Drug Dosing
L146: Approximately 5 mCi of ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA (range from 4.5-5.5 mCi) was administered by intravenous injection. The F-DOPA mass dose ranged from (b) (4) mg per injection. Approximately one hour before dosing with ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA, patients were administered 150 mg of carbidopa orally to block peripheral metabolism of ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA. A 150 mg dose of carbidopa was selected from the investigator's own experience as well as that described in other peer-reviewed publications.
L147: Image Acquisition and Interpretation
L148: Approximately 70 minutes following ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA administration, patients voided the bladder. PET images of the brain were collected for 20 minutes starting 80 minutes after ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA administration.
L149: Three readers blinded to patient clinical information independently interpreted ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA PET images visually. The scan images had all identifiable marking removed.
L150: The 3 readers evaluated the shape and size of the putamen and caudate bilaterally. Reduced PET signal in the putamen or both putamen and caudate, unilaterally or bilaterally, was interpreted as a positive scan. Normal comma-shaped activity in the putamen and caudate bilaterally was interpreted as a negative scan.
L151: Standard of Reference
L152: Clinical diagnosis at 6 to 9 months after ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA PET was used as the reference standard. This reference standard clinical diagnosis was established by a single movement disorder specialist through clinical evaluation of each patient at baseline (prior to the PET scan) and at follow-up a minimum of 6 months after the PET scan. The movement disorder specialist was not a referring physician. At all times, the movement disorder specialist was blinded to ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA PET results and any clinical information collected by referring physicians. Serial patient evaluation allowed the movement disorder specialist to assess interval change in signs and symptoms or interval response to trial therapy.
L153: At baseline and at follow-up, the movement disorder specialist classified each patient as PS ("Dopaminergic Degeneration" per case report form) or non-PS ("No Dopaminergic Degeneration" per case report form). The categorization at follow-up was used as the reference standard in the study. Of note, the movement disorder specialist was not reminded of their own baseline diagnoses at the time of follow-up evaluation. Of the 56 patients who completed follow-up, 33 patients were categorized as PS and 23 patients were categorized as non-PS. In approximately 80% (45/56) of patients, the movement disorder specialist's categorization of PS
L154: or non-PS was concordant at baseline and follow-up, indicating a high level of reproducibility of clinical evaluations.
L155: To improve upon weaknesses in the reference standard of Study #17, Study #18 included detailed case report forms that were completed by the movement disorder specialist both at baseline clinical evaluation and follow-up clinical evaluation. One of these case report forms was the Unified Parkinson Disease Rating Scale (UPDRS), a standardized and well-accepted clinical tool for the evaluation of patients with Parkinsonian symptoms.
L156: The UPDRS is divided into the following 4 sections:
L157: 1) Mentation, behavior, and mood2) Activities of daily living3) Motor examination4) Complications of therapy
L158: Each section of the UPDRS contains multiple questions or items that add to a final score of 0 to 199 points. Baseline and follow-up UPDRS scores of the 56 patients who completed the study were provided in the Applicant's submission.
L159: In addition to the UPDRS, the movement disorder specialist completed a survey at each clinical evaluation for the presence or absence of 21 diagnostic features as well as a separate list to weigh evidence of a PS diagnosis against evidence of a non-PS diagnosis. Another case report form captured the final classification of PS or non-PS at each clinical evaluation. On this form, the movement disorder specialist also chose from a more detailed list of sub-diagnoses for exploratory purposes. At follow-up evaluation, the PS patient category included the following sub-diagnoses:
L160: • Parkinson's disease• Parkinson's disease with dementia• Progressive supranuclear palsy• Corticobasal degeneration• Multiple systems atrophy• Non-specific Parkinsonism
L161: At the follow-up evaluation, the non-PS patient category included the following sub-diagnoses:
L162: • Essential tremor• Non-specific tremor• Non-specific gait disorder• No movement disorder• Psychogenic Parkinsonism• Vascular Parkinsonism• Drug induced Parkinsonism• Dystonia and dystonic tremor
L163: Of note, 18F-FDOPA PET was evaluated for its ability to distinguish between PS and non-PS groups, but not to identify a particular sub-diagnosis.
L164: Data Quality/Integrity and Compliance With Good Clinical Practices
L165: The Applicant did not submit a statement attesting that Study #18 was conducted in accordance with Good Clinical Practice. The study was conducted under an expanded access IND. The Applicant emphasized in the resubmission that the study was conducted under Institutional Review Board (IRB) oversight.
L166: In previous review cycles, the FDA worked with the Applicant to develop case report forms to document data collection and avoid potential bias (e.g. by unblinding of PET readers). In addition to the movement disorder specialist's case report forms discussed above for establishing the clinical reference standard, there were 6 additional case report forms used to collect imaging data and other clinical information in Study #18.
L167: • Case Report Form #1 collected the initial clinical diagnosis of the referring physician.• Case Report Form #2 documented study eligibility.• Case Report Form #3 recorded adverse events during the day of 18F-FDOPA administration.• Case Report Form #4 recorded adverse events at telephone safety follow-up.• Case Report Form #5 recorded the Striatal Occipital Ratio (SOR) values for the left and right putamen and caudate. The SOR was calculated after the blinded PET reading for exploratory purposes.• Case Report Form #6 recorded 18F-FDOPA PET interpretations as positive or negative by a single PET reader.
L168: Blank copies of each case report form were provided in the submission. A blank adverse event tracking log was also provided. Overall, the review team had no concerns regarding data quality and integrity.
L169: Efficacy Results – Primary Analysis
L170: The primary endpoints of Study #18 were positive percent agreement (PPA) and negative percent agreement (NPA) between binary 18F-FDOPA PET results (positive or negative) and the binary reference standard clinical diagnosis at follow-up (PS or non-PS). PPA is defined as the percentage of patients with positive 18F-FDOPA PET results among all patients with a clinical reference standard of PS. NPA is defined as the percentage of patients with negative 18F-FDOPA PET results among all patients with a clinical reference standard of non-PS. While these definitions of PPA and NPA mathematically might equate to sensitivity and specificity, respectively, the review team believes that PPA and NPA are more appropriate terms for Study #18 since a clinical reference standard is not as definitive as a histopathological truth standard.
L171: The following table shows PPA and NPA of each reader relative to the clinical reference standard.

L175: While all 3 readers demonstrated relatively high NPA values, the lower bound of the 95% CI for PPA extended below 50% in Reader 2 and Reader 3.
L176: Efficacy Results – Exploratory Brain Regional Quantitative 18F-FDOPA Uptake Analysis
L177: The Applicant also conducted an exploratory quantitative analysis of 18F-FDOPA PET based on uptake measurements in 6 striatal brain regions: left posterior putamen, right posterior putamen, left anterior putamen, right anterior putamen, left caudate, and right caudate. Each region's 18F-FDOPA uptake was compared to the uptake in the occipital lobe, which was used as an internal control region to calculate the Striatal Occipital Ratio (SOR).

L182: In all 6 striatal brain regions, the SOR ranges overlap in patients with 18F-FDOPA PET interpreted visually as negative or positive. This overlap in range appears to limit the potential clinical utility of quantitative analysis of 18F-FDOPA PET. No further exploration of the performance of SOR was undertaken.
L183: 8.1.2. Major Review Findings From Prior Review Cycles
L184: Major Review Findings of NDA Submission Dated October 23, 2012
L185: This prior 505(b)(2) NDA submission was predominantly literature-based. It contained the Applicant's own retrospective chart review of clinical experience at the Feinstein Institutes (Study #15), a publication (Study #16) of clinical follow-up of a subset of subjects in Study #15, and 7 additional publications. Overall, the FDA review found the submitted clinical data to be
L186: inadequate to support the proposed indication and issued a CR letter dated 10/22/2013. The FDA recommended that the Applicant conduct a prospective clinical study to support the proposed indication.
L187: The main study, Study #15, was based on a retrospective chart review of 185 patients and 11 normal controls at the Applicant's institution. Out of the 185 patients, 158 were positive on ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA PET and responded to Parkinson's disease drug treatment (e.g. levodopa). The other 27 patients were negative on ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA PET and did not respond to Parkinson's disease drug treatment. The Applicant inferred that positive ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA PET results in the 158 patients correctly indicated loss of dopaminergic neurons in these patients. The Applicant further inferred that negative ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA PET results in the 27 patients correctly indicated the absence of dopaminergic neurons loss in these patients. The review team concluded that the study had serious deficiencies including lack of a pre-specified protocol and use of an invalid standard of reference. Furthermore, the FDA inspection confirmed that the study did not meet Good Clinical Practice standards.
L188: Of the additional 7 publications, most did not provide relevant clinical data to demonstrate the efficacy of ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA in the product's intended population for the proposed indication. However, the review team considered one publication that compared ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA PET to an approved comparator, I-123 FP-CIT SPECT (DaTscan), to be supportive of efficacy:
L189: Eshuis et al.7 Direct comparison of FP-CIT SPECT and F-DOPA PET in patients with Parkinson's disease and healthy controls
L190: This study was conducted at a single center in Europe and evaluated 11 early-stage PD patients, 17 advanced-stage PD patients, and 20 age-matched healthy control subjects. Early-stage PD patients had disease duration of less than 3 years and were not using anti-Parkinson's medication. Advanced-stage PD patients had disease duration of 5 to 15 years and were using anti-Parkinson's medication with a documented response. All PD patients had at least 2 cardinal symptoms of PD.
L191: Each of the PD patients in the study underwent I-123 FP-CIT SPECT and ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA PET. Each control subject had either I-123 FP-CIT SPECT or ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA PET (10 in each group). Semi-quantitative striatooccipital ratios (SOR) were obtained in 6 brain regions (bilateral putamen, bilateral caudate, and bilateral striatum). FP-CIT SPECT and FDOPA PET scans in PD patients were considered abnormal when the SOR values were less than 2 standard deviations below the mean SOR values of the healthy controls. Receiver operating curves (ROC) were produced and sensitivity and specificity were calculated.
L192: The study reported similar semi-quantitative performance characteristics between ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA PET and an approved imaging comparator for differentiation of patients with PD from healthy subjects. The study is considered supportive.
L193: 7 Eshuis et al. Direct comparison of FP-CIT SPECT and F-DOPA PET in patients with Parkinson's disease and healthy controls. Eur J Nucl Med Mol Imaging (2009) 36:454–462
L194: Major Review Findings of NDA Resubmission Dated December 15, 2015
L195: The submission included a prospective clinical study (Study #17) with a design similar to that of Study #18. A total of 45 patients completed Study #17 under an expanded access IND at the Applicant's own institution. FDA review found this study to have several critical deficiencies related to the clinical standard of reference as follows from the CR letter dated 09/15/2016:
L196: 1. A substantial number of patients did not complete clinical follow-up at the pre-specified minimum time interval of 1-year:• Out of the 32 patients with positive ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA PET results, only 20 patients had clinical follow-up at the pre-specified interval of at least 1-year to serve as a reference standard.• Out of the 13 patients with negative ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA PET results, only 5 patients had any clinical follow-up to serve as a reference standard and only one of these patients had clinical follow-up at the pre-specified interval of at least 1-year.2. Potential bias in generation of the reference standard related to the fact that ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA PET results were known to the reference standard clinicians prior to their determination of follow-up patient diagnosis.
L197: During this review cycle the FDA review team consulted two independent Special Government Employee (SGE) consultants who were both members of FDA MIDAC (Medical Imaging Drug Advisory Committee). See Section 9 for further information regarding this consultation.
L198: 8.1.3. Additional Literature Support
L199: In addition to the Eshuis et al. (2009) study described above, the FDA identified the following publication from a literature search it conducted during the last review cycle ending in 2016.
L200: N. Ibrahim et al: The sensitivity and specificity of F-18 FDOPA PET in a movement disorder clinic, American Journal of Medical Molecular Imaging 2016; 6 (1): 102-109
L201: This retrospective study analyzed 27 patients who underwent ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA PET for motor symptoms suspicious for PD between 2001 to 2008 at the University of Wisconsin. The patients' medical records were assessed for clinical course of illness, response to levodopa, and length of follow-up. The eventual diagnosis by a referring neurologist after at least 2 years of clinical follow-up (range 2-9 years) was used as the reference standard.
L202: Patients were premedicated with 200 mg of carbidopa orally 1 hour prior to the injection of 5 to 10 mCi of ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA. Three nuclear medicine physicians interpreted the ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA PET scans in a binary fashion similar to the method used in Study #18. The publication reported that out of a total of 28 ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA PET scans, one was false negative, 20 were true positives, and 7 were true negatives. No false positive results were reported.
L203: A strength of the study was that patients were followed clinically for at least two years to establish the standard of reference. However, several weaknesses of the study limit its support for imaging efficacy. Similar to Study #17, the physician who made the reference standard clinical diagnosis was not blinded to ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA PET results. In addition, the PET readers were
L204: aware of initial clinical data and other imaging results that were available at the time of the PET acquisition.
L205: 8.1.4. Integrated Assessment of Effectiveness
L206: Study #18 had a well-controlled, prospective design and adequately addressed the deficiencies cited in the previous FDA CR letter from 2016. The study evaluated 56 adult patients with at least one cardinal Parkinsonian symptom for whom a referring clinician had diagnostic uncertainty. The majority of these patients had symptoms for 2 years or less and therefore represented the anticipated population of predominant intended clinical use. A minimum of 6 months of clinical follow-up by a movement disorder specialist was used as the reference standard to categorize patients as PS or non-PS. Such a clinical reference standard is commonly employed in studies of PS due to the infeasibility of obtaining histopathological confirmation of disease. The movement disorder specialist in Study #18 used standardized clinical assessments for PS and was blinded to ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA PET images and interpretation results. Three blinded readers independently interpreted ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA PET images as either positive or negative. While the study found a relatively low positive percent agreement (with a point estimate range of 49% to 73% for 3 readers) between ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA PET reads and the reference standard of clinical follow-up, high negative percent agreement was demonstrated by all 3 readers (with a point estimate range of 83% to 91%).
L207: 8.2. Review of Safety
L208: 8.2.1. Review of the Safety Database
L209: Study #18 added 68 patients to the prior experience in 53 patients who were administered ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA in Study #17. Patients from Study #17 are included in the safety database because deficiencies only impacted efficacy assessment rather than safety assessment in that study. In total, Study #17 and Study #18 provided a combined experience in 121 patients who had safety assessment procedures prespecified in study protocols and safety data recorded on detailed case report forms.
L210: In prior review cycles, the Applicant submitted a retrospective chart review from their own institution (Study #15). In Study #15, an additional 196 subjects were injected with ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA, including 185 patients and 11 normal control subjects. The Applicant reported no adverse reactions in Study #15. However, since there was no prespecified safety assessment protocol, this limited safety information from Study #15 is only considered as supportive.
L211: In addition to the safety data from the Applicant's studies, potential safety issues from published literature were assessed.
L212: 8.2.2. Safety Results
L213: In Study #17 and #18, all patients were observed for any physical discomfort during ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA PET visits and adverse events were collected. After the PET scans, patients were contacted by phone within 24 hours to collect information regarding any additional adverse events.
L214: Safety Results From Study #18
L215: Table 5 below summarizes safety results from Study #18. There were no deaths or serious adverse events observed. All 12 observed adverse events (AEs) were mild in severity and resolved without intervention. The principal investigator assessed all AEs as unlikely to be related 18F-FDOPA.

L219: Safety Results From Study #17
L220: There were no deaths or serious adverse events observed in Study #17. All AEs were mild in severity and resolved without intervention.
L221: One patient in Study #17 reported a bruise and slight swelling 2 days after the scan, possibly at the 18F-FDOPA injection site following intravenous catheter placement. There was no infection at the site and the AE resolved. The study investigators considered this AE to be possibly related to the study drug. All other AEs observed in Study #17 were considered unlikely to be related to 18F-FDOPA by the Applicant.
L222: 8.2.3. Safety Issues From Published Literature
L223: In a publication by Koopmans et al. (2005),8 one case of the induction of carcinoid crisis after rapid intravenous injection of 18F-FDOPA was reported in a patient referred for imaging of metastatic carcinoid. However, no induction of carcinoid crisis was observed by Nataf et al.
L224: 8 Koopmans, K., Brouwers, A., De Hooge M., Van der Horst-Schrijvers, A., Kema, I., Wolffenbuttel, B., De Vries, E., Jager, P. (2005), Carcinoid crisis after injection of 6-18F-fluorodihydroxyphenylalanine in a patient with metastatic carcinoid. J. Nucl. Med., 46, 1240-1243.
L225: (2006)9 in a similar population of 170 patients (majority referred for carcinoid tumors) exposed to 18F-FDOPA over a period 4 years. While both studies administered a similar volume (roughly 8 mL), Nataf et al. injected the dose slowly over 1 min compared to the several second injection of Koopmans et al. Another important difference between the two groups was the specific activity of 18F-FDOPA preparations. In the case reported by Koopmans et al., specific activity was 6 GBq/mmol, whereas Nataf et al. used a commercial preparation with a 5-fold greater specific activity of at least 30 GBq/mmol at calibration.
L226: In an appended reply to Nataf et al., Koopmans et al. performed described another 100 18F-FDOPA PET scans performed with slow injection over 3 min without any witnessed reactions. Koopmans et al. concluded that rapid injection together with relatively low specific activity of 18F-FDOPA could potentially result in a first-pass bolus effect that might have had enough pharmacologic activity to trigger carcinoid crisis in their original patient with known metastases.
L227: Of note, in the Nataf et al. study, the rate of intravenous injection of 18F-FDOPA was slowed to diminish a transient and mild burning sensation at the injection site that had been reported in clinical experience. The mechanism of this adverse event was thought to be related to the acidic pH of the administered 18F-FDOPA preparation (pH 4.5–5).
L228: 8.2.4. Integrated Assessment of Safety
L229: 18F-FDOPA is structurally similar to the therapeutic drug, levodopa, with the only difference being the presence of an F-18 atom instead of a hydrogen atom at position 6 of the aromatic ring, as shown in the following figure.
L230: Figure 1. Structural Comparison of 18F-FDOPA and Levodopa
L231: The figure displays two chemical structures side-by-side. The structure on the left is 18F-FDOPA, which consists of a benzene ring with a hydroxyl group at position 3, a hydroxyl group at position 4, and a fluorine-18 atom at position 6. A propanoic acid side chain is attached at position 1. The structure on the right is Levodopa, which is identical to 18F-FDOPA but lacks the fluorine atom at position 6, having a hydrogen atom instead.[18F]FDOPALevodopaChemical structures of [18F]FDOPA and Levodopa
L232: Source: Left: https://pubchem.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/compound/56494#section=2D-Structure;Right: https://pubchem.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/image/f1.html?cid=6047]
L233: 9 Nataf, V., Balard, M., De Beco, V., Kerrou, K., Gutman, F., Grahek, D., Montravers, F., Talbot, J. (2006), Safety of 18F-FDOPA injection for PET of carcinoid tumor. J. Nucl. Med., 47, 1732.]
L234: Oral levodopa has been used for treatment of Parkinson's disease for over 40 years at doses ranging up to 2000 mg/day. For comparison, the one-time intravenous mass dose of ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA in Study #18 ranged from (b) (4) mg. Given the high bioavailability of oral levodopa, ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA safety is supported by the extensive clinical experience with much higher and repeated dosing of the this closely related drug. Similarly, the basic adverse event collection performed in the Applicant's clinical studies is sufficient without additional detailed safety monitoring such as laboratory testing.
L235: Adverse event information from the 121 patients exposed to ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA in Study #17 and Study #18 raises no safety concerns. All reported AEs were mild in severity, non-serious and resolved without intervention. Only one case of bruising and swelling near the injection site was assessed as possibly related to ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA by an investigator, but more likely was related to intravenous catheter placement. Additionally, no adverse reactions were reported in 196 patients who were administered ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA in Study #15, although formal adverse event monitoring was not prespecified.
L236: A single published report of carcinoid crisis appears possibly related to ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA injection. Other published data suggest mitigation of this potential risk by slowing the rate of ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA injection. In any event, a slower rate of ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA injection seems reasonable given its acidic pH and published clinical experience with presumably related transient mild burning sensation at the injection site of some patients. Of note, foreign labeling for ^{18}\text{F}-FDOPA recommends slow intravenous injection over approximately one minute (Chevalme et al. (2007)).10

The study #18 protocol was reviewed and agreed upon by the FDA review team.

L255: These analyses show that the results between this subgroup and the overall population are consistent. Other subgroups are based on gender and age of the patients. The statistical review team also analyzed these subgroups and found consistency with overall population results. This consistency demonstrates that the benefits and limitations of 18F-FDOPA PET imaging apply to various subgroups as well.
L256: 8.4. Conclusions and Recommendations
L257: Study #18 adequately addressed the deficiencies identified in the Applicant's previous NDA submissions. While the study found a relatively low positive percent agreement between 18F-FDOPA PET reads and the reference standard of clinical follow-up, high negative percent agreement was independently demonstrated by all 3 readers. Subgroup analysis revealed similar performance of 18F-FDOPA in a clinically relevant population of patients who had symptoms for 2 years or less. 18F-FDOPA safety is supported by extensive clinical experience with much higher and long-term dosing of the closely related therapeutic drug, levodopa. Available safety data from clinical trials demonstrate a benign safety profile for 18F-FDOPA. The review team recommends approval of 18F-FDOPA PET imaging as an adjunct to other diagnostic evaluations in patients with suspected PS.
L258: 9. Advisory Committee Meeting and Other External Consultations
L259: No Advisory Committee meeting was conducted.
L260: During the review cycle of the resubmitted NDA in 2016, the FDA review team consulted two independent Special Government Employees (SGE) for a review 18F-FDOPA PET images from Study #17. Both consultants were well known nuclear medicine physicians and members of MIDAC (Medical Imaging Drug Advisory Committee). Neither consultant was aware of the other's participation. No clinical data were available to either consultant. Questions that were asked of the reviewer consultants appear below with a summary of their responses:
L261: 1. Please comment on the likelihood of an ability in practice to discriminate between “positive” and “negative” patients based on these images. Please mark each image as adequate or inadequate for interpretation.
L262: Reviewer 1 found all images to be adequate with color images being less optimal than the black and white images. It was easy to tell apart negative from positive.
L263: Reviewer 2 found all images easy to read in black and white and had not assessed color images at all. He characterized them all as adequate.
L264: 2. Please comment on the spectrum of difficulty in interpreting the provided images and provide a binary assessment of diagnostic confidence in interpreting each image.
L265: Reviewer 1 felt that the spectrum of difficulty was reasonable and was confident in assessing every image.
L266: Reviewer 2 rated diagnostic confidence on a scale from A to D, with A representing the highest level of confidence. The majority (34) of studies were ranked as A, 6 as B, and 5 as C.
L267: 3. Do you agree with the submitted interpretation of the provided images?
L268: Reviewer 1 disagreed with interpretation of images in 4 out of 45 patients (2 positive and 2 negative).
L269: Reviewer 2 disagreed in 5 out of 45 cases, all of which he considered to be positive whereas the on-site blinded readers interpreted them as negative.
L270: 10. Pediatrics
L271: The Applicant stated that 18F-FDOPA is not intended for pediatric patients. The proposed indication for 18F-FDOPA is generally not applicable to pediatric patients because of the lack of PS pathophysiology in this population. Relevant pediatric studies with 18F-FDOPA for the proposed indication would thus be impossible or highly impractical. On June 5, 2013, the FDA Pediatric Review Committee (PeRC) discussed the application and agreed with granting a full waiver of pediatric studies.
L272: 11. Labeling Recommendations
L273: Section 1: Indications and Usage
L274: • 18F-FDOPA should be indicated for use in PET to visualize dopaminergic nerve terminals in the striatum for the evaluation of adult patients with suspected PS.• To reflect 18F-FDOPA performance characteristics and the current field of PS diagnosis in general, 18F-FDOPA PET should be described as an adjunct to other diagnostic evaluations.
L275: Section 2: Dosage and Administration
L276: • Radioactivity dose should be the same as that used in Study #18 (5 mCi).• As in Study #18, peripheral dopaminergic blockade with 150 mg of oral carbidopa is recommended 60 to 120 minutes prior to the administration of 18F-FDOPA. Of note, a maximum daily oral dose of 200 mg of carbidopa is FDA approved for co-administration with therapeutic levodopa.• 18F-FDOPA should be administered as an intravenous infusion over 1 minute to reduce potential injection site pain as reported in published foreign experience.• A visual and binary image interpretation method is recommended using standard transaxial PET images of the brain.
L277: Section 6: Adverse Reactions
L278: • Clinical trials experience should describe the lack of adverse reactions in the 121 patients exposed to 18F-FDOPA in Study #17 and Study #18.• Injection site pain should be added to postmarketing experience to reflect published foreign findings.
L279: Section 7: Drug Interactions
L280: • This section of labeling should include the clinical pharmacology recommendations listed at the end of Section 6.1.1 of this review.
L281: Section 14: Clinical Studies
L282: • This section should describe Study #18 and include the PPA and NPA for each of the 3 readers along with the respective 95% CI.• To reflect the performance characteristics of 18F-FDOPA PET, Section 14 should state that effectiveness as a screening or confirmatory test has not been established.• To reflect the design of Study #18, Section 14 should state that effectiveness of 18F-FDOPA PET for monitoring disease progression or response to therapy has not been established.

With these fulfillments in place, the Applicant has provided adequate information to ensure the identity, strength, quality and purity of the proposed drug product.

L311: Office Director Decisional Memo

FromCharles J. Ganley, M.D.

SubjectOffice Director Decisional Memo

NDANDA #200655

Applicant NameFeinstein Institute for Medical Research

PDUFA Goal DateSeptember 15, 2015 (extended from June 15, 2015)

Proprietary Name / Established (USAN) NameNo Proprietary NameFluorodopa F 18 (FDOPA F 18)

Dosage Forms / StrengthInjection / (b) (4) mCi/mL

Indication(s)/PopulationsFluorodopa F 18 Injection is a (b) (4) used in Positron Emission Tomography (PET) to visualize dopaminergic (b) (4) in the striatum (b) (4) adult patients with suspected parkinsonian syndromes (PS). (b) (4)Fluorodopa F 18 PET is an adjunct to other diagnostic investigations.

Action:Complete Response
L313: The Division Director memo has a complete list of review staff.
L314: Summary Recommendation
L315: The sponsor received a complete response (CR) letter on October 22, 2013 for chemistry deficiencies and clinical data deficiencies. Sufficient information has been submitted to address the chemistry deficiencies but the clinical trial that was conducted to respond to the clinical deficiencies was not adequately conducted. The study was missing important clinical patient follow-up data necessary for the determination of the standard of reference1. Additionally, the clinicians making the clinical diagnosis post-scan, which would serve as the standard of reference, were aware of the interpretations of the scans prior to the diagnosis. For these reasons, the study does not adequately address the clinical deficiency cited in the CR letter and does not support the approval of the drug for marketing. This memo concurs with the recommendations of the division director, CDTL and the statistical and clinical reviews to not approve the application. The sponsor needs to conduct additional studies or supplement the trial submitted if it can be salvaged. There are, however, serious problems with the conduct of the submitted study and the collection of data that raise serious concerns about the strength of this data and its use going forward.
L316: Background Information
L317: The CDTL memo provides a summary of the history of NDA 200655 and it will not be repeated in this memo. FDOPA F 18 is a radiopharmaceutical PET imaging agent being evaluated to aid in the diagnosis of parkinsonian syndromes relative to other diseases that may cause overlapping symptoms (e.g. essential tremor). Amino acid decarboxylase converts FDOPA to fluorodopamine (FDOPAMINE) in the brain.2 FDOPAMINE is taken up by dopaminergic neurons. FDOPAMINE accumulates in the dopaminergic neurons in basal ganglia of the brain. In diseases with a depletion of the number of dopaminergic neurons, such as Parkinson's Disease, FDOPA F 18 PET could serve as a possible tool to differentiate Parkinsonian Syndromes (PS) pathology3 from
L318: 1 To establish efficacy in clinical studies, we recommend that the functional, physiological, or biochemical measurements of the medical imaging agent be compared with those of a reference product or a procedure of known high validity (i.e., a truth standard). Ideally, we recommend that the high validity of this reference product or truth standard be documented thoroughly and critically before its use in clinical studies intended to demonstrate effectiveness of the test imaging agent. There is no truth standard in this study. In lieu of the standard of truth, the clinical diagnosis after 1 year of follow-up served as a standard of reference to assess the performance of the FDOPA F 18 scans.
L319: 2 FDOPA is also metabolized by peripheral catechol-o-methyl transferase (COMT) before crossing the blood brain barrier. This can reduce uptake. Carbi-dopa is pre-administered to reduce COMT activity.
L320: Non-Parkinsonian Syndromes (NPS)4 pathology when there are difficulties in making a diagnosis for patients presenting with signs such as tremor.
L321: The sponsor submitted a study report (study #17)5 on 45 subjects who underwent FDOPA F 18 scanning. The prospective evaluation required each patient to be followed for 1 – 2 years and a clinical diagnosis, either PS or NPS, made based on clinical evaluation. The clinical diagnosis was to serve as the standard of reference (not the standard of truth). “Positive” scans are abnormal, indicative of decreased dopamine uptake in the striatum, and are suggestive of PS. “Negative” scans are normal scans, indicative of normal dopamine uptake, and are suggestive of NPS.
L322: Results
L323: Forty-five subjects were enrolled in an expanded access protocol and received FDOPA F 18. The scans were read by 3 readers, a physicist and 2 neurologists, and were determined to be positive or negative. In 44 of the 45 scans, there was 100 percent concurrence between the three readers. The results of the scans are as follows:
L324: Scan InterpretationClinical Diagnosis(Standard of Reference)Actual Follow-up

1 year or greater(patient #s)Less than oneyear(patient #s)



32 Positive2 lost to follow-up02(b) (6)

30 PS(b) (6)(b) (6)

6 NPS(b) (6) 15(b) (6)

Total2124
L325: Sensitivity and specificity cannot be adequately calculated because a significant proportion of the patients are either lost to follow-up or had a follow-up of less than one year. Additionally, the clinicians making the clinical diagnosis were aware of the scan results at the time of follow-up. Nine subjects had no follow-up for the assessment of a clinical diagnosis. An additional fourteen subjects did not have follow-up for 1 to 2 years as specified per protocol. Because the clinicians were aware of the scan results, it is not clear whether that knowledge biased the final clinical diagnosis in follow-up by the clinician.
L326: In table 2 of the April 26, 2016 statistical review, the diagnosis at referral is listed for 36 of the 45 patients. The following observations are relevant in the demonstration of the potential clinical utility.
L327: • The 9 patients who did not have a clinical diagnosis at referral were also the 9 patients who were lost to follow-up after scan (no follow-up for clinical diagnosis). Seven of the nine had negative scans.
• Five patients6 were diagnosed with NPS. At referral, three of them were thought to have PS and for two of them either PS or NPS were possible.
L328: 3 Parkinsonian Syndromes includes diseases manifested by a depletion of dopaminergic neurons in the basal ganglia. These conditions include but are not limited to Idiopathic Parkinson’s Disease, Progressive Supranuclear Palsy, and Corticobasal Degeneration.
L329: 4 Non-Parkinson Syndromes include diseases such as Essential Tremor, SWEDD (Scans Without Evidence of Dopaminergic Deficit) and Dopa-Responsive Dystonia.
L330: 5 The study was conducted under an expanded access IND and the investigator was permitted to charge for the scan.
L331: 6 Patient # (b) (6)
L332: • Five other patients7 had possible diagnoses of PS or NPS at referral. All five were diagnosed with PS.
L333: The division had the scans reviewed by two special government employees (SGEs) with experience in reading FDOPA F 18 scans with the intent of determining the quality of the scans, whether the scans are readable and also to get input on their interpretation of the scans. Both SGEs felt the scan quality was good and they were all readable. For their reads, they agreed with each other on 40 of the 45 scan interpretations (89%). For the three readers in the study, they agreed with each other on 44 of the 45 scan interpretations (98%). It was noted in the statistical review that it is not clear from the report that the readers were blinded to clinical information and whether they worked independently of each other. There were six scans where at least one of the SGEs interpretations differed from the interpretation of the three readers. One of the patients had no follow-up and no diagnosis. For the remaining five scans, three were diagnosed with PS and two with NPS.
L334: Conclusion
L335: The study was poorly conducted. FDOPA F 18 image interpretation results were conveyed to the referring physician prior to the clinical diagnosis. As such, the clinical diagnosis may be influenced by the scan result which makes it difficult to depend on the clinical diagnosis as the standard of reference. Without confidence in the standard of reference, it is difficult to evaluate the performance of this test. There was no image interpretation charter or standardized reader training. It is not clear whether clinical information was provided to the readers for interpretation of the scan and whether they were read independently, both of which may bias their interpretations.
L336: The clinical follow-up was poor. It is hard to understand why a referral diagnosis was not available in nine patients when this information should be easily obtainable. It is more understandable why follow-up post-scan was a problem because patients need to show up for their visits. The sponsor noted that they made attempts at follow-up but were unsuccessful. It is surprising, however, that more than half of the patients failed to have follow-up as per protocol especially for patients who were paying for the test out of pocket and had a vested interest in knowing the accuracy of the test.
L337: I agree with the assessment of the division that this clinical data is not adequate. The first step is to determine whether this data can be salvaged. It may require a re-read of the scans but with a charter for image interpretation or some standardized reader training. Readers should be independent and not have prior knowledge of clinical history. For the determination of clinical diagnosis, it may not be sufficient to depend on the clinical diagnosis in this submission. An option would include having an independent clinician perform an examination of the patients and arrive at a clinical diagnosis. To do this, limited information could be provided to the clinician and the patient would have to be counseled not to reveal their diagnosis or previous test results. It would be difficult to have a clinical diagnosis derived from a chart review unless the information related to the results of the scan can be purged and if the information is limited to clinical symptoms and clinical course. Attempts can be made to contact the patients lost to follow-up.
L338: The division recommends that new patients be continued to be enrolled to supplement this study. That would be acceptable but only if there are substantial changes in how the study is conducted, particularly as to how the reads are conducted and the withholding of scan results from the person making the clinical diagnosis. Follow-up of patients needs to be improved. This data was obtained under an expanded access protocol. The division should determine whether the current protocol includes sufficient measures to encourage collection of information to support a marketing application or whether changes to the protocol need to be made.
L339: The chemistry deficiencies in the CR letter were adequately addressed. A PMC was agreed upon for this cycle in the event that the application was approved. The sponsor submitted the information that was to be included in the PMC just prior to the current PDUPA data. This information was not reviewed and will be addressed in the next cycle.
