L345: Medical Officer ReviewAmendment to Review of NDA 200655
L346: Date:September 2, 2016FromBrenda Ye, MDNDA # and Supplement #NDA 200655, Supporting Document #50ApplicantFeinstein Institute for Medical ResearchDate of Original SubmissionDecember 15, 2015Original PDUFA Goal DateJune 15, 2016Date of Major AmendmentApril 11, 2016Proprietary Name / EstablishedFlorodopa-F-18 InjectionDosage forms / StrengthSolution for Injection, 0.42-8.33 mCi/mLProposed Indication(s)Positron Emission Tomography (PET) imaging visualization of dopaminergic (b) (4) in striatum of adult patients with suspected parkinsonian syndromes (PS)Recommended:Complete Response
L347: The Major Amendment
L348: In response to FDA request, the applicant submitted FDOPA PET images for all 45 patients in the clinical study conducted by the Feinstein Institute of Medical Research (Study #17). All patient information was de-identified in the submission. The FDOPA PET image submission, dated 04/08/16 (received 04/11/16), was determined by the FDA review team to be a major amendment. Along with the FDOPA PET images, image interpretation (positive/negative) for each patient was also submitted.
L349: The reason for the FDA's request and designation as a major amendment is that the application is supported by a very limited clinical database (n = 45) and the clinical team would like to assess whether the images obtained and interpreted by the applicant represent the spectrum of images which would be typically obtained and interpreted if FDOPA were to be used in clinical practice.
L350: Review of FDOPA PET images by the clinical team
L351: The clinical team has independently reviewed the submitted FDOPA PET images. Each patient's image set includes axial PET images through the brain and a composite image integrating underlying positron emission data through the basal ganglia. Overall image quality was good and would be acceptable to readers in clinical practice.
L352: There was no image interpretation instructions included with the submission, or elsewhere in the NDA submission. The clinical team attempted to interpret each patient's image set as positive or negative depending on positron emission signal in the caudate and putamen bilaterally. Any loss of positron emission signal was interpreted as positive. The clinical team then compared its interpretation with the submitted image interpretation determined in the clinical study. The clinical team largely agreed with the submitted image interpretation, although there are several borderline cases in which the clinical team did not fully agree with the image interpretation provided by the applicant.
L353: Special Government Employee (SGE) consultation
L354: The clinical team identified two imaging expert as Special Government Employee (SGE) to independently review the submitted FDOPA PET images from the clinical study conducted by the applicant. The two Special Government Employees were Dr. Henry Royal of Washington University and Dr. Peter Herscovitch of the National Institutes of Health (NIH).
L355: Dr. Royal was first contacted and sent CD-ROMs containing FDOPA PET images of all 45 patients, the image interpretation list submitted by the applicant and the following discussion questions:
L356: 1. Please comment on the likelihood of an ability in practice to discriminate between "positive" and "negative" patients based on these images. Please mark each image as adequate or inadequate for interpretation.
L357: 2. Please comment on the spectrum of difficulty in interpreting the provided images and provide a binary assessment of diagnostic confidence in interpreting each image.3. Do you agree with the submitted interpretation of the provided images?4. In your clinical judgment, what would be possible clinical utility of FDOPA if it were approved? To the best of your understanding, does it have any known or theoretical advantages or disadvantages as compared to the imaging drug already marketed in US for a similar indication?
L358: After receiving the FDOPA PET images, Dr. Royal brought up the following question: Was there a formal training program for the blinded readers and, if so, can he have an access to it as well? In other words, were there image interpretation instructions written down some place as to what constitutes a positive or negative image?
L359: The clinical team actually had similar questions when reviewing the submitted FDOPA PET images. How much of a reduction is “reduction”? What if just one caudate has slightly reduced signal?
L360: Subsequently, the 2nd SGE, Dr. Peter Herscovitch, also had the same question: does the NDA specify how the images should be read, and what are the characteristics of an abnormal scan?
L361: Since the NDA submission did not address these questions, a clinical information request was sent to the Applicant on June 10, 2016:
L362: 1. In reference to the three blinded readers who have interpreted the FDOPA images, was there any training provided to them prior to the reading sessions?• Was there a reading charter?• Were there any training images?• If the answer is yes to any of these three questions above, please provide details.2. In reference to image interpretation, are there any specific instructions which would help a reader identify an image as positive or negative? The proposed labeling speaks of (b) (4) “(b) (4)” as indicative of an abnormal image. Does it include any (b) (4)? Can you be more specific?
L363: The applicant responded on the same day:
L364: “The readers have the tool to convert the DICOM images to a single combined image of the region using 4 to 5 slides. This single slide will clearly demonstrate the condition of the caudate and putamen for interpretation. It will clearly show the loss of dopaminergic neurons if any in these regions for easy interpretation. Any center can get this program from (b) (4) etc. and can make it user friendly. That is what we have done.
L365: 1) There was no training for the readers and is not required
L366: 2) There was no reading charter3) There is no specific instruction to read the scan, but one has to have the computer program to convert the DICOM images to a single slide for interpretation."
L367: Reviewer's comments: the applicant probably calls each axial slice through the basal ganglia as a 'slide'.
L368: The Applicant also stated that 99% of the time, only the single-image was read and there was no need for the DICOM. The Applicant explained that the images were read by looking at the combined scan (the single image generated by the "program") and assessing the shape and size of the putamen and caudate to see any loss of dopaminergic neurons. The Applicant confirmed that the procedures as to how the images should be read by the practitioners, or by the 3-blinded readers, were not included in the NDA re-submission dated 12/15/15. Two of the readers were neurologists and the third reader was Dr. Dhawan, a physicist. The applicant stated that in his institution, the FDOPA PET images are read by Dr. Dhawan and the neurologists. Nuclear Medicine or Radiology physicians are not involved in reading the images. The images are read by looking at the composite single image through basal ganglia and assessing the shape and size of the putamen and caudate to see any loss of dopaminergic neurons.
L369: On Monday, June 13, 2016 the FDA review team and the applicant held a teleconference, during which the applicant stated that the patients were referred for imaging by a neurologist and afterwards, Dr. Dhawan or Dr. David Eidelberg would contact the referring neurologist with the image interpretation.
L370: Reviewer's comments: Therefore the applicant confirmed that FDOPA PET image interpretation results were conveyed to the referring physician, who then during follow-up visits, would make a final clinical diagnosis of the patient. This follow-up clinical diagnosis therefore was made by the referring physician who incorporates FDOPA PET imaging results. Since the final follow-up clinical diagnosis was used as the standard of truth in the study, the standard of truth was biased with the FDOPA PET results. This was previously uncovered and discussed (see clinical review document dated 04/22/2016), but is now solidly confirmed by the applicant. This bias in standard of truth is a major deficiency of the clinical study.
L371: It should be noted that the 'clinical study' was conducted under expanded access IND with a request to charge. So the 'study' follows more of the clinical practice rather than be strictly controlled in a typical clinical study setting.
L372: During the teleconference, the FDA asked again what is meant by
L373: (b) (4)
L374: One reader, a neurologist, described his FDOPA PET image interpretation procedure, during which he systematically reads through all the axial images plus the composite single image through basal ganglia.
L375: Weakness identified during the review of the Major Amendment
L376: Since there was no predefined image interpretation definition, and there was no reader training, FDOPA PET image interpretation procedure in the study appears to be up to each reader. During a teleconference with the applicant, one of the neurologist readers discussed his FDOPA PET image reading methodology which is very systematic and he looks through all of the axial FDOPA PET images. Dr. Chaly (a chemist), on the other hand, in his official response to FDA information request, stated that readers mostly rely on the single composite image through basal ganglia.
L377: Reviewer's comments: The reviewer suspects that of the 3 readers, reader(s) less knowledgeable in neuroanatomy (e.g. the physicist reader), likely relies mostly on the single composite image, as stated by Dr. Chaly. Given that this is a single center study, it is unclear whether the procedure to make this single composite image is standardized across different imaging centers.
L378: Of note, this 'clinical study' (study #17) was conducted under expanded access IND with a request to charge, rather than in a strict clinical trial setting. Therefore the FDA review team did not comment on the lack of an image interpretation charter or a lack of standardized reader training, when approving the expanded access IND with a request to charge in June 2012.
L379: It is also noted that the image interpretation results are largely consistent among the three readers. Therefore the lack of predefined image interpretation definition and a lack of reader training apparently did not significantly compromise inter-reader consistency
L380: Discussion and Recommendation
L381: Overall, the most serious deficiency was the bias of the standard of truth by results of FDOPA PET. This was uncovered during the previous review (see clinical review dated April 22, 2016) and was further confirmed during the review period of the major amendment.
L382: As noted in the reviewer's previous clinical review dated April 22, 2016, a substantial percentage of study subjects (20% overall) did not have the standard of truth (final clinical diagnosis pending or not available). This is worse in subjects with a negative FDOPA PET, in which only about 50% of the subjects have a final clinical diagnosis (SOT).
L383: On the positive side, during the 3 month review period of the major amendment, Good Clinical Practice (GCP) inspection was completed and was found to be satisfactory. The same person who conducted the inspection for the original submission followed up on all original findings at the current inspection for this resubmission.
L384: During 3 month review period of the major amendment, the FDA 505(b)(2) committee also discussed the application. The applicant submitted literature in the original submission of October 22, 2012, and did not submit additional literature in the resubmission of December 15, 2015 (the resubmission contained clinical data obtained by the applicant). The review team considered the submitted literature inadequate to support clinical efficacy. However, the submitted literature was found to be able to support nonclinical and clinical pharmacology aspects of FDOPA.
L385: The chemical structure of FDOPA (fluorodopa) is nearly identical to that of levodopa. Although fluorodopa is a new chemical entity (NCE) due to the presence of the Fluorine atom, its structural similarity to levodopa confers a nearly-identical clinical pharmacology and toxicology profile. No clinical pharmacology studies were conducted by the applicant. The primary information for clinical pharmacology review has been derived from the fourteen publications submitted in the original application. Because fluorodopa (FDOPA) is a small molecule and the administered formulations (those in the literature and the one by the applicant) are (b) (4) solutions, the formulations used in the publications will result in the same clinical pharmacology as the applicant's drug product. Therefore the published literature provides adequate information on the clinical pharmacology of FDOPA to support the NDA. The published literature has been also found to be adequate to satisfy nonclinical pharmacology/toxicology requirements of the application.
L386: This application presents a difficult regulatory decision. On one hand, the applicant complied with FDA recommendation to conduct a clinical study, and literature data support mechanisms of the drug at least from the perspectives of pharmacology/toxicology and clinical pharmacology as discussed above. On the other hand, the clinical study conducted by the applicant has serious deficiencies, particularly the bias in the standard of truth. In addition, supporting data on the performance characteristics of FDOPA PET negative patients are very limited. Overall suggest Complete Response for the applicant to at least collect additional data on FDOPA PET negative cases.

L395: 1. Introduction
L396: Recommendation for Regulatory Action
L397: On December 15, 2015 the Feinstein Institute for Medical Research submitted this 505(b)(2) NDA application for Fluorodopa F 18 Injection, a radioactive diagnostic agent for use with Positron Emission Tomography (PET) to visualize dopaminergic (b) (4) in the striatum in adults with suspected Parkinsonian syndromes. The present submission for this new molecular entity (NME) is in response to an FDA complete response action taken on October 22, 2013.
L398: The objective of this review is to summarize the recommendation for a Complete Response (CR) action reached by the Division of Medical Imaging Products. The signatory authority for the action for this NME is the Office of Drug Evaluation IV in the Office of New Drugs in the Center for Drugs Evaluation and Research.
L399: Regulatory history of Fluorodopa F 18
L400: Fluorodopa F 18 is a radiopharmaceutical for PET imaging. Historically fluorodopa F 18 was produced in the US in compliance with United States Pharmacopoeia (USP) PET compounding standards and monographs and under licensure by the Nuclear Regulatory Commission (NRC). Fluorodopa F 18 was legally marketed for clinical use in positron emission tomography (PET) imaging of neurologic and oncologic conditions. The applicant (Feinstein Institute) has legally produced fluorodopa F 18 and used the drug in clinical practice at the North Shore University Hospital Center for several years.
L401: At a July 28, 2000 public meeting FDA and the Institute for Clinical PET held an informal discussion of the scientific literature for fluorodopa F 18. The meeting concluded that available literature data for fluorodopa F 18 were insufficient to support efficacy in any patient population. It was decided that further discussion of fluorodopa F 18 at an FDA advisory was not warranted at the time.
L402: The more recent legislative history of fluorodopa F 18 and other PET drugs in clinical use begins with the determination by FDA that approved applications for PET drugs are required because these drugs are not deemed generally recognized as safe and effective under section 201(p)(1) and (p)(2) of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act.
L403: On December 9, 2009 FDA issued regulations establishing current good manufacturing practices (cGMPs) for PET drugs. The regulations in 21 CFR Part 212 apply to the production, quality control, holding, and distribution of new PET drugs. The publication of these procedures triggered the implementation timeline specified by the 1997 FDAMA. At the request of drug manufacturers, FDA extended the timeline until June 12, 2012. After that date any facility wishing to continue to produce PET drugs for clinical use was required to have submitted an NDA, an ANDA or to be producing the drugs under an IND. All PET producers must be operating under an approved NDA or ANDA, or effective IND, by December 12, 2015.
L404: On October 30, 2009 the applicant submitted the initial marketing application for fluorodopa F 18. FDA refused to file the application because of inadequate clinical, nonclinical, and CMC data. Notably the NDA did not appear to contain data from adequate and well-controlled studies. FDA acknowledged that many patients had received fluorodopa F 18 injection at the applicant's clinical site. FDA encouraged the applicant to summarize the safety and diagnostic efficacy data from that experience.
L405: The application reviewed in the first review cycle was submitted on October 22 2012. FDA took a Complete Response action on October 22, 2013 because of deficiencies in product quality, clinical, statistical, and good clinical practice.
L406: FDA encouraged the applicant to submit an expanded access IND with a request to charge for fluorodopa F 18. FDA allowed the expanded access IND to proceed and granted the request to charge on June 21, 2012.
L407: Fluorodopa F 18 was first approved for marketing for clinical use in 2010 in France and subsequently in the European Union through mutual recognition under the “well established clinical use” procedure (see previous reviews for details).
L408: Ioflupane I 123
L409: Ioflupane I 123 (DaTscan) is a radiopharmaceutical for Single Photon Emission Computed Tomography (SPECT) imaging approved in the US in 2011 as an adjunct to other diagnostic evaluations in adults with Parkinsonian syndromes (PS) for differentiating essential tremor from tremor due to PS. Ioflupane I 123 targets the dopamine transporter and the resulting image is similar to the image produced by fluorodopa F 18.
L410: 2. Background
L411: Fluorodopa F 18 is chemically similar to dopamine and like dopamine it is taken up by dopaminergic neurons. Parkinsonian syndromes are characterized by diminished striatal dopamine uptake principally due to loss of dopaminergic neurons. Fluorodopa F 18 uptake and retention can be visualized in a PET image and might be useful in the evaluation of patients suspected of having a Parkinsonian syndrome.
L412: 3. CMC
L413: I concur with the recommendation for approval made in the integrated quality review performed by Dr. Leutzingier. The major chemistry manufacturing and control issues in the NDA included the specifications for the chiral precursor, drug product specifications, data for stability at low pH, validation data for the HPLC purity test and for the bacterial endotoxin assay. These deficiencies have been addressed in this submission.
L414: I concur with the overall Office of Compliance recommendation for approval based on the acceptable inspection of the manufacturing site. The previous deficiencies related to current good manufacturing practices, standard operating procedures, and various product specification issues have been resolved.
L415: FDA expects a shift from research to GMP-grade drug product precursor to occur after an eventual the NDA approval. The agreement under this commitment would be fulfilled within one year after an eventual NDA approval.
L416: 4. Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L417: I concur with the conclusions reached by the pharmacology and toxicology reviewer Dr. Sally Hargus that there are no outstanding pharmacology or toxicology issues that preclude approval of this application. Dr. Hargus deferred review of the labeling. The applicant did not submit any original nonclinical data and Dr. Hargus determined that no original data were required.
L418: 5. Clinical Pharmacology/Biopharmaceutics
L419: I concur with the assessment of the clinical pharmacology reviewer Dr. Christy John that pending revision of the labeling, there are no major clinical pharmacology issues that preclude approval of this application.
L420: Dr. John does note that in the present application the recommended dose of fluorodopa F18 Injection is (b) (4) 5 mCi. However, in study 17 a dose ranging between 4 and 5 mCi was used. There are no data provided in support of the proposed range and the potential for a non-diagnostic scan exists with the lower dose. Therefore, Dr. John's initial finding that a mass dose of approximately (b) (4) mg and a radioactive dose of 5 mCi are supported by a review of publications remains unchanged.
L421: The reviewer also evaluated the Applicant's measurements of signal intensity in the striatum (diagnostic target) compared to the intensity in the occipital cortex (background) in the patient's scans in study 17. Calculation of the striatal-occipital ratio of signal intensity showed that on average, the signal differed as a function of disease. However, there was considerable overlap between the ratios in positive and negative scans and the measurement is therefore not useful for interpretation of scans in individual patients. I agree with that assessment.
L422: The current submission contains no new information on drug metabolism, drug interactions, or specific populations and none are needed.
L423: 6. Clinical Microbiology
L424: This section is not applicable to this NDA. No clinical microbiology data were provided and none are needed.
L425: 7. Clinical/Statistical-Efficacy
L426: I concur with the unanimous recommendation by the statistical review team (Drs. Misra and Zalkikar with concurrence by Dr. Gwise) and with the recommendation made by the cross-discipline team leader (Dr. Gorovets) for a complete response action.
L427: In the previous application, the studies conducted by the Applicant (studies 15 and 16) were gravely deficient (see the previous cycle reviews). ON the other hand, the present application does contain verifiable evidence of fluorodopa F 18 performance characteristics from a new study (study 17). The
L428: study is single-center, single-arm and enrolled 45 patients. The primary efficacy end point was the visualization of dopaminergic neurons in the striatum expressed as a binary variable: “loss” / “no loss.” Loss of dopaminergic neurons in the striatum was based upon the judgment of three blinded independent readers. The final clinical diagnosis by the referring physician was used as the reference standard. See The CTDL review for details of the patient population studied.
L429: However, as discussed by the primary clinical reviewer Dr. Ye (see review dated April 22, 2016) the new clinical study has important limitations. The sample size is small, the reference standard is subject to bias because the diagnosis was made by the referring physician who was not blinded to the fluorodopa F 18 PET scan results. Moreover for a substantial percentage of study subjects the reference standard of the clinical diagnosis is missing (see patient disposition summary by Drs. Ye and Gorovets). Among the 13 patients with negative scans, only five underwent a clinical follow up and only one of had a final diagnosis at the specified interval of 1 year or more after the scan. Among the patients with a positive scan only 20 had a final diagnosis at 1 year or more after the scan.
L430: I note the initial recommendation (4/22/2016) by Dr. Ye for approval of the application. The recommendation is largely based on the assessment that fluorodopa F18 does what it purports to do i.e. it provides for visualization of the striata. I do not find this argument persuasive because of the risks posed by an incorrect diagnosis. To assess the likelihood of this risk, adequate diagnostic performance data are needed. IT should be noted that the final recommendation by the clinical reviewer was pending at the time of this summary review.
L431: The limitations of the data from study 17 are summarized by the statistical review team and are reproduced here from that review. In this data summary only patients with available reference standard (final diagnosis at specified follow up period) are considered evaluable. The table shows agreement figures for the three clinical protocol readers (NDA readers) and the special government employees readers (SGE readers) that assisted FDA in the posthoc review of the images. If the missing data are imputed using worst case assumptions, the severe limitations of the data (particularly the negative percent agreement) become even more pronounced. The term percent agreement (instead of sensitivity and specificity) is used here because the final clinical diagnosis is considered to be an imperfect standard.
L432: Table 1: Positive and Negative Percent Agreement in Evaluable Patients in study 17
L433: Positive percent agreement: % of PS patients with abnormal scan.(95% Exact Confidence Interval)Negative percent agreement: % non-PS patients with a normal scan.(95% Exact Confidence Interval)NDA reader 129/31=94% (79 , 99)5/5=100% (48 , 100)reader 230/31=97% (83 , 99.8)5/5=100% (48 , 100)reader 330/31=97% (83 , 99.8)5/5=100% (48 , 100)SGE reader 127/31=87% (70 , 96)4/5=80% (28 , 99)reader 229/31=94% (79 , 99)4/5=80% (28 , 99)
L434: The inadequacy of the performance data will require that the Applicant obtain the missing follow up information (if possible) and resume enrollment in the clinical study.
L435: 8. Safety
L436: I concur with the previous conclusions by the clinical reviewers (Drs. Gorovets and Ye) that fluorodopa F 18 does not pose important safety risks based on the single administration of a small mass dose and a small radioactive dose. The extrapolation to fluorodopa of the clinical safety experience with levodopa seems reasonable.
L437: Dr., Ye summarizes the safety experience in the 45 patients in study 17. Study patients were monitored for any physical discomfort. Patients were followed up with a phone call within 24 hours after the scan to collect information regarding adverse events. Forty-four patients responded. The applicant reports that five patients experienced minor adverse events. The events are judged to be unrelated to fluorodopa F 18.
L438: 9. Advisory Committee Meeting
L439: No advisory committee meeting was conducted.
L440: 10. Pediatrics
L441: The applicant requested a full waiver of pediatric studies in an amendment to the NDA on January 3, 2013. Dr. Ye and Dr. Gorovets agreed that studies of fluorodopa F 18 would be impossible or highly impractical in pediatric patients because Parkinsonian Syndromes are found mostly in adults. On June 5, 2013, the FDA Pediatric Review Committee agreed with granting a full pediatric waiver.
L442: 11. Other Relevant Regulatory Issues
L443: I concur with the assessment by Dr. John Lee of the Division of Clinical Compliance Evaluation that the clinical data provided by the applicant in the present submission are reliable. The single confirmatory study for this NDA was audited at a good clinical practice inspection of the Applicant site. No important deficiencies were identified and a Form FDA 483 was not issued. The overall study conduct appeared GCP-compliant, including oversight of study conduct. All audited data were adequately verifiable against source records and case report forms. The inspection outcome was classified as no action indicated (NAI).
L444: I concur with the assessment by Dr. Patel of the Division of (DRISK) that no REMS would be needed for this product were it to be approved. The Applicant did not propose any risk management activities for Fluorodopa beyond routine pharmacovigilance and labeling.
L445: 12. Labeling
L446: No substantive reviews of the labeling were conducted by NDA review team because of the clinical and statistical deficiencies with the application.
L447: I note the review of the prescribing information and the vial and lead shield labels and the recommendation by Dr. Rutledge of the Division of Medication Error Prevention and Analysis
L448: (DMEPA). Consideration of the findings and recommendations by DMEPA is deferred.
L449: 13. Decision/Action/Risk Benefit Assessment
L450: • Regulatory Action
L451: I concur with the recommendations for a complete response action made by the statistical review team and by the CDTL. The present application provides insufficient confirmatory evidence of efficacy primarily due to the small sample size and missing data. Therefore, the test scan's performance (in particular specificity) is not adequately characterized.
L452: As noted previously, the Applicant lacks the resources and personnel with the experience and qualifications to conduct an adequate and well controlled clinical study of fluorodopa F 18. There appears to be little or no interest from other government agencies, professional organizations, or commercial manufacturers for the clinical development of the drug.
L453: • Risk Benefit Assessment
L454: Inspectional findings of the Applicant study site show that the data are reliable. However, important limitations of the clinical study lead to insufficient characterization of the performance of fluorodopa F 18.
L455: During this review cycle the clinical and statistical secondary reviewers reevaluated the collective strength of evidence from the literature. The reviewers reaffirmed the lack of adequate and well controlled studies. As such, substantial evidence of the efficacy of the drug is lacking.
L456: Fluorodopa F 18 does not pose important safety risks based on the single administration of a small mass dose and a small radioactive dose
L457: • Recommendation for Postmarketing Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies
L458: I concur with the DRISK reviewer that at this time no risk management strategy appears to be necessary.
L459: • Recommendation for other Postmarketing Requirements and Commitments
L460: The Applicant agreed on April 14, 2016 to submit a Prior Approval Supplement if the NDA, when re-submitted, is approved. The supplement would provide for a cGMP synthesis of the drug substance precursor. The PMC would be fulfilled within one year of the approval of the NDA.

Recommended:Complete Response
L467: 1. Introduction
L468: This document combines the Cross Disciplinary Team Leader (CDTL) review of the resubmission of the New Drug Application (NDA) 200655 for F18 FDOPA with the clinical review of the major amendment to this application submitted on 4/11/16. The primary clinical review has been filed prior to the arrival of the major amendment. The reviews from other disciplines have been filed by now as well. An addendum to the primary clinical review is pending.
L469: The major amendment was submitted as a response to our Information Request for the PET images obtained in the course of the study conducted by the sponsor in accordance with the Complete Response (CR) recommendations. The CR was issued following the review of the original application in 2013. The PET images were submitted for a review by two Special Government Employee (SGE) consultants as part of the two identical but independent of each other Divisional Assignments. The current review of the major amendment reflects the results of these consultations as well as the information provided by the applicant in response to our additional Information Requests. An overview of any other relevant information, historical or more recent, has also been conducted.
L470: The primary clinical reviewer has recommended an approval pending the results of the SGE consultations. All other disciplines, except Statistics, have recommended approval as well. The inspections of the manufacturing facility and the clinical site, found unacceptable during the first review cycle, have now found no outstanding deficiencies. However, the submitted clinical data, from a statistical standpoint, have been deemed inadequate and insufficient. The current review addresses the differences in conclusions among the review disciplines.
L471: The reference is being made to the 2013 CDTL review of the original 2012 application and to the 2013 CR letter for a detailed account of the deficiencies encountered during the first cycle review and now attempted to be addressed in the current submission.
L472: The applicant proposes the following indication for use of F18 FDOPA: “For use with Positron Emission Tomography (PET) to visualize dopaminergic (b) (4) in the striatum (b) (4) adult patients with suspected parkinsonian syndromes (PS).”
L473: Fluorodopa F 18 PET is an adjunct to other diagnostic investigations”.
L474: 2. Background
L475: F18 FDOPA is accumulated in striatum and its mechanism of action is well understood. There are non-clinical data supporting the potential usefulness of the drug. Its analogue, levodopa (L-DOPA), is a widely used anti-parkinsonian medication. The PET drug has been used in a variety of human research protocols studying the dopaminergic function of striatum. However there has been a paucity of clinical data addressing its utility and diagnostic performance in a relevant patient population.
L476: F18 FDOPA was one of the PET drugs allowed by the FDA in 2000 to be used without an IND or an approved NDA pending the development of the cGMP regulations governing the production of PET drugs. Back in 2000 FDA has also conducted a literature review of the use of the drug in patients with Parkinsonian syndromes and similar disorders but determined the evidence to be insufficient for finding of safety and effectiveness of the use of F18 FDOPA in these patients.
L477: The current applicant has opened an IND 78861 for the use of F18 FDOPA in Parkinsonian patients and submitted an NDA which FDA has at first Refused to File. The updated and revised application was then submitted and filed in 2012. Following the publication of the PET regulations and guidances, the applicant was allowed to start providing an expanded access to the drug under the IND and was eventually authorized to charge for the drug under the applicable IND regulation.
L478: The prior history of this drug development is presented in a greater detail in the 2013 CDTL review. Of note, (b) (4)
L479: The clinical and statistical reviews of the original 2012 application emphasized three studies: an unpublished retrospective study conducted by the applicant (study 15; n=185), a published study based on a subset of patients from the same study (study 16; n=27) and a published study comparing F18 FDOPA to DaTscan (study 21; n=28). The submitted data were found to be only exploratory and “not sufficient to provide substantial evidence of effectiveness”. The CR letter has recommended, among other things, that the applicant conduct an adequate and well-controlled clinical trial in the intended patient population. It specifically advised the applicant “to obtain a standard of reference (follow up clinical diagnosis) in both Fluorodopa F18 PET
L480: positive patients and Fluorodopa F18 PET negative patients”. It further stated that a clinical diagnosis as a standard of reference should be made “at a pre-defined time interval following the Fluorodopa F 18 PET scan”.
L481: Since then the applicant has continued with the Expanded Access program under the IND 78861 and attempted to conduct a prospectively designed clinical investigation in accordance with the recommendations of the CR letter. The applicant made one important modification to the pre-specified entry criteria, reducing the required to be present “cardinal signs” of Parkinsonism from two to one (tremor, rigidity, bradykinesia or abnormal gait). This was acceptable to the FDA reviewers.
L482: In the current resubmission, the applicant reports on the experience from this single center single arm investigation (study 17) as a response to the 2013 CR letter. In this summary review, the details of the study design and conduct come from the filed primary clinical review and three statistical reviews (primary, secondary and the addendum) as well as from the application itself and additional information provided by the applicant in the course of the review of the Major Amendment. The study protocol although not found in the current application has been located in the recent annual reports to the IND. The integrity of study conduct has been verified and confirmed by the clinical site inspection.
L483: Going over the current clinical and statistical reviews a word of terminological clarification is in order. The applicant and the clinical reviewer, as well as the 2013 CR letter for that matter, speak of “sensitivity” and “specificity” as the performance measures upon which the efficacy of the F18 FDOPA PET is being assessed. The statistical reviewers appear to prefer Positive Percent Agreement and Negative Percent Agreement, respectively. These are defined in the statistical reviews as follows: Positive Percent Agreement represents the percent of patients with abnormal (positive) FDOPA images among all the patients with a clinical diagnostic reference standard of Parkinsonian Syndrome; Negative Percent Agreement represents the percent of patients with normal (negative) FDOPA images among all the patients with clinical diagnostic reference standard of a non-Parkinsonian Syndrome.
L484: The preference for this terminology appears to be based on the fact that here there is no standard of “truth” per se but rather an agreed upon standard of “reference” (a clinical follow-up at a pre-defined time point, in this case). The claim of “visualization” is also being interpreted as a functional claim, i.e. the test visualizes a loss or no loss of dopaminergic (b) (4) and not a diagnostic claim because no specific diagnosis is being made by the test or verified by the reference standard. It should be noted however that the reference standard here is a group of clinical diagnoses which is used to verify the test and collectively can be thought of as a “diagnosis”. Regardless, an important precedent has been set in the DaTscan label, (b) (4) while using the terms like positive and negative percent agreements as measures of clinical efficacy.
L485: Whether, in accordance with the 21CFR315 regulations, “visualization” is interpreted as a functional or a diagnostic claim, a clinical follow-up, from a terminological standpoint, is a standard of reference (and has been referred to as such in the 2013 CR letter). For the purposes
L486: of this review, we accept the statisticians' terminology for the efficacy measures while recognizing that "sensitivity" and "specificity" written in quotations might be more understandable to a clinician.
L487: 3. CMC/Device
L488: A number of CMC deficiencies have been identified during the first review cycle and the following items were included in the 2013 CR letter: (1) the absence of a CGMP supply of the precursor (b) (4), (2) absence of a test for chirality of drug substance precursor, (3) absence of justification for non-USP conforming product pH, (4) need for finalization of the specifications for the drug product, and (5) absence of validation of the drug product HPLC chiral identity and purity. The resolution of all these issues has been summarized by Dr. Leutzinger in the 4/19/16 Integrated Quality Assessment.
L489: It is noted that implementation of the shift from research to GMP-grade precursor would not take place until after the NDA approval which has resulted in a Post-Marketing Commitment (PMC). The agreement under this PMC is expected to be fulfilled within one year from the NDA approval. The remainder of the CMC issues has been resolved. Product microbiology issues as well as the facility issues have been all resolved as well. From the product quality standpoint the recommended action is approval.
L490: 4. Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L491: There were no Pharmacology/Toxicology issues in the CR letter. The recommendation back then was approval. No new nonclinical data have been provided in the resubmission. The current recommendation is approval.
L492: 5. Clinical Pharmacology/Biopharmaceutics
L493: The original application was acceptable from the Clinical Pharmacology perspective. In the resubmission, Study 17 has provided quantitative imaging data which were reviewed. As summarized in the review, in addition to visual image interpretation, the applicant assessed Striatal-Occipital Ratio (SOR) which is the ratio of radioactivity in the striatum to that of the occipital cortex used as a background. The reviewer's analysis of the data shows that because there is considerable overlap between SORs in patients with Parkinson's and non-Parkinson's SOR cannot be used as an aid to image interpretation.
L494: The Clinical Pharmacology reviewer also notes that the dose used in Study 17 is 4.05 to 5 mCi and recommends the latter to be the labeled dose. The overall recommendation is approval.
L495: 6. Clinical Microbiology
L496: N/A
L497: 7. Clinical/Statistical- Efficacy
L498: Study 17 (see section 2 – Background above) enrolled 45 patients with clinical evidence of a movement disorder and an inconclusive diagnosis of a Parkinsonian or a non-Parkinsonian Syndrome (PS vs. NPS) who were referred for FDOPA PET imaging. As part of the pre-specified clinical protocol the images were read on-site by three independent readers blinded to clinical data. The interpretations were binary (PS vs. NPS) based on normal vs. reduced visualization of striatum. Exploratory quantitative assessments of Striatal Occipital Ratio (SOR) were also performed. In addition, images were separately read at a different time point by one of the readers for the purposes of patient care. With an exception of one patient there were no disagreements among blinded readers or between blinded and clinical reads.
L499: For each patient, the clinical FDOPA PET image interpretation was communicated to the referring physician whose follow up clinical evaluation served as a standard of reference (somewhat incorrectly referred to by the applicant as a “standard of truth”). The protocol called for a clinical follow up assessment at a 1 to 2 year interval after PET imaging. PS diagnoses on clinical follow up included Parkinson’s disease (PD), multiple system atrophy (MSA), Gait Freeze, Cortical Basal Ganglion Degeneration (CBD), Pisa Syndrome, and progressive supra-nuclear palsy (PSP). NPS diagnoses included essential tremor (ET), Dopamine Responsive Dystonia (DRD), “Subject without Evidence of Dopaminergic Deficit” (SWEDD), and “no-PD”.
L500: Out of 45 patients, 32 had images interpreted as PS and 13 as NPS. In 30 of 32 patients with reduced visualization of striatum, i.e. with images positive for PS, the standard of reference was also recorded as positive for PS and in two the follow up was missing. Out of 13 patients with negative images, seven were lost for follow up, five were recorded as NPS by clinical follow up and one was recorded as PS (a false negative). According to this breakdown there were no false positives.
L501: The clinical review team had a concern about the interpretability of the images used by the applicant for the efficacy assessment and requested the submission of images for further review. The purpose was not to re-adjudicate the results but rather to assess reader confidence and image quality. Given a very small database the team wanted to assess how representative these images were of the ones that would be obtained and interpreted in practice.
L502: At that point in the review process, the primary clinical reviewer, pending the results of the SGE consultation (see below), was recommending an approval, based on the understanding that the applicant, on the face of it, appeared to have followed the FDA recommendations in carrying out the clinical investigation. The primary statistical reviewer while expressing certain concern about data limitations was commenting that the drug might still have some clinical utility. The secondary statistical reviewer has advised against the approval finding the submitted data to be insufficient for adequate assessment of effectiveness and recommended that the applicant continue with the data collection.
L503: In response to the FDA request the applicant has submitted de-identified images from all 45 patients to the NDA, black and white, in the standard format. In addition, color images obtained with the use of a display program that combined several slices into one image of
L504: striatum were submitted along with the list of patient by patient image interpretation by each of the three blinded readers. These were sent along with a Divisional Assignment to each of the two independent Special Government Employee (SGE) consultants, both members of MIDAC (Medical Imaging Drug Advisory Committee), Dr. Henry Royal of Washington University and Dr. Peter Herscovitch of the NIH. Both are well known Nuclear Medicine physicians. Neither one was aware of the other's participation. No clinical data were available to either SGE. Of note, (b) (4)
L505: The following questions were included in the Divisional Assignment and are accompanied here by the responses (the details can be found in the respective minutes):
L506: 1. Please comment on the likelihood of an ability in practice to discriminate between "positive" and "negative" patients based on these images. Please mark each image as adequate or inadequate for interpretation.
L507: Dr. Royal found all images to be adequate with color images being less optimal than the black and white. It was easy to tell apart negative from positive.
L508: Dr. Herscovitch found all images easy to read in black and white and has not assessed color images at all. He characterized them all as adequate.
L509: 2. Please comment on the spectrum of difficulty in interpreting the provided images and provide a binary assessment of diagnostic confidence in interpreting each image.
L510: Dr. Royal felt that the spectrum of difficulty was reasonable and was confident in assessing every image.
L511: Dr. Herscovitch rated diagnostic confidence on a scale from A to D. The majority (34) were ranked as A, six as B, and five as C.
L512: 3. Do you agree with the submitted interpretation of the provided images?
L513: Dr. Royal disagreed with interpretation of images in four patients (two positive and two negative).
L514: Dr. Herscovitch disagreed in five cases all of which he considered to be positive whereas the on-site blinded readers interpreted them as negative. (Of note, in none of these five a pre-defined standard of reference is available for our review).
L515: 4. In your clinical judgment, what would be possible clinical utility of FDOPA if it were approved? To the best of your understanding, does it have any known or theoretical advantages or disadvantages as compared to the imaging drug already marketed in US for a similar indication?
L516: Dr. Royal thought the clinical utility would be similar to that of DaTscan with PET (FDOPA) most likely resulting in a better image resolution that SPECT (DaTscan).
L517: Dr. Herscovitch also thought the utility would be similar. FDOPA would not need thyroid blocking although it does require premedication with a single dose of carbidopa. Acquisition time would be shorter and radiation dose somewhat smaller with FDOPA. Resolution would be better with FDOPA but the significance of this could be hard to judge without comparing the performance data. A theoretical concern was raised about a potential under-diagnosing of an early PD by FDOPA because of a metabolic up-regulation.
L518: During the discussions with the SGEs the issue of reader training was raised and further clarified with the applicant in the subsequent telephone conferences. It was confirmed that there was no reader training and no imaging charter. The three independent blinded readers in Study 17 were a medical physicist and two neurologists, all with many years of experience in reading FDOPA scans. Although it was considered somewhat problematic that no Nuclear Medicine physicians were involved in Study 17, the clinical team felt that any adequately trained radiologist or a Nuclear Medicine physician should be able to read these images, a view supported by the SGEs. The image interpretation instructions were also further reviewed in the follow up discussions with the applicant.
L519: In addition to clarifying the issues surrounding image interpretation the clinical team queried the applicant on the issue of the standard of reference (or “truth”). The applicant has confirmed that the FDOPA image interpretation was indeed available to a clinician providing the follow up assessment against which the ability of the test to visualize the neuronal loss or no loss was being measured. Given the obvious concern with a possible bias the issue of the time interval between the imaging test and the follow up clinical assessment has again come up and is illustrated in the Table 2 of the Addendum to the Statistical review.
L520: Upon further review it appears that only one out of 13 patients with negative images had a clinical follow up at the pre-defined time interval of one year or longer. One had it at 11 months, one at 3 months, one at three weeks, one at two weeks, one at one week and seven had no known follow up at all. Out of 32 patients with positive images, only 20 had a standard of reference obtained at 1 year or more following the F18 FDOPA PET.
L521: In view of this data deficiency both clinical and statistical review teams have determined that the resubmitted application is not adequate for approval. (At the time of this memo filing, the addendum to the primary clinical review has not been completed yet.)
L522: In addition to the review of the resubmitted application the clinical and statistical reviewers have reexamined the literature based evidence. The internal 2001 divisional package with the literature review was re-reviewed but no new evidence found. Although there were over 500 publications at the time of the original review including 60 clinical studies in patients with movement disorders, none were considered adequate and well controlled and none involved image interpretation by independent readers blinded to clinical data. The search of a more recent literature revealed no new publications since the 2012 review submitted in the original application by this applicant, except for a small (n = 28) single center retrospective study identified by the current review team (N. Ibrahim et al: The sensitivity and specificity of F-FDOPA PET in a movement disorder clinic, American Journal of Medical Molecular Imaging 2016; 6 (1): 102-109). The readers here again were not blinded to clinical data and clinicians were not blinded to the imaging data, however the patients were followed clinically for at least two years and the clinical follow up served as a standard of reference. Twenty patients with positive images were verified to be clinically positive for a Parkinsonian syndrome. Seven negative images were clinically negative and one was false negative.
L523: 8. Safety
L524: No new safety concerns have been identified during the review of this resubmission.
L525: 9. Advisory Committee Meeting
L526: There was no AC meeting. The SGEs were consulted as described above.
L527: 10. Pediatrics
L528: Pediatric waiver has been granted during the review of the original application as PD does not occur in children.
L529: 11. Other Relevant Regulatory Issues
L530: No data integrity or study conduct issues have been identified during the clinical site inspection. The sponsor again has submitted a waiver of the new drug exclusivity.
L531: 12. Labeling
L532: In view of the planned Complete Response no labeling review has been conducted.
L533: In response to the Information Request earlier on the review process the sponsor has updated the Dosimetry table which might still have to be finalized during an eventual labeling review.
L534: 13. Recommendations/Risk Benefit Assessment
L535: The CDTL recommendation is to issue a CR to this resubmitted F18 FDOPA application.
L536: Insufficient clinical follow up in Study 17 is the main deficiency in this re-submitted application for the use of F18 FDOPA PET in patients with suspected Parkinsonian syndromes. (b) (4)
L537: , no substantial evidence of effectiveness has been demonstrated to assess the drug's benefit. Although there are no safety issues with the use of this drug, the risk of misdiagnosis cannot be adequately assessed either.
L538: To summarize, out of 45 patients, 21 patients were followed for over 1 year and provided adequate data for efficacy assessment. One patient had an NPS diagnosis on the standard of reference determination and an FDOPA image interpretation consistent with NPS. This would give a negative percent agreement (or “specificity”) of 100% with the lower bound of 95% CI of only 2.5% which is unacceptable. Twenty patients had a standard of reference diagnosis of PS and an FDOPA image interpretation of PS. This gives a positive percent agreement (“sensitivity”) of 100% for all three readers with a 95% CI: (83%, 100%), according to additional calculations performed by Dr. Zalkikar.
L539: The above measurements have been carried out on the “evaluable” patients only. If one were to use an “intent-to-diagnose” population and impute as wrong some or all of the imaging data based on missing reference data for patients with negative images (resulting in high false negatives) the “sensitivity” estimate would decrease with a significant drop in the lower bound of the 95% CI. It should be noted that at least one of the two SGEs disagreed with the five negative image interpretations in whom there were no standard of reference, so these could have easily become all false negatives depending on the standard of reference determination.
L540: Cross Disciplinary Team Leader Review
L541: F18 FDOPA NDA 200655 Resubmission
L542: Alex Gorovets, MD 080116
L543: Although there is a good understanding of this drug's mechanism of action and a reasonable expectation that it should image what it purports to image there are still no adequate confirmatory clinical data available. Whereas an investigational use can be allowed to continue it would be premature to approve the drug for marketing. The current applicant has a long standing history and experience with the use of F18 FDOPA in patients with suspected movement disorders and whether this applicant would produce the drug for distribution to others is unclear.
L544: While not directing bearing on the decision to approve or not to approve, it should be also noted that with the exclusivity waived and generic drug development possible it would be inappropriate to approve the current application without adequate performance data. The latter consideration is particularly relevant given the availability in this country of an approved product (DaTscan) for which the performance data for the same indication are available.
L545: In the current resubmission the applicant has also provided the available European labels for F18 FDOPA. The issue of European approvals has been covered in the 2013 CDTL review and there are no review updates at this time.
L546: It is unfortunate that in spite of its prospective design Study 17 has been terminated prematurely and thus incomplete data have been submitted for our review and analysis. We therefore recommend that the applicant continue collecting data on the patients enrolled so far and enroll additional patients for further analyses as pre-specified in the protocol. We would also encourage the applicant to contact us for planning an appropriate statistical analysis of the collected data.
L547: Our preliminary estimation at this time is that the applicant would have to altogether enroll at least five to six patients with a negative reference standard, pre-defined as a clinical follow up over one year. The required number of patients with a positive reference standard might depend on the amount of missing data among patients with negative images (these projections have not been formally confirmed by the statistical review team).
L548: Here are the draft comments to the applicant:
L549: Clinical/Statistical:
L550: Your resubmitted application contains the report of the prospectively designed "Study #17" conducted by you following the previous Complete Response issued by us on 10/22/13. We have identified the following deficiency:
L551: In the Study #17 you have imaged 45 movement disorder patients with F18 FDOPA PET. Of these patients, 32 had images interpreted as positive for visualization of a loss of dopaminergic neurons in striatum and 13 as negative. Out of these 13 patients only five had undergone a clinical follow up which was supposed to serve as a standard of reference and only one of these had a clinical follow up at a pre-defined time interval following the F18 FDOPA PET (&gt; 1 year). Some patients had an interval as short as one or two weeks. Therefore, out of 13 patients with no loss of dopaminergic neurons in striatum as visualized on the F18 FDOPA PET only one had this interpretation adequately verified.
L552: Cross Disciplinary Team Leader Review
L553: F18 FDOPA NDA 200655 Resubmission
L554: Alex Gorovets, MD 080116
L555: This is not acceptable especially in view of the availability of the image interpretation to the clinician providing the follow up. Such a standard of reference is not adequate for the proposed visualization claim.
L556: We also note that in patients with “positive” images only 20 had a standard of reference obtained 1 year or more following F18 FDOPA PET. This is also inadequate.
L557: To address this deficiency we recommend that you obtain the clinical follow up at a minimum of 1 year after the imaging test for the patients you have already enrolled and extend the Study #17 to enroll additional patients who will also go on to have a clinical follow up at a pre-defined interval as an adequate standard of reference.
L558: We also recommend documenting clearly, at follow up, which clinical features in each patient were consistent with Parkinsonian or Non-Parkinsonian syndrome.
L559: We invite you to seek a meeting with us to design an appropriate Statistical Analysis Plan for the completion and extension of the Study #17.

The PMC should be fulfilled within one year of the approval of the NDA. The supplement will provide for a cGMP synthesis of (b) (4), the drug substance precursor, as manufactured by (b) (4).

L579: 1 Executive Summary
L580: 1.1. Product Introduction
L581: Fluorodopa F 18 Injection is a radiopharmaceutical proposed to be used in Positron Emission Tomography (PET) to visualize striatal dopaminergic (b) (4) in patients with suspected parkinsonian syndromes (PS). At the onset of Parkinsonian Syndrome, (b) (4)
L582: Applicant Proposed Indication:
L583: Fluorodopa F 18 Injection is a (b) (4) used in Positron Emission Tomography (PET) to visualize dopaminergic (b) (4) in the striatum (b) (4) adult patients with suspected parkinsonian syndromes (PS). (b) (4)
L584: Fluorodopa F 18 PET is an adjunct to other diagnostic investigations.
L585: 1.2. Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L586: At least on the surface, the applicant followed the FDA review team's guidance of conducting a new clinical study to address the clinical deficiencies cited in the Complete Response (CR) Letter. The new clinical study (Study #17) has pre-specified clinical protocol and uses follow-up clinical diagnosis of at least one-year as the standard of truth. Data integrity is being investigated with clinical inspection (GCP inspection scheduled, inspection results pending).
L587: However the new clinical study (Study #17) has its own limitations. The final clinical diagnosis (standard of truth) was biased because the final clinical diagnosis was made by the referring physician who received F-DOPA PET imaging results prior to making a final clinical diagnosis, which is used as the SOT of the study. In addition, a substantial percentage of study subjects (20% overall) did not have the standard of truth (final clinical diagnosis pending or not available). This is worse in subjects with a negative FDOPA PET, in which only about 50% of the subjects have a final clinical diagnosis (SOT).
L588: If one looks at all of the submitted clinical studies, in this review cycle or the previous review cycle, the total application package looks a bit stronger. In the previous review cycle, Studies #15/16 conducted by the applicant was found to be gravely deficient. However Study # 21, a literature publication comparing the performance characteristics of FDOPA and DaTscan,
L589: Clinical ReviewBrenda YeNDA 200655 Class 2 ResubmissionFluorodopa F-18 Injection (FDOPA)
L590: provided some evidence of efficacy of F-DOPA, even though Study #21 had its own limitations as cited in the Complete Response Letter.
L591: If one looks at only the patient population who have positive F-DOPA PET and exhibit at least one sign/symptom of Parkinsonian syndrome, available data provide some supporting evidence of the efficacy of the FDOPA PET imaging. In this patient population, the FDOPA PET imaging appears to have a fairly high positive predictive value of confirming striatal dopaminergic neuronal loss.
L592: Given that at least on the surface, the applicant followed the FDA review team's guidance and some clinical utility could be found for F-DOPA PET imaging in a selected patient population (patients with positive F-DOPA PET and at least one sign/symptom of Parkinsonian syndrome), the reviewer recommends approval pending Special Government Employee (SGE) consultation and satisfactory Good Clinical Practice (GCP) inspection.
L593: 2 Regulatory Background
L594: 2.1. U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
L595: The current submission is a resubmission in response to FDA Complete Response (CR) letter dated 10/22/2013. The FDA Complete Response letter had 6 sections with specific comments:
L596: 1 Clinical/Statistical2 Good Clinical Practice (GCP) Inspection3 Product Quality4 Labeling5 Safety Update6 Additional Comments
L597: With regard to clinical review, the relevant clinical comments in the CR letter include:
L598: "Your application contains no data from adequate and well controlled clinical trials. Significant deficiencies have been identified in the referenced literature studies and in the studies you have conducted in support of this application. The data submitted in your application are not sufficient for evaluating the diagnostic effectiveness and usefulness of Fluorodopa F 18 PET in the proposed patient population.
L599: 1. The following deficiencies have been identified with clinical Study #15, which was conducted at the Feinstein Institute of Medical Research:
L600: a. The study is a retrospective, single center study.b. The study was not conducted according to a pre-specified protocol, and hence, definitions of the primary efficacy endpoint and the standard of truth that would normally be specified by a protocol, are not well-defined for the study.c. The study used each patient's clinical diagnosis at the time of referral for a Fluorodopa F 18 PET scan as the standard of truth for measuring the test's performance characteristics. We do not find such a standard to be clinically meaningful and do not agree with the cited diagnostic performance measurements of Fluorodopa F 18 as 100% for sensitivity and 100% for specificity.d. Of the 185 study patients, 158 patients had a pre-imaging clinical diagnosis of Parkinson's disease and were responding to Parkinson's disease medications. For these patients a Fluorodopa F 18 PET scan provides no additional clinically useful information. Deficiencies concerning the remaining 27 study patients are discussed below (Study #16).e. You have provided no documentation that Fluorodopa F 18 PET images have been read independently by three readers blinded to clinical information. Furthermore, inspection of the study site revealed that none of the 47 audited cases contained documentation that each patient's images were read by three blinded readers.2. Study #16 is a publication with the follow-up results of 27 Fluorodopa F 18 PET negative patients enrolled in Study #15. Study #16 has the following major deficiencies:a. Per your amendment dated April 5, 2013, only 8 of the 27 Fluorodopa F 18 PET negative patients from Study #15 were followed for 2 to 4 years to obtain the patient's final clinical diagnosis. Clinical data from such a small number of patients do not provide a reliable assessment of the performance characteristics of Fluorodopa F 18 PET. Furthermore, inspection of the study site revealed that none of the subject files contained documentation (clinical follow-up confirmation) of the continued absence of classical Parkinson's Disease symptoms and signs.b. The reasons for not obtaining follow up on the remaining 19 patients are not provided.
L601: Among the remaining literature publications in your application (Studies #17-23), only the publication by Eshuis et al. (Study #21) provides clinical data for the assessment of the efficacy of Fluorodopa F 18 as a diagnostic radiopharmaceutical. However, Study #21 does not provide substantial evidence of efficacy for the following reasons:
L602: a. The study is a small single center study and is exploratory in design.b. All study patients had a diagnosis of Parkinson's disease at the time of enrollment. The study excluded patients with atypical signs and patients not responding to Parkinson's disease medication. Therefore, the study does not evaluate a patient population that might benefit from a diagnostic imaging test.c. The definition of positive scans (mean standard uptake value in patients below two standard deviations of the mean in healthy controls) is an exploratory quantitative measure and its threshold is arbitrarily set.d. It is unclear how specificity was calculated, as all patients included in the analysis of the study had confirmed Parkinson's disease.
L603: To address these deficiencies, we recommend the following:
L604: 1. Conduct an adequate and well-controlled clinical trial which might provide substantial evidence of effectiveness of Fluorodopa F 18 in the intended patient population. We encourage you to develop such a study using a pre-defined standard of reference for measuring the performance characteristics (sensitivity and specificity) of Fluorodopa F 18, a pre-specified statistical analysis plan, and image interpretations made by independent readers blinded to clinical information. Note in order to measure the sensitivity and specificity of Fluorodopa F 18, you need to obtain the standard of reference (follow-up clinical diagnosis) in both Fluorodopa F 18 PET positive patients and Fluorodopa F 18 PET negative patients. You should use as a standard of reference the clinical diagnosis made by a movement disorder specialist at a predefined time interval following the Fluorodopa F 18 PET scan.2. The study population must include subjects who might benefit from the Fluorodopa F 18 PET scan. Such patients could include those in whom Parkinsonian and essential tremor could not be differentiated on clinical grounds, those with suspected early onset of Parkinsonian syndrome, and/or those not responding to a Parkinson disease medication. To facilitate the interpretation of the study's results, we recommend that you consider exclusion of subjects with features suggestive of multiple system atrophy or progressive supranuclear palsy, response to Parkinson's disease medication, a clinical history exceeding five years, and concomitant medication known or suspected of interacting with striatal uptake of Fluorodopa F 18.3. We reference the Good Clinical Practice (GCP) deficiencies identified during our inspection of your clinical study site and communicated to you on March 22, 2013. These deficiencies call into question the reliability of the clinical data in your application. It is necessary to conduct a study in compliance with GCP standards to ensure the quality and integrity of the data."
L605: Clinical ReviewBrenda YeNDA 200655 Class 2 ResubmissionFluorodopa F-18 Injection (FDOPA)
L606: 2.2. Foreign Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
L607: Fluorodopa F 18 PET was approved in France in 2010 followed by several other countries in the European Union, including England. Fluorodopa F 18 PET approval in France and other EU countries was based on the clinical study publications alone from the literature, and was not based on any particular clinical study conducted by the pharmaceutical companies who hold the approval for marketing. The application resubmission includes labeling from three manufacturers marketing Fluorodopa F 18 Injection in various EU countries.
L608: 3 Sources of Clinical Data and Review Strategy
L609: APPEARS THIS WAY ON ORIGINAL
L610: In response to the FDA Complete Response letter, in which the FDA listed serious design and conduct deficiencies of the previous clinical study (study #15 and #16), the applicant states in the resubmission that since the clinical studies were already completed in the past, nothing could be done to correct the deficiencies in these old studies. Since in the Complete Response letter, FDA suggested a new study to satisfy the deficiencies indicated in the Clinical/Statistical and GCP comments, the applicant conducted a new prospective clinical study under the Expanded Excess IND 78861 for F-18 FDOPA from Feinstein Institute. The study title is “Fluorodopa F 18 PET Imaging for the Differential Diagnosis of Parkinsonian Syndromes” (Study #17). This new clinical study (study #17) is submitted to address the Clinical/Statistical and GCP deficiencies listed in the FDA Complete Response Letter.
L611: 4 Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
L612: STUDY# 17: FLUORODOPA F 18 PET IMAGING FOR THE DIFFERENTIAL DIAGNOSIS OF PARKINSONIAN SYNDROMES
L613: 4.1.1. Study Design
L614: Overview and Objective
L615: “Study #17” is a prospective single center clinical study conducted at the Feinstein institute under Expanded IND 78861 with a request to charge for F-18 F-DOPA. The main objective of the study was to determine the sensitivity and specificity of Fluorodopa PET in patients suspected of Parkinsonian syndromes. The study title is “Fluorodopa F 18 PET Imaging for the Differential Diagnosis of Parkinsonian Syndromes”.
L616: Trial Design
L617: PET scans were evaluated based on the visualization of the size and shape of the putamen/caudate in the striatum. Each patient had a Fluorodopa PET scan to determine the condition of the striatum and the patients were followed up one to two years past the date of their PET scan in order to establish the standard of truth (SOT). In some cases the SOT was received earlier than a year based on the decision by the referring physician.
L618: A total of 45 patients were given Fluorodopa F 18 PET scans. Three independent image readers read the scans to evaluate the shape and size of the putamen/caudate to determine the loss or no loss of dopaminergic neurons. Visual interpretation of the scans were recorded either as positive (loss of dopaminergic neurons) or negative (no loss of dopaminergic neurons).
L619: Enrollment Criteria
L620: Enrollment criteria: Patients with 1-2 signs and symptoms of Parkinsonian syndrome (tremor,
L621: Clinical ReviewBrenda YeNDA 200655 Class 2 ResubmissionFluorodopa F-18 Injection (FDOPA)
L622: rigidity, gait disturbance)
L623: Reviewer's comments: Approximately one year after the Expanded Access IND for F-18 FDOPA with a Request to Charge request was granted in June 2012, the applicant requested to relax the enrollment criteria from two signs and symptoms of Parkinsonian syndromes to one sign/symptom of Parkinsonian syndromes, because under the previously set enrollment criteria the study recruited almost no patients in a year. The applicant submitted this request along with an annual renewal for a request to charge under the Expanded Access IND for F-DOPA. The agency granted the requested enrollment change and the charge renewal request.
L624: Inclusion Criteria: All patients in this study were referred by Neurologists and exhibited at least one of the cardinal features of parkinsonian syndromes (bradykinesia, rigidity, tremor, and/or gait disorder).
L625: Reviewer's comments: Another 'enrollment criterion' that was not specified in the protocol but was implemented by the applicant was that only patients who were willing to pay for the FDOPA PET were studied, since the applicant states it has limited funding and resources. The application states that "As indicated to FDA since the Fluorodopa Expanded Access IND was not reimbursed by CMS we had to depend upon patients who are willing to pay for the clinical studies".
L626: Study Drug Dosing
L627: Fluorodopa F 18 Injection (150-185 MBq, 4.05-5.0 mCi) was administered by intravenous injection.
L628: Carbidopa (150 mg) was administered orally to patients approximately 60-90 minutes prior to administration of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection. The optimal dose of 150 mg was determined based on the investigator's own experience as well as other peer-reviewed publications. Other clinical study groups have used lower than 150 mg of Carbidopa to generate well resolved images. A maximum dose of 200 mg was recommended by Bristol Myers Squibb according to the package insert for carbidopa published in September 2006.
L629: Image Interpretation
L630: Three separate onsite readers read the scans to evaluate the shape and size of the putamen/caudate to determine the loss or no loss of dopaminergic neurons. A reduced uptake in the putamen or both putamen and caudate, unilaterally or bilaterally indicates the loss of dopaminergic neurons. The scans were interpreted visually and the results were reported as positive or negative.
L631: Clinical ReviewBrenda YeNDA 200655 Class 2 ResubmissionFluorodopa F-18 Injection (FDOPA)
L632: Reviewer's comments: it is unclear whether the readers were blinded. The application describes the procedure as blinded image interpretation, although what was blinded to the readers is not described clearly in the application.
L633: Standard of Truth
L634: The applicant followed the referring physician up to 1 year and in some cases up to 2 years to determine the final clinical diagnosis, which is the standard of truth (SOT). In some cases the SOT was received earlier than one year.
L635: Reviewer's comments: The final clinical diagnosis used as the standard of truth was made by the referring Physician, who also received results of the FDOPA PET from the Feinstein Institute. Therefore, the standard of truth is biased by the results of the F-DOPA PET results.
L636: 4.1.2. Study Results
L637: Compliance with Good Clinical Practices
L638: The applicant did not submit a statement attesting that the new clinical study (Study #17) was conducted in accordance with Good Clinical Practice. However, the applicant emphasized in the resubmission that the study was conducted under Institutional Review Board (IRB).
L639: Because of the data integrity issues uncovered during the last review cycle (cited in the FDA CR letter), the review team again consulted the FDA Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI). At the time of the completion of this clinical review, OSI GCP inspection has been scheduled for early May 2016, with the GCP inspection report expected in the ensuing weeks.
L640: Patient Disposition
L641: The total number of patients referred to The Feinstein Institute for this study was 45 (or 46).
L642: Reviewer's comments: It is unclear from the resubmission if the total number of patients referred to the clinical study was 45 or 46. The submission narratives state 45 subjects, but some of the study tables, including the recently submitted patient images suggested 46 subjects.
L643: Out of 45 (or 46) patients, 32 had positive PET scans and 13 had negative PET scans. The applicant was able to obtain the Standard of Truth (SOT) in 30 patients who had positive PET scan results. Two patients who had positive PET scan results have a pending SOT. Out of the 13 negative scans, the applicant was able to collect SOT for 6 patients and the other 7 are pending.
L644: The application states that “Out of the 13 negative scans, we received SOT's for 6 patients and the other 7 are pending. We have made several attempts to collect SOT's from the referring physicians for the 7 patients, but were not successful. We will continue our attempts but we are not very confident. It was difficult to collect SOT's from patients with negative PET scans.”
L645: Reviewer's comments: The difficulty to collect SOTs from patients with negative PET scans likely reflects the difficulty the referring physician has in making a final clinical diagnosis for patients with negative FDOPA PET, even given a one-year clinical follow-up you period. These are likely patients who exhibited one or more sign/symptoms of Parkinsonian syndrome, but didn't respond to standard anti-Parkinson medication.
L646: Out of the 45 patients, there were 11 patients exceeding 5 years of disease duration. In 11 patients, 7 had a positive PET scan and 4 patients had a negative scan. The applicant obtained final clinical diagnosis (SOT) for all 7 patients who had a positive scan. All four patients with a negative scan have pending SOT's (Figure 1).

L650: Reviewer's comments: Note that out of the seven patients with negative FDOPA PET and pending standard of truth, four of them have disease duration exceeding five years. More than half (4 of 7) of patients with negative F-DOPA PET and pending standard of truth have a disease duration greater than five years. So a longer follow-up period is unlikely to improve the accuracy of the final clinical diagnosis, which is used as the standard truth. It seems that for patients with negative F-DOPA PET, the scan's clinical value to a clinician is limited.

L656: Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint
L657: Pre-specified primary endpoints of the study were sensitivity and specificity of F-DOPA PET imaging with final clinical diagnosis as the Standard of Truth (SOT). The accuracy of the calculated sensitivity and specificity relies on the accuracy of the standard of truth. However in this study, the reported final clinical diagnosis (standard of truth) was biased, because the final clinical diagnosis was made by the referring physician, who received results of the FDOPA PET imaging prior to making a final clinical diagnosis. In addition, although the standard of truth was obtained in over 90% of patients with positive FDOPA PET, final clinical diagnosis (SOT) was missing/pending in over 50% of the patients with negative FDOPA PET imaging (Table 2). The reviewer therefore considers the sensitivity and specificity values reported in the resubmission unreliable.
