L683: Reviewer's comments: From Table 4 and Table 5, one can see that bilateral posterior putamen is more often affected when there is striatal dopaminergic neuronal loss, as shown by reduced uptake of F-DOPA in the region compared to that of the occipital lobe, the internal control region. On the other hand, the anterior putamen and caudate may remain negative or affected less on F-DOPA PET (F-DOPA uptake not reduced compared to that of the occipital lobe, the internal control region) even when the entire scan is visually read positive and patient's clinical diagnosis is Parkinson Disease. Potentially these regional quantitative measures could be helpful in case the visual image interpretation of the FDOPA PET is border line.
L684: 5 Review of Safety
L685: 5.1. Review of the Safety Database
L686: Study #17 added 45 patients to the safety database of F-DOPA. In the study, patients were monitored visually for any physical discomfort. After the PET scans, all patients were followed up with a phone call within 24 hours to collect information regarding any possible adverse events. Forty-four patients responded to the phone call.
L687: 5.2. Safety Results
L688: In Study #17, there were five cases in which the patients had minor events. In all cases, after discussing with the patients, the Principle Investigator (PI) concluded that the minor adverse events were not caused by the administration of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection.
L689: Minor adverse events collected from the patients within 24 hours (after the PET study) are listed below:
L690: • In one subject, the patient reported mild burning in the throat in the evening of the scan. Based on the discussion with the patient, the PI concluded that the burning sensation was attributed to a pre-existing GERD condition.• Another patient reported a small bump on the right arm and cheek several hours after the scan. The patient told the PI that the patient has very sensitive skin. The bump on the arm and cheek were gone after several hours. From their discussion the PI concluded that the adverse event was not due to Fluorodopa F 18 Injection.• One patient had a mild headache in the evening of the scan and experienced left arm tremor for 15 minutes the following day. The PI followed up with the patient and concluded that these events were not related to Fluorodopa F 18 Injection.
L691: Clinical Review
L692: Brenda Ye
L693: NDA 200655 Class 2 Resubmission
L694: Fluorodopa F-18 Injection (FDOPA)
L695: • One patient reported a bruise and slight swelling 2 days after the scan, possibly at the injection site. The patient consulted with the doctor and the doctor told the patient that it could be due to the IV and these types of events are general risks of IV. There was no infection at the site and the swelling had gone down in a few days. The PI was notified of the report.• One patient reported feeling "off" and tired since the test. BP was slightly higher than usual. Symptoms were gone by the time the patient was contacted for adverse event reporting. The patient did not seek out medical treatment. The PI concluded that this particular event might be due to cessation of the neupro patch and supplements for the test subject. The PI concluded that the symptoms were not likely related to the scan.

L730: Summary
L731: In a letter dated October 25, 2013 the applicant request a face to face meeting with FDA to clarify the deficiencies in the FDA's October 22, 2013 complete response letter to the NDA. The applicant states that the proposed agenda of the meeting is to provide responses to the CR letter to clarify misunderstandings and confusions in their previous responses to FDA. The applicant's list of specific clinical questions does not contain actual questions, data or verifiable information. There are no concrete proposals to provide new clinical evidence of effectiveness. The claims, anecdotes, and explanations are not substantive enough to be the basis of a meaningful discussion.
L732: Assessment and Plan
L733: From a clinical perspective the meeting is denied because it does not contain meaningful questions or proposals for discussion. The new information the applicant provides should be included in an eventual NDA resubmission.

L737: 11/06/2013
L738: MEMORANDUM
L739: DEPARTMENT OF HEALTH &amp; HUMAN SERVICES
L740: Public Health ServiceFood and Drug AdministrationCDER/OND/ODE-IV
L741: Date: 10/15/2013From: Shaw T. Chen, M.D., Ph.D., Deputy Director, Office of Drug Evaluation-IVTo: File, NDA-200655Subject: Complete Response for NDA 200655, FDOPA (F-18 Fluorodopa)
L742: This is the ODE memo to concur with the decision to issue a Complete Response (CR) for this NDA, as recommended by the Division of Medical Imaging Products (DMIP). FDOPA is a Positron Emission Tomography (PET) imaging agent to visualize dopaminergic (b) (4) in the striatum of adult patients with suspected Parkinson's Disease (PD) or Parkinsonian Syndromes (PS).
L743: This submission is deficient in many critical elements and overall does not support the approval of this NDA. Of the review disciplines, only pharmacology-toxicology and clinical pharmacology recommend approval, other reviewers, including CMC, clinical/statistical and inspections of both manufacturing facilities and clinical data, all have described serious deficiencies that could not be resolved at the end of this review cycle. The Division has reached a conclusion to issue a CR and provided a list of issues for the applicant to resolve and move forward. This position is concurred by the Office.
L744: The long and complicated regulatory history of this drug's development has been described in detail by Dr. Ye in her medical review and summarized in the CDTL's memo by Dr. Gorovets and in the Division Director's memo by Dr. Marzella. Some PET agents, including FDOPA, have been used clinically for many years under the expanded access program of their active INDs and are allowed to charge patients to recover costs. This NDA submission represents an attempt to remediate this temporary measure and to obtain a formal marketing status for the drug. However, after an earlier submission was refused to file by the Division on 3/19/2010 (see medical reviews for reasons), the applicant's efforts have not produced an approvable package with this resubmission.
L745: Major regulatory and scientific issues of this NDA are summarized as follows, a complete list of all CR deficiencies will be provided in more detail in the action letter.
L746: I. Efficacy and Safety of FDOPA
L747: As pointed out by the medical reviewers, there is no Integrated Summary of Efficacy (ISE) in this 505(b)(2) application which relies in part on published literature for evidence of safety and efficacy of FDOPA in the proposed indication. An extensive literature review by FDA in conjunction with the public discussion at the Advisory Committee (AC) on July 28, 2000 concluded that, despite substantial clinical experience and numerous publications, the efficacy of FDOPA had never been demonstrated convincingly in adequately designed studies. More recent publications that were not available for the past AC meeting are presented in the current
L748: submission (of the 8 identified in the application as supportive for efficacy, 7 were published after 2000). Of the publications, the review team consider two are of better designs and potentially useful (Studies 016 and 021), but dismiss the other 6 reported studies as non-supportive - a conclusion concurred by this Office. In addition, this submission also presented the report of a study conducted by the sponsor (Study 015). These 3 studies are described in further details and commented in the medical reviews by Drs. Ye/Gorovets/Marzella. The main reasons that their results cannot be relied upon as evidence of efficacy are reiterated as follows.
L749: Study 015, "Clinical Study of Fluorodopa F-18 Injection Conducted by the Feinstein Institute for Medical Research",
L750: The fatal flaw of this study is the use of clinical diagnosis (including the response to treatment for PD), instead of anatomical or histopathological findings, as the standard of truth. As imaging data are usually employed to confirm clinical diagnosis, the latter cannot be used to validate the former during development, even if the images were interpreted independently by three readers blinded to clinical information (as the applicant claims). In other words, as Dr. Ye also noted, if the clinical diagnosis is the truth, then there is no need for the imaging study. The integrity of the data is also questioned by the clinical reviewer and the GCP inspector as no documentation of such multiple readings has been submitted. As described in the medical reviews, the applicant has concluded that, using the clinical diagnosis as the standard of truth, the test performs with 100% sensitivity and 100% specificity, and the readers agreed with each other completely. Again, without an appropriate standard of truth, this cannot be a reasonable estimate of the performance characteristics of the imaging study.
L751: Also noted by the medical review team, no protocol has been provided for this retrospective analysis. Thus no key elements of a prospectively designed protocol such as hypothesis testing, analytical plan, definition of primary endpoint, are available for rigorous regulatory review.
L752: Study 016, "Regional Metabolic Changes in Parkinsonian Patients with Normal Dopaminergic Imaging", Eckert et al, Movement Disorders, 22: 167-173, 2007
L753: As commented by Dr. Gorovets, this clinical follow-up of a subset of 27 patients in Study 15, who did not respond to treatment and had a negative F-18 FDOPA PET scan, could potentially serve as an estimate of specificity. The number of patients, however, is too small to provide adequate confidence in the estimate and another problem, again, is validation of the standard of truth. Absence of PD or PS requires well documented long term follow-up. Out of these 27, eight patients (the applicant increased to all 27 at the Late Cycle Meeting, but without follow-up submission of data in an amendment) were followed for a minimum of 2 years and were eventually diagnosed with a condition other than PS. Despite FDA's request for appropriate documentation of clinical follow up for this important patient cohort, no such data have been submitted to date.
L754: Study 021, "Direct Comparison of FP-CIT SPECT and FDOPA PET in Patients with Parkinson's Disease and Healthy Controls", Eshuis, et al, Eur J Nuc Med Mol Imaging 36: 454-462, 2009
L755: This is an exploratory study to compare sensitivity and specificity of DaTscan and F-18 FDOPA PET. This study enrolled patients in various stages of PD (11 early-, 17 advanced-stage and 20 age-matched controls), but lack subjects without a definitive diagnosis of PD yet (those who may benefit from the imaging study). The one-year follow-up clinical diagnosis was used as the
L756: standard of truth. While the study did show that DaTscan SPECT and FDOPA PET were similar in their performance, the data are too limited to serve as evidence of their equivalence. The reviewers (e.g., see Dr. Ye's review) are also concerned about the definition of positive scans, which is based on an exploratory quantitative measurement, and how the specificity was calculated (age-match control as the standard of truth for negative?).
L757: Overall, data from these three studies are not sufficient to provide substantial evidence of effectiveness.
L758: Safety
L759: While not much safety information was submitted in this application, lack of such data is not considered a CR issue. Based on the experience of its use under IND, reports in literature, non-clinical evaluation and other relevant data (e.g., clinical pharmacology, experience with Levodopa), the review team is not concerned about the safety of this drug. This conclusion is concurred.
L760: II. Other Deficiencies
L761: The deficiencies identified by other review disciplines, as summarized in the reviews by CDTL and the Division Director, are all serious enough to serve as the basis for issuing a CR to this application. See the CR letter for a complete listing, the major ones include:
L762: Integrity of clinical data
L763: Many violations of GCP standards (see inspection report by Dr. John Lee of OSI and summary of the issues by Dr. Gorovets) raise questions about the reliability of most of the information provided by the applicant.
L764: CMC &amp; GMP
L765: Major remaining CMC issues are specifications for the precursor and drug product, data for stability at low pH and validation of the HPLC purity test. Approval by microbiology reviewers is also pending a “complete response to microbiology deficiencies”, which include (b) (4) the lack of adequate validation for bacterial endotoxins assay at low pH.
L766: Violations of GMP reflect in general the above deficiencies in CMC/microbiology. To this date, the applicant has not addressed satisfactorily the issues identified in the inspection report (see Form 483). The Office of Compliance (OC)'s current recommendation for this NDA is “Withhold”.
L767: III. Path Forward
L768: For the product to be approvable in the next review cycle, the sponsor needs to follow the instruction in the CR letter and address all the deficiencies identified. Among those listed, a well-designed new clinical study with an acceptable truth standard is the most critical. The applicant should also comply with GCP regulations, provide better documentation of the trial.
L769: IV. Availability under IND
L770: While this submission cannot support marketing of FDOPA as an approved imaging agent, there is no new data suggesting that the product quality and clinical effects of FDOPA have changed significantly from the past IND experience. The Division's recommendation to keep the IND active, to continue expanded use and to allow charging patients for cost, is thus reasonable and concurred by the Office.
L771: V. Conclusions
L772: The benefit/risk assessment is straightforward for this product, as provided in the reviews by the clinical team and the Division Director's memo. Based on the data presented in this submission, FDOPA cannot be approved as a PET agent for the proposed indication of imaging in Parkinson's Disease or Parkinsonian Syndrome. A CR should be issued as recommended by the Division.

L786: 1. Introduction
L787: The Feinstein Institute for Medical Research has submitted this 505(b)(2) NDA application for Fluorodopa F 18 Injection, a radioactive diagnostic agent for use with Positron Emission Tomography (PET) to visualize dopaminergic (b) (4) in the striatum in adults with suspected Parkinsonian syndromes.
L788: Regulatory history of fluorodopa F 18 in the US
L789: Fluorodopa F 18 is a radiopharmaceutical for PET imaging. Historically fluorodopa F 18 was produced in the US in compliance with United States Pharmacopoeia (USP) PET compounding standards and monographs and under licensure by the Nuclear Regulatory Commission (NRC). Fluorodopa F 18 was legally marketed for clinical use in positron emission tomography (PET) imaging of neurologic and oncologic conditions. The applicant (Feinstein Institute) has legally produced fluorodopa F 18 and used the drug in clinical practice at the NorthShore University Hospital Center for several years.
L790: The more recent legislative history of fluorodopa F 18 and other PET drugs in clinical use begins with the determination by FDA that approved applications for PET drugs are required because these drugs are not deemed generally recognized as safe and effective under section 201(p)(1) and (p)(2) of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic (FDCA) Act.
L791: The 1997 Food and Drug Administration Modernization Act (FDAMA, section 121) directed FDA to establish appropriate procedures for the approval of PET drugs and appropriate requirements for current good manufacturing practice (cGMP) for such drugs. Before establishing the procedures and requirements the Act required FDA to consult with patient advocacy groups, professional associations, manufacturers, and physicians and scientists licensed to make or use PET drugs. FDA could begin to require the submission of New Drug Applications (NDAs) two years after the establishment of the procedures and requirements for the approval of PET drugs.
L792: FDA conducted a review of the scientific literature for the most commonly used PET drugs including fluorodopa F 18 and held meetings of the Medical Imaging Drugs Advisory Committee (MIDAC) and other public meetings to discuss the findings of the reviews.
L793: At a July 28, 2000 public meeting FDA and the Institute for Clinical PET held an “Informal Discussion of Literature Reviews of Safety and Efficacy Data for PET: 150-water and 18F-fluorodopa” The meeting concluded that available literature data for fluorodopa F 18 were insufficient to support efficacy in any patient population. It was decided that discussion of fluorodopa F 18 at MIDAC was not warranted at the time. The PET community anticipated the development of additional data from new studies and encouraged FDA to plan to reassess the evidence of fluorodopa F 18 efficacy. For the meeting transcript1 and the literature listings2 see the links below.
L794: 1 http://www.fda.gov/downloads/Drugs/DevelopmentApprovalProcess/Manufacturing/UCM182861.txt
L795: 2 http://www.fda.gov/downloads/Drugs/DevelopmentApprovalProcess/Manufacturing/UCM183457.pdf
L796: In a March 10, 2000 notice in the Federal Register, FDA announced its findings that certain commonly used PET drugs, when produced under conditions specified in approved applications, can be found to be safe and effective for certain indications.3 FDA also announced the approval procedures for these PET drugs and indications and invited manufacturers of these drugs to submit applications for approval.
L797: FDA had approved NDAs for three PET drugs: sodium fluoride F 18 to define areas of altered osteogenic activity, rubidium chloride 82 for assessing regional myocardial perfusion in the diagnosis and localization of myocardial infarction and fludeoxyglucose (FDG) F 18 injection for the identification of regions of abnormal glucose metabolism associated with foci of epileptic seizures. FDA also announced its findings of safety and effectiveness for FDG F 18 for use in oncology and for assessment of myocardial hibernation, and for ammonia N 13 for evaluation of myocardial blood flow. FDA also issued a guidance document that describes how to complete either a 505(b)(2) or a 505(j) application for the PET products addressed in the Federal Register notice.
L798: On December 9, 2009 FDA issued regulations establishing cGMP for PET drugs. The regulations in 21 CFR Part 212 apply to the production, quality control, holding, and distribution of new PET drugs. The publication of these procedures triggered the implementation timeline specified by the 1997 FDAMA. At the request of drug manufacturers, FDA extended the timeline until June 12, 2012. After that date any facility wishing to continue to produce PET drugs for clinical use was required to have submitted an NDA, an ANDA or to be producing the drugs under an IND. All PET producers must be operating under an approved NDA or ANDA, or effective IND, by December 12, 2015.
L799: The clinical reviewer (Dr. Ye) and the cross discipline team leader (CDTL, Dr. Gorovets) provide a more detailed discussion of the difficult regulatory history of this application.
L800: On October 30, 2009 the applicant submitted a marketing application for fluorodopa F 18. FDA refused to file the application because of inadequate clinical, nonclinical, and CMC data. Notably the NDA did not appear to contain data from adequate and well-controlled studies. FDA acknowledged that many patients had received fluorodopa F 18 injection at the applicant's clinical site. FDA encouraged the applicant to summarize the safety and diagnostic efficacy data from that experience.
L801: The applicant has raised concerns about the ability of his institution to continue to make fluorodopa F 18 available for clinical use to patients with PS. The applicant has also criticized FDA for what they perceive is a lack of fairness in the action FDA took with fluorodopa F 18 (refusal to file) compared to the action FDA took with other PET drugs (e.g. fludeoxyglucose F18, ammonia N 13) for which FDA conducted a literature review and made a finding of safety and effectiveness.
L802: 3 http://www.fda.gov/ohms/dockets/98fr/031000a.txt
L803: FDA encouraged the applicant to submit an expanded access IND with a request to charge for fluorodopa F 18. FDA allowed the expanded access IND to proceed and granted the request to charge on June 21, 2012.
L804: Regulatory history of fluorodopa F 18 in the EU
L805: Fluorodopa F 18 was approved for marketing for clinical use in Europe through mutual recognition under the “well established clinical use” procedure. France served as the reference member state. According to Article 10A of Directive 2001/83/EC, this approval procedure applies if a medicinal product has been in well-established use within the European Community for at least 10 years, and has recognized efficacy and an acceptable level of safety. Under this procedure the results of pre-clinical tests or clinical trials are not needed for approval and can be replaced by appropriate scientific literature. The review team examined the assessment report for fluorodopa F 18 provided by the French Health Authorities (Dr. Y M Chevalme of ANSM) and determined that no new clinical trial data had been developed for this EU approval. In the EU, fluorodopa F 18 has a neurology indication similar to that of ioflupane I 123 (DaTscan, approved in the US and EU) and additionally has an oncology indication. Fluorodopa F 18 is marketed in the EU under the name Dopacis or IASODopa.
L806: Ioflupane I 123
L807: Ioflupane I 123 (DaTscan) is a radiopharmaceutical for Single Photon Emission Computed Tomography (SPECT) imaging approved in the US in 2011 as an adjunct to other diagnostic evaluations in adults with Parkinsonian syndromes (PS) for differentiating essential tremor from tremor due to PS. Ioflupane I 123 targets the dopamine transporter and the resulting image is similar to the image produced by fluorodopa F 18.
L808: Focus of summary review
L809: The summary review focuses on the serious deficiencies in the clinical studies submitted by the applicant. These deficiencies include the lack of a well defined prospective clinical protocol, statistical analysis plan, procedure for blinded independent interpretation of the PET images, and lack of an adequate truth standard. In addition the FDA review of the literature did not identify adequate and well controlled clinical trials. The integrity of the data from the studies conducted by the applicant has also been questioned.
L810: The proposed remedy is to conduct an adequate and well controlled trial in patient with early features of Parkinsonism with a final clinical diagnosis established by a consensus panel of movement disorder experts who evaluate data over an adequate period of follow up (e.g. 3 years).
L811: 2. Background
L812: Fluorodopa F 18 is chemically similar to dopamine and like dopamine it is taken up by dopaminergic neurons. Parkinsonian syndromes are characterized by diminished striatal dopamine uptake principally due to loss of dopaminergic neurons. Fluorodopa F 18 uptake and retention can be visualized in a PET image and might be useful in the evaluation of patients suspected of having a Parkinsonian syndrome.
L813: 3. CMC/Device
L814: I concur with the conclusions reached by the chemistry reviewer Dr. Martin Haber regarding the manufacturing of the drug product and drug substance. Dr Haber determined that major chemistry manufacturing and control issues in the NDA include the specifications for the chiral precursor, drug product specifications, data for stability at low pH, and validation data for the HPLC purity test. I agree with Dr. Haber's recommendation for a complete response action.
L815: I concur with the overall Office of Compliance recommendation to withhold approval because the manufacturing site inspection was not acceptable. The form 483 listed 10 observations related to current good manufacturing practices, standard operating procedures, and various product specification issues.
L816: I concur with the recommendation by the product quality microbiology reviewer Dr. Robert Mello for a complete response action. Dr Mello determined that (b) (4) the bacterial endotoxin assay is not validated at the low pH of the drug product.
L817: 4. Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L818: I concur with the conclusions reached by the pharmacology and toxicology reviewer Dr. Sally Hargus that there are no outstanding pharmacology or toxicology issues that preclude approval of this application. Dr. Hargus deferred review of the labeling. The applicant did not submit any original nonclinical data and Dr Hargus determined that no original data were required.
L819: The applicant submitted summaries of 20 publically available reports on the pharmacology and toxicology of fluorodopa F 18. In addition the applicant cited the review of the preclinical data conducted by FDA and the results of the review discussed at the 2000 Public Meeting with the Institute for Clinical PET. The FDA supervisory pharmacologist Dr. Adebayo Laniyonu performed that review. At that time, the available nonclinical information on fluorodopa F 18, and the nonclinical and clinical scientific literature on levodopa were judged to be sufficient to support the clinical use of fluorodopa F 18 for PET imaging studies.
L820: Dr. Hargus concludes that at this time the available information is acceptable and conventional pharmacology and toxicology studies for fluorodopa F 18 are not needed because of: clinical experience with fluorodopa F 18, without safety signals; historical database and clinical experience with levodopa administered at daily doses approximately a thousand-fold greater than the one-time mass dose of fluorodopa F 18; similarity in the metabolism and disposition of levodopa and fluorodopa F 18; very low risk of toxicity based on the radioactive dose of fluorodopa F 18 of (b) (4) 5 mCi.
L821: 5. Clinical Pharmacology/Biopharmaceutics
L822: I concur with the conclusions reached by the clinical pharmacology reviewer Dr. Christy John that there are no outstanding clinical pharmacology issues that preclude approval of this application. Dr. John deferred review of labeling. Dr. John's clinical pharmacology review is based on 14 scientific publications submitted by the applicant. No clinical pharmacology studies were conducted by the applicant and none were needed.
L823: Dr. John found that the proposed mass dose (approximately \frac{10}{4} mg) and the radioactive dose (approximately 5 mCi) was based on review of publications and are acceptable. Fluorodopa F 18 undergoes decarboxylation by aromatic amino acid decarboxylase; to decrease decarboxylation and increase bioavailability to the brain, patients are pretreated with carbidopa. Dr John found the selected carbidopa dose of 150 mg based on the publications to be acceptable. The applicant's clinical study required patients to discontinue any Parkinsonian medications at least 12 hours prior to the PET study to avoid the possibility of interaction with fluorodopa F 18 and this procedure was judged to be reasonable.
L824: 6. Clinical Microbiology
L825: This section is not applicable. No clinical microbiology data were provided and none are needed.
L826: 7. Clinical/Statistical-Efficacy
L827: I concur with the assessment of the clinical and statistical reviewers that the application does not contain any acceptable verifiable evidence of fluorodopa F 18 performance characteristics or evidence of the drug's clinical utility. I agree with the recommendation for a complete response action. The following is a summary of the key evidence and of the conclusions reached by Drs. Ye and Zalkikar for the two studies (study 15 and 16) conducted by the applicant.
L828: Study 15
L829: The principal efficacy study (study 15) is titled: "Clinical Study of Fluorodopa F-18 Injection Conducted by the Feinstein Institute for Medical Research". This retrospective single-center study enrolled 196 subjects. Of these 196 subjects, 11 were healthy volunteers and 185 were patients who were referred by a movement disorder specialist and exhibited at least two of the features of Parkinsonian syndromes (bradykinesia, rigidity, tremor, and/or gait disorder). Subjects received 150 mg of carbidopa one hour before fluorodopa F 18 Injection (4-5 mCi).
L830: The primary efficacy endpoint was the qualitative binary visual assessment of the fluorodopa F 18 PET scan. The scan was judged to be positive if the uptake of fluorodopa F 18 in the
L831: striatum was reduced as evidenced by a diminished size and shape of the putamen and caudate (crescent shaped structure) unilaterally or bilaterally. The PET scan was judged to be negative (i.e. consistent with no loss of dopaminergic neurons) if full crescent-shaped structures were visualized in the striatum. The applicant states that a total of 158 out of 185 patients were classified as positive by three independent readers blinded to the clinical diagnosis. The applicant concludes that the study outcome was no false positive and no false negative scans and complete agreement among the three independent readers.
L832: The critical weakness of the study is that each patient's clinical diagnosis at the time of the PET scan was considered the standard of truth. For the 158 patients whose initial clinical diagnosis was "Parkinson's disease" and who were responding to treatment, a fluorodopa F 18 scan provides no additional clinically useful information. FDA therefore does not agree with the cited diagnostic performance measurements as 100% for sensitivity and 100% for specificity.
L833: The study was not conducted according to a pre-specified protocol, and definitions of primary efficacy endpoint and standard of truth that would normally be specified by a protocol, are not well-defined for the study. The NDA contains no documentation that fluorodopa F 18 PET images were read independently by three readers or that the readings were performed without clinical information. The FDA inspectors determined that none of the audited cases contained documentation that the images were read by three blinded readers.
L834: Study 16
L835: This study is a continuation of the clinical study # 15 conducted by the applicant to further evaluate the patients that exhibited Parkinsonian symptoms but had a negative fluorodopa F 18 scan. The applicant states that the 27 patients with negative scans were followed for 2-4 years. Drs. Ye and Zalkikar determined that only 8 of the 27 patients were followed to obtain the patient's final clinical diagnosis. Clinical data from such a small number of patients and with so much missing data do not provide a reliable assessment of the performance characteristics of fluorodopa F 18.
L836: 8. Safety
L837: I agree with the conclusions of the preclinical and clinical reviewers (Drs. Gorovets, Hargus, Laniyonu, and Ye) that fluorodopa F 18 does not pose important safety risks based on the single administration of a small mass dose and a small radioactive dose. The extrapolation to fluorodopa of the clinical safety experience with levodopa seems reasonable.
L838: The applicant states that none of the subjects of the study experienced adverse events. No other clinical safety data were collected. The original FDA literature review of fluorodopa F 18 and the present update have not identified safety signals. The adequacy of safety assessments in published studies generally cannot be assessed and the data cannot be verified.
L839: 9. Advisory Committee Meeting
L840: No advisory committee meetings are planned.
L841: 10. Pediatrics
L842: The applicant requested a full waiver of pediatric studies in an amendment to the NDA on January 3, 2013. Dr Ye and Dr. Gorovets agreed that studies of fluorodopa F 18 would be impossible or highly impractical in pediatric patients because Parkinsonian Syndromes are found mostly in adults. On June 5, 2013, the FDA Pediatric Review Committee agreed with granting a full pediatric waiver.
L843: 11. Other Relevant Regulatory Issues
L844: There are important relevant regulatory issues related to OSI audits for compliance with good clinical practice (GCP) in the conduct of the applicant's studies. Form 483 was issued for failure to prepare and maintain adequate case histories, and inadequate investigational drug disposition records with respect to dates, quantity, and use by subjects. Specifically, Dr. Lee's report states that the subject files typically lacked documentation of the presence at enrollment of at least two features of PD and/or responsiveness to PD drugs. None of the case records examined contained evidence of PET scan interpretation by three separate blinded readers. For study 16 none of the subject files contained clinical follow-up confirmation of absence of PD symptoms and signs.

L848: 13. Decision/Action/Risk Benefit Assessment
L849: • Regulatory Action
L850: I concur with the recommendations for a complete response action made by the NDA reviewers in the following disciplines: chemistry manufacturing and controls, microbiological quality, clinical, and statistics. This application provides no acceptable measurements of the test scan's performance characteristics or evidence of the test scan's clinical utility.

L852: • Risk Benefit Assessment
L853: The important clinical and statistical deficiencies of the clinical studies conducted by the applicant preclude any conclusions about the efficacy of fluorodopa F 18. Review and inspectional findings of the clinical experience summarized by the applicant in the NDA shows that the data are not reliable.
L854: The FDA review of the scientific literature in 2000 led to the conclusion that the publications at that time did not support the efficacy of fluorodopa F 18. In this NDA review cycle FDA has re-evaluated the more recent scientific literature and has examined reports prepared for marketing authorizations by other health authorities. However, FDA has not identified reports of adequate and well controlled studies of fluorodopa F 18 for the use proposed by the applicant. As such there is no substantial evidence of efficacy of the drug.
L855: At the time of the NDA late-cycle meeting FDA provided to the applicant a complete listing of the deficiencies identified in the NDA. Subsequently FDA inspectors have identified serious GCP deficiencies. The CR letter will describe in some detail how to address the NDA deficiencies. However the applicant has stated that they lack the resources or personnel with the experience and qualifications to design and conduct an adequate and controlled clinical study of fluorodopa F 18. The applicant has been unable to obtain support from other government agencies, professional organizations, or commercial manufacturers for the clinical development of the drug.

L864: Cross-Discipline Team Leader Review
L865: DateSeptember 09, 2013FromAlex Gorovets, MDSubjectCross-Discipline Team Leader ReviewNDA#200655ApplicantFeinstein Institute for Medical ResearchDate of Submission10-22-2012PDFA Goal Date10-22-2013Proprietary Name / Established (USAN) namesFluorodopa F-18 injection/F-18 FluorodopaDosage forms / Strength(b) (4) 5.0 mCiProposed Indication(s)For use in Positron Emission Tomography (PET) to visualize dopaminergic (b) (4) in the striatum in adult patients with suspected parkinsonian syndromesRecommended:Complete Response
L866: 1. Introduction
L867: The subject of this Cross Disciplinary Team Leader (CDTL) review is the New Drug Application (NDA) 200655 for F-18 Fluorodopa (FDOPA), a radiopharmaceutical proposed for use with Positron Emission Tomography (PET) in patients with parkinsonian syndromes.
L868: The applicant is the Feinstein Institute for Medical Research where the drug is being produced. It is a 505(b)(2) application which relies in part on published literature for evidence of safety and efficacy of F-18 FDOPA in the proposed indication and also provides data from a single study conducted by the applicant. There is no approved marketing application in US for F-18 FDOPA.
L869: The applicant proposes the following indication for F-18 FDOPA: Fluorodopa F 18 Injection is a (b) (4) used in Positron Emission Tomography (PET) to visualize dopaminergic (b) (4) in the striatum in adult patients with suspected parkinsonian syndromes (PS). (b) (4)
L870: Fluorodopa F 18 PET is an adjunct to other diagnostic evaluations.
L871: This review document addresses the evidence submitted by the applicant in support of the stated claim, considers the issues of clinical utility and product quality, and provides an assessment of risk and benefit of FDOPA-PET. The document refers to analyses and review
L872: findings from multiple disciplines. During the review process, no significant disagreements have been encountered among the reviewers.
L873: 2. Background
L874: Rationale for Product Development
L875: Fluorodopa is chemically closely related to dopamine. In the basal ganglia region of the brain, F-18 labeled fluorodopa (FDOPA) is converted to F-18 Fluorodopamine by aromatic amino acid decarboxylase (AAAD). Like dopamine, it is taken up by the dopaminergic neurons. The main component of the basal ganglia, striatum consisting of caudate nucleus and putamen, is the primary area of the brain affected by dopaminergic neuron loss in movement disorders like Parkinson's disease (PD) and other parkinsonian syndromes (PS). Such a neuronal loss is associated with a decline in an overall striatal dopamine uptake which is reflected by a diminished F-18 labeled fluorodopa uptake as would be visualized on a PET image.
L876: PD is a well characterized progressive neuro-degenerative disorder of unknown etiology most noted for its tremor in addition to certain other motor abnormalities and neurologic signs. Diagnosis is established clinically and certain symptoms can be treated medically. Early diagnosis is sometimes problematic and PD not uncommonly, especially in general practice, has to be differentiated from Essential Tremor, a benign condition, which can also be treated medically but quite differently from PD.
L877: A normal striatal complex has a characteristic comma-like appearance on a radionuclide uptake scan. In parkinsonian patients, the uptake is diminished and the typical appearance is lost.
L878: Regulatory History
L879: F-18 FDOPA has long been a research tool in studies of Parkinson's related, or parkinsonian, syndromes and movement disorders but its clinical usefulness has been long debated as well. The primary clinical reviewer of this application, Dr. Brenda Ye, in her summary of the regulatory history of FDOPA (see pages 8-9 of her review) cites the 2000 Public Meeting involving FDA where it was concluded "that available literature data for F-18 FDOPA were insufficient to support efficacy in any patient population". The rest of her summary documents the lengthy history of the regulatory relationship between the FDA and the current applicant.
L880: F-18 FDOPA has been produced at Feinstein Institute and used in clinical practice at the NorthShore University Hospital Center for many years as an "unapproved" PET drug with a USP monograph. As explained in the recently published FDA Guidance on Investigational New Drug Applications for Positron Emission Tomography (PET) Drugs, the Food and Drug Administration Modernization Act of 1997 (FDAMA, or "the Modernization Act") provided that "certain unapproved PET drugs would not be considered adulterated until the new current good manufacturing practice (CGMP) regulations for PET drugs (21 CFR part 212) took effect, if the production facility maintained compliance with United States Pharmacopeia (USP) monograph expectations for the specific drug".
L881: The Guidance cites twelve PET drugs which had USP monographs as of September 1, 2011. Fluorodopa F-18 injection is listed as one of these drugs.
L882: The Guidance further states that “the publication of the final rule on CGMP for PET drugs on December 10, 2009 triggered the requirement that all producers of PET drugs submit applications by December 12, 2011” and that “all PET producers must be operating under an approved NDA or ANDA, or effective IND, by December 12, 2015.”
L883: The current applicant has an active IND (78861) since 2008 which has existed mostly in a “pre-IND stage” until 2012 when the enrolment of patients into an Expanded Access program under this IND has been approved by the FDA.
L884: The applicant has first submitted the current NDA (200655) in 2009 but the FDA found the application to be seriously deficient and issued a “Refuse to File” letter. So this is now a re-submission following the refuse-to-file determination.
L885: During this review cycle, multiple deficiencies and review issues have been identified by reviewers from various disciplines and communicated to the applicant before and after the Filing meeting, after the Midcycle meeting and at the Late Cycle Meeting. Of note, following the Filing meeting, the review team estimated that deficiencies remaining at the time were mostly review issues which would have to be further analyzed, and the application was filed.
L886: Other Relevant Background
L887: A Single Photon Emission Computed Tomography (SPECT) agent, I-123 Ioflupane, or DaTscan, has been recently approved in US and in Europe for dopamine transporter visualization in striatum. It targets a different neuronal protein, a dopamine transporter, but an overall imaging appearance is similar to FDOPA. In patients with PS, I-123 Ioflupane is indicated for use in differentiating essential tremor from tremor due to PS.
L888: F-18 FDOPA has been approved in Europe for a neurology indication similar to DaTscan and an oncology indication and is distributed by a commercial manufacturer under the name DOPACIS.
L889: 3. CMC/Device
L890: The following is excerpted from the primary Chemistry, Manufacturing and Controls (CMC) review by Dr. Martin Haber (a list of the required specifications is included to help assess the extent of ongoing deficiencies).
L891: Fluorodopa F-18 Injection drug product is a sterile product for intravenous injection containing 5 – 100 mCi of fluorodopa F-18 in (b) (4) mL of sterile water [the proposed volume has been since modified]. The drug product specifications follow the USP drug product monograph for fluorodopa F 18 injection and include tests for appearance, radionuclidic
L892: identity, radiochemical identity and purity, chemical identity, radionuclidic purity, assay (amount of radioactivity), specific activity (mCi/nmol), pH, (b) (4), bacterial endotoxin testing, sterility testing, osmolality and chemical impurity testing for breakthrough. The expiry is limited by the short F-18 half-life and is set at 10 hours after synthesis. (b) (4)
L893: The drug substance is 6-[18F]-fluoro-L-3, 4-dihydroxyphenylalanine or F-18 labeled FDOPA. Carrier added F-18 fluorine is produced in a cyclotron at the Feinstein Institute. (b) (4)
L894: The recommended dose is (b) (4) 5 mCi. The sterile drug product (5-100 mCi at end of synthesis) is supplied in a capped (b) (4) multi-dose (b) (4) glass vial.
L895: A number of CMC deficiencies have been identified throughout the current review cycle and various information requests have been sent to the applicant and specifically, among others, on 12/28/2012 and 05/31/2013. At the time of the completion of the primary review on 06/13/2013, the CMC reviewer was recommending a Complete Response because the sponsor had not responded adequately to those requests. In particular, the specifications for the drug product and drug substance precursor have not been finalized, data justifying a non-USP conforming pH have not been provided and validation data for the HPLC chiral identity and purity method have not been provided.
L896: The Product Quality Microbiology reviewer, Dr. Robert Mello, has also identified a number of deficiencies which were being communicated to the applicant throughout the review cycle. These include the issues related to sterility testing, endotoxin testing, (b) (4) testing as well as the description of the whole set-up where the drug is produced to ensure that the drug production at this site, from a microbiology perspective, complies with the regulations that apply to a drug approved for marketing. At the time the primary review has been filed on 06/04/2013, the reviewer has concluded that “due to the deficiencies in sampling and validation of (b) (4)” the sterility and non-pyrogenicity of the drug product could not be fully assured.
L897: Following the Good Manufacturing Practice (GMP) inspections in May 2013, the applicant was issued a Form 483 listing 10 observations. Overall, the inspectional observations paralleled the CMC and Microbiology review issues and deficiencies. So far, the applicant’s responses have not satisfied all of the deficiencies, and currently, the Office of Compliance (OC) recommendation for this NDA is “Withhold”.
L898: The CMC and the Product Microbiology reviewers have just filed review updates based on applicant’s submissions in August. Of note, the final drug product volume has now been revised by the applicant from (b) (4) mL to the range (b) (4) mL. The persistent review issue has been the pH of the drug product being measured as (b) (4) whereas the specified pH range in the Fluorodopa F-18 USP monograph is (b) (4) (e.g., higher). The new proposed specification range for the product pH is now (b) (4). Adequate supporting stability data at
L899: different pH values have not been provided. Validation data for the HPLC purity test or a validation plan have still not been provided. At this time and for this review cycle, the CMC recommendation for this NDA is still Complete Response. Major remaining CMC issues are specifications for the chiral precursor, drug product specifications, data for stability at low pH and validation data for the HPLC purity test.
L900: As for the Microbiology, the updated assessment states that the application is “approvable” pending a “complete response to microbiology deficiencies”. Given that, most likely, no additional amendments, even if submitted, will be reviewed in this cycle, the recommended action, as clarified during the Wrap-up meeting on 08/28/2013, is a Complete Response. The remaining deficiencies are (b) (4) and the lack of adequate validation of the bacterial endotoxins assay to address the low pH (b) (4) of the drug product.
L901: It is important to note that at the Wrap-up meeting both disciplines, the CMC and the Product Microbiology, confirmed that, although at present the remaining deficiencies prevent an approval of this NDA based on the applicable NDA regulations, it would be safe to continue to use this drug from a product quality standpoint under the currently active IND application.
L902: 4. Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L903: The main aspect of pharmacologic and toxicologic evaluation of F-18 FDOPA is that F-18 FDOPA and Levodopa, a well established therapeutic drug for PD, are nearly identical molecules. Prescribed therapeutic doses of Levodopa for chronic daily use significantly exceed a one-time intravenously administered F-18 FDOPA mass dose of about (b) (4) mg or less as cited in this discipline review. The radioactive dose of (b) (4) 5.0 mCi is thought to present an acceptable risk in terms of radiation exposure.
L904: Of further note is that, as described in the review by the primary Pharmacology/Toxicology reviewer, Dr. Sally Hargus, FDA has already conducted a review and evaluation of F-18 FDOPA Injection in preparation for a Public Meeting in July 2000. FDA has determined at the time that the available nonclinical pharmacology information on F-18 FDOPA, together with the Pharmacology/Toxicology information and clinical literature on Levodopa, can be considered adequate to support clinical use of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection for PET imaging studies.
L905: No additional nonclinical data were required by FDA to be submitted with the current application. The Pharmacology/Toxicology review team recommends approval.
L906: 5. Clinical Pharmacology/Biopharmaceutics
L907: The primary Clinical Pharmacology reviewer, Dr. Christy John, confirms that the proposed dose of F-18 FDOPA is (b) (4) 5 mCi administered as an intravenous injection. The selection of dose was based on the applicant’s review of literature. The primary information for clinical pharmacology review of this submission has been derived from fourteen publications submitted by the applicant. No clinical pharmacology studies were conducted by the applicant.
L908: The mass dose associated with the proposed radioactive dose is cited in this review as (b) (4) mg which is different from the (b) (4) mg dose cited in the Pharmacology/Toxicology review. The difference stems from a wide range of the product's Specific Activity (SA). Issues associated with the product's SA have been addressed in a greater detail in the CMC review. The product specification calls for a minimal SA of (b) (4) mCi per mmol. The mass dose, cited by the Clinical Pharmacology review, has been derived from this SA value. The actual product's SA can be significantly greater leading to a dose calculation cited in the Pharmacology/Toxicology review.
L909: As part of its mechanism of action and as already described in the Background section above, F-18 FDOPA undergoes decarboxylation by the enzyme AAAD. In order to decrease decarboxylation by AAAD and increase FDOPA bioavailability to the brain, patients are pretreated with carbidopa, a known inhibitor of AAAD. According to Dr. John, the applicant has selected the carbidopa dose of 150 mg based on the assessment of peer-reviewed publications.
L910: Following the intravenous administration, F-18 FDOPA is cleared from the blood with a biologic half-life of about 1 to 3 hours. The half-life of the F-18 radioisotope is known to be 110 minutes. Radiation dosimetry studies have identified urinary bladder as a critical organ and, although the exposure has been estimated to be well below doses that would cause concern, adequate hydration and post-administration voiding would be routinely recommended.
L911: No drug interaction studies have been reported. However, as pointed out by Dr. John, it is not known whether drugs that are used to treat Parkinsonian syndromes can alter the disposition of FDOPA or the accumulation/retention of 18F in the striatum or the rest of the brain. For this reason, the reviewer agrees with the applicant's recommendation to discontinue any Parkinsonian medications at least 12 hours prior to the PET study. No special population studies or organ impairment studies have been reported. Based on the clinical experience with Levodopa, an effect of F-18 FDOPA on QT/QTc prolongation is not expected.
L912: The Clinical Pharmacology review team recommends approval.
L913: 6. Clinical Microbiology
L914: N/A
L915: 7. Clinical/Statistical- Efficacy
L916: In assessing the efficacy data, the applicant as well as the primary clinical reviewer, Dr. Ye, and the primary statistical reviewer, Dr. Satish Misra, have emphasized the so called Study-15, a single center unpublished retrospective study conducted by the applicant. Altogether, in the
L917: current submission, the applicant attempts to rely on reports from nine studies, of which eight are publications. Of these eight, six publications as detailed by the primary reviewer do not appear to contribute to the evaluation of efficacy and will not be discussed further. The other two studies presented as possibly providing evidence of efficacy of F-18 FDOPA are Study-16 and Study-21. Study-16 is a published study of patients who actually represent a subset of patients from Study-15. Study-21 is a published study comparing F-18 FDOPA and DaTScan, an approved imaging agent.
L918: Study-15, titled “Clinical Study of Fluorodopa F-18 Injection Conducted by the Feinstein Institute for Medical Research”, involved 185 patients who were referred by neurologists presumed to be movement disorder specialists, with all patients having had at least two out of four “cardinal” signs of PD (tremor, bradykinesia, rigidity, abnormal gait). Of these, 158 patients have responded to therapy and were designated as “PD” on enrollment. A clinical indication for referring these patients for imaging is unclear as they already had a clinical diagnosis consistent with PD and had responded to treatment. There were also 27 patients who have not responded to treatment and were designated as “non-PD”. A reason for selecting any of the patients for this imaging study is unclear as no protocol has been provided. It is not clear how long the PD signs have been present in each patient prior to motor disorder specialist evaluation or prior to imaging.
L919: Each patient received 4.5 – 5 mCi dose of F-18 FDOPA prior to undergoing PET imaging. The images were visually interpreted as positive or negative, with exploratory quantitative measurements being recorded as well. According to the applicant, all the F-18 FDOPA images were interpreted independently by three readers blinded to clinical information. An important issue questioning the integrity of the submitted data has been raised in this regard by the clinical reviewer and confirmed during the GCP inspections of the clinical site as no record has been presented documenting that each image has in fact been interpreted independently by each of the readers.
L920: The results of Study-15 showed that all patients with “PD” had positive images, all patients with “non-PD” had negative images, and all readers agreed with each other. The applicant has concluded that the test performs with 100% sensitivity and 100% specificity. The clinical reviewer and the statistical reviewer both have found this interpretation to be unreasonable because the study was conducted without an acceptable standard of truth and no performance characteristics could be estimated from such a study. Not only the study does not appear to have been designed to demonstrate the clinical utility of FDOPA PET imaging in a population of patients who could benefit from such imaging, it also does not appear to have been designed to address the measurement of the test’s reliability and performance. There was no pre-specified standard of truth, no known statistical analysis plan and no proposed hypothesis testing. The applicant states in the application that patients have been followed for three to four years after FDOPA imaging, indicating its accuracy, but no such data have been provided. So this appears to be an exploratory study showing data with 100% correlation between the imaging and the clinical diagnoses which require confirmation in a clinically relevant setting.
L921: Study-16 is a published study (Regional Metabolic Changes in Parkinsonian Patients with Normal Dopaminergic Imaging: Eckert et al Movement Disorders, Volume 22, No 2, pp 167-
L922: 173, 2007) consisting of a clinical follow-up of a subset of patients from Study-15. These were the 27 patients who did not respond to treatment and had a negative F-18 FDOPA PET scan. Out of these 27, eight patients were studied with FDG scans, which were unrevealing, but they also ended up being followed for a minimum of 2 years by a movement disorder specialist to obtain patient's final clinical diagnosis. All eight were diagnosed with a condition other than a parkinsonian syndrome.
L923: The results of Study-16 suggest a potential clinical utility for FDOPA PET in confirming an absence of PD in patients not responding to PD therapy. However, these are observational data in only eight patients. As noted by Dr. Ye, following the Post-Midcycle Communication teleconference, the applicant submitted an NDA amendment to further clarify that only eight out of 27 patients had a clinical follow up. Then at the Late Cycle Meeting, the applicant claimed that this was incorrect and that all 27 had a clinical follow up. FDA encouraged the applicant to submit the appropriate data addressing the clinical follow up in this important patient cohort but no such data have been submitted to date.
L924: Study-21 is a publication (Direct Comparison of FP-CIT SPECT and FDOPA PET in Patients with Parkinson's Disease and Healthy Controls: Eshuis, et al Eur J Nuc Med Mol Imaging 36: 454-462, 2009) of an exploratory study which, as analyzed by Drs. Ye and Misra, aimed to compare sensitivity and specificity of DaTscan and F-18 FDOPA PET. The study evaluated 11 early-stage PD patients, 17 advanced-stage PD patients, and 20 age-matched controls. Each of the PD patients in the study underwent both SPECT and F-18 FDOPA PET. All patients were followed for 1 year after the scans, and the follow-up clinical diagnosis was used as the standard of truth. The study used exploratory quantitative imaging measurements to provide binary scan interpretations (positive or negative) and had a variety of other limitations as was pointed out by Dr. Ye. Taking into account its exploratory nature, the study did show that DaTscan SPECT and FDOPA PET were similar in their performance.
L925: In conclusion, the submitted data are quite limited and not sufficient to provide substantial evidence of effectiveness. The clinical and statistical deficiencies have been well summarized in the Briefing package for the Late Cycle Meeting. Both clinical and statistical reviewers recommend a Complete Response.
L926: 8. Safety
L927: There are essentially no safety data submitted in this NDA except for a record of all patients in Study-15 not having a single adverse event, as reviewed by Dr. Ye. The lack of data is balanced by the lack of safety signals from multiple exploratory studies involving F18 FDOPA. There is also a large safety database for Levodopa (see the related discussion above in the Pharmacology/Toxicology section).
L928: 9. Advisory Committee Meeting
L929: No Advisory Committee meetings have been planned for this NDA in this review cycle.
L930: 10. Pediatrics
L931: Pediatric waiver has been given as PD does not occur in children.
L932: 11. Other Relevant Regulatory Issues
L933: As summarized by Dr. John Lee of OSI, the GCP inspections of the applicant's clinical site (Studies -15 and -16) have revealed that available records do not appear to meet the GCP standards for an NDA. A prospective study protocol and case report forms (CRFs) were not used and important study records (including source records) were not available for inspectional review. It is further noted that a Form 483 was issued for: (1) failure to prepare and maintain adequate case histories, and (2) inadequate investigational drug disposition records with respect to dates, quantity, and use by subjects.
L934: Specifically, Dr. Lee's report states that the subject files typically lacked documentation of at least two cardinal features of PD and/or responsiveness to PD drugs and, for all subjects, the case records lacked evidence of PET scan interpretation by three separate blinded readers. Of further importance, for Study 16, none of the subject files contained documentation (clinical follow-up confirmation) of continued absence of classical PD symptoms and signs.
L935: These findings question the reliability of most of the information provided by the applicant as one is left with having to rely on the applicant's testimony rather than data.
L936: 12. Labeling
L937: In view of the significant data deficiencies no labeling discussions have taken place during this review cycle.
L938: 13. Recommendations/Risk Benefit Assessment
L939: • Recommended Regulatory Action
L940: Cross Disciplinary Team Leader recommends a Complete Response (CR) to this application for the use of F18 FDOPA with the indication that states the following: "Fluorodopa F 18 Injection is a (b) (4) used in Positron Emission Tomography (PET) to visualize dopaminergic (b) (4) in the striatum in adult patients with suspected parkinsonian syndromes (PS). (b) (4)
L941: Fluorodopa F 18 PET is an adjunct to other diagnostic evaluations."
L942: • Risk Benefit Assessment
L943: In assessing risks and benefits of this drug and the proposed imaging methodology it is clear that risk is minimal given the low level of radiation exposure, relatively small dose and an extensive related safety database.
L944: However, the challenge here is demonstrating clinically relevant benefit. Having an imaging test to help with the differentiating parkinsonian syndromes from other movement disorders would be clearly useful. There is an approved imaging test already (DaTscan) helping differentiate essential and parkinsonian tremor. Having a PET agent serving a similar clinical purpose but with potentially better imaging resolution would still be useful but no data have been presented by the applicant or found in the literature addressing such an indication. Another possible clinical utility could be in early PD or in patients with early onset tremor but no data in such patients have been presented by the applicant. An area of clinical utility that the applicant did approach somewhat was the use of FDOPA PET in patients with an apparent clinical diagnosis consistent with PD but with no response to medical therapy directed at PD. The efficacy data presented from such patients have been however very limited and could not be relied on for approval.
L945: The available data do show that FDOPA is biologically active and can in fact visualize the striatum but the data obtained at the applicant's clinical site and other presented clinical data do not provide information on the test's clinical utility or on the test's performance in a clinically relevant patient population. This particular application has been also affected by issues raising serious data integrity concerns. There are serious remaining drug specifications issues and quality concerns as well.
L946: So while there is little risk indeed, no benefit has been demonstrated either. During the current review cycle the subject of European approval has been approached. Various European labels for FDOPA produced by a commercial European manufacturer were reviewed. The drug is approved, at least in UK, France and Spain, for PD and certain oncology imaging. The UK colleagues were contacted who reported that the drug had been approved based on earlier approval obtained in France. An English translation of the French review document justifying the approval was reviewed. The approval appears to have been based on "well established use". There was no indication that some data were available to the European regulators which were not available here. While the fact of European approval deserves further consideration, it bears no direct influence on the review of the current NDA at this time, given all the issues with product quality and clinical data deficiencies.
L947: It should also be noted that a concern has been expressed that FDOPA imaging might in fact be not as efficacious as DaTscan imaging in early stages of PD, where it would be most useful, because of their different molecular targets. Whereas the dopamine transporters are known to undergo down regulation early in the course of the PD, the FDOPA target, AAAD, is thought to be subject to up-regulation. So early in the disease a decrease in the striatal uptake of an imaging agent might not be as apparent with an FDOPA type of an agent as with a DaTscan type. This, of course, can all be clarified only by clinical data.
L948: At this time, in considering the marketing approval of FDOPA produced by the current applicant, this reviewer concludes that the lack of risk does not justify the lack of demonstrated benefit.
L949: • Recommendation for Postmarketing Risk Management Activities
L950: No risk management activities are planned.
L951: • Recommendation for other Postmarketing Study Commitments
L952: There are no postmarketing commitments.

L954: The comments to the applicant are being finalized and will communicate three main recommendations: address all observations in both 483 Forms (GCP and GMP), resolve all product deficiencies, and conduct a clinical study in a patient population of intended use with a pre-specified standard of truth.

L977: 2 Introduction and Regulatory Background
L978: 2.1 Product Information
L979: Fluorodopa F-18 injection (F-18 FDOPA) is a PET radiopharmaceutical with a proposed indication for visualizing dopaminergic (b) (4) in the striatum in adult patients with suspected parkinsonian syndromes. In the basal ganglia, F-18 FDOPA is converted to F-18 Fluorodopamine (FDA) by aromatic amino acid decarboxylase (AAAD). Thereafter, it is taken up into the storage vesicle where it can be released like dopamine by dopaminergic neurons. Figure 1 shows representative F-18 FDOPA PET images through basal ganglia, which are comma-shaped in the transaxial PET image slice. The comma-shaped basal ganglia include the
L980: caudate nucleus ('comma head') and putamen ('comma tail'). On the left is an F-18 FDOPA PET image from a normal subject, showing uptake (denoted by red color) throughout the basal ganglia bilaterally. On the right is an F-18 FDOPA PET image from a Parkinson Disease (PD) patient, showing decreased uptake in putamen bilaterally while still retaining F-18 FDOPA uptake in the caudate ('comma head').
L981: Figure 1: Images of F-18 FDOPA PET. Two axial PET scans are shown side-by-side. The left scan, labeled 'normal', shows high uptake (red/yellow) in both the caudate (head) and putamen (tail) of the basal ganglia. The right scan, labeled 'PD', shows reduced uptake in the putamen bilaterally, while the caudate still shows uptake, resulting in a 'comma head' appearance.
L982: Figure 1: Images of F-18 FDOPA PET
L983: 2.2 Currently Available Treatments for Proposed Indications
L984: Although no other drug has been approved for the proposed indication of “visualizing dopaminergic (b) (4) in the striatum in adult patients with suspected parkinsonian syndromes” verbatim, DaTscan (I-123 FP-CIT, i.e. N-\omega-fluoropropyl-2\beta-carbomethoxy-3\beta-(4-[123I]iodophenyl)nortropane) is approved for a similar indication:
L985: DaTscan is indicated for striatal dopamine transporter visualization by SPECT brain imaging to assist in the evaluation of adult patients with suspected Parkinsonian syndromes (PS). In these patients, DaTscan may be used to help differentiate essential tremor from tremor due to Parkinsonian Syndrome (idiopathic Parkinson's disease, multiple system atrophy and progressive supranuclear palsy)
L986: Figure 2 compares F-18 FDOPA PET images (panels a and b) with I-123 FP-CIT (DaTscan) SPECT images (panels c and d), in both healthy controls and patients with Parkinson Disease (PD). All four panels are axial images through basal ganglia (comma-shaped). In healthy controls (panels a and c), there are symmetric uptake/activity throughout the comma-shaped basal ganglia bilaterally. In early-stage PD (panels b and d), there is decreased uptake/activity in the left putamen, while the left caudate and right basal ganglia retain uptake/activity. The I-123 FP-CIT (DaTscan) SPECT images appear grainer than the F-18 FDOPA PET images because of technical differences between SPECT imaging and PET imaging.
L987: Figure 2: Four brain scans arranged in a 2x2 grid. (a) F-DOPA PET scan of a healthy control showing high uptake in the striatum. (b) F-DOPA PET scan of a patient with early-stage PD showing significantly reduced uptake. (c) FP-CIT SPECT scan of a healthy control showing high uptake. (d) FP-CIT SPECT scan of a patient with early-stage PD showing reduced uptake.
L988: Figure 2: F-DOPA PET scans in (a) healthy controls and (b) patients with early-stage PD, and FP-CIT SPECT scans in (c) healthy controls and (d) patients with early-stage PD
L989: 2.3 Important Safety Issues With Consideration to Related Drugs
L990: None
L991: 2.4 Summary of Presubmission Regulatory Activity Related to Submission
L992: F-18 FDOPA was reviewed and discussed during the July 2000 Public Meeting between FDA and the Clinical PET community. The meeting concluded that available literature data for F-18 FDOPA were insufficient to support efficacy in any patient population.
L993: The applicant, Feinstein Institute for Medical Research, approached the FDA in 2008 with additional literature publication. FDA expressed concerns that the selected literature publications were mostly exploratory in nature and lacked confirmatory clinical data on the efficacy of F-18 FDOPA for the proposed indication.
L994: The applicant later submitted an NDA on 10/29/2009. User fee waiver was granted on 1/21/2010. The Agency refused to file the application on 3/19/2010 due to multiple filing
L995: deficiencies in clinical, pharmacology/toxicology, and clinical pharmacology sections (see DARRTS for Refuse-to-File Letter).
L996: The applicant subsequently applied for an Expanded Access IND with Request to Charge on the product, and the Expanded Access IND was allowed to proceed and the Request to Charge granted on 6/21/2012.
L997: Detailed time-line of F-18 FDOPA clinical considerations and the major submissions and responses to the NDA and PRE-IND archives are listed below:
L998: a. July 28, 2000 Public Meeting for an “Informal Discussion of Literature Reviews of Safety and Efficacy Data for PET 150-water and 18F-fluorodopa,” available at: http://www.fda.gov/Drugs/DevelopmentApprovalProcess/Manufacturing/ucm182855.htm. In this meeting, Dr. Ramesh Raman of the FDA presented the results of his review of the literature. He noted that, of 472 researched articles, 21 articles were identified as potentially useful to estimating fluorodopa F18 efficacy, based upon prespecified criteria (e.g., designation of a truth standard). Dr. Raman noted that his review of these articles led him to conclude, “FDOPA marks the dopaminergic system and may be used as a biomarker. However, there was no single study that validated the other findings [need for clinical utility] that I talked about earlier.” Dr. Raman emphasized that many studies compared patients with Parkinson’s Disease to healthy volunteers/normal subjects. This observation is especially important since healthy volunteers/normal subjects do not typically undergo imaging tests and comparisons of imaging results between healthy volunteers and patients do not represent a clinically useful assessment of an imaging test’s diagnostic performance.b. Most of the studies assessed disease progression or response to various treatments [REDACTED] (b) (4) .” Subsequent comments provided detailed assessment of the two publications that reported studies of the largest sample size; FDA explained how well-designed “collective evidence” analyses of previously conducted studies (with verifiable data integrity) may provide the substantial evidence to support drug approval. August 31, 2009, FDA provides a letter that denied the proposed meeting, stating, “The proposed topic and questions pertain to items we expect sponsors to develop and vet within their organizations. Following submission of the NDA, we will review the appropriateness of the proposals, in light of the submitted data. We noted that we have already had one PRE-NDA meeting with you on August 10, 2009. At the present time, resources do not allow us the feasibility of a meeting to address the topic and questions you have proposed. Hence, we are denying the meeting. We encourage you to obtain the resources within your own organization to fully vet your proposals, to solidify the data and to prepare for a thorough NDA submission. We appreciate your efforts in preparation of the NDA. We encourage you to continue trying to obtain medical and regulatory expertise within your organization to help facilitate the development of your NDA.”
