L1007: ” The clinical studies section of the labeling (b) (4)
L1008: m. March 19, 2010, FDA issued a Refusal to File letter regarding NDA 200655, citing deficiencies that precluded ability to review the NDA within the following areas: administrative sections required by 21 CFR 314.50, clinical (lack of an integrated summary of efficacy; lack of supporting evidence for the proposed dose, lack of an integrated summary of safety, etc), pharmacology/toxicology (lack of the information required by 21 CFR 314.50(d)(2)—FDA recommended the sponsor refer to the July 28, 2000 meeting to resolve the deficiency), clinical pharmacology (lack of a human pharmacokinetic and bioavailability section). Within the letter, FDA stated, “Although not specifically a basis for our refusal to file your application, we have the following comments:• The cited clinical efficacy data appears tenuous in its ability to support your proposed labeling. The application contains summaries of multiple publications as the confirmatory data for the clinical efficacy claims. However, this information does not appear to represent data from adequate and well-controlled studies. Only one study (published in two articles) appears to represent a multicenter, confirmatory trial. However this trial (Alan Whone et al, Ann Neuro, 2003) does not appear to be designed for evaluation the efficacy of fluorodopa F18 injection in PET imaging. Instead, the study appears to have been designed for evaluating the response of patients to treatment with ropinirole or levodopa. Therefore, the efficacy endpoint of this study does not match the proposed labeling indication for fluorodopa F-18 injection. Other study reports appear even more deficient in study design or relevance to the proposed claim of efficacy.• We understand that many patients have been exposed to fluorodopa F-18 injection at your clinical site. When redeveloping your application, we encourage you to summarize the safety and nominal diagnostic efficacy data from this experience.”n. July 19, 2010 meeting between FDA and the Sponsor. The Sponsor explained the nature of clinical data at his clinical site. He stated that 185 Parkinsonian syndrome patients have been scanned with fluorodopa and 158 patient scans were found to be “positive” and 27 patient scans were found to be negative; he reported 100% sensitivity and 100% specificity. FDA pointed out that the test drug cannot be used as both a truth standard and a test. FDA also encouraged the Sponsor to re-examine his mathematics since the sensitivity calculation should be calculated as 85% and not 100%, based upon traditional methods for calculating sensitivity (scan reported as “positive” among 158/185 patients with disease). FDA further noted that specificity could not be calculated since all patients apparently had disease. FDA stated that the Sponsor’s summary of his site’s clinical data (in meeting background document) did not appear sufficient to support the
L1009: safety and efficacy of the drug. When asked whether or not the Sponsor could resubmit the NDA, FDA confirmed that the Sponsor could submit an NDA.
L1010: o. September 27, 2010, the Sponsor sends a letter to FDA requesting “FDA to provide us with a letter indicating that the medical institutions will be able to use fluorodopa F18 PET imaging beyond the CGMP deadline (December 2011) with payment under a special IND. The letter further stated, “In the public meeting with FDA on July 28, 2000, the FDA representative indicated to us (in the minutes) that the 21 articles that he reviewed had potentially very valuable efficacy data and very strong statistical methods for imaging. He also indicated that in those studies the objectives were very clearly stated. The weakness, however, was that all the patients in the studies had no diseases. When we presented the more recent clinical study from our institute with patients having parkinsonian syndromes (with disease), FDA asked us to do the studies of patients without diseases. This is the exact reversal of the previous suggestion 10 years back. In the public meeting organized by FDA on July 28, 2000, FDA was satisfied with the efficacy and statistical methods of imaging for the studies reviewed. At that time, FDA did not express many concerns regarding the safety of this drug.” The letter also requested, “Based on the information that was provided to FDA, I would like to request FDA to allow the submission of a NDA for this drug. I also would like to request FDA to consider filing our NDA for a priority review process if and when the NDA is submitted.”p. November 9, 2011, the Sponsor sends a letter to FDA stating, “Fluorodopa F18 PET study cancellations is going to affect patient management from December 2011 onwards unless FDA allows the use of this drug beyond December 2011.q. December 2, 2011, FDA conducts telephone meeting with the Sponsor. FDA explained the IND submission process to the Sponsor.r. December 2011, FDA issues “Notice of FDA Exercise of Enforcement Discretion for PET Drugs.” The notice states, “If a facility wishes to continue to use PET drugs for clinical use after June 12, 2012, they must have submitted a New Drug Application (NDA) or an Abbreviated New Drug Application (ANDA) by that date, or be producing the drugs under an Investigational New Drug application (IND).”s. December 12, 2011, Sponsor sends FDA a letter stating, “I am working hard to submit an NDA for the PET drug fluorodopa F18 injection before the June 12, 2012 deadline. Furthermore, “Any help from the FDA is greatly appreciated. The clinical trials are very expensive, involves millions of dollars and the medical centers cannot afford it. In the public meeting conducted by FDA on July 28, 2000, FDA reviewers presented the published clinical data and there were some discussion to support the clinical data with Additional information that was supplied by us to FDA. We were very close to an agreement regarding the submission of an NDA at that time. If fluorodopa F18 injection is going to be treated with the same standard as regular drug manufactured by pharmaceutical companies, we may not be able to satisfy FDA as in the case of FDG and ammonia. If FDA can treat fluorodopa F18 injection as an innovative PET drug which may disappear from the market, if not protected as described in the FDAMA Act, then we
L1011: can find a common ground to approve this drug.” The letter continues, “Our NDA 200655 did not get a fair review from the FDA clinical reviewers. We put 20 years of hard work by many centers to prepare this NDA. The NDA was not even filed by FDA. This was the last PET drug from the old order needs FDA approval. It was heartbreaking when we received the notice that the drug will not be filed. Unlike the other three PET drugs we created the NDA on our own without FDA help. Instead of helping us the reviewers raised the approval bar to the highest possible level.”
L1012: t. January 19, 2012, the Sponsor sends a letter to FDA stating, “We would like to submit an NDA for fluorodopa F18 injection prior to June 12, 2012 to continue clinical use of this innovative PET drug to help Parkinsonian patients.” The Sponsor again asks for FDA to approve the drug.u. January 30, 2012, FDA sends an electronic communication (archived) to the Sponsor stating, “We acknowledge your correspondence of January 19, 2012, in which you summarize your plans to submit a NDA for fluorodopa F18 injection. You cite new information that you propose as importantly supporting the safety and efficacy of your drug.” In light of new information, the FDA recommended the Sponsor conduct another PRE-NDA meeting.v. April 16, 2012, PRE-NDA meeting between FDA and the Sponsor. In this meeting FDA encouraged the Sponsor to submit an expanded access IND with a clinical protocol. FDA clarified that the Sponsor may also submit a Request to Charge with the Expanded Access IND and that this Request to Charge is different from CMS/reimbursement considerations. FDA tried to explain that the IND process does not involve CMS. FDA detailed regulatory pathways. The Sponsor explained how FDOPA should be approved; FDA tried to explain how comparisons of fluorodopa performance between healthy subjects and patients with Parkinson’s Disease did not represent a sufficient spectrum of disease for evaluation of fluorodopa performance. The Sponsor noted that they will work to submit an Expanded Access IND and also resubmit an NDA.w. May 2, 2012, the Sponsor sends a letter to FDA requesting “exemption from the IRB approval and consent form for the submission of an Expanded Access IND with payment for fluorodopa F 18 injection.”x. May 16, 2012, FDA Advice Letter to the Sponsor stated that “we cannot exempt your proposed IND from the safeguards of the IND process, including the need for obtaining patient consent and obtaining authorization from your institutional review board. Administration of FDOPA to patients for clinical use under an IND necessitates compliance with the safeguards of the IND process, including the regulations governing informed consent (21 CFR 50) and institutional review boards (21 CFR 56).”y. May 22, 2012, the Sponsor submitted an Expanded Access IND along with a Request to Charge for F-18 FDOPA.z. June 21, 2012, the Request to Charge was granted, and the Expanded Access IND was allowed to proceed after potential hold issues were resolved.
L1013: 2.5 Other Relevant Background Information
L1014: Fluorodopa F18 Injection is licensed as Dopacis and IASODOPA in several European countries including the United Kingdom, France, and Spain. Approved indications include uses in neurology and oncology. The neurology indication for Dopacis (F-18 FDOPA) is as follows according to the product's U.K. labeling:
L1015: PET with fluorodopa (18F) is indicated for detecting loss of functional dopaminergic neuron terminals in the striatum in patients with clinically uncertain parkinsonian syndromes. It can be used to differentiate essential tremor from parkinsonian syndromes related to degenerative diseases affecting the nigrostriatal system (Parkinson disease (PD), multisystem atrophy and progressive supranuclear palsy).
L1016: PET with fluorodopa (18F), on its own, is unable to discriminate between different parkinsonian syndromes related to degenerative diseases affecting the nigrostriatal system. It is also unable to discriminate between PD with and without tremor.
L1017: The above neurology indication for Dopacis (F-18 FDOPA) is similar to the labeling indication for DaTscan (I-123 FP-CIT), in the sense that both products are indicated for differentiating essential tremor from tremor due to Parkinsonian Syndromes:
L1018: DaTscan is a radiopharmaceutical indicated for striatal dopamine transporter visualization by SPECT brain imaging to assist in the evaluation of adult patients with suspected Parkinsonian syndromes (PS). In these patients, DaTscan may be used to help differentiate essential tremor from tremor due to Parkinsonian Syndrome (idiopathic Parkinson's disease, multiple system atrophy and progressive supranuclear palsy)
L1019: 3 Ethics and Good Clinical Practices
L1020: 3.1 Submission Quality and Integrity
L1021: The reviewer finds the submission quality of the application poor and raises concerns over the credibility of the clinical study conducted by the applicant.
L1022: Initial re-submission of the NDA on 10/22/2012 contains no study protocol, or case report tabulations (patient level data), or statistical analysis plan, or sample case report form used by PET image readers for the 'pivotal' study (designated as "Study Reports of Controlled Clinical Studies Pertinent to the Claimed Indication" in the application) conducted by the applicant onsite at the Feinstein Institute. The FDA review team regarded these missing information as potential filing deficiencies and requested such information in its 12/4/2012 Information Request to the applicant.
L1023: The applicant submitted patient level data (“Fluorodopa Patient Data FDA”), sample case report form used to collect patient data (“Patient Data Collections Sheet”), study protocol, and Statistical Analysis in an amendment to the NDA on 12/17/2012.
L1024: The “Protocol”
L1025: The submitted “Protocol” is entitled “Measurement of Regional Glucose, Cerebral Blood Flow, and Dopamine Metabolism in Movement Disorders Using Positron Emission Tomography”. The protocol does not have subject enrollment criteria, F-18 FDOPA PET reading methodology, standard of truth, data analysis plan, or safety monitoring plan. The Objectives of the Protocol were “to measure regional cerebral dopamine concentration and cerebral blood flow and glucose metabolism in patients with brain movement disorders in order to determine the specific abnormalities of brain metabolism underlying the various clinical manifestations of these diseases”. The protocol’s objectives are different from the study summaries presented in Module 2 and Module 5 of the NDA. Specifically, Module 2 Section 2.7.6 Synopses of Individual Studies list the objectives of Study #15 to be “Safety and efficacy of Fluorodopa F-18 Injection for differential diagnosis of Parkinsonian Syndromes” as duplicated below:
L1026: STUDY #15: The Study Conducted by The Feinstein Institute for Medical Research
L1027: Number of Study CentersDesign and ControlDrug Dosage and RouteStudy ObjectiveNumber of Patients Included/ CompletedDurationGender M/FDiagnosisPrimary Endpoint



OneBlinded Study4-5 mCi dose administered IntravenouslySafety and efficacy of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection for differential diagnosis of Parkinsonian Syndromes.185/185One day study107/78Parkinsonian SyndromesDetection of loss or no loss of dopaminergic neurons. The results match with the end point.
L1028: Furthermore, F-18 FDOPA and carbidopa dosing are different between the study “Protocol” and the study summaries in Module 2 and Module 5 of the NDA – the “Protocol” uses 5-9 mCi F-18 FDOPA and 75 mg carbidopa given orally 30 minutes prior to FDOPA injection (or 25 mg carbidopa three times a day for 3 days prior to FDOPA injection), but Module 2 and Module 5 summaries of the study use 4-5 mCi F-18 FDOPA and 150 mg carbidopa given once orally. Given that study objectives and study drug dosing are different between the “Protocol” and study summaries in Module 2 and Module 5 of the NDA, it is obvious that the submitted “Protocol” and “Study #15: Clinical Study of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection Conducted by The Feinstein Institute for Medical Research” are two different studies. The fact that the applicant would submit a “Protocol” belonging to another study raises questions whether the “Clinical Study of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection Conducted by The Feinstein Institute for Medical Research” was conducted under a protocol at all. This raises concerns over the overall credibility of the clinical study conducted by the applicant.
L1029: Lack of Documentation of F-18 FDOPA PET Read by Three Readers
L1030: Despite the FDA review team’s request to submit “an example of the forms used by the image readers to document their interpretation”, the applicant did not submit this. The application contains the following summary table entitled “Percent Agreement on Sensitivity and Specificity Between [F-18 FDOPA] PET Scan Readers”:
L1031: SensitivitySpecificityReader I100%100%Reader II100%100%Reader III100%100%
L1032: However the application (including the 12/17/2012 amendment to the NDA on the FDA requested patient level data and case report tabulations) does not contain any additional information on the F-18 FDOPA PET read by the 3 readers. The submitted case report tabulation (“Fluorodopa Patient Data FDA”) does not contain documentation that F-18 FDOPA PET images were read by 3 readers. The sample case report form (“Patient Data Collection Sheet”) documents information on patient’s clinical information, F-18 FDOPA injection dose, F-18 FDOPA PET image acquisition parameters, and the date of the F-18 FDOPA PET, but does not document F-18 FDOPA PET read results. It is unclear to the reviewer whether all 196 subjects’ F-18 FDOPA PET images were read by the same 3 readers or only some of the PET images were read by 3 readers. The application does not contain information on basic information such as names of the three readers, credentials of the three readers, date(s) of the PET read (same as the F-18 FDOPA PET scan date?), and whether the readers were blinded to patient’s clinical information. Without these essential information, the claim made by the applicant as summarized in the above table cannot be validated. The applicant’s failure to submit “an example of the forms used by the image readers to document their interpretation” and the failure to submit documentations of PET read by three readers despite FDA review team’s request again raise concerns on the overall credibility of the clinical study conducted by the applicant.
L1033: Response to FDA Inquiry on the Establishment of Standard of Truth
L1034: The clinical study conducted by the applicant appears to use patient’s initial clinical diagnosis as the standard of truth based on Module 2 and Module 5 summaries. However the study “Protocol” submitted as an amendment to the NDA on 12/17/2012 does not provide details/definitions of the standard of truth. The patient level data (“Fluorodopa Patient Data FDA”) submitted as an amendment on 12/17/2012 appear to designate some patients as having “PD”, and other patients as “Suspected non-PD”. During the Post-Midcycle Communication teleconference with the applicant on 3/25/2013, the FDA asked the applicant to provide more details on how each patient’s clinical diagnosis (used Standard of Truth) was established. The applicant replied that “A clinical study coordinator at the Feinstein Institute held telephone conversations with referring physicians and designated each referred patient as “Parkinson Disease” or “Suspected Non-Parkinson” based on the telephone conversation. No additional records of the telephone conversation were kept. The coordinator was the only one at the Feinstein Institute who had knowledge of the clinical status/diagnosis of each referred patient, and the coordinator retired in 2006. All patients included in the F-18 FDOPA NDA were from 2006 or earlier [italics are excerpt from Post-midcycle Communication Teleconference Minutes in DARRTS]. The applicant essentially clears its responsibility of providing source data documenting how the standard of truth was established in the clinical study conducted by the applicant because the only person who had detailed knowledge of this inquiry retired from the Institute. This further raises concerns over the overall credibility of the clinical study conducted by the applicant.
L1035: 3.2 Compliance with Good Clinical Practices
L1036: Upon initial filing review of the NDA, the clinical reviewer noted that there was no statement of Good Clinical Practice on the clinical study conducted by the applicant. In order for the application to be filed, each pivotal study needs a statement of Good Clinical Practice, stating that all clinical studies were conducted under the supervision of an IRB and with adequate informed consent procedures. These filing deficiencies were conveyed to the applicant on December 4, 2012.
L1037: On December 17, 2012, the applicant submitted an amendment to the NDA. The cover letter of the amendment stated that the clinical study at the Feinstein Institute of Medical Research was conducted under the approval of the IRB and followed Good Clinical Practice. There were informed consent forms for all patients who participated in the study.
L1038: 3.3 Financial Disclosures
L1039: The applicant states that they are only the manufacturer of this drug and have no connection or contact with any of the medical practitioners prescribing this imaging agent, and there are no financial interests between these medical practitioners and the applicant (Feinstein Institute for Medical Research).
L1040: There are no clinical trials sponsored by the applicant, although the applicant conducted one clinical trial onsite (Study #15). The investigators on this trial, Dr. David Eidelbert, M.D. (Head for Neurosciences at Feinstein Institute for Medical Research) and Dr. Thomas Chaly, Ph.D (Chief of Cyclotron/Radiochemistry at the Feinstein Institute for Medical Research) both signed and submitted FDA form 3454 (Certification: Financial Interests and Arrangements of Clinical Investigators) and reported no financial interests and arrangements.

L1045: Clinical Microbiology reviewer recommends Complete Response. Review issues on product sterility have not been resolved.

L1047: The product is manufactured onsite by the applicant at the Feinstein Institute for Medical Research. This facility was inspected by the FDA district office during the NDA review cycle. The FDA district office recommends withhold on the facility.
L1048: 4.4 Clinical Pharmacology
L1049: No review issues from Clinical Pharmacology. The Clinical Pharmacology reviewer finds the proposed dosing regimen for F-18 FDOPA, including the dosing regimen for carbidopa pretreatment, acceptable.

L1052: 5 Sources of Clinical Data
L1053: Upon initial filing review of the application, the clinical reviewer noted the lack of patient level clinical data (case report tabulations) from the ‘pivotal’ study conducted by the applicant (designated as “Study Reports of Controlled Clinical Studies Pertinent to the Claimed Indication” in the application), among other filing deficiencies:
L1054: 1) Both pivotal studies were conducted by the applicant onsite at the Feinstein Institute, yet the applicant has not submitted datasets for the pivotal studies. This is a major filing deficiency. This deficiency was not listed as a filing deficiency in the Agency’s Refuse-to-File Letter, because neither study was included in the original NDA submission.
L1055: The reviewer requests the Feinstein Institute to submit patient level data for at least Pivotal Study #1 (STUDY #15: Clinical Study of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection Conducted by The Feinstein Institute for Medical Research). Patient level data need to include at least the following information for each study subject: patient ID, date of FDOPA PET scan, results of FDOPA PET scan, date patient’s truth standard (true diagnosis) was established, patient’s truth standard and how it was established (e.g. clinical diagnosis), adverse events (yes, no, not available)
L1056: 2) The resubmission still lacks Integrated Summary for Efficacy (ISE) and Integrated Summary for Safety (ISS). Both were listed as filing deficiencies in the Agency’s Refuse-to-File Letter. Since Module 2 Summaries now includes Overview of Efficacy, Overview of Safety, Summary of Clinical Efficacy, and Summary of Clinical Safety. One solution to salvage the repeated deficiencies in the ISE and ISS would be to ask the applicant to designate the clinical overviews and clinical summaries to ‘double-up’ as ISE and ISS. Since the lack of ISE and ISS were listed as filing deficiencies in the Agency’s Refuse-to-File
L1057: Letter, the reviewer recommends the sponsor submit a written statement/designation as an amendment to the NDA.
L1058: 3) Each pivotal study needs a statement of Good Clinical Practice; that all clinical studies were conducted under the supervision of an IRB and with adequate informed consent procedures
L1059: On December 4, 2012, the FDA review team communicated the filing deficiencies to the applicant:
L1060: Regarding NDA 200655: Fluorodopa F-18 Injection, we are currently conducting filing review of your NDA submission dated October 22, 2012, which we consider has much improved over the original NDA submission, 10/29/09. Nonetheless, we have identified the following potentially salvageable filing deficiencies:
L1061: The submission contains two study reports of “Controlled Clinical Studies Pertinent to the Claimed Indication”:
L1062: • “STUDY #15: Clinical Study of Fluorodopa F-18 Injection Conducted by the Feinstein Institute for Medical Research”• “STUDY #16: Regional Metabolic Changes in Parkinsonian Patients with Normal Dopaminergic Imaging”.
L1063: In particular, “STUDY #15: Clinical Study of Fluorodopa F-18 Injection Conducted by the Feinstein Institute for Medical Research” appears pivotal to our review of the drug’s safety and efficacy. This study was conducted by the applicant onsite at the Feinstein Institute for Medical Research, yet the applicant has not submitted case report tabulations (patient level data) for the study. This is a major filing deficiency. This deficiency was not listed as a filing deficiency in the Agency’s previous Refuse-to-File Letter, because the study was not included in the original NDA submission.
L1064: The FDA review team requests the Feinstein Institute for Medical Research to submit patient level data (case report tabulations) for “STUDY #15: Clinical Study of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection Conducted by The Feinstein Institute for Medical Research”. Patient level data need to include at least the following information for each study subject:
L1065: • Patient ID• Patient’s clinical diagnosis upon referral to the Feinstein Institute. If the patient was suspected of Parkinsonism, indicate the date of the initial evaluation and the date at follow up when the eventual diagnosis of Parkinsonism (e.g. Parkinson’s disease, multiple system atrophy, progressive supranuclear palsy) was made. For patients with an established diagnosis of Parkinsonism at the time of the initial referral, please identify the clinical severity of the disease and whether the patient had been on medications for PD.

Listing of patients referred to the Institute for scanning during this time period who were not included in the study and reasons.• Date of Fluorodopa F-18 PET scan• Results of the Fluorodopa F-18 PET scan (can be binary read as you have proposed)• Severity of loss of dopaminergic activity in the patients who had positive scans• Patient's adverse events, if any. L1067: 2. The resubmission still lacks Integrated Summary for Efficacy (ISE) and Integrated Summary for Safety (ISS). Both were listed as filing deficiencies in the Agency's previous Refuse-to-File Letter. Since Module 2 Summaries now includes Overview of Efficacy, Overview of Safety, Summary of Clinical Efficacy, and Summary of Clinical Safety, you may submit a statement designating the clinical overviews and clinical summaries to 'double-up' as ISE and ISS. Alternatively, you may submit a written statement explaining why ISE and ISS are not needed for the NDA.
L1068: 3. Regarding the study conducted by the applicant (STUDY #15: Clinical Study of Fluorodopa F-18 Injection Conducted by the Feinstein Institute for Medical Research) supply a statement of Good Clinical Practice, indicating that the study was conducted in compliance with institutional review board regulations (or was not subject to the regulations), and that it was conducted in compliance with the informed consent regulations. Also supply: 1) a copy of the protocol, including the statistical analytical plan, 2) an example of the case report forms used to collect patient data and 3) an example of the forms used by the image readers to document their interpretations. Additional information may be necessary as our review progresses.
L1069: On 12/17/2012, the applicant submitted patient level data, patient consent form, patient data collection sheet (sample case report form), study protocol, statistical analysis, and a cover letter as an amendment to the NDA. The patient level data (case report tabulations) were submitted as an Excel spreadsheet entitled “NSLIJ Health System Patient Data Set (1995-2004)”. The spreadsheet includes columns for Date of Study, Protocol, patient names, Age, Sex, Clinical Diagnosis, Radiopharmaceutical, Dose (mCi), PET Scan Results, Adverse Events, and Referring Physician for each of the 196 subjects of the study. Subject enrollment (date of the F-18 FDOPA PET) of the study ranged from 1995 to 2004.
L1070: Besides the ‘pivotal’ clinical study conducted by the applicant (STUDY #15: Clinical Study of Fluorodopa F-18 Injection Conducted by the Feinstein Institute for Medical Research), the application also contains eight literature publications on clinical studies of F-18 FDOPA (Studies #16-23). Studies #1-14 of the NDA are literature publications on the biopharmaceutics, pharmacokinetics, and pharmacodynamics of F-18 FDOPA and have been reviewed by the FDA Clinical Pharmacology review team.

L1073: 5.2 Discussion of Individual Studies/Clinical Trials
L1074: 1) There are two “Study Reports of Controlled Clinical Studies Pertinent to the Claimed Indication”:
L1075: Study #15The Feinstein InstituteClinical Study of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection Conducted by The Feinstein Institute for Medical Research

Study #16Eckert, et. al.Regional Metabolic Changes in Parkinsonian Patients with Normal Dopaminergic ImagingMovement Disord 2007;22:167-173
L1076: Study #15 is a ‘pivotal’ study conducted by the applicant onsite at the Feinstein Institute for Medical Research. Study #16 (Eckert, et al.) is a literature publication of clinical follow-up results of a subset of patients in Study #15. These two studies will be further discussed in the next section of the review.
L1077: 2) There are two “Study Reports of Uncontrolled Clinical Studies”:
L1078: Study #17Ito, et. al.Striatal and Extrastriatal Dysfunction in Parkinson’s Disease with Dementia: A 6-[18F]Fluoro-L-Dopa PET StudyBrain 2002;125:1358-1365

Study #18Rakshi, et. al.A Comparison of the Progression of Early Parkinson’s Disease in Patients Started on Ropinirole or L-Dopa: An 18F-Dopa PET StudyJ Neural Transm 2002;109:1433-1443
L1079: Study #17 is a publication of an exploratory study analyzing FDOPA influx constant (Ki) in various brain regions. All subjects were patients with Parkinson Disease, with one set of patients having idiopathic Parkinson Disease with dementia, and the other set of patients having idiopathic Parkinson Disease without dementia. Analyzing FDOPA influx constant (Ki) in various brain regions does not provide relevant clinical data to demonstrate the efficacy of F-18 FDOPA as a diagnostic radiopharmaceutical.
L1080: Study #18 is a publication of an exploratory study on disease progression (all patients were diagnosed with Parkinson Disease) and treatment response (to Ropinirole vs. L-Dopa). The focus of the study was to evaluate patient treatment response rather than to demonstrate the diagnostic efficacy of F-18 FDOPA PET. The study does not provide relevant clinical data to demonstrate the efficacy of F-18 FDOPA as a diagnostic radiopharmaceutical.
L1081: Both studies are exploratory nature, and both studies are conducted in patients who have already been diagnosed with Parkinson Disease (PD). Patients whose diagnoses remain unclear could potentially benefit from another diagnostic test such as F-18 FDOPA PET, but patients who have already been diagnosed with Parkinson Disease would not benefit from another diagnostic test. Therefore neither study was conducted in the product’s intended population. Neither study provides relevant clinical data to demonstrate the efficacy of F-18 FDOPA in the product’s intended population for the proposed indication.
L1082: 3) There are two “Reports of Analyses of Data From More Than One Study”:
L1083: Study #19Whone, et. al.Slower Progression of Parkinson’s Disease with Ropinirole versus Levodopa: The REAL-PET StudyAnn Neurol 2003;54:93-101

Study #20Whone, et. al.A Technique for Standardized Central Analysis of 6-Fluoro-L-Dopa PET from Multicenter StudyJ Nucl Med 2004;45:1135-1145
L1084: Study #19 is a publication of a clinical study designed to compare treatment response to ropinirole vs. L-dopa. All patients recruited were already diagnosed with PD. Study #20 is a publication of the technical portion of Study #19. The clinical study was conducted in patients who were already diagnosed with PD (rather than in patients whose diagnoses remain unclear and could potentially benefit from another diagnostic test such as F-18 FDOPA PET), and the focus of the study was to evaluate patient treatment response rather than to demonstrate the diagnostic efficacy of F-18 FDOPA PET. The study does not provide relevant clinical data to demonstrate the efficacy of F-18 FDOPA for the proposed indication.
L1085: 4) There are three “Other Study Reports”:
L1086: Study #21Eshuis, et. al.Direct Comparison of FP-CIT SPECT and F-DOPA PET in Patients with Parkinson’s Disease and Healthy ControlsEur J Nucl Med Mol Imaging 2009;36:454-462

Study #22Jokinen, et. al.Simple Ratio Analysis of 18F-Fluorodopa Uptake in Striatal Subregions Separates Patients with Early Parkinson Disease from Healthy ControlsJ Nucl Med 2009;50:893-899

Study #23Brown, et. al.Fluorine-18-Fluoro-L-DOPA Dosimetry with Carbidopa PretreatmentJ Nucl Med 1998;39:1884-1891
L1087: Study #21 is a publication of an exploratory study comparing sensitivity and specificity of I-123 FP-CIT SPECT (DaTscan) and F-18 FDOPA PET. This publication provides relevant clinical data on the efficacy (sensitivity and specificity) of F-18 FDOPA as a diagnostic radiopharmaceutical. The reviewer regards the study as one of the ‘pivotal’ clinical studies of the NDA (the other ‘pivotal’ clinical study being Study #15, the clinical study conducted by the applicant). This study will be discussed further in the next section of the review.
L1088: Study #22 is a publication of an exploratory study assessing whether a simple ratio analysis (SOR) of FDOPA uptake in various brain regions would reliably separate PD patients from healthy controls. The exploratory analysis on the simple ratio does not provide relevant clinical data to demonstrate the efficacy of F-18 FDOPA as a diagnostic radiopharmaceutical.
L1089: Study #23 is a publication of an exploratory study showing the effect of carbidopa pretreatment on FDOPA biodistribution and dosimetry. Review issues on carbidopa pretreatment dosing have been discussed by the FDA Clinical Pharmacology review team. The publication does not
L1090: provide relevant clinical data to demonstrate the efficacy of F-18 FDOPA as a diagnostic radiopharmaceutical.
L1091: 6 Review of Efficacy
L1092: Efficacy Summary
L1093: Study #15-23 in the NDA are designated as “Reports of Efficacy and Safety Studies”. As discussed above the reviewer considers the clinical study conducted by the applicant (Study #15 and Study #16) and a publication comparing the sensitivity and specificity of F-18 FDOPA with DaTscan (Study #21) relevant in providing clinical data for the evaluation of the efficacy of F-18 FDOPA as a diagnostic radiopharmaceutical.
L1094: 1) The clinical study conducted by the applicant (Study #15) has serious deficiencies:
L1095: ○ The study is a retrospective single center study○ The study was apparently ‘conducted’ without a pre-specified protocol, and hence key parameters of the study (e.g. definitions of primary efficacy endpoint and standard of truth) that would normally be specified by a protocol, are not well-defined for the study○ The applicant apparently used each patient’s clinical diagnosis at the time when the patient was referred to the Feinstein Institute for F-18 FDOPA PET as the Standard of Truth. Therefore “Possible PD”, “Possible non-PD”, and “Suspected non-PD” were regarded as standard of truth, but in fact these are clinical suspicions rather than truth standard!○ Because of the wrong assumption to regard pre-imaging clinical suspicions (Possible PD vs. Possible non-PD, and “PD” vs. “Suspected-non-PD”) as the standard of truth, the applicant’s conclusions on the performance characteristics (100% sensitivity/specificity/positive predictive value/negative predictive value) are not valid.○ The application does not contain any documentation that each F-18 FDOPA PET was read by three readers (the applicant fails to submit such documentation for PET image interpretations from 3 readers despite FDA’s request). The applicant’s claim that each of the 3 readers had 100% sensitivity and 100% specificity can not be regarded as valid without the applicant supplying source documentation for PET image read by 3 readers○ The applicant used patient’s pre-imaging clinical diagnosis as the standard of truth and apparently demonstrated that F-18 FDOPA PET results were 100% identical to each patient’s pre-imaging clinical diagnosis. Another regulatory problem then arises – if F-18 FDOPA PET simply duplicates each patient’s pre-imaging clinical diagnosis, it appears that F-18 FDOPA PET provides no additional clinically useful information. This would particularly be true for the patients whose pre-imaging clinical diagnosis was already “PD” – what clinically useful information does F-18 FDOPA PET add? One of the important regulatory considerations in evaluating the efficacy of a diagnostic radiopharmaceutical is an assessment of the drug’s ability to provide clinically useful information.
L1096: 2) Study #16 is a publication of follow-up results of F-18 FDOPA PET negative patients in Study #15. It also has major limitations:
L1097: ○ The number of patients followed was small: eight patients out of 27 F-18 FDOPA PET negative patients from Study #15 were followed to obtain patient's final clinical diagnosis. So even though these 8 patients have final clinical diagnosis (all had "non-PD") as the standard of truth, eight patients is a small number○ The vast majority of patients in Study #15 apparently would not receive useful clinical information from another diagnostic imaging test such as F-18 FDOPA PET, as these patients already had a clinical diagnosis based on patient's symptoms and they were responding to antiparkinson medication, therefore F-18 FDOPA PET would not be indicated in these patients. For the small portion of patients who could potentially benefit from such an imaging test, only a small number of patients (8) had established standard of truth. Clinical data from such a small number of patients may not allow for the assessment of the accuracy/performance characteristics of the imaging test F-18 FDOPA PET.
L1098: 3) Most of the eight literature publications (Study #16-23) do not provide relevant clinical data for the assessment of the efficacy of F-18 FDOPA as a diagnostic radiopharmaceutical, except one publication by Eshuis et al (Study #21).
L1099: 4) Although Study #21 is relevant in providing clinical data for demonstrating the efficacy of F-18 FDOPA as a diagnostic radiopharmaceutical, the study has a number of major limitations:
L1100: ○ The study is a small single center study○ The study is exploratory rather than confirmatory in design○ All study patients were already diagnosed with PD, therefore patient population of the study is not a patient population that would clinically benefit from another diagnostic imaging test such as DaTscan or F-18 FDOPA PET○ Patients who potentially could benefit from another diagnostic imaging test such as F-18 FDOPA PET (e.g. patients with atypical signs, patients exhibiting symptoms of Parkinsonian syndromes but were not responding to antiparkinson medication) were excluded from the study○ The definition of positive scans (SOR below two standard deviations of mean SOR from healthy controls) is an exploratory quantitative measure and its threshold is arbitrarily set○ It is unclear how specificity was calculated which involves true negatives; it is unclear whether true negatives were simply healthy controls in the study, as all patients included in the analysis of the study had confirmed PD
L1101: 5) The overall submission of the application is poor and the reviewer raises concerns over the overall credibility of the clinical study conducted by the applicant (see discussions under Submission Quality and Integrity in Section 3.1 of the review)
L1102: 6.1 Indication
L1103: The applicant proposes the following indication for F-18 FDOPA:
L1104: Fluorodopa F 18 Injection is a (b) (4) used in Positron Emission
    Tomography (PET) to visualize dopaminergic (b) (4) in the striatum in adult patients with
    suspected parkinsonian syndromes (PS).
L1105: Fluorodopa F 18 PET is an adjunct to other diagnostic evaluations.
L1106: 6.1.1 Methods
L1107: As discussed above, the clinical review of efficacy focuses on two ‘pivotal’ studies in the submission – the clinical study conducted by the applicant (Study #15 and 16) and a literature publication comparing the sensitivity and specificity of F-18 FDOPA with DaTscan (Study #21).
L1108: 6.1.2 Demographics
L1109: 1) “STUDY #15: Clinical Study of Fluorodopa F-18 Injection Conducted by the Feinstein Institute for Medical Research”
L1110: The clinical study conducted by the applicant (Study #15) evaluated 196 subjects with F-18 FDOPA PET. Of these 196 subjects, 11 were normal controls (8 males, 3 females), and 185 were patients (107 males, 78 females) with at least two cardinal features of Parkinsonian syndromes (bradykinesia, rigidity, tremor, and/or gait disorders).
L1111: The applicant conducted a statistical analysis comparing subject age across 3 subgroups: normal controls, FDOPA PET negative (“NEG-PD”), FDOPA PET positive (“POS-PD”). The analysis showed that there is significant age difference between the FDOPA PET positive group (56.75 \pm 12.47 years) and the FDOPA negative group (45.30 \pm 19.32 years). There was no significant age difference between the normal control group (58.64 \pm 15.21 years) and the FDOPA positive group (56.75 \pm 12.47). Summary demographics and analysis results on the age difference among the 3 subgroups are shown in Table 1.

L1114: Reviewer's comments: In the applicant's datasheet and analysis, FDOPA PET negative patients were designated as Negative for Parkinson Disease ("NEG-PD"); FDOPA PET positive patients were designated as Positive for Parkinson Disease ("POS-PD"). FDOPA PET negative ("NEG-PD") patients were significantly younger than FDOPA PET positive ("POS-PD") patients or normal controls. True Parkinson Disease is found generally in older people. Therefore the apparent high predictive value for F-18 FDOPA PET to predict PD vs. Non-PD is confounded by this significant age bias.
L1115: 2) Study #21: Direct Comparison of FP-CIT SPECT and F-DOPA PET in Patients with Parkinson's Disease and Healthy Controls; Eshuis et al, Eur J Nucl Med Mol Imaging, 2009(36) 454-462 (Study #21)
L1116: Study #21 evaluated 11 early-stage Parkinson Disease (PD) patients, 17 advanced-stage PD patients, and 20 age-matched controls (Table 2). Each of the PD patients in the study underwent both I-123 FP-CIT SPECT and F-18 FDOPA PET. Each control had either I-123 FP-CIT SPECT or F-18 FDOPA PET (10 in each group). The 11 early-stage PD patients (51.3 \pm 10.1 years) were younger than the 17 advanced-stage PD patients (64.5 \pm 6.3 years). This age difference is expected, as Parkinson Disease is a progressive neurodegenerative disease, worsening as a patient's age advances.

L1119: 6.1.3 Analysis of Primary Endpoint(s) – Clinical Study of Fluorodopa F-18 Injection Conducted by the Feinstein Institute for Medical Research (Study #15)
L1120: Study #15 is a retrospective single center study conducted by the applicant onsite at the Feinstein Institute for Medical Research. The study evaluated 196 subjects with F-18 FDOPA PET. Eleven (11) of the 196 subjects were normal controls, and the other 185 subjects were patients referred to Feinstein Institute for F-18 FDOPA PET. According to the submission "all (185) patients were referred by a movement disorder physician and exhibited at least two of the cardinal features of parkinsonian syndromes (bradykinesia, rigidity, tremor, and/or gait disorder)".
L1121: The NDA submitted on October 22, 2012 initially did not contain a study protocol. Upon FDA request, the applicant submitted a "Protocol" that apparently belongs to another study (see
L1122: discussions under Submission Quality and Integrity in Section 3.1 of the review). The reviewer suspects that the study was not conducted under a pre-specified protocol, given the applicant's failure to submit the protocol upon FDA request. Because of the lack of a pre-specified protocol, many important parameters (e.g. definitions of primary efficacy endpoint and standard of truth) that normally would be specified by a protocol are not well defined for the study.
L1123: The study's primary efficacy endpoint apparently was the "detection of loss or no loss of dopaminergic neurons". Criteria for F-18 FDOPA PET Image Classification as Either Negative (no loss of dopaminergic neurons) or Positive (loss of dopaminergic neurons) are shown in Table 3.
L1124: Table 3: Criteria for F-18 FDOPA PET Image Classification as Either Negative or Positive
L1125: Image Classification Based on Visual ExaminationCriteria for the ClassificationNegative (normal)Negative scans were characterized based on the uptake of Fluorodopa F 18 by the caudate and putamen bilaterally. Negative scans show a uniform uptake, as seen by the crescent shaped caudate and putamen.Positive (abnormal)A positive scan shows a reduced uptake of Fluorodopa F 18 by the putamen or putamen/caudate, either unilaterally or bilaterally, depending upon the severity of the disease.
L1126: It is unclear to the reviewer how each patient's standard of truth is established. The applicant apparently uses each patient's clinical diagnosis at the time when the patient was referred to the Feinstein Institute for F-18 FDOPA PET as the Standard of Truth. The following diagram from the application states that "Clinical Diagnosis as the Standard of Truth at the Time When the PET Scan was Requested".

L1128: Reviewer's comments: Note in the applicant's diagram above, Clinical Diagnosis at the time when PET scan was requested was listed as "Possible PD" and "Possible non-PD". However "Possible PD" and "Possible non-PD" are clinical suspicions rather than standard of truth!
L1129: The applicant regarded "Possible PD" and "Possible non-PD" as the standard of truth and counted 158 patients positive for "Possible PD" as true positives, and 27 patients negative for "Possible non-PD" as true negatives. However because the wrong assumption to regard pre-imaging clinical suspicion (Possible PD and Possible non-PD) as the standard of truth, the applicant's conclusions in the above diagram (100% sensitivity/specificity/positive predictive value/negative predictive value) are not valid.
L1130: The reviewer examined the patient level data (spreadsheet "Fluorodopa Patient Data FDA") submitted as an amendment to the NDA. The spreadsheet has a column for Clinical Diagnosis, and each subject's Clinical Diagnosis is listed as either "Normal" or "PD" or "Suspected non-PD". The spreadsheet also has a column for PET Scan Results, and each subject's PET Scan Results is listed as either "Normal" or "Positive". The spreadsheet does not list three PET reader's individual readings as commented in Section 3.1 Submission Quality and Integrity of the review. The study evaluated 196 subjects with F-18 FDOPA PET. There are 11 subjects whose PET Scan Results are Normal and Clinical Diagnosis is normal; 27 subjects whose PET Scan Results are "Normal" and Clinical Diagnosis is "Suspected non-PD", and 158 subjects whose PET Scan Results are "Positive" and Clinical Diagnosis is "PD" (Table 4)

L1133: In response to FDA Information Request, the applicant submitted a sample case report form (“Patient Data Collection Sheet”) that was used to collect patient data in 12/17/2012 amendment to the NDA. The “Patient Data Collection Sheet” has an entry for “Diagnosis”, presumably patient’s clinical diagnosis (or clinical suspicion) when being referred to Feinstein Institute for F-18 FDOPA PET. The applicant submitted one such sample form for a patient whose “Diagnosis” was “PD”.
L1134: The reviewer has requested the FDA Office of Scientific Investigation (OSI) to inspect Feinstein Institute. In particular the reviewer has request the inspection to verify how and when “PD” vs. “Suspected non-PD” were determined, since this designation is being used as the standard of truth.
L1135: Furthermore, the reviewer has requested the FDA OSI inspection to verify that the 27 patients who had negative F-18 FDOPA PET indeed had “Suspected non-PD” as their Diagnosis on the subject’s “Patient Data Collection Sheet”. The reviewer specifically requests this because Study #16, a publication of clinical follow-up results of patients who had negative F-18 FDOPA PET in Study #15, states that “A total of 185 parkinsonian patients were referred to the PET Center at North Shore University Hospital by movement disorders specialists to confirm a clinical diagnosis of PD. This diagnosis was entertained because of the presence of at least two cardinal features of disease (bradykinesia, rigidity, tremor, and/or gait disorder).” Therefore based on the publication (Study #16) all 185 patients (including the 27 patients with negative F-18 FDOPA PET) referred to Feinstein Institute (North Shore University Hospital) had pre-imaging clinical diagnosis of “PD”. However based on the applicant’s “Patient Data Collection Sheet”, the 27 patients who had negative F-18 FDOPA PET all had pre-imaging clinical diagnosis of “Suspected non-PD”. This apparent discrepancy can only be resolved with FDA OSI inspection of the applicant’s source records.
L1136: It should be noted that “Suspected non-PD” is a clinical suspicion rather than a patient’s standard of truth. Clinical follow-up or verification by other established methods would be needed to ascertain that a patient indeed does not have Parkinson Disease, hence can be given “non-PD” as the standard of truth. Similarly a patient’s pre-imaging clinical suspicion of “PD” also needs to be verified by clinical follow-up or other established methods.
L1137: The applicant used patient’s pre-imaging clinical diagnosis as the standard of truth and apparently demonstrated that F-18 FDOPA PET results were 100% identical to each patient’s pre-imaging clinical diagnosis. Another regulatory problem then arises – if F-18 FDOPA PET simply duplicates each patient’s pre-imaging clinical diagnosis, it appears that F-18 FDOPA PET provides no additional clinically useful information. This would particularly be true for the patients whose pre-imaging clinical diagnosis was already “PD” – what clinically useful information does F-18 FDOPA PET add? One of the important regulatory considerations in evaluating the efficacy of a diagnostic radiopharmaceutical is an assessment of the drug’s ability to provide clinically useful information.
L1138: On 12/28/2012, the FDA review team expressed these concerns in its Day 74 Letter (filing letter) to the applicant:
L1139: 1. The clinical protocol describes a study in which patients with movement disorders were to undergo positron emission tomography (PET) imaging to estimate cerebral blood flow, brain glucose metabolism and brain fluorodopa uptake. The study design appears to represent an exploratory study since the protocol lacked a statistical analytical plan, including a primary endpoint for hypothesis testing. Hence, the results discussed in the marketing application appear to represent post-hoc analyses of exploratory clinical data where the pre-imaging clinical diagnosis was used as a standard of truth.2. The use of a pre-imaging clinical diagnosis as a standard of truth suggests that, in this study, FDOPA imaging was incapable of estimating the ability to which the imaging could provide clinically useful information. The study showed FDOPA imaging results that were apparently identical to the pre-imaging clinical diagnosis; hence, the FDOPA imaging results suggest that imaging provided no clinically useful information. One of the important considerations in evaluating the efficacy of a diagnostic radiopharmaceutical is an assessment of the drug’s ability to provide clinically useful information. The FIMR clinical study results appear useful in the generation of exploratory data but the results do not appear to support the ability of FDOPA imaging to provide clinically useful information.
L1140: 6.1.4 Analysis of Secondary Endpoints(s) - Comparison of FP-CIT SPECT and F-DOPA PET in Patients with Parkinson's Disease and Healthy Controls (Study #21)
L1141: I-123 FP-CIT SPECT (DaTscan) is approved in the U.S. and Europe. Its chemical name is N-\omega-fluoropropyl-2\beta-carbomethoxy-3\beta-(4-[123I]iodophenyl)nortropane. FP-CIT selectively binds to dopamine transporter (DaT), and is approved to image dopamine transporter (DaT):
L1142: DaTscan is indicated for striatal dopamine transporter visualization by SPECT brain imaging to assist in the evaluation of adult patients with suspected Parkinsonian syndromes (PS). In these patients, DaTscan may be used to help differentiate essential tremor from tremor due to Parkinsonian Syndrome (idiopathic Parkinson's disease, multiple system atrophy and progressive supranuclear palsy)
L1143: Study #21 is a publication of an exploratory study comparing the sensitivity and specificity of I-123 FP-CIT SPECT vs. F-18 FDOPA PET. The study is a single center study conducted in the Europe. The study evaluated 11 early-stage Parkinson Disease (PD) patients, 17 advanced-stage PD patients, and 20 age-matched controls. Each of the PD patients in the study underwent both I-123 FP-CIT SPECT and F-18 FDOPA PET. Each control subject had either I-123 FP-CIT SPECT or F-18 FDOPA PET (10 in each group). All patients were followed for 1 year after the scans, and the follow-up clinical diagnosis was used as the standard of truth.
L1144: All study patients had strong clinical evidence of having Parkinson Disease:
L1145: • Criteria for early-stage PD:○ strong clinical evidence of PD based on two of the three cardinal symptoms (rest tremor, bradykinesia, and rigidity)○ disease duration of less than 3 years○ were not using antiparkinson medication• Criteria for advanced-stage PD:○ two of the three cardinal symptoms○ disease duration of 5–15 years○ were using antiparkinson medication with a documented response
L1146: Twenty-nine (29) patients were recruited, underwent I-123 FP-CIT SPECT and F-18 FDOPA PET, and followed clinically for one year. Twenty-eight (28) patients had PD as SOT by clinical follow up and were included in the analysis. One patient did not respond to symptomatic antiparkinsonian medication and did not show worsening of extrapyramidal signs after a period of 3 years of clinical follow-up, and this patient was excluded from analysis. Patients with atypical signs, psychiatric disorders, signs of severe cognitive deterioration, severe cardiovascular co-morbidity or on medication known to interfere with dopamine transporters (DaT) or catechol-O-methyltransferase inhibitors were excluded.
L1147: Reviewer's comments: The study has a number of limitations. The study is a small single center study. All study patients had strong clinical evidence of PD, and patients with atypical signs and symptoms were excluded. However, in clinical practice, patients with strong clinical evidence of PD with documented response to antiparkinson medication may not benefit from another
L1148: diagnostic imaging test, either I-123 FP-CIT SPECT or F-18 FDOPA PET. Patients with atypical signs and symptoms could potentially benefit from a diagnostic imaging test such as I-123 FP-CIT SPECT or F-18 FDOPA PET, but these patients were excluded from the study. The study is exploratory in nature with no hypothesis testing.
L1149: DaTscan labeling dosing recommendation is 111-185 MBq (3-5 mCi). In the study, each patient received 185 MBq DaTscan. In addition, each patient was injected 200 MBq F-18 FDOPA intravenously, and had 2.5 mg/kg carbidopa given orally one hour prior to F-18 FDOPA injection. Key parameters of the study are summarized in Table 5:
L1150: Table 5: STUDY #21 - Direct Comparison of FP-CIT SPECT and F-DOPA PET

L1152: Data Analysis
L1153: Quantitative F-18 FDOPA uptake analysis was conducted by computing the Striatal Occipital Ratio (SOR) of bilateral caudate and putamen. Average SOR of caudate and putamen was used to calculate mean whole striatal SOR on each side. Scans considered abnormal when the SORs were less than the mean values minus 2 SD of the healthy controls. Sensitivity and Specificity was calculated for various brain regions: contralateral putamen, ipsilateral putamen, contralateral caudate, ipsilateral caudate, contralateral striatum, and ipsilateral striatum. Results of data analysis are shown in Table 6.

L1156: Reviewer's comments: It should be noted in the above table that in calculating 'sensitivity' and 'specificity', definition of a 'positive scan' was arbitrarily set as an uptake value two standard deviations below the mean uptake (mean SOR) of healthy control, i.e. scans were considered abnormal when the SORs were less than the mean values minus 2 SD of the healthy controls. The arbitrary definition of 'positive scan' is another limitation of the study, and further exemplifies the study's exploratory nature. Furthermore, each patient in the analysis was confirmed to have PD via clinical follow-up, which serves as the standard of truth. It is unclear to the reviewer how 'specificity' was calculated, which involves true negatives, and it is unclear whether true negatives were simply healthy controls in the study.
L1157: It should also be noted that DaTscan is indicated to differentiate essential tremor from tremor due to Parkinsonian syndromes. The study did not include patients with essential tremor. Data in the above table appear to suggest that I-123 FP-CIT SPECT and F-18 FDOPA PET have comparable performance characteristics in a patient population with strong clinical evidence of PD. However due to the exploratory nature of the study, this conclusion needs further verification and support from other confirmatory clinical studies.

L1166: The primary efficacy analysis of the clinical study conducted by the applicant (Study #15) used binary F-18 FDOPA PET reading methodology, i.e. either positive or negative. The applicant also analyzed severity of dopaminergic neuronal Loss (Table 10). The reviewer (b) (4)
L1167: Table 10: Criteria for Positive Scans to Indicate Severity of Dopaminergic Neuronal Loss
L1168: ClassificationCriteria for the Classification



Positive +Reduced uptake in one of the putamen and normal uptake on the other, as compared to normal scans

Positive ++Significantly reduced uptake by the putamen bilaterally

Positive +++Reduced uptake by the putamen bilaterally and reduced uptake by one of the caudate regions

Positive ++++Reduced uptake by both the putamen and caudate bilaterally
L1169: 6.1.6 Subpopulations
L1170: Study #16 “Regional Metabolic Changes in Parkinsonian Patients with Normal Dopaminergic Imaging” is a publication of clinical follow-up results on a subset of patients in Study #15. Patients (all had at least two cardinal features of Parkinson Syndromes) who had negative F-18 FDOPA PET were followed for a minimum of 2 years by movement disorder specialist to obtain patient’s final clinical diagnosis. Based on Module 2 of the NDA, “eight patients that were not responding to PD treatment despite showing parkinsonian symptoms were selected to undergo Fludeoxyglucose F 18 PET scans”. A clinical follow up (average 3.1 \pm 0.9 years) after the FDG metabolic studies on the eight patients did not show any signs of classical PD. Further clinical
L1171: diagnosis indicated that they had either potential psychogenic movement disorder (n=3), DOPA responsive dystonia (n=2), lithium exposure, post-viral parkinsonian syndrome (n=1), or midbrain compression due to a cyst (n=1).
L1172: Reviewer's comments: As commented above, there is an apparent discrepancy of the pre-imaging clinical diagnosis of the F-18 FDOPA PET negative patients studied in this publication. The publication states that "A total of 185 parkinsonian patients were referred to the PET Center at North Shore University Hospital by movement disorders specialists to confirm a clinical diagnosis of PD. This diagnosis was entertained because of the presence of at least two cardinal features of disease (bradykinesia, rigidity, tremor, and/or gait disorder)", therefore all the F-18 FDOPA PET negative patients in this follow-up study should have pre-imaging clinical diagnosis of "PD". However based on the applicant's patient level data ("Fluorodopa Patient Data FDA"), all the 27 patients who had negative F-18 FDOPA PET had pre-imaging clinical diagnosis of "Suspected non-PD". The reviewer has requested the FDA Office of Scientific Investigation to inspect the applicant's source records to resolve this apparent discrepancy.
L1173: Based on the publication, none of followed patients developed clinical signs of classical PD or of an atypical parkinsonian syndrome at a follow-up visit conducted 3 years after imaging. However it was not clear as to how many of the 27 patients who had negative F-18 FDOPA PET were followed, and how many had final clinical diagnosis verified as "non-PD".
L1174: On 3/25/2013 the FDA review team held a Post-Midcycle Communication teleconference with the applicant. The following are clinical discussion summaries from the meeting minutes:
L1175: FDA asked the applicant to clarify the following two items:
L1176: 1. In reference to the study listed as #15 and #16, it is not clear whether only 27 or all 185 patients have not been responding to the anti-Parkinson therapy prior to undergoing an FDOPA scan.2. In reference to the study #16, please clarify how many patients, out of 27, had a follow-up clinical diagnosis. In the application, such data are provided for only eight patients.
L1177: Dr. Chaly responded that 27 patients were not responding to anti-Parkinson medications, while the rest (158 patients) were responding to anti-Parkinson medications upon referral to the Feinstein Institute for F-18 FDOPA PET. A clinical study coordinator at the Feinstein Institute held telephone conversations with referring physicians and designated each referred patient as "Parkinson Disease" or "Suspected Non-Parkinson" based on the telephone conversation. No additional records of the telephone conversation were kept. The coordinator was the only one at the Feinstein Institute who had knowledge of the clinical status/diagnosis of each referred patient, and the coordinator retired in 2006. All patients included in the F-18 FDOPA NDA were from 2006 or earlier.
L1178: Regarding the follow-up of the 27 patients who had negative F-18 FDOPA scans, Dr. Chaly was not able to confirm that all 27 patients were followed till final clinical diagnosis was confirmed. A visiting scholar (not present at the teleconference) to the Feinstein Institute
L1179: was given the full responsibility of the follow-up of these 27 patients, and Dr. Chaly does not know all of the follow-up details.
L1180: Following the Post-Midcycle Communication teleconference, the applicant submitted another amendment to the NDA on 4/5/2013 to further clarify:
L1181: 1) “In studies #15 and #16, 27 of the 185 patients were not responding to anti-Parkinson therapy prior to undergoing an FDOPA scan. These 27 patients were classified as “suspected non-PD” due to the fact that they exhibited parkinsonian symptoms but were not responding to treatment. They were referred to our center for a FDOPA scan in order to confirm that their symptoms were due to an ailment other than Parkinson’s disease. The remaining 158 patients were responding to Parkinson’s treatment and were classified as “PD”.
L1182: 2) Out of the 27 patients referred to in study #16, ten were contacted for follow up studies. Of the ten that were contacted, two were unavailable for unknown reasons. The follow-up study was conducted by Dr. Eckert and involved contacting the referring physicians within the time frame given in his publication (Regional Metabolic Changes in Parkinsonian Patients with Normal Dopaminergic Imaging: Thomas Eckert, MD et.al, Movement Disorders, Volume 22, No 2, pp 167-173, 2007). All eight patients had Fludeoxyglucose F 18 PET scans at some point during the study to check for atypical Parkinson's conditions. The Fludeoxyglucose F 18 PET scans of these eight patients did not disclose patterns of regional metabolic change compatible with classical PD or an atypical parkinsonian variant. None of these patients developed clinical signs of PD or an atypical parkinsonian syndrome at a follow-up visit conducted 3 years after imaging. The clinical diagnoses of these patients at follow up were varied, but included such disorders as potential psychogenic movement disorder, medication side effects, DOPA-responsive dystonia, lithium exposure, postencephal viral infection and midbrain compression secondary to a posterior fossa cyst.
L1183: Based on the applicant’s clarification in the 4/5/2013 amendment to the NDA, the regulatory question of clinical utility again comes to surface. It appears that 158 of the 185 patients who were referred to the Feinstein Institute for F-18 FDOPA PET already had a clinical diagnosis of Parkinson Disease based on the facts that 1) they all exhibited at least two of the cardinal features of Parkinsonian syndromes (dyskinesia, rigidity, tremor, and/or gait disorders), and 2) they were responding to antiparkinson medications. Then what clinically useful information does F-18 FDOPA PET provide to these patients? Code of Federal Regulations (21 CFR 315.5) states that “the effectiveness of a diagnostic radiopharmaceutical is assessed by evaluating its ability to provide useful clinical information related to its proposed indications for use.” It appears F-18 FDOPA PET does not provide useful clinical information to patients who have pre-imaging clinical diagnosis of PD based on their clinical symptoms and the fact that they are responding to antiparkinson medications, and therefore F-18 FDOPA would not be indicated in these patients.
L1184: For the 27 patients who exhibit at least two cardinal features of Parkinson Disease but are not responding to antiparkinson medications, F-18 FDOPA PET could potentially provide useful clinical information, if the imaging test’s accuracy/reliability could be confirmed by clinical data.
L1185: The problem is that only 8 out of 27 such patients were clinically followed to obtain patient's final clinical diagnosis so as to allow verification of the accuracy of the imaging test F-18 FDOPA PET. The other 19 patients who could potentially benefit from an imaging test such as F-18 FDOPA PET unfortunately were not followed, and therefore these patients did not have their standard of truth (final clinical diagnosis) established to allow for assessment of the accuracy of the imaging test F-18 FDOPA PET.
L1186: Overall the clinical reviewer concludes that a patient population who exhibit at least two cardinal features of Parkinsonian syndrome but are not responding to antiparkinson medication could potentially receive useful clinical information from an imaging test such as F-18 FDOPA PET, however clinical data to verify the accuracy/reliability of the imaging test F-18 FDOPA PET are scarce – in this case only 8 such patients with verified standard of truth to allow for the assessment of the performance characteristics of F-18 FDOPA PET.
L1187: 6.1.7 Analysis of Clinical Information Relevant to Dosing Recommendations
L1188: Different clinical studies in the application used different F-18 FDOPA and carbidopa pretreatment dosing. Table 11 compares various dosing regimens used in six clinical studies of the application. The FDA Clinical Pharmacology review team has reviewed the proposed F-18 FDOPA dosing regimen including the dosing regimen for carbidopa pretreatment and found the proposed dosing for F-18 FDOPA ((b)(4) 5 mCi intravenous injection) and carbidopa pretreatment (150 mg given orally one hour prior to F-18 FDOPA injection) acceptable.

L1191: 7 Review of Safety
L1192: Safety Summary
L1193: The NDA re-submitted at the beginning of the NDA review cycle (October 22, 2012) contain no safety data. The applicant states in the submission:
L1194: "No serious or significant adverse events have been reported due to the administration of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection. Generally, compounds belonging to the aromatic amino acid structural class are considered to be safe. The extremely small mass amount of the active ingredient present in the drug product has no therapeutic effect and therefore would not be expected to have any toxicity. In addition, the majority of the metabolites are eliminated from the body within 24 hours through the bladder."
L1195: Following the FDA communication on potentially filing deficiencies of the NDA, the applicant submitted as an amendment to the NDA patient level data for the clinical study conducted by the applicant. The spreadsheet containing patient level data of the study contains a column for "Adverse Events", and all 196 subjects of the study had no adverse events based on the spreadsheet.
L1196: F-18 FDOPA is structurally similar to levodopa, with the only difference between the two being F-18 labeling at position 6 of the aromatic ring (Figure 3)
L1197: Figure 3: Structural Comparison of F-18 FDOPA and Levodopa
L1198: The image displays two chemical structures side-by-side. On the left is [18F]FDOPA, which consists of a benzene ring with a fluorine atom (F-18) at the ortho position relative to a hydroxyl group, and a propanoic acid side chain at the para position. On the right is Levodopa, which has the same structure but lacks the fluorine atom. Both structures show the amino acid side chain (propanoic acid) attached to the benzene ring, with the fluorine atom in [18F]FDOPA at the 6-position (ortho to the hydroxyl group).Chemical structures of [18F]FDOPA and Levodopa
L1199: Based on FDA CMC review, the calculated total mass of F-19 fluorodopa, based on a specific activity of &gt; (b) (4) mCi/mmol, ranges from (b) (4) to (b) (4) mg, depending on the yield of from (b) (4) mCi of product. This corresponds roughly to (b) (4) mg FDOPA/mL of product. This is smaller than the mass dose often used for levodopa, which in clinical practice could be over (b) (4) mg
L1200: daily. The reviewer considers F-18 FDOPA in the proposed single administration dosing, does not pose additional safety concerns.
L1201: 8 Pediatric Research Equity Act (PREA) Requirements
L1202: The applicant initially submitted a request for deferring pediatric studies assessing the safety and effectiveness of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection in pediatric patient. Subsequently the applicant acknowledged that this deferral request submitted with the NDA was a mistake, and the applicant submitted a full pediatric waiver as an amendment to the NDA on 1/3/2013.
L1203: The applicant states that Fluorodopa F 18 Injection is not intended for pediatric patients. The proposed indication for Fluorodopa F 18 injection has extremely limited applicability to pediatric patients because of the pathophysiology of Parkinson's disease, which is found mostly in adult population. Studies with Fluorodopa F 18 Injection would be impossible or highly impractical for pediatric patients as the indication relates to the adult-related condition of Parkinson's Disease. The reviewer agrees with the applicant's rationale and recommends granting the applicant's request for a full pediatric waiver.
L1204: On June 5, 2013, the FDA Pediatric Review Committee (PeRC) discussed the application and agreed with granting full pediatric waiver.

L1278: I-123 FP-CIT SPECT (DaTscan)
L1279: N-\omega-fluoropropyl-2\beta-carbmethoxy-3\beta-(4-[^{123}I]iodophenyl)nortropane
L1280: • Approved in the U.S. and Europe
• Selectively binds to dopamine transporter (DaT) =&gt; DaT imaging agent
• Indicated for striatal dopamine transporter visualization by SPECT brain imaging to assist in the evaluation of adult patients with suspected Parkinsonian syndromes (PS). In these patients, DaTscan may be used to help differentiate essential tremor from tremor due to Parkinsonian Syndrome (idiopathic Parkinson's disease, multiple system atrophy and progressive supranuclear palsy)
• Recommended dosing: 111 to 185 MBq (3 to 5 mCi)

L1320: Clinical Study #21 Limitations
L1321: • Single center study
• All study patients had strong clinical evidence of PD, and these patients may not need another imaging test to confirm diagnosis
            
– Patients with atypical symptoms were excluded, but these are patients who may benefit from an imaging test in further diagnostic work-up

L1323: Clinical Study #21 Limitations
L1324: • Exploratory study• No blinded image read• Calculation of Specificity unclear from the publication, even definitions of positive/negative scans are quantitative exploratory measures• Small study
L1325: 33
L1326: Efficacy Summary
L1327: • 9 clinical studies submitted• 2 studies relevant for evaluating diagnostic efficacy (performance characteristics) of FDOPA• Both studies with serious deficiencies:– Study 15/16: retrospective, single center, initial clinical diagnosis as SOT (lacking clinical utility), most patients without true SOT– Study 21: exploratory, single center, all study patients already diagnosed with PD (not target patient population), definitions of positive/negative scans quantitative exploratory measures, unclear how specificity was calculated
L1328: 34
L1329: FDA logo
L1330: Safety Data
L1331: • No safety data submitted in the NDA• Applicant states that there have been no safety concerns from over 20 years of use of the drug at the Feinstein Institute• Derivative of L-dopa, which has a known safety profile
L1332: 35

L1334: Recommendations
L1335: • Complete Response based on lack of robust clinical data for efficacy• Continue enrolling patients through the Expanded Access IND– of patients scanned with FDOPA PET, the applicant plans to follow 20-30 patients for 2-3 years to obtain standard of truth
