L1339: BRENDA Q YE
L1340: 06/21/2013
L1341: Spelling errors corrected by the reviewer without changing contents of the review.
L1342: Summary of F18 Fluorodopa publications pertaining to use of the imaging drug in neurologic disease evaluation
L1343: NDA: 200655
L1344: Reviewer: Dwaine Rieves/Director/DMIP
L1345: Today's date: November 18, 2011
L1346: 1. Summary:
L1347: The Division of Medical Imaging Products recently received an inquiry from Dr. Thomas Chaly requesting assistance in continuing to provide F18 fluorodopa (FDOPA) to patients at his institution. The division has had several interactions with Dr. Chaly over the past years, including a meeting where the division staff pointed out the lack of sufficient efficacy data from the publications he was citing to support the efficacy of FDOPA, relative to an NDA submission. In response, Dr. Chaly met with the division on July 19, 2010 where he noted that he desired to present certain data from his own institution to support the safety and efficacy of FDOPA.
L1348: At the July 19, 2010 meeting, Dr. Chaly outlined how, in his opinion, the FDOPA test has 100% sensitivity and 100% specificity. The division pointed out errors within his mathematics and also the inability to actually calculate performance criteria due to the lack of a truth standard (medical evidence deficit). The division reiterated the importance of Dr. Chaly obtaining medical assistance in the analysis and interpretation of his clinical data. Dr. Chaly stated that he has been unable to obtain assistance from medical doctors.
L1349: On November 9, 2011, Dr. Chaly sent a letter (email) requesting FDA assistance in order for him to continue providing FDOPA to his clinicians/patients after December 12, 2011. On November 17, 2011, the division talked with Dr. Chaly and encouraged him to submit an IND to support the continued use of FDOPA. The division emphasized that the staff would work expeditiously to review the IND and resolve any concerns such that the FDOPA use could continue uninterrupted. Dr. Chaly again noted that he has not been able to obtain any medical doctors to help him develop FDOPA and he has concerns that his institution's medical doctors will not have the time to help him. He expressed some hope that he could obtain a medical doctor's assistance as a sub-investigator in an IND study. Dr. Chaly also noted that he thought FDA should approve FDOPA since he has used it for many years with no problems and his medical doctors tell him the test is useful.
L1350: 2. Purpose of this memorandum:
L1351: This review memorandum documents that we have once again examined the Pub Med listing of publications in order to determine if published data are sufficient to support the efficacy of FDOPA. As of this date, we continue to find a deficit of published information sufficient to support the approval of FDOPA as a diagnostic imaging agent
L1352: for use in the care of patients with symptoms of Parkinson's Disease. The supplied exploratory information suggests some potential for FDOPA usefulness, although similar information is currently provided by DaTscan, a marketed SPECT drug.
L1353: The following timeline observations are relevant:
L1354: • 2000 FDA held a public discussion of FDOPA; observations at that time did not support the efficacy of FDOPA in clinical practice, although the potential for its use as a biomarker was noted.• 2005 Publication of a systematic review of radiotracer imaging in Parkinson's disease that examined the published literature regarding FDOPA, C11-dihydrotetraabenazine, I123 beta-CIT SPECT (DaTscan) and F18FDG concluded that, "The four tracers fulfill this [biologic relevance] criterion, although they do not measure the number or density of dopaminergic neurons. Biomarkers used as diagnostic tests, prognostic tools, or surrogate endpoints must not only have biologic relevance but also a strong linkage to the clinical outcome of interest. No radiotracers fulfill these criteria and current evidence does not support the use of imaging as a diagnostic tool in clinical practice or as a surrogate endpoint in clinical trials." From: The role of radiotracer imaging in Parkinson's Disease; Neurology 2005: 64(2):208-15.• 2006 American Academy of Neurology publishes "Practice Parameter: Diagnosis and Prognosis of New Onset Parkinson Disease (an evidence-based review);" this was a systematic review of all published reports from 1997 through 2002 based upon multiple database listings of publications. A standard evidence quality assessment was used to assess the usefulness of the information in the reports. The Academy concluded that no reports of FDOPA were conducive to review due to inability to meet study design and conduct quality expectations for a study. The Academy concluded "Beta-CIT and IBZM SPECT are possibly useful in distinguishing PD from ET and normals (five class III studies). There is insufficient evidence to determine if these modalities are useful in distinguishing PD from other forms of parkinsonism."• 2008 Dr. Chaly submits results of his review of all publications (21) supporting the safety and efficacy of FDOPA. The review of this material by the Division (see PreIND 78861) found insufficient evidence of efficacy. Much of the submitted publications referred to therapeutic studies where FDOPA was explored for clinical evidence of an indicator of a therapeutic effect.• 2009 Dr. Chaly submits copies of five additional publications that he regards as potentially useful to help evaluate FDOPA. The division's review (see PreIND 78861) noted multiple limitations within these data, mainly related to lack of an objective of diagnostic performance assessment.
L1355: • 2009 Dr. Chaly submits an NDA which contained the previously cited publications. An RTF letter was issued related to multiple deficiencies in data analyses, lack of safety and dosage data and lack of required NDA clinical components.
• 2009 GE Healthcare submits NDA for DaTscan (ioflupane I 123) Injection; was approved in January 2011 for: “striatal dopamine transporter visualization using single photon emission computed tomography (SPECT) brain imaging to assist in the evaluation of adult patients with suspected Parkinsonian syndromes (PS). In these patients, DaTscan may be used to help differentiate essential tremor from tremor due to PS (idiopathic Parkinson’s Disease, multiple system atrophy and progressive supranuclear palsy). DaTscan is an adjunct to other diagnostic evaluations.” The safety and efficacy data were obtained from studies conducted by GE Healthcare.
• November, 2011 publication of “The role of dopaminergic imaging in patients with symptoms of dopaminergic system neurodegeneration” Brain 2011; 134(3146-3166). This publication describes in detail the diagnostic value of DaTscan and briefly mentions alternatives. It notes that a 2003 publication “found a generally similar imaging pattern between the two approaches “(FDOPA and DaTscan).
• November 18, 2011 I searched PubMed for all clinical trial publications for “fluorodopa PET” within the past 3 years. Below are the references/publications cited (the search was confined to clinical trials/did not include reviews):
      
• 1). Comparison of dopamine turnover, dopamine influx constant and activity ratio of striatum and occipital brain with F18 dopa in brain PET normal controls and patients with Parkinson’s Disease. Eur J Nucl Med Mol Imaging. 2011 Aug;38(8): 1550-9.
• This study examined 9 healthy volunteers and 36 patients with Parkinson’s Disease in order to estimate the “turnover” of dopamine within the brain. The study did not examine any diagnostic parameters. The study found dopamine “turn-over” was different between patients and controls.
• 2). Dopamine cell implantation in Parkinson’s disease: long term clinical and F18 FDOPA PET outcomes. J Nucl Med 2010 Jan; 51(1):7-15.
• This study examined FDOPA uptake in the brains of 33 patients who had embryonic dopamine cell implantation for Parkinson’s Disease. The study did not examine any diagnostic outcomes. The FDOPA observations suggested some transplanted cells were viable for up to four years after implantation.

L1398: 1 Executive Summary
L1399: 1.1 Introduction
L1400: NDA 200655 is a 505(b)(2) New Chemical Entity submission from Feinstein Institute for Medical Research for approval of “Fluorodopa F 18 Injection” ([^{18}\text{F}]\text{FDOPA}), proposed as a Radioactive Diagnostic Agent in Positron Emission Tomography (PET). The proposed indication is to visualize dopaminergic (b) (4) in the striatum in adult patients with suspected Parkinsonian syndromes (PS).
L1401: 1.2 Brief Discussion of Nonclinical Findings
L1402: FDA conducted a review and evaluation of [^{18}\text{F}]\text{FDOPA} Injection in preparation for a Public Meeting in July 2000 between the FDA and the Clinical PET community. At the time, the available nonclinical pharmacology information on [^{18}\text{F}]\text{FDOPA}, and the Pharmacology/Toxicology and clinical literature on Levodopa were considered adequate to support clinical use of [^{18}\text{F}]\text{FDOPA} Injection for PET imaging studies (see attached review of [^{18}\text{F}]\text{FDOPA} Paper NDA, by Adebayo. Laniyonu, Ph.D., Supervisory Pharmacologist, DMIP). The assessment was based upon the criteria listed below.
L1403: • [^{18}\text{F}]\text{FDOPA} and Levodopa are nearly identical molecules. [^{18}\text{F}]\text{FDOPA} contains a Fluorine atom (Fluorine-18) at the 6 position instead of a Hydrogen atom on the aromatic ring as shown in Figure 1.
L1404: The image displays two chemical structures side-by-side. The structure on the left is [^{18}\text{F}]\text{FDOPA}, which consists of a benzene ring with a fluorine atom (F[18]) at the 6-position, a hydroxyl group (OH) at the 3-position, and a 3,4-dihydroxyphenylalanine side chain at the 1-position. The structure on the right is Levodopa, which is identical to [^{18}\text{F}]\text{FDOPA} but lacks the fluorine atom at the 6-position, having a hydrogen atom instead. Both structures show the amino acid side chain with a carboxylic acid group (COOH) and an amino group (NH2).Chemical structures of [18F]FDOPA and Levodopa
L1405: Figure 1. Structural Comparison of [^{18}\text{F}]\text{FDOPA} and Levodopa.Source of structures: http://ncbi.nlm.nih.gov
L1406: • The safety of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection is well supported in terms of historical use of Levodopa for treatment of Parkinson’s disease.• The availability of extensive nonclinical and clinical databases on Levodopa.• The availability of a nonclinical pharmacology database on [^{18}\text{F}]\text{FDOPA}.
L1407: • The safety of Levodopa is widely accepted at prescribed doses up to 2000 mg per day for chronic use (Carbidopa/Levodopa Monograph 2013), which is more than 1000-fold the one-time intravenously-administered [^{18}\text{F}]FDOPA mass dose of (b) (4) mg or less.• The radiologic dose of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection, (b) (4) 5 mCi, is typically acceptable for diagnostic PET procedures, and presents a negligible risk of radiologic-based toxicity in adult patients.
L1408: No original nonclinical studies were submitted in support of NDA 200655, nor were original nonclinical studies required by FDA for the current submission.
L1409: 1.3 Recommendations
L1410: 1.3.1 Approvability
L1411: NDA 200655 is recommended for Approval from Pharmacology and Toxicology perspectives.
L1412: 1.3.2 Additional Nonclinical Recommendations
L1413: None
L1414: 1.3.3 Labeling
L1415: Labeling will be addressed in a separate review document.

L1423: Structure: [18F]FDOPA
L1424: Chemical structure of [18F]FDOPA (Fluorodopa F 18). The structure shows a benzene ring with a fluorine atom (F) at the ortho position relative to a hydroxyl group (OH). The fluorine atom is labeled with [18F] in pink. The ring is also attached to a propanoic acid side chain (CH2-CH(NH2)-COOH). The amino group (NH2) is shown in blue, and the carboxylic acid group (COOH) is shown in red.

L1438: 2.6 Proposed Clinical Population and Dosing Regimen
L1439: Fluorodopa F 18 Injection is a (b) (4) used in Positron Emission Tomography (PET) to visualize dopaminergic (b) (4) in the striatum in adult patients with suspected Parkinsonian syndromes. Patients are usually administered (b) (4) mg of carbidopa 60 minutes before the intravenous injection of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection at a radiologic dose of (b) (4) 5.0 mCi and a mass dose of (b) (4) mg. Positron emission tomography imaging is performed (b) (4) minutes post-injection.
L1440: 2.7 Regulatory Background
L1441: NDA 200655 was first submitted by the Feinstein Institute in 2010. The Agency refused the NDA, citing inadequate clinical, nonclinical, and CMC information. The current submission is a re-submission of NDA 200655. The Division had recommended that the applicant provide Pharmacology and Toxicology information in the revised NDA, which could be accomplished by including a statement in reference to the July 28, 2000 Public Meeting between FDA CDER medical imaging staff and the Clinical PET community. The Agency's recommendation to refer to the July 2000 Public Meeting was not related to a specific safety issue; rather, to the provision of basic information in the NDA on the pharmacological basis for using the diagnostic agent, and the rationale supporting the safety of [18F]FDOPA in PET imaging procedures.
L1442: Fluorodopa F18 Injection was reviewed and discussed during the July 2000 Public Meeting between FDA and the Clinical PET community. Dr. Adebayo Laniyonu presented an overview of [18F]FDOPA and Levodopa pharmacology and toxicology, which was based on his review of nonclinical literature available from the public domain. The collection of references reviewed for the Public Meeting is referred to as the Agency's [18F]FDOPA Master Reference List, which is attached to this review.
L1443: The outcome of the July 2000 Public Meeting discussion was that FDA deferred the decision on the approval of Fluorodopa F18 Injection to a future date. Dr. Laniyonu's Pharmacology and Toxicology Review of [18F]FDOPA as a Paper NDA is attached at the end of this document.
L1444: The nonclinical section of the current NDA 200655 submission contained appropriate reference to the July 28, 2000, Public Meeting between FDA and the Clinical PET community.
L1445: It is pertinent to note that Fluorodopa F18 Injection is licensed as Dopacis® in the United Kingdom and IASODOPA in several other countries (Chevalme, Y-M, et al. 2007). The UK Summary of Product Characteristics (MHRA PL 11876-0020; 2010) describes the indication of Dopacis® “...for detecting loss of functional dopaminergic neuron terminals in the striatum in patients with clinically uncertain Parkinsonian syndromes. It
L1446: can be used to differentiate essential tremor from Parkinsonian syndromes related to degenerative diseases affecting the nigrostriatal system (Parkinson disease, multisystem atrophy and progressive supranuclear palsy).”
L1447: 3 Studies Submitted
L1448: 3.1 Studies Reviewed
L1449: No original nonclinical study reports were submitted by the sponsor for this 505(b)(2) New Molecular Entity NDA. The sponsor submitted summaries of 20 publically available reports on the Pharmacology and Toxicology of [18F]FDOPA. The literature-based references are listed below in Table 2. The sponsor did not give reasons for the choices of articles included in the NDA.
L1450: The sponsor also made reference to a non-GLP toxicity study of [18F]FDOPA in female mice, which was conducted as part of a Master's Thesis project.
L1451: Two literature reports submitted by the sponsor were reviewed in the current NDA review document. Those studies are shown in underlined, italicized font in Table 2. The remaining referenced literature reports, and additional references not included in NDA 200655, were reviewed by Dr. Adebayo Laniyonu, DMIP Pharmacologist in preparation for the July 2000 Public Meeting and were documented in a Paper NDA Review. Dr. Laniyonu's [18F]FDOPA Paper NDA review is attached to this NDA review, as is the FDA [18F]FDOPA Master Reference List used in the July 2000 Public Meeting.

L1471: 5 Pharmacokinetics/ADME/Toxicokinetics
L1472: 5.1 PK/ADME
L1473: Brief Summary:
L1474: Two articles from the biomedical literature were reviewed (below). The main points of the articles were consistent with the comprehensive review of [18F]FDOPA and Levodopa literature previously conducted by FDA.
L1475: NDA SectionPrimary AuthorTitle of ArticleJournal Title; Year; Volume (no.); Page(s)



4.2.2.5.1Khor, S-PThe Pharmacokinetics And Pharmacodynamics Of Levodopa In The Treatment Of Parkinson's DiseaseCurr. Clin. Pharmacol.20072234-43
L1476: Key findings:
L1477: The article is a review of the pharmacokinetics and pharmacodynamics of Levodopa and carbidopa. The authors examined and summarized various factors that influence optimal therapeutic effects of Levodopa in PD patients, with a focus on ADME profiles of long- versus short-acting formulations of Levodopa and carbidopa. The authors averred that the main means of transport of 3-O-methyldopa and Levodopa into brain is via the saturable "large neutral amino acid transport" system. The transport system is rate limiting for entry of Levodopa and 3-O-methyldopa into the brain.
L1478: Reviewer comment: The same is probably true for Fluorodopa and 3-O-methyl-6-fluorodopa, although the authors make no mention of Fluorodopa.
L1479: NDA SectionPrimary AuthorTitle of ArticleJournal Title; Year; Volume (no.); Page(s)



4.2.2.5.3Bartholini, GDistribution And Metabolism Of L-3-O-Methyldopa In RatsBrit. J. Pharmacol.197040461
L1480: Key findings:
L1481: The authors concluded that L-3-O-methyldopa was present uniformly in blood plasma, brain and heart after intra-peritoneal injection in male Wistar rats. The half-life of L-3-O-methyldopa is longer than that of Levodopa (12-13 h compared with about 0.5 h).
L1482: Reviewer's comment:
L1483: The findings in the article are consistent with previously reviewed information on Levodopa.
L1484: 5.2 Toxicokinetics
L1485: No information submitted or required.
L1486: 6 General Toxicology
L1487: 6.1 Single-Dose Toxicity
L1488: NDA SectionPrimary AuthorTitle of ArticleSource



4.2.3.1Hochfeld, WThe Chemical Synthesis, Pharmaceutical Preparation And Toxicity Analysis Of Fluorodopa For Positron Emission Tomography (PET) Brain Imaging In South AfricaMasters Thesis, Department Of Pharmacology, The University Of Pretoria, SA, 2008.
L1489: Key Study Findings: The reference is part of a Master's Thesis document. The author conducted a non-GLP acute toxicity study of Fluorodopa F 18 injection in female mice at the University of Pretoria Department of Pharmacology. Intravenous injection of 218 µg/Kg Fluorodopa F 18 in normal saline did not produce any treatment-related effects during the 7-day post-dose observation period. The NOAEL for [18F]FDOPA was (b) (4)-times the maximum human dose of (b) (4) mg per 60-Kg person (b) (4) µg/Kg), based on body weight.
L1490: Study design
L1491: Two groups of healthy, BALB/c female mice were administered an intravenous injection of 50 µL sodium chloride (0.9% w/v) (Group 1) or 218.18 µg (1 µmol) per Kg of Fluorodopa F 18 (suspended in sodium chloride (0.9% w/v) per Kg body weight.
L1492: Animals were observed for clinical signs during the 4-hour period post-injection, and twice daily thereafter for 7 days. Changes in normal activity were recorded and weights were monitored. On day 7, blood samples were collected via cardiac puncture for serum chemistry tests (AST, ALT, Albumin, Gamma-glutamyl transaminase, lactate dehydrogenase) and hematology tests (CBC with differential). Specimens of major tissues (brain, heart, kidneys, liver, spleen, pancreas, stomach, thymus, intestine (small), intestine (large) gonad, lung) were collected, weighed (brain, heart, kidneys, liver, spleen), and processed for microscopic evaluation.
L1493: Results
L1494: No signs of toxicity were observed. Both groups of animals appeared healthy throughout the 7-day period.
L1495: Food and water intake was similar in both groups.
L1496: There were no significant differences in body weights of Group 1 versus Group 2 animals.
L1497: There were no test article dependent changes in any serum chemistry or hematology tests.
L1498: There were no notable microscopic findings.
L1499: The author concluded there were no adverse findings in female mice seven days after intravenous administration of (b) (4) ug/Kg (b) (4) umol/Kg) Fluorodopa F 18.
L1500: Reviewer's comments:
L1501: The study is supportive of the safety of [18F]FDOPA in general, with the caveat that it was conducted in a University Laboratory as part of a Master's Thesis project. There were many aspects of the study design that didn't meet typical requirements for a toxicity study in a regulatory submission (including but not limited to females only, limited evaluations, only one scheduled termination 7 days post-single dose). The study cannot be considered adequately controlled and was not conducted under any GLP guidelines. However, the study results were consistent with the known clinical safety profile of [18F]FDOPA and Levodopa. The NOAEL of (b) (4) ug [18F]FDOPA per Kg body weight is (b) (4)-times the maximum human dose, based on a maximum clinical mass dose of (b) (4) mg [18F]FDOPA in a 60-kg person (or (b) (4) ug/Kg).
L1502: 6.2 Repeat-Dose Toxicity
L1503: None submitted.
L1504: 7 Genetic Toxicology
L1505: No studies submitted. The radioactive Fluorine 18 atom is considered inherently genotoxic. The Product Label should state so.

L1507: Not required.

L1509: None submitted
L1510: 10 Special Toxicology Studies
L1511: None.
L1512: 11 Integrated Summary and Safety Evaluation
L1513: Fluorodopa F 18 Injection is a metabolic tracer for dopaminergic activity, based on the biochemistry and physiology of striatal function. The receptor biology and biochemical transformations of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection that occur in striatal neurons form the basis of the mechanism of action of [18F]FDOPA in the brain.
L1514: Prior to the submission of NDA 200655, typical pharmacology and toxicology studies required for an NDA application were waived, based on the Agency's review of the
L1515: publicly available nonclinical information, which was conducted by Dr. Adebayo Laniyonu (1). The available information was found to be acceptable in lieu of conventional Pharmacology and Toxicology studies for Fluorodopa F 18 Injection based on the following factors:
L1516: 1) Clinical experience with Fluorodopa F 18 Injection, without reports of safety concerns;2) Comprehensive Levodopa historical database and 30+ years experience in chronic clinical use at daily doses more than 1000-fold the one-time [18F]FDOPA dose of (b) (4) mg;3) Similarity in the metabolism and disposition of Levodopa and [18F]FDOPA; and4) Low mass dose of [18F]FDOPA administered in PET studies.
L1517: Two literature-based reports and a non-GLP toxicity study were reviewed in this document. The information in the reports provided additional, albeit minimal, evidence that Fluorodopa F 18 Injection is safe for the maximum recommended intravenous mass dose of (b) (4) mg and radiologic dose of 5 mCi.
L1518: Based on the factors listed above, I recommend that NDA 200655 may be approved from the Pharmacology and Toxicology perspectives.

L1543: Introduction/Drug History:
L1544: There are published articles concerning the clinical utility of [18F]Fluorodopa in evaluation of alterations in presynaptic dopaminergic system especially as it relates to neurology [1,2,3,4,5,6]. However, the agency has not granted approval for any indication. PET radiopharmaceuticals are usually manufactured close to the site of use due to the relatively short half-life of these products. Unlike a conventional application, this review is not evaluating a known formulation, hence different production pathway may impact on the purity and toxicological profile of [18F]Fluorodopa since different PET centers are responsible for production. A major purpose of this survey is to review the available scientific literature in an attempt to examine the scientific basis of use and, to determine whether Pharmacology/Toxicology studies typically conducted and
L1545: considered critical to support the safety of an NDA application have been reported in the literature. This is not a typical NDA review, because the experiments upon which the scientific evidence are based were not conducted with an NDA format in mind. Nevertheless, all the articles were published in peer reviewed journals and the most of the findings were reported from multiple laboratories. This review aims to (1) identify the concepts that are scientifically valid and that appear to have general support within the scientific community and (2) Identify the areas where more information is required.
L1546: Previous clinical experience:
L1547: [18F]Fluorodopa has been utilized in the evaluation of alterations in presynaptic dopaminergic system especially as it relates to neurological needs from multiple clinical centers. No adverse reaction to the radiopharmaceutical has been documented at clinically relevant doses. (see medical officer review).
L1548: Pharmacology:
L1549: An understanding of the conceptual basis for the use, and limitation of [18F]Fluorodopa in the evaluation of alterations in presynaptic dopaminergic system requires a brief overview of 1) The biosynthesis and metabolism of dopamine and [18F]Fluorodopamine, 2), similarities and differences in the way the body handles F-DOPA and L-DOPA, and, 3) integration of how [18F]Fluorodopa fits into the contextual evaluation of alterations in presynaptic dopaminergic system.
L1550: Biosynthesis and metabolism of Dopamine:
L1551: The diagram is a flowchart titled "Not just an intermediate". It shows a vertical sequence of chemical intermediates in the biosynthesis of catecholamines. The sequence is: Tyrosine, Dopa, DOPAMINE, Norepinephrine, and Epinephrine. Each step is connected by a downward-pointing arrow. The word "DOPAMINE" is in all caps, while the others are in title case.graph TD; A[Tyrosine] --&gt; B[Dopa]; B --&gt; C[DOPAMINE]; C --&gt; D[Norepinephrine]; D --&gt; E[Epinephrine];Flowchart showing the biosynthesis pathway: Tyrosine leads to Dopa, which leads to DOPAMINE, which leads to Norepinephrine, which leads to Epinephrine. The title 'Not just an intermediate' is at the top.
L1552: Fig 1: Schema for the synthesis of norepinephrine and epinephrine
L1553: Once considered solely an intermediate in the biosynthesis of the catecholamines, the role of dopamine as a neurotransmitter in its own right is now firmly established [7]. The early impetus
L1554: for such consideration came largely from Scandinavian investigators [7,8]. They demonstrated distribution of dopamine in peripheral organs, and provided evidence of brain dopamine concentration in greater amount than would have been anticipated solely from a precursor role. More importantly, there were marked differences in regional distribution of dopamine, and the sympathomimetic neurotransmitter, norepinephrine both within the central nervous system and in bovine peripheral tissues.
L1555: The synthesis of dopamine occurs in the terminals of dopaminergic neurons [4,7,8]. Dopamine does not cross the blood brain barrier from the systemic circulation. However, the synthetic precursor, tyrosine is transported across the blood brain barrier through an active transport process. Within the neuronal terminals, it is converted to dihydroxyphenylalanine (L-DOPA) by the enzyme, tyrosine hydroxylase (TH). This conversion represents the rate-limiting step in the biosynthesis of dopamine. L-DOPA is converted rapidly to dopamine by aromatic L-amino acid decarboxylase (AAAD). In the terminal, dopamine is taken up into the storage vesicle by a transporter protein associated with the vesicular membrane where its release is triggered by depolarization and entry of calcium. The released dopamine acts on postsynaptic dopamine receptors. It is noted that L-DOPA can be acted upon by peripheral COMT to produce 3-O methyldopa, which is able to cross the blood brain barrier.
L1556: Dopamine effects are terminated by the enzymes monoamine oxidase (MAO) and cathecol-O-methyl-transferase (COMT) to yield two metabolic products, 3, 4-dihydroxyphenylacetic acid (DOPAC) and 3-methoxy-4-dihydroxyphenylacetic acid (HVA). Dopamine transporter (DAT) located on the presynaptic nerve terminal is involved in terminating dopaminergic transmission by transporting dopamine back into the dopaminergic neuron for storage. DAT has also been suggested to be a possible marker of the integrity of the dopaminergic nerve terminal [7].
L1557: graph TD

  A metabolic pathway diagram showing the synthesis and degradation of dopamine. L-TYROSINE is converted to L-DOPA by TH. L-DOPA can be converted to 3-O METHYLDOPA by COMT or to DOPAMINE* by AAAD. DOPAMINE* can be converted to DOPAC by MAO, to 3-METHOXYTYRAMINE by COMT, or to DOPAMINE-3-SULFATE by PST. DOPAC is converted to HOMOVANILLIC ACID by COMT. 3-METHOXYTYRAMINE is converted to HOMOVANILLIC ACID by MAO. DOPAMINE* is also shown being transported into VESICLES by a TRANSPORTER.
L1558: Fig 2: Schema for the synthesis of Dopamine
L1559: The metabolism of [18F]Fluorodopa (FD) is handled in a similar manner to that of L-DOPA. The uptake and trapping of 18F radioactivity into the striatum after intravenous injection of [18F]Fluorodopa requires the following: (1) a working transporter for FD through the blood-brain barrier, (2), adequate residue of AAAAD activity, and (3) a functioning vesicular storage apparatus for fluorodopamine (FDA) by AAAAD. Thereafter, it is taken up into the storage vesicle where it can be released like dopamine. FDA is acted upon by MAO and COMT to yield 6-[18F]Fluoro-3,4-dihydroxyphenylacetic acid (FDOPAC), and [6-[18F]Fluorohomovanillic acid (FHVA). In the systemic circulation, [18F]Fluorodopa is also acted upon by COMT to produce 3-O-methyl[18F]dopa (3-O-MFDOPA) or decarboxylated by peripheral AAAAD to fluorodopamine (FDA). 3-OMFDOPA can cross the blood brain barrier more rapidly than FD.
L1560: The diagram illustrates the metabolic pathways of fluorodopamine (F-DOPA). F-DOPA is shown as the starting point. It can be converted to 3-O ME-F-DOPA by COMT (Catechol-O-Methyltransferase) or to F-DOPAMINE by AAAAD (Aromatic L-amino acid decarboxylase). F-DOPAMINE can be converted to F-DOPAMINE-3-SULFATE by PST (P-Sulfotransferase), or it can be taken up into VESICLES (represented by a red oval) via a TRANSPORTER. F-DOPAMINE can also be converted to F-DIHYDROXYPHENYLACETIC ACID by MAO (Monoamine Oxidase), or to F-3-METHOXYTYRAMINE by COMT. F-DIHYDROXYPHENYLACETIC ACID is then converted to F-HOMOVANILLIC ACID by COMT. F-3-METHOXYTYRAMINE is also converted to F-HOMOVANILLIC ACID by MAO.Metabolic pathway diagram of fluorodopamine synthesis and degradation.
L1561: Fig3: Schema for the synthesis of fluorodopamine
L1562: 18F-DOPA metabolism:Peripheral &amp; CentralThe diagram illustrates the metabolic pathways of 18F-DOPA (FDOPA) in the blood and brain, separated by the Blood-Brain Barrier (BBB). In the blood, FDOPA can be converted to FDA (Fluorodopamine) by AAAD (Aromatic Amino Acid Decarboxylase) or to 3-OMFDOPA (3-O-Methyl-DOPA) by COMT (Catechol-O-Methyltransferase). Both FDA and 3-OMFDOPA cross the BBB. In the brain, FDA is converted back to FDOPA by AAAD or to 3-OMFDOPA by COMT. The BBB is represented by a vertical dashed red line with red squares.Diagram of 18F-DOPA metabolism in blood and brain. In the blood, FDOPA (FD) is converted to FDA by AAAD (upward arrow) and to 3-OMFDOPA by COMT (downward arrow). FDA and 3-OMFDOPA cross the BBB (red dashed line). In the brain, FDA is converted back to FD by AAAD (rightward arrow) and to 3-OMFDOPA by COMT (downward arrow). Labels include Blood, BBB, and Brain.
L1563: Fig4: 18F-Dopa metabolism: peripheral and central
L1564: In addition to its accumulation within the dopaminergic neurons, it is theoretically possible for FDA to accumulate within the adrenergic nerve terminals since FDA can be converted to [18F]-labeled norepinephrine by the enzyme dopamine hydroxylase.
L1565: Influence of enzyme inhibitors on handling of FDOPA:
L1566: The two principal enzymes influencing the metabolism of FDOPA are aromatic amino acid decarboxylase (AAAD) and catechol-O-methyltransferase (COMT). Both enzymes are present peripherally and centrally. In the absence of enzyme inhibitors, intravenously administered FDOPA is subject to metabolism by these enzymes. Decarboxylation by AAAD leads to formation of fluorodopamine. O-methylation by COMT results in the formation of 3-OMFDOPA. The net result of peripheral metabolism is to reduce the amount of FDOPA available to cross the blood brain barrier. In addition, peripherally formed FDA has physiological actions similar to Dopamine. These actions result from stimulation of both adrenergic and dopaminergic receptors. Unlike FDA, 3-OMFDOPA penetrates the blood brain barrier.
L1567: 18F-DOPA Metabolism:AAAD + COMT inhibitorsThe diagram illustrates the metabolic pathways of 18F-DOPA (FDOPA) in the blood and brain, separated by the blood-brain barrier (BBB). In the blood, FDOPA can be converted to FDA (Fluoro-DOPA) by AAAD (Aromatic L-amino acid decarboxylase) or to 3-OMFDOPA by COMT (Catechol-O-methyltransferase). In the brain, FDOPA is converted to FDA by AAAD and to 3-OMFDOPA by COMT. The BBB is shown as a dashed red line. The diagram is titled '18F-DOPA Metabolism: AAAD + COMT inhibitors'.Diagram of 18F-DOPA metabolism showing the conversion of FDOPA to FDA and 3-OMFDOPA in the blood, and the transport of FDOPA across the blood-brain barrier (BBB) into the brain.
L1568: Fig5: 18F-Dopa metabolism: Influence of AAAD and COMT inhibitors
L1569: In the presence of both AAAD and COMT inhibitors, peripheral metabolism of FDOPA is inhibited, and intravenously administered FDOPA is able to enter the CNS almost in its entirety.
L1570: Pharmacokinetics:
L1571: The major pathways in the peripheral and central metabolism of FDOPA have been discussed under biosynthesis and metabolism. In the absence of a decarboxylase inhibitor, the major peripheral metabolites detected in plasma were 3-OMFDOPA, FDA and, its conjugated metabolites. Carbidopa pretreatment prevents decarboxylation to FDA, increasing FDOPA bioavailability, however, FDOPA can still be metabolized by COMT to 3-OMFDOPA [10, 11, 12, 13].
L1572: The plasma level of FDOPA declines rapidly following intravenous injection [13]. The major metabolite appearing within the first 60 minutes following injection is 3-OMFDOPA. Carbidopa increases both the plasma level and the plasma t_{1/2} of FDOPA [11].

L1577: Both FDA and 3-OMFDOPA are the principal metabolites formed within the CNS (see discussion below).
L1578: Relative importance of 3-o-methylfluorodopa on imaging acquisition:
L1579: 3-O-methylfluorodopa is formed from intravenously administered FDOPA in the absence of a peripheral COMT inhibitor. This metabolite is capable of crossing the blood brain barrier. The question then arises as to whether 3-OMFDOPA affects imaging in terms of contributing to non-specific background signal. Two schools of thought emerged from the review of the literature.
L1580: The first, and to a large extent the majority opinion, suggests that non-specific imaging interference by 3-OMFDOPA is significant, and that the rate and extent of 3-OM-methylation of [18F]DOPA exceed that of [3H]DOPA or [11C]DOPA [10-15].
L1581: In a study examining FDOPA metabolism in monkeys, Barrio and his workers [10] examined the relative importance of 3-OMFDOPA to the metabolite profile of FDOPA following peripheral inhibition of AAAD enzyme. The animals were administered carbidopa (5mg/kg, i.v.) prior to administration of FDOPA, (5-8 mCi, i.v., specific activity 1-2 Ci/mmol). Plasma and brain
L1582: samples were taken for quantification of 18F metabolites. The results indicated that 3-OMFD was the major metabolite detected in all regions of the brain examined (putamen, caudate, frontal cortex and cerebellum). FDA was detected only in caudate and putamen. Other metabolites detected in both regions include FDOPAC, and FHVA.

L1587: The plasma FDOPA metabolites profile at 60 minutes consisted primarily of FDOPA, 3-OMFDOPA, FDA sulfate with 3-OMFDOPA being the predominant metabolite.

L1592: In a human study carried out by the same group [10], male human volunteers were fasted for at least 6 hours, then administered carbidopa, (200 mg p.o). FDOPA (4-8 mCi , i.v.) was given 90 minutes later. Arterial blood samples were collected at intervals. The results suggested that plasma metabolism was restricted to 3-OMFD formation.
L1593: Many laboratories have examined the influence of COMT inhibition of FD metabolism [16, 17, 18, 19]. Ruottinen et.al. [19] studied striatal fluorodopa utilization after COMT inhibition with entacapone in healthy humans and in advanced parkinsonian patients. Entacapone significantly enhanced the fraction of unmetabolized FDOPA in plasma and thus its availability in the brain for decarboxylation both in patients and control subjects.

Pre without entacapone, post with entacapone (Ruottinen, H. M. et.al. 1995, J Neural Transm 10, 91-106)
L1596: The implication of a significant distribution of 3-OMFD throughout the brain from contribution of 3-O-methyl[18F]dopa entering from the periphery in the absence of COMT inhibition is that it contributes to the background activity in PET images thus decreasing the contrast between striatal and reference regions. In analysis employing total radioactivity count, the contributive effect of this metabolite to both background and striatal uptake will complicate the interpretation of images obtained. It is therefore necessary to distinguish the signal in cerebral tissues due to the entrapment of locally decarboxylated compounds especially since 3-OMFDOPA crosses the blood brain barrier more readily than F-DOPA. Alternatively, this interference would make concomitant administration of a COMT inhibitor during FDOPA-PET a necessity.
L1597: The second school of thought emanating mainly from the laboratory of Firnau and co-workers believes that non-specific interference by 3-OMFDOPA is minimal, and that FDA is the major metabolite formed following the administration of FDOPA.
L1598: Firnau and colleagues [20, 21] evaluated the cerebral metabolism profile of FDOPA in rhesus monkeys fasted for twelve hours prior to study commencement. The animals received FDOPA (1.8-5.3 mCi, i.v.). It is pertinent to note that AAAD inhibitor was not used to block the peripheral decarboxylation of FDOPA to FDA. Arterial samples were obtained, and at specific time intervals, animals were euthanized to obtain cerebral tissue samples. The results of the study suggested that FDA was the major striatal metabolite produced, accounting for about 70% of total metabolites at 30 minutes, and for about 50% at 70 minutes. Similar metabolite profile was also obtained in a study conducted on healthy human volunteers that were given FDOPA, 4-6 mCi, I.V.).

L1600: Fig 13: Liquid chromatogramme of the extract of human plasma taken 30 minutes after injection of [18F]fluoro-L-dopa (Firmau, G. et.al. 1988, J Nucl Med 29: 363-369).
L1601: The authors accounted for the results in terms of substrate specificity. Specifically, they argued that in the periphery, FDOPA was less reactive than LDOPA as a substrate for O-methylation and that consequently only FDOPA was available for brain uptake and metabolism to Fluorodopamine. Therefore, one can assume that in studies of cerebral dopamine metabolism involving FDOPA and PET, that the time-activity curve of FDOPA in arterial sample represent the input function of the brain since minimal amount of 3-OMFD is produced peripherally. None specific background contribution from 3-OMFD is minimal. It bears emphasizing that Firmau's laboratory was the only laboratory that reached this conclusion. The preponderance of evidence in the literature suggested that 3-O-methyl[18F]dopa entering from the periphery in the absence of COMT inhibition contributes to the background activity in PET images thus decreasing the contrast between striatal and reference regions.
L1602: My considered opinion is that I agree with the school of thought suggesting that non-specific interference is significant, and should be accounted for image acquisition technique or in image analysis
L1603: Use of [18F]Fluorodopa in evaluation of alterations in presynaptic dopaminergic system
L1604: From a mechanistic perspective, the intuitive appeal of [18F]Fluorodopa as a marker of the integrity of the dopaminergic system is obvious; there is a correlation between the accumulation of [18F] activity and the functional integrity of the dopaminergic neurons, and the activity is attributable to AAAD-mediated formation of FDA [1, 2, 3, 4]. Moreover, preclinical image acquisition studies (PET) have been conducted in primates administered N-methyl-4-phenyl-1,2,3,6-tetrahydropyridine (MPTP) [22-26]. Such primates have served as a model of parkinsonism. Intravenous administration of MPTP produces a disorder like parkinsonism (akinesia, rigidity, postural tremor, flexed posture, eyelid closure and tremor [23-24]). Biochemical correlates include severe nerve cell loss in substantia nigra, and a marked reduction in the dopamine content of the striatum. In PET studies, [18F] accumulation was significantly reduced in the striatum, putamen of the MPTP-treated animals compared to the normal controls. [18F] accumulation in the cortex did not differ between control and MPTP-treated animals. The
L1605: results suggest that vesicular striatal FDA uptake in PET studies is a good index of striatal dopaminergic function. However, as succinctly summarized by Barrio and his colleagues [1], several questions remain largely unanswered as regard the underpinning mechanistic assumptions. For example, 1), how closely does exogenous FDOPA uptake represent endogenous dopamine synthesis given the fact that FDOPA metabolism by AAAD bypasses the influence of tyrosine hydroxylase which is the rate-limiting step in the endogenous dopamine synthesis. 2), Could FDOPA decarboxylation become the rate-limiting step unlike in endogenous conditions where FDOPA decarboxylation is independent of AAAD concentration. 3), is FDOPA kinetics related to other endogenous parameters such as dopaminergic cell number. For example, the sensitivity of FDOPA kinetics on AAAD may not be apparent in the early pre-symptomatic stages of nigrostriatal dopaminergic degeneration when neuronal plasticity may allow for compensatory sprouting of axonal bearing synapses leading to no significant changes in terminal numbers and AAAD concentration
L1606: Toxicology:
L1607: The literature survey did not identify any study that examined the toxicity profile of FDOPA or that quantified impurities or excipients. However, LDOPA (usually administered with a decarboxylase inhibitor) is an approved drug product with more than 30 years of clinical use in the treatment of parkinsonism and related disorders. There is extensive safety database for LDOPA. The side effects of LDOPA are largely attributed to dopamine, formed from its decarboxylation by AAAD. The side effects include nausea, confusion and hallucination, especially in the elderly and those with pre-existing cognitive dysfunction. Peripheral action of dopamine on the cardiovascular system include hypotension, cardiac arrhythmia especially in patients with pre-existing conductance disturbance [7].
L1608: These side effects LDOPA occur at much higher dose range than those anticipated for FDOPA use in PET imaging studies. Daily dose of L-DOPA is about 500-1000 mg. A 10mCi single dose of FDOPA contains approximately (b) (4) mg of the drug substance. The availability of extensive clinical experience with FDOPA without reports of safety-related concern, safety data base for LDOPA, and the fact that FDOPA will be administered in tracer quantity ameliorate the lack of toxicity data for the time being.
L1609: Carcinogenicity:
L1610: No study addressing the carcinogenicity of FDOPA was identified
L1611: Immunotoxicology:
L1612: No study addressing the immunotoxicity of FDOPA was identified
L1613: Reproductive Toxicology:
L1614: No study addressing the reproductive toxicology of FDOPA was identified
L1615: Genotoxicity
L1616: No study addressing the genotoxicity of FDOPA was identified
L1617: Overall Summary:
L1618: This review has relied heavily on the similarity between L-DOPA and FDOPA metabolism, and the way both are handled by the body. That FDOPA acts as a substrate for AAAD, and has been used as a biochemical marker of the dopaminergic system is well documented. What is less clear is the detailed clinical interpretation of the results meaning. Although the number of both preclinical and clinical publications on FDOPA is impressive, results interpretation is confounded by our lack of how disease conditions, technology and methodology affect the fundamental mechanistic assumptions governing the use of FDOPA.
L1619: From a regulatory perspective, the issue of whether pharmacology/toxicology studies typically conducted and considered critical to support the safety of an NDA have been reported in the literature was examined. No study specifically addressing the toxicology, carcinogenicity, genotoxicity, reproductive toxicology or immunotoxicology of FDOPA were identified in the literature. However, extensive clinical experience without reports of safety related concerns, similarity in the metabolism of LDOPA and FDOPA, safety data base for LDOPA, together with the fact that FDOPA is administered in low mass dose, ameliorate lack of data for the time being. Therefore the need for typical pharmacology and toxicology studies can be waived.

L1734: PHARMACOLOGY/TOXICOLOGY Memo for PreNDA 200655
L1735: NDA Number: 200655IND Number 78861Reviewer: Sally Hargus,Ph.D., DMIPApplicant: Feinstein Institute,Thomas ChalyStamp Date:14 and 21 March 2012Drug Name: Fluorodopa F18InjectionType: NDA505(b)(2); NMEMeeting Date:16 April 2012
L1736: The sponsor of NDA 200655 submitted a PreNDA Meeting Package and Questions for FDA regarding this re-submission of [18F]FDOPA for PET detection of dopaminergic striatal (b) (4) in patients with suspected Parkinson's Disease (PD). The procedure is proposed as an adjunct test with other diagnostic evaluations.
L1737: The sponsor had previously submitted an NDA for the same product in 2010. The agency issued a Refuse-to-File letter, due to major deficiencies in the application, such as the complete absence of the nonclinical section and inadequate clinical and CMC information. Regarding the nonclinical deficiency, the Division recommended that the applicant provide Pharmacology and Toxicology information in the revised NDA. The recommendation was not related to a specific safety issue; rather, to provision of basic information on the pharmacological basis of the diagnostic agent and the nonclinical safety profile of FDOPA.
L1738: In the sponsor's current nonclinical section, appropriate reference was made to the July 28, 2000, Public Meeting between FDA and the PET community. The PET drug, Fluorodopa F18 Injection, was reviewed and discussed during the public meeting. At that time, FDA had deferred a decision on the approval of Fluorodopa F18 Injection to a later date (to be determined). The transcript of the meeting is available on the Worldwide Web at http://www.fda.gov/Drugs/DevelopmentApprovalProcess/Manufacturing/ucm182855.htm.
L1739: Also during the 28 July 2000 Public Meeting, Dr. Adebayo Laniyonu presented an overview of Fluorodopa (FDOPA) and L-DOPA pharmacology and toxicology, based on his review of nonclinical literature available from the public domain. The Master List of articles reviewed and discussed at the meeting is available on the Worldwide Web at http://www.fda.gov/Drugs/DevelopmentApprovalProcess/Manufacturing/ucm182855.htm.
L1740: In his written review, Dr. Laniyonu concluded that FDOPA is an acceptable metabolic tracer for dopaminergic activity, and that the need for typical pharmacology and toxicology studies in an application could be waived due to 1) extensive clinical experience without reports of safety related concerns; 2) similarity in the metabolism of LDOPA and FDOPA; 3) safety data base for LDOPA; and 4) low mass dose of FDOPA administered in PET studies (Dr. Adebayo Laniyonu, Review of Fluorodopa, July 2000). However, it was noted that various methodologies for producing Fluorodopa F18 Injection could result in varying profiles of degradants and impurities, which may need to be qualified for safety in some final products.
L1741: No specific nonclinical questions were submitted by the sponsor in the current Meeting Package. The nonclinical section was included in the current preNDA materials and should (or will) be a component of the NDA submission, I presume.
L1742: PHARMACOLOGY/TOXICOLOGY Memo for PreNDA 200655
L1743: The sponsor included a draft label for the product. The label will be reviewed and evaluated during the NDA Review period.
L1744: The sponsor did not provide the maximum mass dose of FDOPA in the label or in the Meeting Materials. The sponsor will be requested to provide the mass dose of FDOPA contained in the product.
L1745: Comments for the sponsor:
L1746: 1. Include the Nonclinical Pharmacology and Toxicology section in your NDA, as required under 21 CFR 314.50(d)(2). The nonclinical information, as stated in Section 4 of the preNDA submission (dated 14 March 2012), was acceptable for filing an NDA.2. Include a statement of the maximum mass dose of [^{18}\text{F}]FluoroDOPA to be administered to a patient undergoing the PET scan. The mass dose of drug substance should be stated in each major section of your NDA.

L1762: 1. EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
L1763: This is an addendum to the primary statistical report (dated 04/26/2016) of NDA 200655 for Fluorodopa F 18 (FDOPA) resubmission. In response to FDA's Complete Response letter given to the applicant in 2013, the sponsor submitted the results from a new clinical trial (#17) that the applicant conducted.
L1764: The safety and efficacy of FDOPA (short-lived radiopharmaceutical used in Positron Emission Tomography (PET)) were evaluated in one single-center, single-arm study (Study #17): 51 subjects were screened, 45 adult subjects with suspected parkinsonian syndromes (PS) were enrolled, and completed the study.
L1765: A literature search and written reviews for F-DOPA as part of developing approval procedures under Section 121 of 1997 FDA Modernization Act (FDAMA) was performed the year 2000 by the FDA imaging team. The reports concluded that the metabolic pathways for endogenous dopamine, the therapeutic drug LDOPA and FDOPA are "qualitatively" similar, and it is therefore reasonable to infer that the human body will also "handle" FDOPA similarly. FDOPA may be considered as a "functional biomarker" of the presynaptic dopaminergic system in normal subjects and in patients with movement disorders such as PD. The report also pointed out several limitations.
L1766: The SGE1 agreed with the sponsor image interpretation for 41/45 (91%) subjects. The SGE1 further stated that images were relatively easy to read and that he does not know of any disadvantages compared to the currently marketed US imaging drug.
L1767: The SGE2 preferred to read the black-and-white images and agreed with the sponsor image interpretation for 42/45 (93%) subjects. He further stated that there are some advantages with FDOPA compared to the currently marketed US imaging drug, such as: shorter uptake, no need for thyroid blocking, better resolution, lower radiation dose / less radiation exposure and less limitation of other drug used.
L1768: These 45 patients were followed-up for up to 2 years by the same referring physician for the final diagnosis. The physician also had knowledge of the F-DOPA scan results. This follow-up clinical diagnosis is available in 36 out of 45 patients. Out of 36, there were 31 positive PET scans and 5 negative PET scans. The remaining 9 subjects did not return for the follow-up evaluation. These 9 patients were all negative PET scans. The sponsor has called the results of these evaluations at the follow-up as standard of truth (SOT). The truth standard was the diagnosis done by the referring physician which also included access to the FDOPA imaging results. This fact renders the data difficult to interpret.
L1769: In this report, evaluations at the follow-up visit are considered a reference standard for visualization, not truth standard for PS disease. In this report, FDOPA image outcomes were compared to a reference clinical diagnostic standard of "PS" or "non-PS". 31 patients (86%) were diagnosed positive (+) for PD; 5 (14%) were diagnosed negative (-) at the 2nd visit by the same neurologist who was the referring physician. .
L1770: Table 1 below shows the appropriate visualization parameters, positive percent agreement and negative percent agreement of the FDOPA image results with the reference clinical diagnostic standard, defined as follows:
L1771: Positive percent agreement represents the percent of patients with abnormal (positive) FDOPA images among all the patients with a clinical diagnostic reference standard of PS. The sample size for this agreement measure in this study is 31. (The sponsor has called it Sensitivity).
L1772: The negative percent agreement represents the percent of patients with normal (negative) FDOPA images among the patients with a non-PS clinical diagnostic reference standard. The sample size for this agreement measure in this study is 5. (The sponsor has called it Specificity).

L1776: The SGE findings does not impact the findings already documented in the statistical reviews. The data provided for negative agreement are not adequate to draw any conclusions due to very small sample size.
L1777: 2. INTRODUCTION
L1778: 2.1 Background
L1779: The safety and efficacy of FDOPA (short-lived radiopharmaceutical used in Positron Emission Tomography (PET)) were evaluated in one single-center, single-arm study (Study #17): 51 subjects were screened, 45 adult subjects with suspected parkinsonian syndromes (PS) were enrolled, and completed the study. There were 3 blinded independent readers. The sponsor stated that no reader training or instructions were provided and it was based on the experience of independent- blinded read by (2) neurologist and (1) physicist.
L1780: All three readers appeared to be qualified (based on experience) and interpreted the images independently. Suggestive evidence of any collaborative image interpretation was not observed. The standard of truth diagnosis was established by a neurologist based on clinical follow up (clinical response to treatment), typically the original referring physician who did not otherwise participate in the study.
L1781: This resubmission of NDA 200655 for Fluorodopa F 18 (FDOPA) consists of a response to FDA's Complete Response letter given to the applicant in 2013, in terms of submission of results from a new clinical trial (#17) that the applicant conducted. Details of previous communications between the applicant and FDA as well as the information about this drug can be found in the primary statistical review.
L1782: The safety and efficacy of FDOPA (short-lived radiopharmaceutical used in Positron Emission Tomography (PET)) were evaluated in one single-center, single-arm study (Study #17). For Study 17: 51 adult subjects were screened, 45 subjects with suspected Parkinsonian Syndromes (PS) were enrolled, and 45 completed the study. These 45 patients were studied under an IND expanded access protocol. There was no patient selection process, no consecutive patients or healthy volunteers. The patients were referred for imaging because the diagnosis was unclear and the patient was given a dose of carbidopa before the scan to improve image quality. The sponsor stated that no reader training or instructions were provided and it was based on the experience of independent- blinded read by (2) neurologist and (1) physicist. These 45 patients were then clinically followed for up to two years for clinical outcome. Three independent readers interpreted the PET scans to evaluate the shape and size of the striatum. The primary imaging endpoint was either positive or negative for dopaminergic neuron loss. The Sponsor stated that any changes in size or shape would be considered significant (PS). The software program used by the Sponsor was not for imaging analysis interpretation, but rather for image display.
L1783: These 45 patients were followed-up for up to 2 years by the same referring physician for the final diagnosis. The physician also had knowledge of the F-DOPA scan results. This follow-up clinical diagnosis is available in 36 out of 45 patients. Out of 36, there were 31 positive PET scans and 5 negative PET scans. The remaining 9 subjects did not return for the follow-up evaluation. These 9 patients were all negative PET scans. The sponsor has called the results of these evaluations at the follow-up as standard of truth (SOT). The truth standard was the
L1784: diagnosis done by the referring physician which also included access to the FDOPA imaging results. This fact renders the data difficult to interpret.
L1785: In this report, evaluations at the follow-up visit are considered a reference standard for visualization, not truth standard for PS disease. In this report, FDOPA image outcomes were compared to a reference clinical diagnostic standard of "PS" or "non-PS". 31 patients (86%) were diagnosed positive (+) for PD; 5 (14%) were diagnosed negative (-) at the 2nd visit by the same neurologist blinded to NDA readings.
L1786: The reference clinical diagnostic standard for "PS" consisted of the following diagnoses: Parkinson's disease (PD), Gait Freeze, Cortical Basal Ganglion Degeneration (CBD), Pisa Syndrome, multiple system atrophy (MSA), and progressive supra-nuclear palsy (PSP). The applicant considered that these conditions have been associated with the loss of dopaminergic neurons. FDOPA imaging was not designed to distinguish among the conditions. The reference clinical diagnostic standard for "non-PS", according to the applicant, consisted of an essential tremor (ET) diagnosis, or other non-PS diagnosis (Dopamine Responsive Dystonia (DRD), Subject without Evidence of Dopaminergic Deficit (SWEDD)). Sixty-five to 100 minutes after FDOPA administration by intravenous injection (150-185 MBq, 4.05-5.0 mCi), subjects underwent PET scan and attenuation correction.
L1787: The primary end point for this study was the visualization of dopaminergic neurons in the striatum expressed as a binary variable: "loss" / "no loss." "Loss" of dopaminergic neurons in the striatum was based upon the reader's judgment of what a "no loss" image looks like, not upon a prior scan in the same patient.
L1788: 2.2 Standard of Truth
L1789: The histopathological examination at autopsy is the gold standard in establishing the diagnosis of movement disorders including PD. Currently and historically, the next best tool to autopsy in establishing a diagnosis is the one made by a movement disorder specialist (a neurologist with additional specialized training in movement disorders). It is the belief of some that the diagnosis of PD particularly in its early stages may be difficult to make.
L1790: David J. Brooks stated in an article – "Imaging Approaches to Parkinson Disease" (Imperial College, London, April 2010; Journal of Nuclear Medicine)
L1791: "The definitive diagnosis of idiopathic Parkinson disease (PD) requires histologic demonstration of intraneuronal Lewy body inclusions in the substantia nigra compacta, and such demonstration is clearly impractical during life....Clinicopathologic studies suggest that when established PD is diagnosed according to current U.K. Brain Bank criteria, there is a 90% concordance between expert clinical impression and the presence of nigral Lewy bodies.
L1792: In early PD, however, the full triad of clinical symptoms and signs (rest tremor, bradykinesia, and rigidity) may not yet be manifested. Differential diagnoses include benign essential or dystonic tremors and parkinsonism associated with exogenous agents such as dopamine receptor-blocking drugs. These conditions are not associated with nigral degeneration or striatal dopamine deficiency. In up to 15% of cases labeled as early PD by investigators, imaging has shown normal dopamine terminal function suggestive of an alternative diagnosis. Given this
L1793: statistic, the ability to noninvasively detect altered nigral structure or striatal dopamine terminal function potentially provides valuable tools that can help increase diagnostic specificity for dopamine-deficient parkinsonian syndromes and rationalize management decisions at initial stages of disease.”
L1794: 2.3 A Summary of FDA Review of F-DOPA in the year 2000
L1795: A literature search and written reviews for F-DOPA as part of developing approval procedures under Section 121 of 1997 FDA Modernization Act (FDAMA) was performed the year 2000. The included:
L1796: • Pharmacology/Toxicology – Adebayo Laniyonu, Ph.D.• Biopharmaceutics – Alfredo Sancho, Ph.D.• Clinical– Ramesh Raman, M.D.
L1797: The review technically began in winter of 1999 and the findings were presented in the public meeting of July 28, 2000. A summary of the conclusions cited by the team based on extensive literature search in the year 2000 is given below:
L1798: • The metabolic pathways for endogenous dopamine, the therapeutic drug LDOPA and FDOPA are "qualitatively" similar, and it is therefore reasonable to infer that the human body will also "handle" FDOPA similarly.• Alteration in the DA system (amongst others) due to neurodegeneration is the pathophysiological hallmark of movement disorders, including PD. PET imaging with FDOPA, based on the known mechanism of action, reflects the status of the dopaminergic system and, therefore, any alteration in its uptake reflects an abnormality in this system.• It is suffice to summarize that FDOPA targets the presynaptic dopaminergic sites in diseases such as PD. The extent of the correlation between imaging and pathology in movement disorders such as PD, is questionable due to methodological and technological limitations.• Based on the search and the literature data, the clinical report concluded that the mechanism of action of FDOPA-PET, in that it targets the dopaminergic system can be established. In this context, FDOPA may be considered as a "functional biomarker" of the presynaptic dopaminergic system in normal subjects and in patients with movement disorders such as PD. The clinical meaning of these alterations (either between normal versus abnormal or within abnormal) has not been established.• Although there were no data that could be considered for efficacy approval, the scientific information so generated from this database was found to be helpful in the
L1799: understanding of FDOPA-PET from a global perspective, i.e., in research setting and in clinical investigations. This scientific information may be regarded as a foundation upon which further clinical studies may be based.
L1800: • Issues and Limitations on Methodology, Technology &amp; Image Analyses
L1801: There are technology and methodology concerns that are generally related to the PET imaging. These are "innate" to most of the agents that use PET. These are applicable to FDOPA-PET and they do play a role in these assessments as they affect several of the findings. Some of the important issues/concerns related to the techniques are:
L1802: 1. Reproducibility2. The Analysis of FDOPA-PET data- Methodological Issues3. Instrumentation Related- Technological Issues4. Image Interpretation Issues- ROI approach, Partial Volume Effects, Qualitative v/s Quantitative Reads5. Effects of Drugs (AAAD inhibitor, COMT inhibitor)
L1803: The findings from the literature are at best "hypotheses" generating and the articles/ studies cannot be pooled in a fashion that can be validated for a claim. The assessment of "lack of robustness" in the existing scientific literature is independent of the concerns on the methodology or technology that was used. As mentioned above, none of the articles met the required criteria that rendered them adequate enough that the data could be validated. Although these articles are contributory in providing valuable scientific information, the existing data is not robust, per se, in achieving a claim.
L1804: 2.4 Comments by FDA Inspection Team
L1805: "The single pivotal study for this NDA was audited at good clinical practice (GCP) inspection of the sponsor-clinical investigator site. No significant GCP deficiencies were observed and a Form FDA 483 was not issued. All three readers appeared to be qualified (based on experience) and interpreted the images independently. Suggestive evidence of any collaborative image interpretation was not observed. The standard of truth diagnosis was established by a neurologist based on clinical follow up (clinical response to treatment), typically the original referring physician who did not otherwise participate in the study.
L1806: The overall study conduct appeared GCP-compliant, including oversight of study conduct. All audited data were adequately verifiable against source records and case report forms (CRFs). The data from the inspected site appear reliable as reported in the NDA."
L1807: 2.5 Comments by SGE1 Dr. Henry D. Royal, M.D., June 27, 2016
L1808: In a teleconference meeting held on Monday, June 27, 2016 with SGE 1 Dr. Henry D. Royal, M.D., Mallinckrodt Institute of Radiology regarding [F-18] Fluorodopa (FDOPA) Injection to discuss the FDA Divisional Assignment dated May 31, 2016 of the PET images, the SGE1 made the following points:
L1809: (1) SGE1 stated that the images for Patient # (b) (6) were not ideal to read because the target to background contrast was decreased.(2) SGE1 stated that the images were relatively easy to read. Regarding the proposed labeling, Section 2.8: Image Interpretation, the SGE stated those instructions are adequate and in practice, a standard work station would suffice and an image software program would not be necessary.(3) The SGE1 disagreed with the Sponsor image interpretations for the following:i) Patient # (b) (6): SGE interpretation – Normal. Sponsor interpretation – Abnormal.ii) Patient # (b) (6): SGE interpretation – Abnormal (because black and white images differed from the color images). Sponsor interpretation - Normal. SGE further stated regarding patient # (b) (6) that he had a hard time convincing himself that the black-and-white (BW) images that were provided matched the color images. He said that he would just ask the submitter to confirm that the BW and color images for Patient / Case (b) (6) are correct.
L1810: However, the SGE1 agreed with the Sponsor image interpretations for the other patients.
L1811: (4) The SGE1 stated he does not know of any disadvantages compared to the currently marketed US imaging drug.
L1812: 2.6 Comments by SGE2 Dr., Peter Herscovitch, M.D., NIH, July 11, 2016
L1813: (1) The SGE2 preferred to read the black-and-white images rather than the color images.(2) The SGE2 stated a few images had higher background which was not ideal to read because the scan intensity was scaled to the maximum.(3) The SGE2 stated that the images were relatively easy to read.(4) The SGE2 agreed with most of the Sponsor image interpretations and disagreed with 3 out of the 45 black-and-white images.(5) The SGE2 stated there are some advantages with FDOPA compared to the currently marketed US imaging drug, such as:i) shorter uptakeii) no need for thyroid blockingiii) better resolutioniv) lower radiation dose / less radiation exposure andv) less limitation of other drug used.
L1814: 3. STATISTICAL EVALUATION
L1815: The safety and efficacy of FDOPA (short-lived radiopharmaceutical used in Positron Emission Tomography (PET)) were evaluated in one single-center, single-arm study (Study #17). For Study 17: 51 adult subjects were screened, 45 subjects with suspected Parkinsonian Syndromes (PS) were enrolled, and 45 completed the study. These 45 patients were studied under an IND expanded access protocol.
L1816: (b) (6)At the onset of PD and related diseases, the patients will have a loss of dopaminergic neurons in the range of 45 to 50%. This loss will be reflected in the PET scan based on the reduced uptake of Fluorodopa F 18 in the striatum. Patients with Parkinson's or related disease (Parkinsonism) will indicate a loss of dopaminergic neurons in the striatum depending upon the severity of the disease (a reduced uptake of Fluorodopa F 18 in the putamen or putamen and caudate unilaterally or bilaterally with the loss of dopaminergic neurons). Patients with essential tremor will have no dopaminergic neuronal loss and the PET scans will be normal. (b) (6)
L1817: . Patients with confirmed Parkinson's disease are treated differently from patients with essential tremor. Therefore, a differential diagnosis for patients with parkinsonian syndromes is essential for the proper management of these two diseases.
L1818: Among the 45 patients in Study # 17, 29% (13/45) were female and the average age was 58.6 years (standard deviation = 13.1). The baseline clinical diagnoses consisted of: probable PD, possible PD, possible ET, and other diagnoses. In this study, FDOPA image outcomes were compared to a reference clinical diagnostic standard of "PS" or "non-PS".
L1819: The primary end point for this study was the visualization of dopaminergic neurons in the striatum expressed as a binary variable: "loss" / "no loss." "Loss" of dopaminergic neurons in the striatum was based upon the reader's judgment of what a "no loss" image looks like, not upon a prior scan in the same patient.
L1820: There were three blinded independent readers. The sponsor stated that no reader training or instructions were provided and it was based on the experience of independent- blinded read by (2) neurologist and (1) physicist. FDA Inspection Team stated that "all three readers appeared to be qualified (based on experience) and interpreted the images independently. Suggestive evidence of any collaborative image interpretation was not observed. The standard of truth diagnosis was established by a neurologist based on clinical follow up (clinical response to treatment), typically the original referring physician who did not otherwise participate in the study. Overall, study conduct appeared to be adequately consistent with the study description presented in the NDA. All audited data were adequately verifiable against source records and CRFs. The data from this CI site appear reliable as reported in the NDA."

L1849: sample size for this agreement measure in this study is 5, which is too small to draw any conclusions. The sponsor has called it Specificity.
