L1875: 1. EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
L1876: The standard of truth (SOT) for the sponsor's original submission was each patient's clinical diagnosis at the time of referral for a Fluorodopa F 18 PET scan as the standard of truth for measuring the test's performance characteristics. This standard was not considered adequate and clinically meaningful in the CR letter. The applicant was advised by DMIP to conduct a new study to address clinical deficiencies cited in the CR letter.
L1877: The sponsor conducted and provided the result of a new prospective clinical study (Study # 17). The standard of truth (SOT) for this study was follow-up by the referring physician up to 1 year and in some cases up to 2 years to determine SOT. This was based on advice in the CR letter – “obtain the standard of reference (follow-up clinical diagnosis) in both Fluorodopa F 18 PET positive patients and Fluorodopa F 18 PET negative patients.” There are sources of bias in determining the SOT. The final diagnosis was made by the same referring physician who also had knowledge of F-DOPA scan results. The entrance to the study was limited to patients who were willing to pay for this diagnostic method. The SOT could not be determined due to lack of follow-up response for scan negative patients (7/13 or 54%).
L1878: The study (#17) enrolled 45 subjects referred by neurologists based on the parkinsonian syndrome. Images were evaluated by three separate onsite readers. Each reader evaluated the scans and reported the result as either positive or negative. Visual assessment of the striatal region in a Fluorodopa F 18 PET scan provided either a positive (Striatal Dopaminergic Neuronal Loss [SDNL] present) or negative (no SDNL present) result. The visual assessment was validated by follow up studies (SOT) with the referring physicians. All 3 readers agreed on 44 out of 45 scans (98%). Two (2) out of 3 readers agreed on all 45 out of 45 scans (100%). In the study # 15/16 submitted in the original submission, there was no variability among three scan readers with regards to the visual assessment of the PET scans. The three blinded PET scan readers were also blinded to the physicians' diagnosis.
L1879: The total number of patients referred to The Feinstein Institute for Medical Research for this study was 45. Out of 45 patients, 32 had positive PET scans and 13 had negative PET scans. Out of 32 positive PET 30 patients were followed up and had positive PET scan results. Two patients who had positive PET scan results have a pending SOT. Out of the 13 negative scans, 6 patients were followed up and had SOTs determined (negative) and the other 7 are pending. The sensitivity for 30 patients that were followed-up was 100% (95% CI (88, 100%)) and specificity for 6 out of 13 patients who had follow-up SOT was also 100% with 95% CI (54-100%). In the previously submitted study # 15/16 with the primary endpoint of the visualization of the striatal region using Fluorodopa F 18 PET, there were no false positive or false negative scan results considering physician's clinical diagnosis as SOT, resulting in 100% sensitivity and 100% specificity.
L1880: This study also has limitations related to establishing SOT and substantial number of patients in the negative PET scans group (7 out of 13 – 54%) did not have SOT determined due to non-response during follow-up telephone calls.
L1881: This study # 17 provides additional clinical evidence. Based on the results from the collective clinical evidence (results of study # 15/16 and 21 together with study # 17) and the sponsor
L1882: following DMIP guidance, there is some clinical utility for F-DPOA PET imaging in a selected patient population.
L1883: The division has planned an SGE review the FDOPA PET images from the 45 patients in the clinical trial # 17 to confirm that they are consistent with the clinical presentation of condition by the sponsor.
L1884: 2. INTRODUCTION
L1885: Fluorodopa, also known as F-DOPA, is a fluorinated form of Levodopa (L-DOPA) primarily synthesized as its fluorine-18 isotopologue for use as a radiotracer in positron emission tomography (PET). Positron emission tomography (PET) is a nuclear medical imaging technique that produces a three-dimensional image or picture of functional processes in the body. PET technology using short-lived radioisotopes is a state of the art imaging technology.
L1886: Fluorodopa F 18 Injection belongs to the aromatic amino acid class of compounds. Once injected, Fluorodopa F 18 will cross the blood brain barrier to reach the striatal region where it undergoes decarboxylation to form fluorodopamine. As in the case of L-DOPA, the storage of the fluorodopamine in the striatum will depend upon the condition of the striatal region. Therefore, the storage of fluorodopamine F 18 in the striatum will depend upon the extent of neuronal loss. If the striatal region has no loss of dopaminergic neurons, Fluorodopa F 18 uptake in that region will be normal. However, if there is dopaminergic neuronal loss in the striatum, the uptake of fluorodopamine will be reduced depending upon the extent of the neuronal loss. According to the sponsor, (b) (4)
L1887: Fluorodopa F 18 follows the same biological pathway as L-DOPA and the drug is excreted through the bladder in a similar way. The sponsor states that the safety and effectiveness of L-DOPA is well established.
L1888: This radiotracer (F-DOPA) has already been approved by the European Union a long time back as a diagnostic imaging for the differential diagnosis of parkinsonian syndromes. Fluorodopa F 18 PET has been used for the last 20 years around the world. The sponsor points out that Fluorodopa F 18 PET was approved in France in 2010 followed by several countries in the European Union. In Europe several pharmaceutical companies have received marketing authority for Fluorodopa F 18 Injection.
L1889: 2.1 Overview
L1890: The safety and efficacy of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection was presented by the FDA reviewers at the public meeting conducted by FDA on July 28, 2000. It was pointed out that the available literature data were insufficient to support efficacy in any patient population. A pre-IND meeting was held in 2008 and original NDA was submitted on 10/29/2009 with a PDUFA start date of 1/21/2010 (date user fee waiver granted). Refuse to File letter was issued on 3/19/2010.
L1891: The sponsor re-submitted a 505(b)(2) NDA after refuse to file. In this NDA (# 200655), the applicant submitted information on 9 studies. All these studies were post-hoc evaluation including primary clinical study # 15 conducted by the sponsor. Eight (8) out of 9 studies were from literature publications. Three (3) out of 9 studies were found relevant for evaluating diagnostic efficacy (performance characteristics) of Fluorodopa F 18 (F-DOPA) injection, but all had deficiencies. Two studies (# 15 &amp; # 16) were retrospective, single center, and with clinical diagnosis (after Parkinson's drug treatment response or non-response) of "Possible PD", "Possible non-PD" and "Suspected non-PD" as Standard of Truth (SOT). Using this standard of truth, the applicant concluded the performance characteristics of 100% sensitivity, and 100% specificity for the study. A protocol for study # 15 was not submitted to the Agency for review.
L1892: Study #16 was a continuation of the clinical study # 15 by the applicant to further evaluate the patients that exhibited Parkinsonian symptoms but had a negative fluorodopa F 18 scan. The applicant states that the 27 patients with negative scans were followed for 2-4 years. A further amendment (April 5, 2013) by the applicant listed only eight patients out of 27 F-18 FDOPA PET negative patients from Study #15 were followed to obtain patient's final clinical diagnosis. Clinical data from such a small number of patients and with so much missing data do not provide a reliable assessment of the performance characteristics of fluorodopa F 18.
L1893: Study # 21 was exploratory, single center with all study patients already diagnosed with PD (not the target patient population), and it is unclear how specificity and sensitivity were calculated.
L1894: The clinical value in terms of providing differential diagnosis of patients with parkinsonian symptoms or proving supplemental value to physician's clinical diagnosis was not established in these retrospective studies.
L1895: A complete response letter was issued by FDA on 10-22-2013. Deficiencies cited included lack of adequate and well controlled clinical trials; significant deficiencies in the referenced literature studies and in the studies conducted by the sponsor in support of this application.
L1896: The data submitted in this application were not sufficient for evaluating the (b) (4) usefulness of Fluorodopa F 18 PET in the proposed patient population. The details of the CR letter related to clinical/statistical issues are given in Appendix A.
L1897: Feinstein Institute was approved by FDA annually to continue using Fluorodopa F 18 Injection under an Expanded Access IND 78861.
L1898: The sponsor submitted response to FDA for the "Complete Response letter dated 10/22/2013 for the NDA 200655" on 12-15-2015. The sponsor conducted a prospective clinical study based on the IRB/FDA approved protocol to evaluate the sensitivity, specificity, and usefulness of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection PET studies for patients with suspected cases of Parkinsonian syndromes under the FDA approved Expanded Access IND. The sponsor pointed out that the Fluorodopa Expanded Access IND was not reimbursed by CMS and therefore depended upon patients who were willing to pay for the clinical studies. There was no obligation for the patients to return to the referring physician for the follow up SOT after a particular period of time. This resulted in assessing SOT for some patients. All patients in the study had signed consent forms and the consent for the follow up for the SOT.
L1899: The focus of this review is response from the sponsor related to CR letter issued by FDA.
L1900: 2.1.1 Indication and Doses
L1901: Proposed indication: Fluorodopa F 18 Injection is a (b) (4) used in Positron Emission Tomography (PET) to visualize dopaminergic (u) (4) in the striatum (b) (4) adult patients with suspected parkinsonian syndromes (PS). (b) (4) Fluorodopa F 18 PET is an adjunct to other diagnostic investigations.
L1902: The proposed dosage form is injection: Recommended dose for adults is (b) (4) 5.0 mCi ( (b) (4) 0.185 GBq), Route of Administration is intravenous. At the Feinstein Institute, subjects were dosed with 150 mg of carbidopa approximately 60-90 minutes prior to administration of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection dose between 4.05 -5.0 mCi to generate a well resolved image. Fluorodopa F18 is not recommended for pediatric patients. Fluorodopa F 18 is an imaging agent and is not for treatment purposes.
L1903: 2.1.2 Identified Study in the review
L1904: In the complete response, FDA suggested to conduct a new prospective study that could potentially address issues in the CR letter. The sponsor completed a clinical study (study # 17) based on the IRB/FDA approved protocol. The title of the study is:
L1905: STUDY# 17: Clinical Study of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection Conducted by the Feinstein Institute for Medical Research, 350 Community Drive, Manhasset, New York 11030
L1906: 2.2 Data Sources
L1907: Electronic datasets were submitted as a result of Information Request sent to the sponsor on 18 Feb. 2016. The data were submitted in SAS xpt format on 25 Feb. 2016.
L1908: The NDA in eCTD located at: \\CDSESUB4\NONECTD\NDA200655\5967723
L1909: 3. STATISTICAL EVALUATION
L1910: The sponsor conducted a new prospective clinical study (study # 17, n=45) based on the IRB/FDA approved protocol to evaluate the sensitivity, specificity, and usefulness of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection PET studies with suspected cases of Parkinsonian syndromes. The sponsor states that Fluorodopa F 18 PET (b) (4)
L1911: At the onset of PD and related diseases, the patients will have a loss of dopaminergic neurons in the range of 45 to 50%. This loss will be reflected in the PET scan based on the reduced uptake of Fluorodopa F 18 in the striatum. Patients with Parkinson's or related disease (Parkinsonism) will indicate a loss of dopaminergic neurons in the striatum depending upon the severity of the disease (a reduced uptake of Fluorodopa F 18 in the putamen or putamen and caudate unilaterally or bilaterally with the loss of dopaminergic neurons). Patients with essential tremor will have no dopaminergic neuronal loss and the PET scans will be normal. (b) (4)
L1912: Patients with confirmed Parkinson's disease are treated differently from patients with essential tremor. Therefore, a differential diagnosis for patients with parkinsonian syndromes is essential for the proper management of these two diseases.
L1913: 3.1 Data and Analysis Quality
L1914: There was one prospective study (#17) in response to CR letter conducted by the applicant (Feinstein Inst.). The applicant submitted patient level data in proper SAS xpt format.
L1915: 3.2 Evaluation of Efficacy
L1916: 3.2.1 Objective
L1917: • To determine the sensitivity, specificity, and usefulness of Fluorodopa PET for the differential diagnosis of parkinsonian syndromes.• To determine the inter-rater-reliability of the readers of the PET scan.• To determine how quantitative measures for PET correlate with PD diagnosis.• To monitor safety by visual observation of patients.
L1918: 3.2.2 Primary Criteria for evaluation
L1919: The primary efficacy measure was visualization of the size and shape of the putamen/caudate in the striatum. The visual assessment of PET scan images was done by three qualified readers as either positive (loss of dopaminergic neurons - demonstrated a reduced uptake of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection) or negative (no loss of dopaminergic neurons - showed a normal uptake of Fluorodopa F 18). The patients were followed up one to two years past the date of their PET
L1920: scan in order to establish the standard of truth (SOT). In some cases the SOT was received earlier than a year based on the decision by the referring physician. The protocol defined plan was to collect SOT's from 20-30 patients to establish sensitivity and specificity for this study.
L1921: 3.2.3 Inclusion Criteria
L1922: All patients in this study were referred by Neurologists and exhibited at least one of the cardinal features of parkinsonian syndromes (bradykinesia, rigidity, tremor, and/or gait disorder). The referring physicians were not sure whether the patients have Parkinsonism (Loss of dopaminergic neurons in the striatum) or essential tremor (no loss of dopaminergic neurons). The patients were referred directly to the patient scheduling center of The Feinstein Institute for Medical Research. No specific populations have been studied in this clinical study.
L1923: 3.2.4 Number of Subjects
L1924: The total number of patients referred to The Feinstein Institute for Medical Research for this study was 45. Out of 45 patients, 32 had positive PET scans and 13 had negative PET scans. Out of 32 positive PET 30 patients were followed up and had positive PET scan results. Two patients who had positive PET scan results have a pending SOT. Out of the 13 negative scans, 6 patients were followed up and had and had SOTs determined (negative) and the other 7 are pending. Several attempts were made to collect SOT's from the referring physicians for the 7 patients, but were not successful. The sponsor stated that it was difficult to collect SOT's from patients with negative PET scans. Protocol defined plan was to collect SOT's from 20-30 patients to establish sensitivity and specificity for this study.
L1925: 3.2.5 Demographic and Baseline Characteristics
L1926: The study population for efficacy in Study 17 includes 45 adults. The characteristics of subjects are given in the following Table 2.

L1929: 3.2.6 Primary Endpoint
L1930: The primary endpoint for this study was the visualization of either the loss or no loss of dopaminergic neurons in the striatum. Visualization was based on the assessment of the shape and size of the putamen/caudate region. The clinical endpoint is the differentiation of patients with Parkinsonism (IPD, CBD, PSP, etc.) from essential tremor and related diseases (DRD, SWEDD, etc.) based on the PET scan results. Patients with essential tremor are expected to have normal (negative) PET scans indicating that their clinical symptoms were not in fact due to IPD or related diseases. Fluorodopa F18 PET is not indicated to differentiate between PD, MSA, or PSP.
L1931: 3.2.7 Readers and Image Evaluation
L1932: The PET scans were evaluated visually by 3 readers. Visual interpretation of the scans were recorded either as positive (loss of dopaminergic neurons) or negative (no loss of dopaminergic neurons). The reliability of the visual detection was assessed based on the interpretation of the PET scan by the 3 readers. A reduced uptake of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection in the putamen or both putamen and caudate, unilaterally or bilaterally indicates the loss of dopaminergic neurons. Any loss of dopaminergic neurons in the striatum will appear as a reduction in the size of the crescent-shaped region in the PET scan.
L1933: 3.3 Results and Conclusions
L1934: There were 45 patients enrolled in this study. Patients were referred by neurologists based on the parkinsonian syndrome. Images were evaluated by three separate onsite readers. Each reader evaluated the scans and reported the result as either positive or negative. A positive scan shows a reduced uptake of Fluorodopa F 18 by the putamen or putamen/caudate, either unilaterally or bilaterally, depending upon the severity of the disease. Negative scans were characterized based on the uptake of Fluorodopa F 18 by the caudate and putamen bilaterally. Negative scans show a uniform uptake, as seen by the crescent shaped caudate and putamen.
L1935: Visual assessment of the striatal region in a Fluorodopa F 18 PET scan provided either a positive (Striatal Dopaminergic Neuronal Loss [SDNL] present) or negative (no SDNL present) result. The visual assessment was validated by follow up studies (SOT) with the referring physicians.
L1936: Out of the 45 subjects, 7 subjects with negative scans did not go back to their referring physician to record the SOT. Attempts were made 3 times in each case to get the SOT from these patients but there was no response. Supporting documents were included in their chart. Two patients who had positive PET scan results have a pending SOT In the case of PD patients the response was fast.
L1937: Under Parkinsonism, the main group is Idiopathic Parkinson's Disease (IPD) or generally called PD. The second group is Atypical Parkinson's (APD). Based on the experience in the medical management of these patients, some of these patients under APD are grouped as Cortical Basal
L1938: Ganglion Degeneration (CBD), Progressive Supra-Nuclear Palsy (PSP), Multiple System Atrophy (MSA), Gait Freezing, etc. by the physicians. In rare cases where patients have a normal PET scan, the physicians have listed MSA along with essential tremor. In the case of patients with parkinsonian syndromes and a normal PET scan, the physicians have grouped them as Essential Tremor (ET), Dopamine Responsive Dystonia (DRD), Subject without Evidence of Dopaminergic Deficit (SWEDD,) etc. Therefore, the referring physicians have used these classifications when reporting the SOT.
L1939: The details are provided in Table 3. The results of visual assessment of the striatal region in a Fluorodopa F 18 PET scan by 3 readers are:
L1940: • All 3 readers agreed on 44 out of 45 scans (98%)• Two (2) out of 3 readers agreed on all 45 out of 45 scans (100%).

L1949: Sensitivity and specificity:
L1950: Sensitivity and specificity were computed for those with a known SOT. There were four categories of sensitivity and specificity - calculations depending on whether MSA was considered a dopamine loss condition and or whether freezing gait was considered a dopamine loss condition. Category 1 corresponds to sensitivity and specificity when freezing gait is considered a dopamine loss and MSA is not considered a dopamine loss. Category 2 is the computation of sensitivity and specificity when freezing gait is considered a dopamine loss and MSA is also considered a dopamine loss. Category 3 is the calculation of sensitivity and specificity when freezing gait is not considered a dopamine loss and MSA is also not considered a dopamine loss. Finally, Category 4 is the calculation of sensitivity and specificity when freezing gait is not considered a dopamine loss and MSA is considered a dopamine loss. The results are provided in the following Table 4:

L1954: **Negative Predictive Value
L1955: The point estimates of the sensitivity ranged from 97% to 100% with 95% CI ranging from 83 – 100%. The point estimates of the specificity ranged from 96% to 100% with 95% CI ranging from 42 – 100%. In the previously submitted study # 15 with the primary endpoint of the visualization of the striatal region using Fluorodopa F 18 PET, there were no false positive or false negative scan results considering physician’s clinical diagnosis as SOT, resulting in 100% sensitivity and 100% specificity.
L1956: 3.4 Evaluation of Safety
L1957: Applicant states that F 18 is a short-lived radioisotope, at the end of the study there is only trace amounts of radiation left in the brain and the patient has minimal radiation risk. There were no adverse events after the administration of Fluorodopa F 18 and there was no premature discontinuation from the study due to complication. There have been no safety concerns from over 20 years of use of the drug at the Feinstein Institute. This is a derivative of L-dopa, which has a known safety profile.
L1958: Patients in this study # 17 were monitored visually for any physical discomfort. Patient charts did not indicate any safety issue. After the PET scans, all patients were followed up with a phone call within 24 hours to collect information regarding any possible adverse events. All patients responded to phone call, except one patient. Among the responding patients (44/45) there were five cases in which the patients had minor events. The Principal Investigator (PI) contacted the patients to investigate the nature of the adverse events. In all cases, after discussing with the patients, the PI concluded that the minor adverse events were not caused by the administration of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection.
L1959: 4. FINDINGS IN SPECIAL/SUBGROUP POPULATIONS
L1960: 4.1 Gender, Race, Age, and Geographic Region
L1961: For the study # 17, findings were assessed for each of the primary variables by gender and age. The results indicated a general consistency across each stratification for each reader. There was no difference in the efficacy data obtained from the male and female group of patients either in this study.
L1962: Since Fluorodopa F 18 is used in different regions around the world, the subject population includes people with a wide range of ethnic backgrounds. Males and females of various ages, heights and weights were also included. According to the applicant, there is no indication that the drug behaves differently in any specific subpopulation. There were no observable differences in the PET images among subjects due to any type of characteristic other than disease state. As no adverse events have been reported, there is also no indication that Fluorodopa F 18 Injection would pose a risk to any subpopulation.
L1963: 4.2 Other Special/Subgroup Populations
L1964: Patients with a long term clinical history (over 5 years) were identified as a special subgroup of interest for the study # 17. Out of the 45 patients, there were 11 patients (10 male; 1 female) exceeding 5 years of disease duration. In 11 patients, 7 had a positive PET scan and 4 patients had a negative scan. The SOT was received for all 7 patients who had a positive scan and the SOT confirmed PD (sensitivity = 100%). Four patients with a negative scan have pending SOT's (specificity could not be computed).
L1965: 5. SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS
L1966: 5.1 Statistical Issues and Collective Evidence
L1967: The sponsor provided the result of a new prospective clinical study (Study # 17). This study was conducted based on the IRB/FDA approved protocol to evaluate the sensitivity, specificity, and usefulness of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection PET studies for patients with suspected cases of Parkinsonian syndromes. The protocol was also to satisfy the physicians request to study parkinsonian patients under the FDA approved Expanded Access IND. Since the Fluorodopa Expanded Access IND was not reimbursed by CMS, the sponsor pointed out that they had to depend upon patients who were willing to pay for the clinical studies. There was no obligation for the patients to return to the referring physician for the follow up SOT after a particular period of time. There were limitations and sources of bias in this study.
L1968: This was a single center study and enrollment in the study was limited to patients who were willing to pay for the clinical studies and referred by their neurologist. Follow-up clinical diagnosis in both Fluorodopa F 18 PET positive patients and Fluorodopa F 18 PET negative patients was expected. The total number of patients referred to The Feinstein Institute for Medical Research for this study was 45. Out of 45 patients, 32 had positive PET scans and 13 had negative PET scans. Out of 32 positive PET 30 patients were followed up and had positive PET scan results. Two patients who had positive PET scan results have a pending SOT. Out of the 13 negative scans, 6 patients were followed up and had had SOTs determined (negative) and the other 7 are pending. The truth for negative patients was determined only for 6 patients out of 13 negative patients.
L1969: 5.2 Conclusions and Recommendations
L1970: The standard of truth (SOT) for the sponsor's original submission was each patient's clinical diagnosis at the time of referral for a Fluorodopa F 18 PET scan as the standard of truth for measuring the test's performance characteristics. This standard was not considered adequate and clinically meaningful in the CR letter. The supplicant was advised by DMIP to conduct a new study to address clinical deficiencies cited in the CR letter.
L1971: The sponsor conducted and provided the result of a new prospective clinical study (Study # 17). The standard of truth (SOT) for this study was follow-up by the referring physician up to 1 year and in some cases up to 2 years to determine SOT. This was based on advice in the CR letter – “obtain the standard of reference (follow-up clinical diagnosis) in both Fluorodopa F 18 PET positive patients and Fluorodopa F 18 PET negative patients.” There are sources of bias in determining the SOT. The final diagnosis was made by the same referring physician who also had knowledge of F-DOPA scan results. The entrance to the study was limited to patients who were willing to pay for this diagnostic method. The SOT could not be determined due to lack of follow-up response for scan negative patients (7/13 or 54%).
L1972: The study (#17) enrolled 45 subjects referred by neurologists based on the parkinsonian syndrome. Images were evaluated by three separate onsite readers. Each reader evaluated the scans and reported the result as either positive or negative. Visual assessment of the striatal
L1973: region in a Fluorodopa F 18 PET scan provided either a positive (Striatal Dopaminergic Neuronal Loss [SDNL] present) or negative (no SDNL present) result. The visual assessment was validated by follow up studies (SOT) with the referring physicians. All 3 readers agreed on 44 out of 45 scans (98%). Two (2) out of 3 readers agreed on all 45 out of 45 scans (100%). In the study # 15/16 submitted in the original submission, there was no variability among three scan readers with regards to the visual assessment of the PET scans. The three blinded PET scan readers were also blinded to the physicians' diagnosis.
L1974: The total number of patients referred to The Feinstein Institute for Medical Research for this study was 45. Out of 45 patients, 32 had positive PET scans and 13 had negative PET scans. Out of 32 positive PET 30 patients were followed up and had positive PET scan results. Two patients who had positive PET scan results have a pending SOT. Out of the 13 negative scans, 6 patients were followed up and had SOTs determined (negative) and the other 7 are pending. The sensitivity for 30 patients that were followed-up was 100% (95% CI (88, 100%)) and specificity for 6 out of 13 patients who had follow-up SOT was also 100% with 95% CI (54-100%). In the previously submitted study # 15/16 with the primary endpoint of the visualization of the striatal region using Fluorodopa F 18 PET, there were no false positive or false negative scan results considering physician's clinical diagnosis as SOT, resulting in 100% sensitivity and 100% specificity.
L1975: This study also has limitations related to establishing SOT and substantial number of patients in the negative PET scans group (7 out of 13 – 54%) did not have SOT determined due to non-response during follow-up telephone calls.
L1976: This study # 17 provides additional clinical evidence. Based on the results from the collective clinical evidence (results of study # 15/16 and 21 together with study # 17) and the sponsor following DMIP guidance, there is some clinical utility for F-DOPA PET imaging in a selected patient population.
L1977: The division has planned an SGE review the FDOPA PET images from the 45 patients in the clinical trial # 17 to confirm that they are consistent with the clinical presentation of condition by the sponsor.

L1986: Appendix A: Clinical/Statistical issues identified in CR letter and Recommendations
L1987: Your application contains no data from adequate and well controlled clinical trials. Significant deficiencies have been identified in the referenced literature studies and in the studies you have conducted in support of this application. The data submitted in your application are not sufficient for evaluating the diagnostic effectiveness and usefulness of Fluorodopa F 18 PET in the proposed patient population.
L1988: 1. The following deficiencies have been identified with clinical Study #15, which was conducted at the Feinstein Institute of Medical Research:a. The study is a retrospective, single center study.b. The study was not conducted according to a pre-specified protocol, and hence, definitions of the primary efficacy endpoint and the standard of truth that would normally be specified by a protocol, are not well-defined for the study.c. The study used each patient's clinical diagnosis at the time of referral for a Fluorodopa F 18 PET scan as the standard of truth for measuring the test's performance characteristics. We do not find such a standard to be clinically meaningful and do not agree with the cited diagnostic performance measurements of Fluorodopa F 18 as 100% for sensitivity and 100% for specificity.d. Of the 185 study patients, 158 patients had a pre-imaging clinical diagnosis of Parkinson's disease and were responding to Parkinson's disease medications. For these patients a Fluorodopa F 18 PET scan provides no additional clinically useful information. Deficiencies concerning the remaining 27 study patients are discussed below (Study #16).e. You have provided no documentation that Fluorodopa F 18 PET images have been read independently by three readers blinded to clinical information. Furthermore, inspection of the study site revealed that none of the 47 audited cases contained documentation that each patient's images were read by three blinded readers.2. Study #16 is a publication with the follow-up results of 27 Fluorodopa F 18 PET negative patients enrolled in Study #15. Study #16 has the following major deficiencies:a. Per your amendment dated April 5, 2013, only 8 of the 27 Fluorodopa F 18 PET negative patients from Study #15 were
L1989: followed for 2 to 4 years to obtain the patient's final clinical diagnosis. Clinical data from such a small number of patients do not provide a reliable assessment of the performance characteristics of Fluorodopa F 18 PET. Furthermore, inspection of the study site revealed that none of the subject files contained documentation (clinical follow-up confirmation) of the continued absence of classical Parkinson's Disease symptoms and signs.
L1990: b. The reasons for not obtaining follow up on the remaining 19 patients are not provided.3. Among the remaining literature publications in your application (Studies #17-23), only the publication by Eshuis et al. (Study #21) provides clinical data for the assessment of the efficacy of Fluorodopa F 18 as a diagnostic radiopharmaceutical. However, Study #21 does not provide substantial evidence of efficacy for the following reasons:a. The study is a small single center study and is exploratory in design.b. All study patients had a diagnosis of Parkinson's disease at the time of enrollment. The study excluded patients with atypical signs and patients not responding to Parkinson's disease medication. Therefore, the study does not evaluate a patient population that might benefit from a diagnostic imaging test.c. The definition of positive scans (mean standard uptake value in patients below two standard deviations of the mean in healthy controls) is an exploratory quantitative measure and its threshold is arbitrarily set.
L1991: To address these deficiencies, we recommend the following:
L1992: 1. Conduct an adequate and well-controlled clinical trial which might provide substantial evidence of effectiveness of Fluorodopa F 18 in the intended patient population. We encourage you to develop such a study using a pre-defined standard of reference for measuring the performance characteristics (sensitivity and specificity) of Fluorodopa F 18, a pre-specified statistical analysis plan, and image interpretations made by independent readers blinded to clinical information. Note in order to measure the sensitivity and specificity of Fluorodopa F 18, you need to obtain the standard of reference (follow-up clinical diagnosis) in both Fluorodopa F 18 PET positive patients and Fluorodopa F 18 PET negative patients. You should use as a standard of reference the clinical diagnosis made by a movement disorder specialist at a predefined time interval following the Fluorodopa F 18 PET scan.
L1993: 2. The study population must include subjects who might benefit from the Fluorodopa F 18 PET scan. Such patients could include those in whom Parkinsonian and essential tremor could not be differentiated on clinical grounds, those with suspected early onset of Parkinsonian syndrome, and/or those not responding to a Parkinson disease medication.
L1994: To facilitate the interpretation of the study's results, we recommend that you consider exclusion of subjects with features suggestive of multiple system atrophy or progressive supranuclear palsy, response to Parkinson's disease medication, a clinical history exceeding five years, and concomitant medication known or suspected of interacting with striatal uptake of Fluorodopa F 18.
L1995: 3. We reference the Good Clinical Practice (GCP) deficiencies identified during our inspection of your clinical study site and communicated to you on March 22, 2013. These deficiencies call into question the reliability of the clinical data in your application. It is necessary to conduct a study in compliance with GCP standards to ensure the quality and integrity of the data.

L2010: Executive Summary
L2011: This resubmission of NDA 200655 for Fluorodopa F 18 (FDOPA) consists of a response to FDA's Complete Response letter given to the applicant in 2013, in terms of submission of results from a new clinical trial (#17) that the applicant conducted. Details of previous communications between the applicant and FDA as well as the information about this drug can be found in the primary statistical review.
L2012: The safety and efficacy of FDOPA (short-lived radiopharmaceutical used in Positron Emission Tomography (PET)) were evaluated in one single-center, single-arm study (Study #17) that evaluated 45 adult patients with suspected parkinsonian syndromes (PS)). In this study, FDOPA image outcomes were compared to a reference clinical diagnostic standard of "PS" or "non-PS". The reference clinical diagnostic standard for "PS" consisted of the following diagnoses: Parkinson's disease (PD), multiple system atrophy (MSA), Gait Freeze, Cortical Basal Ganglion Degeneration (CBD), Pisa Syndrome, and progressive supra-nuclear palsy (PSP). The applicant considered that these conditions have been associated with the loss of dopaminergic neurons. FDOPA imaging was not designed to distinguish among the conditions. The reference clinical diagnostic standard for "non-PS", according to the applicant, consisted of an essential tremor (ET) diagnosis, or other non-PS diagnosis (Dopamine Responsive Dystonia (DRD), Subject without Evidence of Dopaminergic Deficit (SWEDD)). Sixty-five to 100 minutes after FDOPA administration by intravenous injection (150-185 MBq, 4.05-5.0 mCi), subjects underwent PET scan and attenuation correction.
L2013: The primary end point for this study was the visualization of dopaminergic neurons in the striatum expressed as a binary variable: "loss" / "no loss." "Loss" of dopaminergic neurons in the striatum was based upon the reader's judgment of what a "no loss" image looks like, not upon a prior scan in the same patient.
L2014: Limitations of this study are as follows: FDOPA images were evaluated by 3 on-site readers. It is not clear from the applicant's report whether these readers were blinded to clinical information or not; and whether these readers were independent or not. It is also not clear whether these readers had any other role in patient assessment or not. The reference clinical diagnostic standard is the clinical diagnoses established by a referring physician after at least 1 year of follow-up. This reference clinical diagnostic standard is available in 36 out of 45 patients in this study. The applicant calls this reference standard as standard of truth (SOT) which is not appropriate, neither are the applicant's calculations of sensitivity and specificity. These are not appropriate performance characteristics for this study, where the primary endpoint is visualization of dopaminergic neurons. Sensitivity and specificity are appropriate when the TRUTH standard (independent from imaging, for example autopsy results) is available. Here, in the study, we have reference standard for visualization, not truth standard for PS disease.
L2015: Among the 45 patients in Study # 17, 29% (13/45) were female and the average age was 58.6 years (standard deviation = 13.1); the baseline clinical diagnoses consisted of: probable PD, possible PD, possible ET, and other diagnoses. Table below shows the appropriate visualization parameters, positive percent agreement and negative percent agreement of the FDOPA image results with the reference clinical diagnostic standard, defined as follows.
L2016: Positive percent agreement represents the percent of patients with abnormal (positive) FDOPA images among all the patients with a clinical diagnostic reference standard of PS. The sample size for this agreement measure in this study is 31.
L2017: The negative percent agreement represents the percent of patients with normal (negative) FDOPA images among the patients with a non-PS clinical diagnostic reference standard. The sample size for this agreement measure in this study is 5, which is too small to draw any conclusions.
L2018: The applicant should have raised their sample-size to at least 200 patients, given the 20% non-response to reference clinical diagnostic standard and 14% “non-PS” patients.
L2019: Positive and Negative Percent Agreements for Study# 17
L2020: Positive percent agreement(% patients with an abnormal FDOPA imageamong patients with PS, N=31)Negative percent agreement(% patients with a normal FDOPA imageamong patients with non-PS, N=5)Reader 129/31=94%5/5=100%Reader 230/31=97%5/5=100%Reader 330/31=97%5/5=100%
L2021: The FDA is currently planning to have an SGE review the FDOPA images from this study to confirm that the applicant’s results are consistent with the clinical presentation of condition.
L2022: The effectiveness of FDOPA as a confirmatory test for monitoring disease progression or response to therapy has not been established.
L2023: The applicant collected Striatal-Occipital Ratio (SOR) in Study 17. Striatal Occipital Ratio is the ratio of radioactivity in the striatum (target) to that of the occipital cortex (background). These SOR values were not used for the visual interpretation of the PET scan results in this study. The clinical pharmacology reviewer’s analysis of the data showed that, on average, SOR differed as a function of disease. However, because there was considerable overlap between SORs in patients with Parkinson’s and healthy subjects, SOR cannot be used as an aid to scan interpretation.
L2024: This secondary reviewer recommends that the applicant be given, again, a complete response letter citing the limitations (clinical/statistical and compliance with good clinical practice (GCP)) of their clinical study #17.

L2037: 1. EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
L2038: In this NDA (# 200655), the applicant submitted information on 9 studies. All these studies are post-hoc evaluation including primary clinical study # 15 conducted by the sponsor. Eight (8) out of 9 studies are from literature publications. Three (3) out of 9 studies were found relevant for evaluating diagnostic efficacy (performance characteristics) of Fluorodopa F 18 (F-DOPA) injection, but all had deficiencies. Other 6 publications did not include any information relevant to the confirmation of performance characteristic characteristics of F-DOPA and not relevant to the confirmation of efficacy or safety of F-DOPA.
L2039: Studies 15 &amp; 16 are retrospective, single center, and with clinical diagnosis (after Parkinson's drug treatment response or non-response) as Standard of Truth (SOT) (lacking clinical utility).
L2040: Study # 15 was intended to validate the efficacy and safety of Fluorodopa F 18 PET as an imaging tool in the differential diagnosis of patients with parkinsonian symptoms. Clinical diagnosis information was provided by the referring physician. A protocol for study # 15 was not submitted to the Agency for review. The primary efficacy measure was visual assessment of PET Scan images by three qualified individuals as either positive (loss of dopaminergic neurons - demonstrated a reduced uptake of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection) or negative (no loss of dopaminergic neurons - showed a normal uptake of Fluorodopa F 18), and a follow-up of 27 patients with negative F-18 FDOPA PET for 2-4 years (study 16) who were enrolled in the study #15.
L2041: The three blinded PET scan readers were also blinded to the physicians' diagnosis. There was no variability among three scan readers with regards to the visual assessment of the PET scans. For every one of the patients that were initially classified as "possible PD (Parkinson's Disease)", a positive PET scan result was recorded and for each patient that was initially classified as "possible non-PD", a negative PET scan result was recorded. For each healthy control subject, a negative PET scan result was recorded. There were no false positive or false negative scan results considering physician's clinical diagnosis as SOT, resulting in 100% sensitivity and 100% specificity for each reader.
L2042: Study # 16 had additional follow-up information on 27 out of a total of 185 patients. These patients had clinical diagnosis of suspected non-PD and were FDOPA scan negative patients. These patients were followed-up for 2-4 years and verified that none of these patients developed Parkinsonian syndrome - i.e. 2-4 year clinical follow-up as Standard of Truth (SOT) in FDOPA scan negative patients provides supplementary information to the clinical diagnosis. Other patients (FDOPA scan positive) were not followed-up.
L2043: Sponsor stated that Fluorodopa F 18 PET is not intended for screening patients for parkinsonian syndromes which are borderline cases. Since Parkinson's drug treatment has several side effects, an early detection based on the PET scan is important to avoid unwanted drug treatment for patients with parkinsonian syndromes, but no Parkinson's (or related) Disease. It appears that in about 15% of the cases where the physicians are in doubt, early detection using a PET scan is helpful to avoid unwanted treatment. These patients are likely to benefit as was seen in Study 15 (27/185 = 14.6%). However, these 15% patients with negative PD group were significantly younger than Normal group
L2044: or Positive PD group and thus raising the question whether the difference in FDOPA PET results could largely be explained by age difference. Study # 15 was not properly designed to evaluate baseline differences.
L2045: Study # 21 is exploratory, single center with all study patients already diagnosed with PD (not the target patient population), and it is unclear how specificity and sensitivity were calculated.
L2046: The clinical data for efficacy and collective information submitted in this NDA including use of clinical diagnosis as SOT are not adequate to support an approval. The clinical value in terms of providing supplemental value to physician's clinical diagnosis is not provided. Therefore based on collective information and statistical analysis, a Complete Response is recommended due to lack of robust clinical data for efficacy and lack of follow-up for all patients. Feinstein Institute should continue using Fluorodopa F 18 Injection under an FDA approved Expanded Access IND (with a protocol and prospective analysis plan) and follow patients for at least 2 years to obtain outcome data which can be used as a standard of truth/reference.
L2047: 2. INTRODUCTION
L2048: Fluorodopa, also known as F-DOPA, is a fluorinated form of Levodopa (L-DOPA) primarily synthesized as its fluorine-18 isotopologue for use as a radiotracer in positron emission tomography (PET). Positron emission tomography (PET) is a nuclear medical imaging technique that produces a three-dimensional image or picture of functional processes in the body. PET technology using short-lived radioisotopes is a state of the art imaging technology.
L2049: Fluorodopa F 18 Injection belongs to the aromatic amino acid class of compounds. Once injected, Fluorodopa F 18 will cross the blood brain barrier to reach the striatal region where it undergoes decarboxylation to form fluorodopamine. As in the case of L-DOPA, the storage of the fluorodopamine in the striatum will depend upon the condition of the striatal region. Therefore, the storage of fluorodopamine F 18 in the striatum will depend upon the extent of neuronal loss. If the striatal region has no loss of dopaminergic neurons, Fluorodopa F 18 uptake in that region will be normal. However, if there is dopaminergic neuronal loss in the striatum, the uptake of fluorodopamine will be reduced depending upon the extent of the neuronal loss. According to the sponsor, (b) (4)
L2050: Fluorodopa F 18 follows the same biological pathway as L-DOPA and the drug is excreted through the bladder in a similar way. The safety and effectiveness of L-DOPA is well established.
L2051: This radiotracer (F-DOPA) has already been approved by the European Union a long time back as a diagnostic imaging for the differential diagnosis of parkinsonian syndromes. Fluorodopa F 18 PET has been used for the last 20 years around the world.
L2052: 2.1 Overview
L2053: The safety and efficacy of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection was presented by the FDA reviewers at the public meeting conducted by FDA on July 28, 2000. It was pointed out that the available literature data were insufficient to support efficacy in any patient population. A pre-IND meeting was held in 2008 and original NDA was submitted on 10/29/2009 with a PDUFIA start date of 1/21/2010 (date user fee waiver granted). Refuse to File letter was issued on 3/19/2010.
L2054: This is a 505(b)(2) NDA re-submission/after refuse to file document number 19, and contains one clinical trial conducted by the applicant onsite, and the rest of the submission are from literature publications. This application is a substantial improvement over the original NDA submission which had no module 2 summaries and lack of integrated summary of efficacy (ISE) and integrated summary of safety (ISS) were cited as filing deficiencies in the RTF letter (3/19/2010). Information about ISE and ISS in this NDA is provided and individual summaries of several studies have been provided.
L2055: Feinstein Institute was approved by FDA to continue using Fluorodopa F 18 Injection under an Expanded Access IND on 6/21/2012. The current NDA was resubmitted on 10/22/2012.
L2056: 2.1.1 Indication and Doses
L2057: Proposed indication: Fluorodopa F 18 Injection is a (b) (4) used in Positron Emission Tomography (PET) to visualize dopaminergic (b) (4) in the striatum in adult patients with suspected parkinsonian syndromes (PS). (b) (4)
L2058: Fluorodopa F 18 PET is an adjunct to other diagnostic evaluations.
L2059: The proposed dosage form is injection. Strengths are 0.42mCi/mL to 8.33mCi/mL. Route of Administration is intravenous. At the Feinstein Institute, subjects were dosed with 150 mg of carbidopa one hour prior to administration of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection dose between 4-5mCi to generate a well resolved image. Fluorodopa F 18 is an imaging agent and is not for treatment purposes.
L2060: 2.1.2 Identified Studies in the review
L2061: The sponsor provided reports of 9 Efficacy and Safety Studies (Studies #15-23). This included one 'controlled' study by the Applicant and reports of 8 publications.
L2062: The F-18 FDOPA PET efficacy data are derived from the following three studies.
L2063: Study # 15: Clinical Study of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection Conducted by The Feinstein Institute for Medical Research:
L2064: The Study # 15 is the primary clinical study conducted by the Feinstein Institute for Medical Research. The patient-level data were submitted to the NDA.
L2065: Study # 16: Regional Metabolic Changes in Parkinsonian Patients with Normal Dopaminergic Imaging:
L2066: Publication of a follow-up study on a subset of patients in Study #15 of F-18 FDOPA PET Negative Patients (27) published in a Journal "Movement Disord", 2007:22: 167-173 by Eckert, et al. – "Regional Metabolic Changes in Parkinsonian Patients with Normal Dopaminergic Imaging."
L2067: Study # 21: Direct Comparison of FP-CIT SPECT and F-DOPA PET in Patients with Parkinson's Disease and Healthy Controls – This paper reports results of a direct comparison of FP-CIT SPECT and F-DOPA PET in Patients with Parkinson's Disease and Healthy Controls. Report is based on the publication of an exploratory study comparing sensitivity and specificity of DaTscan and FDOPA.
L2068: Other studies are briefly mentioned below. For details, readers are referred to the clinical report.
L2069: Study # 17: Striatal and Extrastriatal Dysfunction in Parkinson's Disease with Dementia: A 6-18F]Fluoro-L-Dopa PET Study.
L2070: Publication of an exploratory study analyzing FDOPA influx constant (K_i) in various brain regions. With this study, the authors conclude a significant dysfunction in both the striatal and extrastriatal regions in patients with Parkinsonian syndromes. There was no information relevant to the confirmation of efficacy or safety of FDOPA in this paper.
L2071: Study # 18: A Comparison of the Progression of Early Parkinson's Disease in Patients Started on Ropinirole or L-Dopa: An 18F-Dopa PET Study.
L2072: Fluorodopa F 18 Injection was used to measure the progression of patients diagnosed with early-onset Parkinson's disease who had been treated with either ropinirole or levodopa. This was an exploratory study on disease progression (all patients were diagnosed with Parkinson Disease) and treatment response (to Ropinirole vs L-Dopa). The authors conclude that that after 2 years of treatment for patients with early parkinsonian syndromes, there is no significant difference in the rate of progression between patients started with ropinirole compared with L-dopa. The results were not related to the confirmation of efficacy or safety of FDOPA in this paper.
L2073: Study # 19: Slower Progression of Parkinson's Disease with Ropinirole versus Levodopa: The REAL-PET Study. Publication of a clinical study designed to compare treatment response to ropinirole vs. L-dopa; all patients recruited patients were already diagnosed with PD. This study was not designed to assess the diagnostic efficacy of FDOPA PET; and no relevant for confirmation of diagnostic performance characteristics of FDOPA included in this paper.
L2074: Study # 20: A Technique for Standardized Central Analysis of 6-Fluoro-L-Dopa PET from Multicenter Study. This study was a publication of technical portion of Study #19.
L2075: Study # 22: Simple Ratio Analysis (SOR) of 18F-Fluorodopa Uptake in Striatal Subregions Separates Patients with Early Parkinson Disease from Healthy Controls –
L2076: The purpose of this study was to determine whether a simple ratio analysis and K_i values calculated from the PET scans of the PD patients demonstrate a definite difference from those values obtained for the healthy normal subjects. The authors conclude that a single 15 minute scan 75 minutes post injection of Fluorodopa F 18 will provide well resolved images to detect dopaminergic deficit. The paper does not provide any relevant information for confirmation of diagnostic performance characteristics of FDOPA
L2077: Study # 23: Fluorine-18-Fluoro-L-DOPA Dosimetry with Carbidopa Pretreatment.
L2078: The purpose of this study was to determine the absorbed radiation dose in various organs in human subjects after the administration of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection. The study also determined the effective dose as a means of characterizing the biologic significance of radiation dose. A total of 23 subjects were included in this study. The results are based on publication of an exploratory study showing the effect of carbidopa pretreatment on FDOPA biodistribution and dosimetry. (clinpharm study no efficacy or safety discussed)

L2082: 3. STATISTICAL EVALUATION
L2083: There were three studies for the efficacy analysis, namely sponsor's study numbers 15, 16 and 21. These studies are the main focus of this review. The sponsor also provided additional information on several studies based on publications, as mentioned above.
L2084: 3.1 Data and Analysis Quality
L2085: There were two studies listed as controlled clinical studies (both conducted in the U.S.), including one conducted by the applicant (Feinstein Inst.) on site (study # 15), and the other, a publication of a follow-up study of a subset of patients in the onsite Feinstein study (study # 16). Since the applicant conducted both pivotal studies, the applicant was requested to submit datasets (patient level information) to allow substantive review to occur. On 12/17/2012, the applicant submitted patient level data, patient consent form, patient data collection sheet (sample case report form), study protocol, statistical analysis, and a cover letter as an amendment to the NDA. The deficiency regarding the lack of ISE and ISS was also resolved. During the course of statistical review, some additional information was also requested which the sponsor promptly provided.
L2086: 3.2 Evaluation of Efficacy
L2087: 3.2.1 Study Design
L2088: Study # 15: This is the primary retrospective single center study conducted by the Feinstein Institute for Medical Research. A protocol for this study was not submitted to the Agency for review. This study was intended to validate the efficacy and safety of Fluorodopa F 18 PET as an imaging tool in the differential diagnosis of patients with parkinsonian symptoms. In this study, two groups of patients were used. Normal subjects were also included in the study for comparison. Clinical diagnosis information was provided by the referring physician. Clinical diagnosis was based on the United Parkinson's Disease Rating Scale (UPDRS) and physical examination of the patient. No other diagnostic testing was used for the assessment of the clinical symptoms.
L2089: Based on the physician's clinical diagnosis, the patient was referred to the Feinstein Institute medical center for a Fluorodopa F 18 PET scan. A patient was deemed eligible for the study only if they showed at least two cardinal symptoms of parkinsonian syndromes (bradykinesia, rigidity, tremor, and/or gait disorder). This was the primary criteria for patient inclusion. Study Drug Dosing was 4-5 mCi intravenous. Patient preparation: subjects were dosed with 150 mg of carbidopa one hour prior to administration of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection dose between 4-5mCi to generate a well resolved image.
L2090: PET Scan was performed. Images (PET Scan) were interpreted visually by three qualified individuals as either positive (loss of dopaminergic neurons - demonstrated a reduced uptake of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection) or negative (no loss of dopaminergic neurons - showed a normal uptake of Fluorodopa F 18). The image readers were blinded to the clinical diagnosis information provided by the referring physician. The readers were also blinded to the striatal occipital ratio (SOR) calculation which was used to further
L2091: confirm the image interpretation. There was a defined inclusion criteria, and image interpretation by blinded readers. These readings were compared and the diagnostic accuracy was evaluated. A report was generated based on the assessment of the scan and the results were conveyed to the referring physician.
L2092: Study # 16: This study is a follow-up of F-18 FDOPA PET Negative Patients (27) from study # 15 published in a Journal “Movement Disord”, 2007:22: 167-173 by Eckert, et al. – “Regional Metabolic Changes in Parkinsonian Patients with Normal Dopaminergic Imaging”
L2093: 3.2.2 Objective
L2094: The primary objective was to assess loss or no loss of dopaminergic neurons in the striatum of adult patients with suspected Parkinsonian syndromes (PS).
L2095: 3.2.3 Primary Criteria for evaluation
L2096: The primary efficacy measure was visual assessment of PET Scan images by three qualified individuals as either positive (loss of dopaminergic neurons - demonstrated a reduced uptake of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection) or negative (no loss of dopaminergic neurons - showed a normal uptake of Flurodopa F 18), and a follow-up of 27 patients with negative F-18 FDOPA PET for 2-4 years (study 16) who were enrolled in the study #15.

L2107: Note: Since patients were only followed-up for FDOPA scan negative patients for 2-4 years and verified that none of these patients developed Parkinsonian syndrome - i.e. 2-4 year clinical follow-up as Standard of Truth (SOT) in FDOPA scan negative patients, the negative predictive value for these patients in each age group or for each gender is 100% using SOT.
L2108: 3.3 Results and Conclusions
L2109: 3.3.1 Study # 15
L2110: The primary endpoint is the visualization of the striatal region using Fluorodopa F 18 PET as a biomarker. Sponsor states that (b) (4)
L2111: A reduced uptake of Fluorodopa F 18 due to neuronal loss will reduce the size and shape of the putamen and caudate (crescent shape structure) depending upon the disease severity. If there is no loss of neurons, the PET scan will be normal (full crescent shaped structure). These two conditions will be clearly demonstrated in the PET scan and the results are interpreted as positive (loss of dopaminergic neurons) or negative (no loss of dopaminergic neurons). This is the pre- specified event by which the efficacy is established.
L2112: A total of 185 patients were referred by movement disorder specialists and classified as either “possible-PD” or “possible-non PD” based on their symptoms and response to typical Parkinson’s disease treatment.
L2113: In addition to the patient population, eleven healthy normal controls were included in the study.
L2114: Subjects were dosed with 150 mg of carbidopa one hour prior to administration of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection (4-5 mCi).
L2115: A visual examination of each subjects PET scan by three separate blinded readers resulted in 27 of the 185 patients (15%) being classified as having no loss of dopaminergic neurons. A Striatal Occipital Ratio (SOR) quantification of these patients revealed normal SOR values (Mean = 2.4781; SD= 0.1559) which were same as the SOR values calculated for the normal healthy controls (Mean = 2.5452; SD = 0.1297).
L2116: The remaining 158 patients were classified by visual assessment of the PET scan as having a loss of dopaminergic neurons. The SOR values for this group of patients (Mean = 1.6854; SD = 0.1232) were much lower than the SOR values of normal subjects or those patients with no dopaminergic loss.
L2117: There was no variability among scan readers with regards to the visual assessment of the PET scans. The three blinded PET scan readers were also blinded to the physicians’ diagnosis. For every one of the patients that were initially classified as “possible PD”, a positive PET scan result was recorded and for each patient that was initially classified as “possible non-PD”, a negative PET scan result was recorded. For each healthy control subject, a negative PET scan result was recorded. There were no false positive or false negative scan results considering physician’s clinical diagnosis as SOT, resulting in 100% sensitivity and 100% specificity.
L2118: 3.3.2 Study # 16
L2119: This study # 16 is a continuation of the clinical study # 15 conducted by The Feinstein Institute for Medical Research to further evaluate the patients that exhibited parkinsonian symptoms but had normal dopaminergic imaging. There were 27 patients with negative F-18 FDOPA PET and these patients were followed for 2-4 years. The results were published in a Journal “Movement Disord”, 2007:22: 167-173 by Eckert, et al. – “Regional Metabolic Changes in Parkinsonian Patients with Normal Dopaminergic Imaging.” These patients were referred by a movement disorder physician and exhibited at least two of the cardinal features of parkinsonian syndromes (bradykinesia, rigidity, tremor, and/or gait disorder. They (“suspected non-PD”) were referred due to the nonresponse of the patient to typical Parkinson’s disease treatment.
L2120: 3.3.3 Integrated Results of Study # 15 and # 16
L2121: Table 4 summarizes PET scan results versus clinical diagnosis for two studies (# 15 and # 16).

L2125: 3.3.4 Study # 21: Comparison of FP-CIT SPECT and F-DOPA PET
L2126: I-123 FP-CIT SPECT (DaTscan) is approved in the U.S. and Europe. It selectively binds to dopamine transporter (DaT) =&gt; DaT imaging agent. It is indicated for striatal dopamine transporter visualization by SPECT brain imaging to assist in the evaluation of adult patients with suspected Parkinsonian syndromes (PS). In these patients, DaTscan may be used to help differentiate essential tremor from tremor due to Parkinsonian Syndrome (idiopathic Parkinson’s disease, multiple system atrophy and progressive supranuclear palsy). Recommended dosing is 111 to 185 MBq (3 to 5 mCi).
L2127: F-18 FDOPA PET vs. I-123 DaTscan SPECT
L2128: The effectiveness of FP-CIT SPECT imaging versus F-DOPA PET imaging in patients with Parkinson’s disease was directly compared in this study using a patient population with strong
L2129: clinical evidence of having Parkinson’s disease. Healthy controls in a similar age range as the patients were also used in this study.
L2130: This was a single center study in Europe. The inclusion criteria: patients with strong clinical evidence of having Parkinson’s disease. The exclusion criteria: patients with atypical signs, psychiatric disorders, signs of severe cognitive deterioration, severe cardiovascular co-morbidity or on medication known to interfere with dopamine transporters (DaT) or catechol-O-methyltransferase inhibitors. The study drug dosing was 1&gt; 2.5 mg/kg carbidopa PO 1 hour prior to FDOPA 2&gt; 200 MBq F-18 FDOPA IV 3&gt; 185 MBq DaTscan IV.
L2131: A total of 29 patients were recruited. The Standard of Truth (SOT): all patients were followed for 1 year after the scans. This resulted in 28 patients included in the analysis (One patient was excluded). Each of the 28 patients had both FDOPA PET and DaTscan SPECT in order to compare the relative efficacies of both radiotracers.
L2132: There were 11 early-stage patients who had strong clinical evidence of PD based on two of the three cardinal symptoms (rest tremor, bradykinesia and rigidity), disease duration of less than 3 years, and were not using antiparkinson medication.
L2133: There were 17 advanced-stage patients who had two of the three cardinal symptoms, disease duration of 5–15 years, and were using antiparkinson medication with a documented response.
L2134: There were 20 controls (10 FDOPA + 10 DaTscan).

The age of patients with advanced disease was higher than the age of patients with early-stage disease.

L2145: 3.4 Evaluation of Safety
L2146: Applicant did not submit any safety data in the NDA. Applicant states that 18F is a short-lived radioisotope, at the end of the study there is only trace amounts of radiation left in the brain and the patient has minimal radiation risk. There were no adverse events after the administration of Fluorodopa F 18 and there was no premature discontinuation from the study due to complication. There have been no safety concerns from over 20 years of use of the drug at the Feinstein Institute. This is a derivative of L-dopa, which has a known safety profile.
L2147: 4. FINDINGS IN SPECIAL/SUBGROUP POPULATIONS
L2148: 4.1 Gender, Race, Age, and Geographic Region
L2149: For the study # 15, findings were assessed for each of the primary variables by gender and age. The results indicated a general consistency across each stratification for each reader. There was no difference in the efficacy data obtained from the male and female group of patients either in the published data.
L2150: Since Fluorodopa F 18 is used in different regions around the world, the subject population includes people with a wide range of ethnic backgrounds. Males and females of various ages, heights and weights were also included. According to the applicant, there is no indication that the drug behaves differently in any specific subpopulation. There were no observable differences in the PET images among subjects due to any type of characteristic other than disease state. As no adverse events have been reported, there is also no indication that Fluorodopa F 18 Injection would pose a risk to any subpopulation.
L2151: 4.2 Other Special/Subgroup Populations
L2152: There were no special subgroups or populations identified by the clinical team for analyses in this review.
L2153: 5. SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS
L2154: 5.1 Statistical Issues and Collective Evidence
L2155: There were two relevant clinical studies – one conducted by applicant (study # 15) with a follow-up of 27 scan negative patients (study # 16). These studies had several deficiencies:
L2156: • They were single center retrospective studies. A protocol was not submitted for review to the Agency. There were design issues in the study, especially borderline cases of patients with very mild symptoms of parkinsonian syndromes were not fully evaluated. Patients with at least two cardinal symptoms of parkinsonian syndromes were included in the study.• Study drug lacks clinical utility if patient’s diagnosis is already clear. For patients who were suspected of Parkinson Disease, there is no further follow-up of most patients to confirm patient’s clinical diagnosis (only patients with negative FDOPA PET were followed).• Although 27 FDOPA PET negative patients were followed for \geq 2 year to obtain patient’s true clinical diagnosis, there is age difference between FDOPA PET positive patients and FDOPA PET negative patients raising the question whether the difference in FDOPA PET results could largely be explained by age difference.
L2157: Sponsor’s supportive study # 21 is a direct comparison of FP-CIT SPECT and F-DOPA PET in patients with Parkinson’s Disease and Healthy Controls. The results are based on the publication of an exploratory study comparing sensitivity and specificity of DaTscan and FDOPA. This study also has following limitations:
L2158: • It is a single center study, data were not provided and the methodology used is not clear. The results could not be verified.• All study patients had strong clinical evidence of PD, and these patients may not need another imaging test to confirm diagnosis.• Patients with atypical symptoms were excluded, but these are patients who may benefit from an imaging test in further diagnostic work-up.
L2159: 5.2 Conclusions and Recommendations
L2160: Information on 9 studies was submitted. All these studies are post-hoc evaluation including primary clinical study # 15 conducted by the sponsor. Eight (8) out of 9 studies are from literature publications. Three (3) out of 9 studies were found relevant for evaluating diagnostic efficacy (performance characteristics) of Fluorodopa F 18 (F-DOPA) injection, but all had serious deficiencies. Other 6 publications did not include any information relevant to the confirmation of performance characteristic characteristics of F-DOPA and not relevant to the confirmation of efficacy or safety of F-DOPA.
L2161: Studies 15 &amp; 16 are retrospective, single center, and with clinical diagnosis as Standard of Truth (SOT).
L2162: Study # 15 was intended to validate the efficacy and safety of Fluorodopa F 18 PET as an imaging tool in the differential diagnosis of patients with parkinsonian symptoms. Clinical diagnosis
L2163: information was provided by the referring physician. A protocol for study # 15 was not submitted to the Agency for review. The primary efficacy measure was visual assessment of PET Scan images by three qualified individuals as either positive (loss of dopaminergic neurons - demonstrated a reduced uptake of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection) or negative (no loss of dopaminergic neurons - showed a normal uptake of Fluorodopa F 18), and a follow-up of 27 patients with negative F-18 FDOPA PET for 2-4 years (study 16) who were enrolled in the study #15.
L2164: There was no variability among three scan readers with regards to the visual assessment of the PET scans. The three blinded PET scan readers were also blinded to the physicians' diagnosis. For every one of the patients that were initially classified as "possible PD (Parkinson's Disease)", a positive PET scan result was recorded and for each patient that was initially classified as "possible non-PD", a negative PET scan result was recorded. For each healthy control subject, a negative PET scan result was recorded. There were no false positive or false negative scan results considering physician's clinical diagnosis as SOT, resulting in 100% sensitivity and 100% specificity for each reader.
L2165: Study # 16 had additional follow-up information on 27 out of a total of 185 patients. These patients had clinical diagnosis of suspected non-PD and were FDOPA scan negative patients. These patients were followed-up for 2-4 years and verified that none of these patients developed Parkinsonian syndrome - i.e. 2-4 year clinical follow-up as Standard of Truth (SOT) in FDOPA scan negative patients provides supplementary information to the clinical diagnosis. Other patients (FDOPA scan positive) were not followed-up.
L2166: Sponsor stated that Fluorodopa F 18 PET is not intended for screening patients for parkinsonian syndromes which are borderline cases. Since Parkinson's drug treatment has several side effects, an early detection based on the PET scan is important to avoid unwanted drug treatment for patients with parkinsonian syndromes, but no Parkinson's (or related) Disease. It appears that in about 15% of the cases where the physicians are in doubt, early detection using a PET scan is helpful to avoid unwanted treatment. These patients are likely to benefit as was seen in Study 15 (27/185 = 14.6\%). However, these 15% patients with negative PD group were significantly younger than Normal group or Positive PD group and thus raising the question whether the difference in FDOPA PET results could largely be explained by age difference. Study # 15 was not properly designed to evaluate baseline differences.
L2167: The clinical data for efficacy and collective information submitted in this NDA including use of clinical diagnosis as SOT are not adequate to support an approval. The clinical value in terms of providing supplemental value to physician's clinical diagnosis is not provided. Therefore based on collective information and statistical analysis, a Complete Response is recommended due to lack of robust clinical data for efficacy and lack of follow-up for all patients. Feinstein Institute should continue using Fluorodopa F 18 Injection under an FDA approved Expanded Access IND and follow 20-30 patients for at least 2 years to obtain a standard of truth/reference.

L2196: Page #1Executive Summary31.1Recommendations41.2Post-Marketing Requirements and Commitments41.3Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Findings52Question Based Review52.1General Attributes52.2General Clinical Pharmacology52.3Intrinsic Factors72.4Extrinsic Factors72.5General Biopharmaceutics72.6Analytical Section73Detailed Labeling Recommendations84Appendices94.1Applicant's Proposed Package Insert4.2Clinical Pharmacology Review of Original NDA 200-6554.3OCP Filing Form
L2197: 1 EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
L2198: The applicant has re-submitted NDA 200655 for Fluorodopa F18 Injection in response to the complete response letter the Agency issued on October 22, 2013. The major issues identified in FDA's complete response letter were clinical, product quality and manufacturing facilities. The original submission was acceptable from a clinical pharmacology perspective and no post-marketing requirements or commitments were recommended.
L2199: This re-submission contains only one additional clinical study, Study 17. Study 17 used Fluorodopa F18 PET scans to differentiate between Parkinsonism (Idiopathic Parkinson's Disease and Progressive Supranuclear Palsy) and non-Parkinsonism [e.g., Essential Tremors, Dopamine Responsive Dystonia, and SWEDD (scan without dopaminergic deficit)]. Patients with essential tremor are expected to have normal (negative) PET scans. The studied dose was 4.05 - 5 mCi of Fluorodopa, which is not significantly different than that used in the clinical study submitted in the original NDA (Study 15).
L2200: The primary end point for Study 17 was the visualization of dopaminergic neurons in the striatum expressed as a binary variable: "loss" / "no loss." "Loss" of dopaminergic neurons in the striatum was based upon the reader's judgment of what a "no loss" image looks like, not upon a prior scan in the same patient. The sensitivity of fluorodopa F18 injection, defined as "loss" of dopaminergic neurons in the striatum of patients having a clinical diagnosis of Parkinson's, was 100%. Specificity could not be determined as 7 of 13 scan negative (i.e., "no loss") patients did not have Standard of Truth (SoT).
L2201: The applicant collected Striatal-Occipital Ratio (SOR) in Study 17. Striatal Occipital Ratio is the ratio of radioactivity in the striatum (target) to that of the occipital cortex (background). These SOR values were not used for the visual interpretation of the PET scan results. The reviewer's analysis of the data shows that, on average, SOR differed as a function of disease. However, because there was considerable overlap between SORs in patients with Parkinson's and healthy subjects, SOR cannot be used as an aid to scan interpretation.
L2202: The applicant's proposed dose for the package insert is (b) (4) 5.0 mCi. However, Study 17 used a dose of 4.05-5 mCi. There is no basis for using a dose range and there is a potential for an unclear scan if the dose is too low. We recommend a dose of 5 mCi.
L2203: The current submission has no new information on metabolism, drug interactions, or specific populations. The following (indented) is reproduced from the review of the original NDA.
L2204: "No drug interaction studies have been reported in literature. However, it is not known whether drugs that are used to treat Parkinsonian syndromes can alter the disposition of Fluorodopamine F18 or the accumulation/retention of 18F in the striatum or rest of the brain. For this reason, the applicant's clinical trials required patients to discontinue any Parkinsonian medications at least 12 hours prior to the PET study. The proposed package insert includes this restriction. No special population studies or organ impairment studies have been reported."
L2205: 1.1 Recommendations
L2206: From a clinical pharmacology perspective, we recommend approval of NDA 200-655 based on 100% sensitivity in detecting loss of dopaminergic neurons in the striatum of patients clinically diagnosed with Parkinson’s Disease in Study 17†.

L2217: 1.3 Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Findings
L2218: This re-submission contains only one additional clinical study, Study 17. Study 17 used Fluorodopa F18 PET scans to differentiate between Parkinsonism (Idiopathic Parkinson's Disease and Progressive Supranuclear Palsy) and non-Parkinsonism [e.g., Essential Tremors, Dopamine Responsive Dystonia, and SWEDD (scan without dopaminergic deficit)]. Patients with essential tremor are expected to have normal (negative) PET scans.
L2219: The studied dose was 4.05 - 5 mCi of Fluorodopa, which is not significantly different than that used in the clinical study submitted in the original NDA (Study 15).
L2220: The primary end point for Study 17 was the visualization of dopaminergic neurons in the striatum expressed as a binary variable: "loss" / "no loss." "Loss" of dopaminergic neurons in the striatum was based upon the reader's judgment of what a "no loss" image looks like, not upon a prior scan in the same patient. The sensitivity of fluorodopa F18 injection, defined as "loss" of dopaminergic neurons in the striatum of patients having a clinical diagnosis of Parkinson's, was 100%. Specificity could not be determined as 7 of 13 scan negative (i.e., "no loss") patients did not have Standard of Truth (SoT).
L2221: The applicant collected Striatal-Occipital Ratio (SOR) in Study 17. Striatal Occipital Ratio is the ratio of radioactivity in the striatum (target) to that of the occipital cortex (background). These SOR values were not used for the visual interpretation of the PET scan results. The reviewer's analysis of the data shows that, on average, SOR differed as a function of disease. However, because there was considerable overlap between SORs in patients with Parkinson's and healthy volunteers, SOR cannot be used as an aid to scan interpretation.
L2222: The applicant's proposed dose for the package insert is (b) (4) 5.0 mCi. However, Study 17 used a dose of 4.05-5 mCi. There is no basis for using a dose range and there is a potential for an unclear scan if the dose is too low. We recommend a dose of 5 mCi.
L2223: 2 QUESTION BASED REVIEW
L2224: 2.1 GENERAL ATTRIBUTES
