L2226: 2.2 GENERAL CLINICAL PHARMACOLOGY
L2227: 2.2.1 What are the design features of the clinical pharmacology and clinical studies used to support dosing or claims?
L2228: This NDA re-submission contains only one additional clinical study (Study #17). 4.05 - 5 mCi of Fluorodopa was administered to patients intravenously. The "cold" mass dose associated with
L2229: this radioactive dose is (b) (4) mg. 150 mg of carbidopa was administered orally to patients 60-90 minutes prior of Flurodopa 18 injection. These doses are not significantly different than those in the clinical study submitted in the original NDA, which was approvable from a clinical pharmacology perspective.
L2230: The applicant’s proposed dose for the package insert is (b) (4) 5.0 mCi. However, Study 17 used a dose of 4.05-5 mCi. There is no basis for using a dose range. We recommend a dose of 5 mCi.
L2231: PET scans were evaluated by three different readers. Patients were followed up to two years following PET scan a clinical diagnosis was used as a Standard-of-Truth (SoT). The primary endpoint for Study 17 was the visualization of dopaminergic neurons in the striatum expressed as a binary variable: “loss” / “no loss.” “Loss” of dopaminergic neurons in the striatum was based upon the reader’s judgment of what a “no loss” image looks like, not upon a prior scan in the same patient. The sensitivity of fluorodopa F18 injection, defined as “loss” of dopaminergic neurons in the striatum in patients having a clinical diagnosis of Parkinson’s, was 100%. Specificity could not be determined as 7 of 13 scan negative (i.e., “no loss”) patients did not have Standard of Truth (SoT).
L2232: The applicant collected quantitative data (Striatal-Occipital Ratio: SOR) during the study. These SOR values were not used for the visual interpretation of the PET scan results. The applicant provided no analysis of the SOR data. The reviewer’s plot of the data is shown as Figure 1. The data show that SOR did not differ as a function of disease. Thus, there is not potential to use SOR as an aid to scan interpretation.
L2233: Figure 1. Striatal-Occipital Ratio (SOR) by Striatal Brain Region and Standard-of-Truth (SoT; based on clinical diagnosis)
    LPP= Left posterior putamen; RPP= Right posterior putamen; LAP= Left anterior putamen; RAP= Right anterior putamen; LC= Left caudate; RC= Right caudate
    PD= Parkinson’s disease; not PD= not Parkinson’s disease
  Scatter plot showing Striatal-Occipital Ratio (SOR) for various brain regions (LPP, RPP, LAP, RAP) and clinical diagnoses (PD, not PD). The y-axis represents SOR from 1 to 3.5. Data points are categorized by brain region and diagnosis. LPP (Left posterior putamen) shows lower SOR values (1.3-3.0) for PD and (1.4-2.5) for not PD. RPP (Right posterior putamen) shows SOR values (1.4-2.9) for PD and (1.8-2.4) for not PD. LAP (Left anterior putamen) and RAP (Right anterior putamen) show higher SOR values, mostly between 1.8 and 3.4, for both PD and not PD groups. LC (Left caudate) and RC (Right caudate) show a few data points around SOR 2.0-2.5.
L2234: 2.2.2 What is the basis for selecting the response endpoints (i.e., clinical or surrogate endpoints) or biomarkers (collectively called pharmacodynamics (PD) and how are they measured in clinical pharmacology and clinical studies?
L2235: Striatal-Occipital ratio was selected as an endpoint because the occipital cortex has limited dopaminergic innervation. The lack of dopaminergic nerve terminals that project to the occipital cortex makes it an appropriate control tissue for background.

L2273: 1. Executive Summary
L2274: The applicant, Feinstein Institute of Medical Research (FIMR), has resubmitted (after a refusal to file in March 2010) NDA (200-655) for Flurodopa F 18 Injection as a diagnostic PET (Positron Emission Tomography) imaging “to visualize dopaminergic (b) (4) in the striatum in adult patients with suspected Parkinsonian syndromes (PS). (b) (4)
L2275: Fluorodopa F 18 PET is an adjunct to other diagnostic evaluations.”
L2276: This application is supported by literature data along with one clinical trial conducted by the applicant. There was 100% correlation between the visual assessment of the PET images and the clinical diagnoses.
L2277: The proposed dose is an intravenous injection of (b) (4) 5 mCi. The selection of dose was based on the applicant’s review of literature prior to conducting their study. The applicant used a dose of 4-5 mCi for their pivotal clinical trial. The “cold” mass dose associated with this radioactive dose is (b) (4) mg.
L2278: The primary information for clinical pharmacology review for this submission is derived from fourteen published literature submitted by the applicant. No clinical pharmacology studies were conducted by FIMR.
L2279: Fluorodopa F 18 undergoes decarboxylation by the enzyme aromatic amino acid decarboxylase (AAAD) to produce Fluorodopamine F 18. In order to decrease decarboxylation by AAAD, and increase bioavailability to the brain, patients are pretreated with carbidopa, an inhibitor of AAAD. The applicant selected the carbidopa dose of 150 mg based on their assessment of peer-reviewed publications.
L2280: No drug interaction studies have been reported in literature. However, it is not known whether drugs that are used to treat Parkinsonian syndromes can alter the disposition of Fluorodopamine F 18 or the accumulation/retention of 18F in the striatum or rest of the brain. For this reason, the applicant’s clinical trials required patients to discontinue any Parkinsonian medications at least 12 hours prior to the PET study. The proposed package insert includes this restriction.
L2281: No special population studies or organ impairment studies have been reported.
L2282: 1.1. Recommendations
L2283: The Office of Clinical Pharmacology, Division of Clinical Pharmacology 5, has reviewed the submission NDA 203-155. The application is acceptable from a clinical pharmacology standpoint, provided an agreement is reached in labeling.
L2284: 1.2. Phase 4 Requirements and Commitments
L2285: We have no recommendations for post-marketing requirements or commitments.
L2286: 1.3. Summary of Important Clinical Pharmacology and Biopharmaceutics Findings
L2287: FIMR conducted a clinical trial to support the efficacy of Fluorodopa F 18 injection for PET imaging for the determination of the loss of functional dopaminergic neurons. All patients in the study were referred to FIMR by a movement disorder physician and exhibited at least two of the cardinal features of Parkinsonian syndromes (bradykinesia, rigidity, tremor, and/or gait disorder). Patients entered the study with a classification of “Parkinson’s Disease (PD) suspected” or “PD not suspected” from the movement disorder specialist. This clinical diagnosis by the movement disorder specialist was the standard of truth for interpretation of the PET scan results. There was 100% correlation between the visual assessment of the PET images and the clinical diagnoses. The clinical efficacy results are summarized in Table 1.
L2288: FDA Table 1. Clinical Efficacy Results
L2289: Clinical DiagnosisPossible PDPossible Non-PDVisual Assessment ResultsTotal SubjectsPOSITIVE PET Scan1580158NEGATIVE PET Scan02727
L2290: Fluorodopa F 18 is L-Dopa labeled with the short-lived radioisotope 18F. The proposed package insert dose is (b) (4) 5 mCi administered intravenously. Based on literature, not their own dose finding, the applicant used a radio-dose of 4-5 mCi for the pivotal clinical trial. The total dose (primarily non-radioactive) ranged from (b) (4) to (b) (4) mg. Non-radioactive L-Dopa is currently used for the treatment of Parkinson's Disease at an oral dose that is greater than (b) (4) fold-fold higher than proposed dose.
L2291: The absorbed radiation dose for 10 human subjects (8 men and 2 women; age 50 ±8.8 yr) from intravenous administration of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection was estimated. Four men and one woman were normal volunteers and the remaining subjects had a diagnosis of typical/atypical Parkinsonian Syndromes. All subjects had normal renal function. The identified critical organ is the urinary bladder. Based upon version 3 of the MIRDose program, an average dose to the bladder wall in humans was estimated as 0.15 ±0.08 mGy/MBq (0.57 ±0.28 rads/mCi), the maximum bladder wall radiation dose was 0.31 mGy/MBq (1.13 rads/mCi), and the minimum was 0.07 mGy/MBq (0.27 rads/mCi). These values are below the 50-mGy (5-rad) maximum per year dose permitted by FDA for radiation workers.
L2292: Post-administration, Fluorodopa F 18 undergoes decarboxylation by the enzyme aromatic amino acid decarboxylase (AAAD) to produce Fluorodopamine F 18. In order to decrease decarboxylation by AAAD, and increase bioavailability to the brain, patients are pretreated with carbidopa, an
L2293: inhibitor of AAAD. The applicant selected the carbidopa dose of 150 mg based on their assessment of peer-reviewed publications.

L2300: 2.2. General Attributes of the Drug
L2301: 2.2.1. What are the highlights of the chemistry and physical-chemical properties of the drug substance and the formulation of the drug product?
L2302: Fluorodopa F 18 Injection is a small molecule labeled with 18F, a positron emitting isotope. The active ingredient 6-[18F] Fluoro-L-3, 4-dihydroxyphenylalanine has the molecular formula of C9H10FNO4 and a molecular weight of 214.18 daltons. It has the following chemical structure.
L2303: NC(Cc1ccc(O)c(O)c1F)C(=O)OChemical structure of fluorodopa (6-[18F]Fluoro-L-3, 4-dihydroxyphenylalanine). The structure shows a benzene ring with two hydroxyl groups at positions 3 and 4, and a fluorine atom at position 6. A propanoic acid chain is attached at position 1, with an amino group at the alpha position.
L2304: FDA Figure 1. Chemical structure of fluorodopa (6-[18F]Fluoro-L-3, 4-dihydroxyphenylalanine)
L2305: Fluorodopa is formulated as an aqueous solution containing acetic acid and sodium chloride. (b) (4)
L2306: The multi dose formulated drug product contains 0.0155-0.3082 GBq (0.42-8.33 mCi/mL) of fluorodopa F 18, 12.72 mg acetic acid and 108 mg sodium chloride in 12±1mL of sterile water for injection.
L2307: 2.2.2. What are the proposed mechanism of action and therapeutic indications?
L2308: Fluorodopa F 18 Injection is an analogue of L-Dopa. After intravenous injection it is transported through the blood and taken up by the brain where it is decarboxylated to fluorodopamine (18FDA) in dopaminergic neurons and stored in synaptic vesicles. Once released from the dopaminergic neuron, 18FDA may be transported back into the pre-synaptic neuron where it may be subsequently catabolized or again stored in synaptic vesicles.
L2309: 2.2.3. What are the proposed dosages and routes of administration?
L2310: The proposed dose is (b) (4) 5.0 mCi ( (b) (4) 0.185GBq) administered as (b) (4).
L2311: 2.2.3 What is the radiation absorbed dose associated with the proposed dose of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection?
L2312: The absorbed radiation dose for 10 human subjects (8 men and 2 women; age 50 ±8.8 yr) from intravenous administration of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection was estimated. Four men and one woman were normal volunteers and the remaining subjects had a diagnosis of typical/atypical Parkinsonian Syndromes. No known renal dysfunction conditions were noted among the subjects. The identified critical organ is the urinary bladder. Based upon version 3 of the MIRDOSE program, an average dose to the bladder wall in humans was found to be 0.15 ±0.08 mGy/MBq (0.57 ±0.28 rads/mCi) the maximum bladder wall radiation dose was 0.31 mGy/MBq (1.13 rads/mCi), and the minimum was 0.07 mGy/MBq (0.27 rads/mCi). These values are below the 50-mGy (5-rad) maximum dose permitted by FDA for radiation workers.
L2313: 2.2.4. What drugs (substances, products) indicated for the same indication are approved in the US?
L2314: Currently, DaTscan (Ioflupane I 123) Injection, a radio-iodinated dopamine transporter binding ligand, has been approved for a similar indication. DaTscan is indicated for striatal dopamine transporter visualization using single photon emission computed tomography (SPECT) brain imaging to assist in the evaluation of adult patients with suspected Parkinsonian Syndromes (PS).
L2315: 2.3. General Clinical Pharmacology
L2316: 2.3.1. What are the design features of the clinical pharmacology and biopharmaceutics studies and the clinical studies used to support dosing or claims?
L2317: No clinical pharmacology studies were performed by the applicant to support the dosing claim. The dose selected by FIMR was based on literature reports. The report that was most relied on appeared to be that of Whone, et al. (A Technique for Standardized Central Analysis of 6-Fluoro-L-Dopa PET from a Multicenter Study. Alan L. Whone, Dale L. Bailey, Philippe Remy, Nicola Pavese, and David J. Brooks. J Nucl Med, 45, 1135-1145, 2004), where 186 patients were administered a dose of 4-4.9 mCi. The dose selected by the applicant is (b) (4) than that used in the Whone et al. study.
L2318: FIMR conducted a clinical trial to support the efficacy of Fluorodopa F 18. All patients in the study were referred to FIMR by a movement disorder physician and exhibited at least two of the cardinal features of Parkinsonian syndromes (bradykinesia, rigidity, tremor, and/or gait disorder). Patients entered the study with a classification of “Parkinson’s Disease (PD) suspected” or “PD not suspected” from the movement disorder specialist. This clinical diagnosis by the movement disorder specialist was the standard of truth for interpretation of the PET scan results. There was 100% correlation between the visual assessment of the PET images and the clinical diagnoses. The clinical efficacy results are summarized in Table 1, below.

L2330: Subjects were followed for 3-4 years to determine the accuracy of the PET results. Follow-up confirmed the 100% accurate evaluation (applicant's analysis).
L2331: 2.3.2. What is the basis for selecting the response endpoints and how are they measured in clinical pharmacology studies?
L2332: 2.3.3. Are the active moieties in plasma and clinically relevant tissues appropriately identified and measured to assess pharmacokinetic parameters and exposure response relationships?
L2333: The active moieties in plasma and clinically relevant tissues were not identified, only 18F was monitored. No exposure-response relationship was studied.
L2334: 2.4. Exposure-Response
L2335: 2.4.1. What are the characteristics of the exposure-response relationship for effectiveness?
L2336: 2.4.2. What are the characteristics of the exposure-response relationships for safety?
L2337: Exposure-response, including dose-response, relationships were not studied.
L2338: Effective imaging depends on the distribution of 18F. Preferential distribution to the striatum is the baseline condition that allows for effective imaging. This preferential distribution is determined by comparing the Fluorodopa uptake in the striatum to that of the occipital cortex (the occipital value is used as background). A successful scan has a sufficiently high striatal-to-occipital ratio (SOR). Within the striatum, pathology (e.g., Parkinsonian syndrome), reduces accumulation 18F. A positive image, i.e., an image where a diagnosis of “Parkinsonian syndrome” is made, lacks the intensity of signal in the striatum that occurs in the absence of pathology.
L2339: 2.4.3. Does this drug prolong QT/QTc Interval?
L2340: The applicant has not provided any information regarding the effect of fluorodopa to prolong QT/QTc interval. However, the mass amount of fluorodopa administered is less than (b)(4) mg. The clinical dose used to treat Parkinsons patients is 1500-1700 mg daily, or &gt; (b)(4) X that used for imaging. We do not expect any effect of Fluorodopa F 18 on QT/QTc prolongation.
L2341: 2.4.4. Is the dose and dosing regimen selected consistent with the known E-R relationship?
L2342: No exposure-response relationship was determined.
L2343: 2.5. Pharmacokinetics
L2344: 2.5.1. What are the single and multiple dose PK parameters of parent drug and relevant metabolites in healthy adults?
L2345: 2.5.2. How does the PK of the drug and its relevant metabolites in healthy adults compare to that in patients with the target disease?
L2346: 2.5.3. What are the inter- and intra-subject variability of the PK parameters in volunteers and patients with the target disease?
L2347: Pharmacokinetics were not performed by the applicant. The SINEMET (carbidopa-levodopa) package insert states that the plasma half-life of levodopa is about 50 minutes, without carbidopa.
L2348: The clinical study was conducted with administration of 150 mg of carbidopa one hour prior to administration of Fluorodopa F 18. The effect of carbidopa will be discussed in Section 2.7.8.
L2349: 2.5.4. What are the characteristics of drug absorption?
L2350: Fluorodopa F 18 Injection is administered as a single-time intravenous injection.
L2351: 2.5.5. What are the characteristics of drug distribution?
L2352: At 20 minutes post-injection, less than 50% of initial radioactivity is left in plasma. Imaging is performed one hour post-administration.
L2353: 2.5.6. Does the mass balance study suggest renal or hepatic as the major route of elimination?
L2354: Mass balance study results are not reported.
L2355: 2.5.7. What is the percentage of total radioactivity in plasma identified as parent drug and metabolites?
L2356: Firnau et al (J. Nucl Med. 29, 363-369, 1988), detected four metabolites in human plasma after injection of Fluorodopa F 18: 6-[18F]fluoro-O-sulfato-L-dopa, 6-[18F]fluorodopamine, 3-O-methyl-6-[18F]fluorodopa, and 6-[18F]fluorohomovanillic acid. Estimation of the percentage of total radioactivity from each of the moieties is not reported. One of the metabolites, 3-O-methyl-6-[18F]fluorodopa, appears to not accumulate in the striatal region. The contribution of the other moieties to PET imaging is unknown. Although there were quantitative differences, the metabolites identified from 6-fluoro-L-dopa in the blood were found to be the same as those identified following administration of 14-carbon-L-dopa, suggesting qualitatively similar metabolic pathway.
L2357: 2.5.8. What are the characteristics of drug metabolism?
L2358: FDOPA is a substrate for AAAD and catechol-O-methyltransferase (COMT). In the striatum, FDOPA is decarboxylated to fluorodopamine by AAAD in presynaptic dopaminergic terminals. Plasma FDOPA is also converted to 3-OMFD in the periphery.
L2359: 2.5.9. Is there evidence for excretion of parent drug and/or metabolites into bile?
L2360: Excretion into bile or feces was not studied.
L2361: 2.5.10. Is there evidence for enterohepatic recirculation for parent and/or metabolites?
L2362: There is not evidence for enterohepatic recirculation for parent and/or metabolites, but only limited concentration-time data are reported, and data following non-IV administration are not reported.
L2363: 2.5.11. What are the characteristics of drug excretion in urine?
L2364: Dosimetry study indicated that bladder was the target organ and that a majority of radioactivity was excreted through bladder. However, the percentages of parent and metabolites excreted in urine were not determined.
L2365: 2.5.12. Based on PK parameters, what is the degree of the proportionality of the dose-concentration relationship?
L2366: Dose proportionality results are not reported.
L2367: 2.5.13. How do the PK parameters change with time following chronic dosing?
L2368: Fluorodopa F 18 Injection is administered only one time for diagnostic imaging. Repeat dosing results are not reported.
L2369: 2.5.14. Is there evidence for a circadian rhythm of the PK?
L2370: There is not evidence for a circadian rhythm of the PK, but only limited concentration-time data are reported, and data are not reported by time-of-day.

L2391: 2.7. Extrinsic Factors
L2392: 2.7.1. Is there an in vitro basis to suspect in vivo drug-drug interactions?
L2393: In vitro investigation of drug metabolism is not reported.
L2394: Inhibitors of monoamine uptake, such as anti-depressants, could potentially alter striatal accumulation of 18F by inhibiting transport of any dopamine 18F derived from fluoro dopa 18F.
L2395: 2.7.2. Is the drug a substrate of CYP enzymes?
L2396: 2.7.3. Is the drug an inhibitor and/or an inducer of enzymes?
L2397: In vitro investigation of drug metabolism is not reported.
L2398: 2.7.4. Is the drug a substrate, an inhibitor and/or an inducer of transporter processes?
L2399: The ability of flurorodopa to act as an a substrate, an inhibitor and/or an inducer of transporter processes is not reported.
L2400: 2.7.5. Are there other metabolic/transporter pathways that may be important?
L2401: No; the processes discussed in Section 2.5.8 are the only processes likely to be important (see also, 2.7.7.).
L2402: The mass dose of fluorodopa is (b) (4) mg. The clinical dose of levodopa is up to 1700 mg daily, and levodopa has not been reported to perpetrate drug interactions. Thus, the current (b) (4) mg dose of fluorodopa is unlikely to perpetrate drug interactions.
L2403: There are no reports in the imaging literature of the effect of drugs on imaging results of Fluorodopa F 18 injection.
L2404: 2.7.6. What extrinsic factors influence exposure and/or response, and what is the impact of any differences in exposure on effectiveness or safety responses?
L2405: The effect of extrinsic factors influence on exposure and/or response is not reported.
L2406: 2.7.7. What are the drug-drug interactions?
L2407: The package inserts for carbidopa-levodopa products list drug interactions related to 1) an excess of monoamine effects that can occur due to excess synthesis and release of monoamines, and 2) lack of efficacy due to inhibition of D2 receptors which are the mechanism of action for L-DOPA's efficacy in Parkinsonian syndromes. Neither of these mechanisms are relevant to fluorodopa because the dose is too low to significantly alter monoamine transmission and imaging success is due to pre-synaptic accumulation, not occupation of D2 receptors. For these reasons drug interactions are unlikely.
L2408: 2.7.8. Does the label specify co-administration of another drug?
L2409: The applicant administered 150 mg of carbidopa orally to all patients undergoing a Fluorodopa F 18 scan sixty minute before the injection of fluorodopa. Carbidopa is an aromatic amino acid decarboxylase (AAAD) inhibitor that does not distribute to the central nervous system. It prevents peripheral metabolism of fluorodopa, allowing increased fluorodopa to be taken up into the brain.
L2410: The following (indented) is reproduced from the SINEMET (carbidopa-levodopa) package insertThe plasma half-life of levodopa is about 50 minutes, without carbidopa. When carbidopa and levodopa are administered together, the half-life of levodopa is increased to about 1.5 hours. At steady state, the bioavailability of carbidopa from SINEMET tablets is approximately 99% relative to the concomitant administration of carbidopa and levodopa.
L2411: Literature estimates of the the carbidopa dose needed to maximize L-DOPA concentrations vary from 75 mg/day to 160 mg/day.
L2412: 2.7.9. What other co-medications are likely to be administered to the target population?
L2413: Therapy for Parkinson's syndromes includes dopamine reuptake inhibitors and dopamine releasing agents (DRAs) such as psychostimulants (bupropion, vanoxerine etc) of the amphetamine class. It is recommended that patients not take dopamine drugs for 12 hours prior to Fluorodopa F 18 imaging.
L2414: 2.7.10. Is there a known mechanistic basis for pharmacodynamic drug-drug interactions?
L2415: See 2.7.7.

L2461: 1. Executive Summary
L2462: The applicant, Feinstein Institute of Medical Research (FIMR), has resubmitted (after a refusal to file in March 2010) NDA (200-655) for Flurodopa F 18 Injection as a diagnostic PET (Positron Emission Tomography) imaging “to visualize dopaminergic (b) (4) in the striatum in adult patients with suspected Parkinsonian syndromes (PS). (b) (4)
L2463: Flurodopa F 18 PET is an adjunct to other diagnostic evaluations.”
L2464: This application is supported by literature data along with one clinical trial conducted by the applicant. There was 100% correlation between the visual assessment of the PET images and the clinical diagnoses.
L2465: The proposed dose is an intravenous injection of (b) (4) 5 mCi. The selection of dose was based on the applicant’s review of literature prior to conducting their study. The applicant used a dose of 4-5 mCi for their pivotal clinical trial. The “cold” mass dose associated with this radioactive dose is (b) (4) mg.
L2466: The primary information for clinical pharmacology review for this submission is derived from fourteen published literature submitted by the applicant. No clinical pharmacology studies were conducted by FIMR.
L2467: Flurodopa F 18 undergoes decarboxylation by the enzyme aromatic amino acid decarboxylase (AAAD) to produce Fluorodopamine F 18. In order to decrease decarboxylation by AAAD, and increase bioavailability to the brain, patients are pretreated with carbidopa, an inhibitor of AAAD. The applicant selected the carbidopa dose of 150 mg based on their assessment of peer-reviewed publications.
L2468: No drug interaction studies have been reported in literature. However, it is not known whether drugs that are used to treat Parkinsonian syndromes can alter the disposition of Flurodopamine F 18 or the accumulation/retention of 18F in the striatum or rest of the brain. For this reason, the applicant’s clinical trials required patients to discontinue any Parkinsonian medications at least 12 hours prior to the PET study. The proposed package insert includes this restriction.
L2469: No special population studies or organ impairment studies have been reported.
L2470: 1.1. Recommendations
L2471: The Office of Clinical Pharmacology, Division of Clinical Pharmacology 5, has reviewed the submission NDA 203-155. The application is acceptable from a clinical pharmacology standpoint, provided an agreement is reached in labeling.
L2472: 1.2. Phase 4 Requirements and Commitments
L2473: We have no recommendations for post-marketing requirements or commitments.
L2474: 1.3. Summary of Important Clinical Pharmacology and Biopharmaceutics Findings
L2475: FIMR conducted a clinical trial to support the efficacy of Fluorodopa F 18 injection for PET imaging for the determination of the loss of functional dopaminergic neurons. All patients in the study were referred to FIMR by a movement disorder physician and exhibited at least two of the cardinal features of Parkinsonian syndromes (bradykinesia, rigidity, tremor, and/or gait disorder). Patients entered the study with a classification of “Parkinson’s Disease (PD) suspected” or “PD not suspected” from the movement disorder specialist. This clinical diagnosis by the movement disorder specialist was the standard of truth for interpretation of the PET scan results. There was 100% correlation between the visual assessment of the PET images and the clinical diagnoses. The clinical efficacy results are summarized in Table 1.
L2476: FDA Table 1. Clinical Efficacy Results
L2477: Clinical DiagnosisTotal SubjectsPossible PDPossible Non-PDVisual Assessment ResultsPOSITIVE PET Scan1580158NEGATIVE PET Scan02727
L2478: Fluorodopa F 18 is L-Dopa labeled with the short-lived radioisotope ^{18}\text{F}. The proposed package insert dose is (b) (4) 5 mCi administered intravenously. Based on literature, not their own dose finding, the applicant used a radio-dose of 4-5 mCi for the pivotal clinical trial. The total dose (primarily non-radioactive) ranged from (b) (4) to (b) (4) mg. Non-radioactive L-Dopa is currently used for the treatment of Parkinson's Disease at an oral dose that is greater than (b) (4) fold-fold higher than proposed dose.
L2479: The absorbed radiation dose for 10 human subjects (8 men and 2 women; age 50 \pm 8.8 yr) from intravenous administration of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection was estimated. Four men and one woman were normal volunteers and the remaining subjects had a diagnosis of typical/atypical Parkinsonian Syndromes. All subjects had normal renal function. The identified critical organ is the urinary bladder. Based upon version 3 of the MIRDose program, an average dose to the bladder wall in humans was estimated as 0.15 \pm 0.08 mGy/MBq (0.57 \pm 0.28 rads/mCi), the maximum bladder wall radiation dose was 0.31 mGy/MBq (1.13 rads/mCi), and the minimum was 0.07 mGy/MBq (0.27 rads/mCi). These values are below the 50-mGy (5-rad) maximum per year dose permitted by FDA for radiation workers.
L2480: Post-administration, Fluorodopa F 18 undergoes decarboxylation by the enzyme aromatic amino acid decarboxylase (AAAD) to produce Fluorodopamine F 18. In order to decrease decarboxylation by AAAD, and increase bioavailability to the brain, patients are pretreated with carbidopa, an
L2481: inhibitor of AAAD. The applicant selected the carbidopa dose of 150 mg based on their assessment of peer-reviewed publications.
L2482: 2. Question Based Review

L2488: 2.2. General Attributes of the Drug
L2489: 2.2.1. What are the highlights of the chemistry and physical-chemical properties of the drug substance and the formulation of the drug product?
L2490: Fluorodopa F 18 Injection is a small molecule labeled with 18F, a positron emitting isotope. The active ingredient 6-[18F] Fluoro-L-3, 4-dihydroxyphenylalanine has the molecular formula of C9H10FNO4 and a molecular weight of 214.18 daltons. It has the following chemical structure.
L2491: Chemical structure of fluorodopa (6-[18F]Fluoro-L-3, 4-dihydroxyphenylalanine). The structure shows a benzene ring with two hydroxyl groups at positions 3 and 4, and a fluorine atom at position 6. A propanoic acid chain is attached at position 1. The fluorine atom is at the 6-position of the ring, and the propanoic acid chain is at the 1-position. The fluorine atom is at the 6-position of the ring, and the propanoic acid chain is at the 1-position.
L2492: FDA Figure 1. Chemical structure of fluorodopa (6-[18F]Fluoro-L-3, 4-dihydroxyphenylalanine)
L2493: Fluorodopa is formulated as an aqueous solution containing acetic acid and sodium chloride. (b) (4)
L2494: The multi dose formulated drug product contains 0.0155-0.3082 GBq (0.42-8.33 mCi/mL) of fluorodopa F 18, 12.72 mg acetic acid and 108 mg sodium chloride in 12±1mL of sterile water for injection.
L2495: 2.2.2. What are the proposed mechanism of action and therapeutic indications?
L2496: Fluorodopa F 18 Injection is an analogue of L-Dopa. After intravenous injection it is transported through the blood and taken up by the brain where it is decarboxylated to fluorodopamine (18FDA) in dopaminergic neurons and stored in synaptic vesicles. Once released from the dopaminergic neuron, 18FDA may be transported back into the pre-synaptic neuron where it may be subsequently catabolized or again stored in synaptic vesicles.
L2497: 2.2.3. What are the proposed dosages and routes of administration?
L2498: The proposed dose is (b) (4) 5.0 mCi ( (b) (4) 0.185GBq) administered as (b) (4).
L2499: 2.2.3 What is the radiation absorbed dose associated with the proposed dose of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection?
L2500: The absorbed radiation dose for 10 human subjects (8 men and 2 women; age 50 ±8.8 yr) from intravenous administration of Fluorodopa F 18 Injection was estimated. Four men and one woman were normal volunteers and the remaining subjects had a diagnosis of typical/atypical Parkinsonian Syndromes. No known renal dysfunction conditions were noted among the subjects. The identified critical organ is the urinary bladder. Based upon version 3 of the MIRDOSE program, an average dose to the bladder wall in humans was found to be 0.15 ±0.08 mGy/MBq (0.57 ±0.28 rads/mCi) the maximum bladder wall radiation dose was 0.31 mGy/MBq (1.13 rads/mCi), and the minimum was 0.07 mGy/MBq (0.27 rads/mCi). These values are below the 50-mGy (5-rad) maximum dose permitted by FDA for radiation workers.
L2501: 2.2.4. What drugs (substances, products) indicated for the same indication are approved in the US?
L2502: Currently, DaTscan (Ioflupane I 123) Injection, a radio-iodinated dopamine transporter binding ligand, has been approved (b)(4) DaTscan is indicated for striatal dopamine transporter visualization using single photon emission computed tomography (SPECT) brain imaging to assist in the evaluation of adult patients with suspected Parkinsonian Syndromes (PS).
L2503: 2.3. General Clinical Pharmacology
L2504: 2.3.1. What are the design features of the clinical pharmacology and biopharmaceutics studies and the clinical studies used to support dosing or claims?
L2505: No clinical pharmacology studies were performed by the applicant to support the dosing claim. The dose selected by FIMR was based on literature reports. The report that was most relied on appeared to be that of Whone, et al. (A Technique for Standardized Central Analysis of 6-Fluoro-L-Dopa PET from a Multicenter Study. Alan L. Whone, Dale L. Bailey, Philippe Remy, Nicola Pavese, and David J. Brooks. J Nucl Med, 45, 1135-1145, 2004), where 186 patients were administered a dose of 4-4.9 mCi. The dose selected by the applicant is (b)(4) than that used in the Whone et al. study.
L2506: FIMR conducted a clinical trial to support the efficacy of Fluorodopa F 18. All patients in the study were referred to FIMR by a movement disorder physician and exhibited at least two of the cardinal features of Parkinsonian syndromes (bradykinesia, rigidity, tremor, and/or gait disorder). Patients entered the study with a classification of “Parkinson’s Disease (PD) suspected” or “PD not suspected” from the movement disorder specialist. This clinical diagnosis by the movement disorder specialist was the standard of truth for interpretation of the PET scan results. There was 100% correlation between the visual assessment of the PET images and the clinical diagnoses. The clinical efficacy results are summarized in Table 1, below.

L2518: Subjects were followed for 3-4 years to determine the accuracy of the PET results. Follow-up confirmed the 100% accurate evaluation (applicant's analysis).
L2519: 2.3.2. What is the basis for selecting the response endpoints and how are they measured in clinical pharmacology studies?
L2520: 2.3.3. Are the active moieties in plasma and clinically relevant tissues appropriately identified and measured to assess pharmacokinetic parameters and exposure response relationships?
L2521: The active moieties in plasma and clinically relevant tissues were not identified, only 18F was monitored. No exposure-response relationship was studied.
L2522: 2.4. Exposure-Response
L2523: 2.4.1. What are the characteristics of the exposure-response relationship for effectiveness?
L2524: 2.4.2. What are the characteristics of the exposure-response relationships for safety?
L2525: Exposure-response, including dose-response, relationships were not studied.
L2526: Effective imaging depends on the distribution of 18F. Preferential distribution to the striatum is the baseline condition that allows for effective imaging. This preferential distribution is determined by comparing the Fluorodopa uptake in the striatum to that of the occipital cortex (the occipital value is used as background). A successful scan has a sufficiently high striatal-to-occipital ratio (SOR). Within the striatum, pathology (e.g., Parkinsonian syndrome), reduces accumulation 18F. A positive image, i.e., an image where a diagnosis of “Parkinsonian syndrome” is made, lacks the intensity of signal in the striatum that occurs in the absence of pathology.
L2527: 2.4.3. Does this drug prolong QT/QTc Interval?
L2528: The applicant has not provided any information regarding the effect of fluorodopa to prolong QT/QTc interval. However, the mass amount of fluorodopa administered is less than (b)(4) mg. The clinical dose used to treat Parkinsons patients is 1500-1700 mg daily, or &gt; (b)(4) X that used for imaging. We do not expect any effect of Fluorodopa F 18 on QT/QTc prolongation.
L2529: 2.4.4. Is the dose and dosing regimen selected consistent with the known E-R relationship?
L2530: No exposure-response relationship was determined.
L2531: 2.5. Pharmacokinetics
L2532: 2.5.1. What are the single and multiple dose PK parameters of parent drug and relevant metabolites in healthy adults?
L2533: 2.5.2. How does the PK of the drug and its relevant metabolites in healthy adults compare to that in patients with the target disease?
L2534: 2.5.3. What are the inter- and intra-subject variability of the PK parameters in volunteers and patients with the target disease?
L2535: Pharmacokinetics were not performed by the applicant. The SINEMET (carbidopa-levodopa) package insert states that the plasma half-life of levodopa is about 50 minutes, without carbidopa.
L2536: The clinical study was conducted with administration of 150 mg of carbidopa one hour prior to administration of Fluorodopa F 18. The effect of carbidopa will be discussed in Section 2.7.8.
L2537: 2.5.4. What are the characteristics of drug absorption?
L2538: Fluorodopa F 18 Injection is administered as a single-time intravenous injection.
L2539: 2.5.5. What are the characteristics of drug distribution?
L2540: At 20 minutes post-injection, less than 50% of initial radioactivity is left in plasma. Imaging is performed one hour post-administration.
L2541: 2.5.6. Does the mass balance study suggest renal or hepatic as the major route of elimination?
L2542: Mass balance study results are not reported.
L2543: 2.5.7. What is the percentage of total radioactivity in plasma identified as parent drug and metabolites?
L2544: Firnau et al (J. Nucl Med. 29, 363-369, 1988), detected four metabolites in human plasma after injection of Fluorodopa F 18: 6-[18F]fluoro-O-sulfato-L-dopa, 6-[18F]fluorodopamine, 3-O-methyl-6-[18F]fluorodopa, and 6-[18F]fluorohomovanillic acid. Estimation of the percentage of total radioactivity from each of the moieties is not reported. One of the metabolites, 3-O-methyl-6-[18F]fluorodopa, appears to not accumulate in the striatal region. The contribution of the other moieties to PET imaging is unknown. Although there were quantitative differences, the metabolites identified from 6-fluoro-L-dopa in the blood were found to be the same as those identified following administration of 14-carbon-L-dopa, suggesting qualitatively similar metabolic pathway.
L2545: 2.5.8. What are the characteristics of drug metabolism?
L2546: FDOPA is a substrate for AAAD and catechol-O-methyltransferase (COMT). In the striatum, FDOPA is decarboxylated to fluorodopamine by AAAD in presynaptic dopaminergic terminals. Plasma FDOPA is also converted to 3-OMFD in the periphery.
L2547: 2.5.9. Is there evidence for excretion of parent drug and/or metabolites into bile?
L2548: Excretion into bile or feces was not studied.
L2549: 2.5.10. Is there evidence for enterohepatic recirculation for parent and/or metabolites?
L2550: There is not evidence for enterohepatic recirculation for parent and/or metabolites, but only limited concentration-time data are reported, and data following non-IV administration are not reported.
L2551: 2.5.11. What are the characteristics of drug excretion in urine?
L2552: Dosimetry study indicated that bladder was the target organ and that a majority of radioactivity was excreted through bladder. However, the percentages of parent and metabolites excreted in urine were not determined.
L2553: 2.5.12. Based on PK parameters, what is the degree of the proportionality of the dose-concentration relationship?
L2554: Dose proportionality results are not reported.
L2555: 2.5.13. How do the PK parameters change with time following chronic dosing?
L2556: Fluorodopa F 18 Injection is administered only one time for diagnostic imaging. Repeat dosing results are not reported.
L2557: 2.5.14. Is there evidence for a circadian rhythm of the PK?
L2558: There is not evidence for a circadian rhythm of the PK, but only limited concentration-time data are reported, and data are not reported by time-of-day.

L2579: 2.7. Extrinsic Factors
L2580: 2.7.1. Is there an in vitro basis to suspect in vivo drug-drug interactions?
L2581: In vitro investigation of drug metabolism is not reported.
L2582: Inhibitors of monoamine uptake, such as anti-depressants, could potentially alter striatal accumulation of 18F by inhibiting transport of any dopamine 18F derived from fluoro dopa 18F.
L2583: 2.7.2. Is the drug a substrate of CYP enzymes?
L2584: 2.7.3. Is the drug an inhibitor and/or an inducer of enzymes?
L2585: In vitro investigation of drug metabolism is not reported.
L2586: 2.7.4. Is the drug a substrate, an inhibitor and/or an inducer of transporter processes?
L2587: The ability of flurorodopa to act as an a substrate, an inhibitor and/or an inducer of transporter processes is not reported.
L2588: 2.7.5. Are there other metabolic/transporter pathways that may be important?
L2589: No; the processes discussed in Section 2.5.8 are the only processes likely to be important (see also, 2.7.7.).
L2590: The mass dose of fluorodopa is (b) (4) mg. The clinical dose of levodopa is up to 1700 mg daily, and levodopa has not been reported to perpetrate drug interactions. Thus, the current (b) (4) mg dose of fluorodopa is unlikely to perpetrate drug interactions.
L2591: There are no reports in the imaging literature of the effect of drugs on imaging results of Fluorodopa F 18 injection.
L2592: 2.7.6. What extrinsic factors influence exposure and/or response, and what is the impact of any differences in exposure on effectiveness or safety responses?
L2593: The effect of extrinsic factors influence on exposure and/or response is not reported.
L2594: 2.7.7. What are the drug-drug interactions?
L2595: The package inserts for carbidopa-levodopa products list drug interactions related to 1) an excess of monoamine effects that can occur due to excess synthesis and release of monoamines, and 2) lack of efficacy due to inhibition of D2 receptors which are the mechanism of action for L-DOPA's efficacy in Parkinsonian syndromes. Neither of these mechanisms are relevant to fluorodopa because the dose is too low to significantly alter monoamine transmission and imaging success is due to pre-synaptic accumulation, not occupation of D2 receptors. For these reasons drug interactions are unlikely.
L2596: 2.7.8. Does the label specify co-administration of another drug?
L2597: The applicant administered 150 mg of carbidopa orally to all patients undergoing a Fluorodopa F 18 scan sixty minute before the injection of fluorodopa. Carbidopa is an aromatic amino acid decarboxylase (AAAD) inhibitor that does not distribute to the central nervous system. It prevents peripheral metabolism of fluorodopa, allowing increased fluorodopa to be taken up into the brain.
L2598: The following (indented) is reproduced from the SINEMET (carbidopa-levodopa) package insertThe plasma half-life of levodopa is about 50 minutes, without carbidopa. When carbidopa and levodopa are administered together, the half-life of levodopa is increased to about 1.5 hours. At steady state, the bioavailability of carbidopa from SINEMET tablets is approximately 99% relative to the concomitant administration of carbidopa and levodopa.
L2599: Literature estimates of the the carbidopa dose needed to maximize L-DOPA concentrations vary from 75 mg/day to 160 mg/day.
L2600: 2.7.9. What other co-medications are likely to be administered to the target population?
L2601: Therapy for Parkinson's syndromes includes dopamine reuptake inhibitors and dopamine releasing agents (DRAs) such as psychostimulants (bupropion, vanoxerine etc) of the amphetamine class. It is recommended that patients not take dopamine drugs for 12 hours prior to Fluorodopa F 18 imaging.
L2602: 2.7.10. Is there a known mechanistic basis for pharmacodynamic drug-drug interactions?
L2603: See 2.7.7.
